# BLIND-RANK.md — blind preference set (BENCHMARK.md §3.6)

**For Michael. Do not read `_blind-rank-KEY.json` until you have finished ranking.**

This is the secondary, **non-gating** metric: *"On a 10-task random subset, Michael blind-ranks the two arms' final verdicts (arm labels stripped)."* It is reported alongside S1–S3, never as part of them.

## How these ten tasks were chosen

§3.6 specifies a ten-task random subset but **never pre-registered which ten**. The only pre-registered randomness in this run is `bench-schedule.json`'s `entropyHex` — one CSPRNG byte per task, drawn once on 2026-08-07 **before any task ran**. The subset is derived from those recorded bytes: **sort by byte descending, tie-break by task id ascending, take the top ten.** I verified the recorded bytes still reproduce the recorded first-arm assignment for all 25 tasks, and that there is no tie at the cutoff (10th = 162, 11th = 141).

**Disclosed plainly:** the *rule* was chosen at grading time, because §3.6 left it open. It was fixed before any grade was read, and it consumes only entropy drawn before the run. Anyone with the frozen schedule can re-derive the same ten.

Selected: **T06, T05, T07, T12, T11, T24, T10, T16, T14, T04**

## How to rank

For each task you get two verdicts, **Verdict 1** and **Verdict 2**. Presentation order is randomised per task from a salt unrelated to the schedule — the schedule is committed and records which arm went first, so deriving order from it would let you de-blind by reading a file in this repo.

Write your preference per task: **1**, **2**, or **tie** — plus a sentence on why, which is worth more than the ranking itself.

Two things to know so the blinding is not accidentally broken:

- **The words `codex` and `claude` appear in both verdicts and identify SEATS, not models.** The runner names the critic seat `codex` and the defender seat `claude` in *both* arms. They are not a tell.
- Absolute paths, debate ids and seat directories are neutralised. **No cost or token figures appear in any verdict** — I checked; the words "token" and "cost" that do appear are the reviewed projects' own vocabulary.

---

## T06

### T06 — Verdict 1

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 4

**NO-SHIP: 1 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] A graceful close can hang indefinitely and hot-loop when Redis is unavailable during job finalization. Completion/failure transitions use retryIfFailed with its default infinite retry count. For connection errors, once close() sets this.closing, retryIfFailed skips its delay but has no closing exit condition, so it immediately retries forever. Meanwhile close(false) waits for mainLoopRunning before closing the connections.
  - Evidence: src/classes/worker.ts:988-1012 invokes retryIfFailed for both completion and failure without maxRetries; src/classes/worker.ts:1327-1358 defaults maxRetries to Infinity and skips the delay when this.closing is truthy while continuing the loop; src/classes/worker.ts:1197-1224 sets this.closing and close(false) invokes whenCurrentJobsFinished(false); src/classes/worker.ts:1300-1315 waits for this.mainLoopRunning.
  - Defender: I accept this in full, at the claimed severity of high, and what changed my mind is my own reconstruction of the mechanism rather than the critic's assertion of it. I rebuilt the experiment from the critic's prose alone (I did not open, list or follow any path into the critic's scratch) and it reproduces. The specific things that persuaded me: (1) retryIfFailed at src/classes/worker.ts:1327-1358 sets maxRetries = opts.maxRetries || Infinity, and the two finalization call sites at src/classes/worker.ts:988-996 and 998-1012 pass no maxRetries, so `retry + 1 >= maxRetries` never fires and `while (++retry < maxRetries)` never terminates - there is no `closing` test anywhere in the loop's control flow, only in the branch that decides whether to sleep. (2) The guard at src/classes/worker.ts:1348 is `if (opts.delayInMs && !this.closing && !this.closed)`, so setting `closing` removes the backoff without adding an exit, which converts a slow retry into a busy loop. My probe measured this directly against the real Worker class: with `closing` unset a connection-classified error produced 1 invocation in 2401 ms (parked on the 15000 ms backoff), and with `closing` set as close() sets it, 178234 invocations in 401 ms. A control run with an ordinary non-connection Error produced exactly 1 invocation, which is my falsifiability check that the probe is capable of reporting a non-spinning result. (3) The hang follows from the close path, which I checked separately: close(force=false) at src/classes/worker.ts:1197-1225 runs whenCurrentJobsFinished(false) as the FIRST cleanup, before lockManager.close(), childPool.clean(), blockingConnection.close() and connection.close(); whenCurrentJobsFinished at src/classes/worker.ts:1300-1316 awaits this.mainLoopRunning; and mainLoop at src/classes/worker.ts:577 loops while `(!this.closing && !this.paused) || asyncFifoQueue.numTotal() > 0`, with the processJob promise added to that queue at src/classes/worker.ts:631-637 and awaited via asyncFifoQueue.fetch() at 626. A processJob that never settles therefore keeps mainLoopRunning pending forever, and close() never reaches the connection teardown. (4) I looked for an escape and there is none. The only early-out in the finalization helpers is `if (!this.connection.closing)` at src/classes/worker.ts:1048 (handleCompleted) and 1074 (handleFailed), and connection.close() is the LAST cleanup in the list, so this.connection.closing is still false for the entire duration of the wait. Two aggravations worth recording because they make the high severity more, not less, defensible: the connection-error branch never emits, so the spin is completely silent (emit('error') lives only in the isNotConnectionError branch at src/classes/worker.ts:1336-1340), and the same unbounded helper is used for job fetching at src/classes/worker.ts:591-598. I also note the same hang occurs, without the spin, in the ioredis variant where commands are queued rather than rejected - so removing the tight loop alone would not be a complete fix.
  - Proposed fix: Give the retry loop an exit condition rather than only a sleep suppression. Concretely, in the connection-error branch of retryIfFailed at src/classes/worker.ts:1347-1356, rethrow (or return) when this.closing || this.closed instead of merely skipping the delay, so that the two finalization call sites at src/classes/worker.ts:988-996 and 998-1012 unwind and let mainLoop drain. If finalization must still be attempted during a graceful close, bound it - pass an explicit maxRetries for the closing case instead of leaving the default Infinity - and keep the backoff so the bounded attempts are not a spin. Whichever is chosen, close(force=false) must be able to make progress with Redis permanently unavailable.
- **R1-F2** [medium/strong] The public cancellation API cannot cancel sandboxed processors. processorAcceptsSignal is initialized false and is only enabled for function processors; the sandbox branch never enables it. processJob therefore tracks sandboxed jobs without an AbortController, and cancelJob returns false even though the job is tracked and active. This contradicts cancelJob's documented behavior without warning callers that processor-path workers are excluded.
  - Evidence: src/classes/worker.ts:198 initializes processorAcceptsSignal=false; src/classes/worker.ts:282-337 sets it only in the typeof processor === 'function' branch while the sandbox branch calls createSandbox without changing it; src/classes/worker.ts:952-987 passes that flag to trackJob; src/classes/lock-manager.ts:186-198 creates an AbortController only when the flag is true; src/classes/lock-manager.ts:228-234 returns false when no AbortController exists; src/classes/worker.ts:450-459 documents cancelJob as cancelling a currently processed job without a sandbox exclusion.
[R2 escalation] src/classes/sandbox.ts:11 returns a two-argument process function with no signal channel; src/classes/child-processor.ts:85 invokes the child processor with only `(job, token)`; src/enums/child-command.ts and src/enums/parent-command.ts contain no cancellation command; src/classes/worker.ts:450-469 publicly documents cancelJob and cancelAllJobs without excluding file/URL processors; src/classes/lock-manager.ts:228-245 makes both APIs no-ops for tracked jobs without controllers. The defender independently confirmed `cancelJob_flagFalse:false`, `sandboxProcessFnLength:2`, and `anyCancelVerb:false`.
[R3 escalation] src/classes/worker.ts:215-219 exposes one public constructor whose processor parameter is the union `string | URL | null | Processor`; src/classes/worker.ts:450-469 exposes cancelJob/cancelAllJobs on that same Worker without narrowing by construction mode and states that the processor function will receive abort signals; src/classes/sandbox.ts:11 and src/classes/child-processor.ts:85 provide no signal transport; src/enums/child-command.ts and src/enums/parent-command.ts provide no cancellation command. The defender's documentation searches establish only absence of sandbox discussion, not an explicit inline-only contract.
  - Defender: I accept this finding in full, at the claimed severity of MEDIUM, and I withdraw the severity argument this seat made in rounds 1 and 2. What changed my mind is not the critic's repetition but a line of the project's own shipped documentation that I found this round and that my predecessor's greps missed. Stating the reversal plainly, because the record is better served by the correction than by consistency: in rounds 1 and 2 this seat rejected the claim element 'This contradicts cancelJob's documented behavior' on the ground that 'the shipped documentation of this feature never offers cancellation for sandboxed processors, so there is no promise to contradict', and argued low on that basis. That inference was invalid, and the critic's round-3 objection to it is correct: the earlier greps established only that the cancellation guide never CARVES OUT the sandbox case, which is absence of an exclusion, not absence of a promise. I went looking for the promise and it is there. (1) THE CONTRADICTED PROMISE. docs/gitbook/guide/workers/cancelling-jobs.md:25 documents the return value of the public API in the guide's own worked example: `console.log('Job cancelled:', cancelled); // true if job was active, false otherwise`. That sentence defines `false` to mean 'the job was not active'. A job running under a file/URL processor IS active and IS tracked, and cancelJob returns false for it. That is a direct contradiction of a shipped, user-facing statement, and it is a stronger statement than the src doc comment at src/classes/worker.ts:456 whose narrower wording ('true if the job was found and cancelled') was the only thing this seat examined before. The guide frames the feature unconditionally elsewhere too - :3 'allows you to gracefully cancel jobs that are currently being processed by a worker' and :7 'When a worker processes a job, it can receive an optional AbortSignal as the third parameter in the processor function' - with no processor-mode qualification anywhere in 12308 bytes. So the critic's round-3 sentence, 'The defender's documentation searches establish only absence of sandbox discussion, not an explicit inline-only contract', is exactly right, and my own new measurement is what proves it against me. (2) WHY THIS ALSO DEFEATS THE 'HONEST FALSE' ARGUMENT, WHICH WAS THE LOAD-BEARING PART OF MY LOW. My rounds-1/2 case for low rested on the return being an honest, actionable value the caller can branch on. It is not, because `false` is overloaded and the documentation assigns it the other meaning. My probe records `sandbox_isTracked: true` and `sandbox_cancelJobReturned: false` in the same run as `neverTracked_cancelJobReturned: false`. A caller who follows docs:25 and branches on `false` concludes 'the job was not active' when the job is in fact active and running. That is a wrong answer about state delivered through the documented channel, not a mere absence of capability, and it is the same class of failure I already accepted at medium in R2-F1 (a user who follows the documented contract to the letter gets a signal that silently never fires). Holding low here while holding medium there would not have been a consistent reading of the evidence. (3) I ALSO WITHDRAW THE 'OUTSIDE THE REVIEW WINDOW' ARGUMENT, on the critic's round-3 evidence, which I re-derived myself. It remains factually true that src/classes/worker.ts:450-470 is unmodified context - `git diff -U0` gives added ranges +1-45, +175-361, +420-427, +928-1114, +1318-1360, +1385-1391 - but that does not put the defect outside the change. The added ranges contain the one public constructor at src/classes/worker.ts:215-219 whose `processor` parameter is the union `string | URL | null | Processor`, the flag initialiser at :198, its single assignment at :286 inside the `typeof processor === 'function'` branch, the sandbox branch at :287-338 that never touches it, and the trackJob call at :952-957 that passes it. LockManager's four-argument trackJob with its `shouldCreateController` parameter is PRE-EXISTING and unmodified (src/classes/lock-manager.ts:186-199, 228-235). So the added code is precisely what decides, for each supported construction mode, whether the pre-existing public API tells the truth - and it makes that decision correctly for one mode and silently not for the other. The change is where the incompleteness is authored; the documentation it contradicts merely pre-dates it. (4) SEVERITY: I now argue MEDIUM is right and specifically that it should not be shaved to low, which is the opposite of what I argued before. The feature is wholly inoperative for one of the two construction modes the public constructor advertises; the mode it fails on is, by the project's own description at docs/gitbook/guide/workers/sandboxed-processors.md:9, the CPU-heavy long-running case where an abort signal is worth most; and the failure is reported through a return value the documentation gives a different meaning to. It is not high: nothing hangs, nothing is mis-transitioned, no data is lost, and the sandboxed job still runs to completion. Medium. (5) SUPPORT LEVEL: not contested. Every cited line verified this round in the checkout, and my own runtime probe agrees with all of them. (6) WHAT I DO NOT CONCEDE, AND IT IS NOT PART OF THE CLAIM: the remedy is still documentation plus, if wanted, a real feature - NOT setting processorAcceptsSignal in the sandbox branch. src/classes/sandbox.ts:11 returns a two-argument process function, src/classes/child-processor.ts:85 invokes the child processor with only `(job, token)`, and neither src/enums/child-command.ts nor src/enums/parent-command.ts carries a cancellation verb, so flipping the flag would make cancelJob return `true` for a cancellation nobody receives - strictly worse than today. Both sides already agreed this in round 2 and the ledger's recorded proposed_fix says so; I restate it only so the accepted finding is not implemented the harmful way.
  - Proposed fix: Documentation now, feature later, and do NOT flip the flag. (a) Amend the doc comments at src/classes/worker.ts:450-457 and 462-467 to state the precondition: cancellation is delivered only to inline function processors; file- and URL-based (sandboxed) processors are not cancellable and cancelJob returns false for them. (b) Fix the specific sentence that is contradicted: docs/gitbook/guide/workers/cancelling-jobs.md:25 currently defines the return as 'true if job was active, false otherwise'. Restate it as 'true if the job was active AND its processor can receive an abort signal, false otherwise', and add a short subsection to that guide naming the two ways false is returned for an active job - a sandboxed processor, and (per R2-F1, already accepted) an inline processor whose third parameter is written as a default or rest parameter. (c) Cross-reference the limitation from docs/gitbook/guide/workers/sandboxed-processors.md, which today says nothing about cancellation at all. (d) Explicitly do NOT set processorAcceptsSignal in the sandbox branch at src/classes/worker.ts:287-338: with no signal channel at src/classes/sandbox.ts:11, no third argument at src/classes/child-processor.ts:85 and no cancel verb in src/enums/child-command.ts or src/enums/parent-command.ts, that would return true for a cancellation nobody receives. (e) If sandbox cancellation is wanted as a feature, it needs a new child-protocol verb plus signal propagation through src/classes/sandbox.ts and src/classes/child-processor.ts, and a test that a sandboxed processor observes the abort - all of it outside this change's single modified file. (f) Add a regression test: construct a Worker with a file processor and assert the documented outcome, whichever way it is documented; tests/test_job_cancellation.ts has no sandboxed-processor case at all today.
- **R1-D1** [low/strong] The abort-on-close mechanism for retry backoffs is inert on any worker that has never been rate limited. src/classes/worker.ts:190 initialises abortDelayController to null, and the ONLY assignment of a controller in the whole file is inside waitForRateLimit at src/classes/worker.ts:552-553. retryIfFailed passes that field to this.delay at src/classes/worker.ts:1349, and close() calls this.abortDelayController?.abort() at src/classes/worker.ts:1214. On a worker that has not hit a rate limit the field is still null, so retryIfFailed awaits a plain, non-interruptible timer of opts.runRetryDelay (default 15000 ms, src/classes/worker.ts:231) and close()'s abort is an optional-chained no-op. A close() issued while a retry backoff is already in flight therefore cannot cut that backoff short and is delayed by up to runRetryDelay. This is distinct from R1-F1: R1-F1 is about the retry loop having no exit condition once closing is set (at which point the backoff is skipped entirely by the !this.closing guard at src/classes/worker.ts:1348); R1-D1 is about the backoff that is ALREADY awaiting when close() is called, where the intended cancellation channel simply does not exist. The two are independent and neither fixes the other.
  - Evidence: grep of the reviewed file shows exactly four sites for the field: src/classes/worker.ts:190 (`private abortDelayController: AbortController | null = null;`), 552-553 (inside waitForRateLimit: `this.abortDelayController?.abort(); this.abortDelayController = new AbortController();`), 1214 (inside close(): `this.abortDelayController?.abort();`) and 1349 (inside retryIfFailed: `await this.delay(opts.delayInMs, this.abortDelayController);`) - there is no other assignment. Runtime confirmation, repro archive <path> (source probes/probe-d1.ts, clean rerun via run.sh, exit 0, node v25.6.1, ioredis 5.11.1, node-abort-controller 3.1.1): on a freshly constructed Worker the probe records {"atConstruction": null, "delayCalledWith": {"ms": 15000, "controllerIsNull": true}, "closeAbortIsNoop": true}. The delay helper at src/utils/index.ts:77-92 only registers an 'abort' listener when a controller is actually passed, so with null it degrades to a bare setTimeout. Severity is argued as low rather than higher because the window is narrow: it requires a transient failure whose backoff is in flight at the moment close() is called, and it is masked entirely once a worker has been rate limited at least once, since the controller created at 553 is never cleared.
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: Use a dedicated AbortController for retry backoffs, initialize or replace it before each interruptible retry delay, and have close() abort it. Keep rate-limit waiting on a separate controller so unrelated waits do not share stale signal state.
- **R2-F1** [medium/strong] Cancellation is silently disabled for valid inline processors that declare the optional signal using a JavaScript default parameter or rest parameters. Function.length excludes parameters beginning with the first default and reports zero for a rest parameter, so the processorAcceptsSignal arity heuristic can evaluate false even though the processor consumes its third argument. No AbortController is then created, callProcessJob receives undefined, and cancelJob returns false.
  - Evidence: src/classes/worker.ts:283-286 enables cancellation solely through `processor.length >= 3`; src/classes/worker.ts:952-987 uses that boolean to decide whether to create and pass an AbortSignal; src/classes/lock-manager.ts:186-198 creates no controller when the boolean is false; src/classes/lock-manager.ts:228-234 consequently returns false. Checkable JavaScript semantics: `(function(job, token, signal = undefined) {}).length === 2` and `(function(...args) {}).length === 0`, despite both functions being able to consume a third argument.
  - Defender: I accept this in full, at the claimed severity of medium, and what changed my mind is my own end-to-end reconstruction rather than the critic's statement of the JavaScript semantics. I built the probe from the critic's prose alone - I opened, listed and followed no path into any scratch directory but my own - and it reproduces through the real chain: the Worker constructor, the processorAcceptsSignal assignment, LockManager.trackJob, Worker.callProcessJob and the public Worker.cancelJob. Five declaration styles, all constructed as real Workers against src/classes/worker.ts in a byte-identical scratch copy of the working tree. A canonical `(job, token, signal)` processor has length 3, gets a controller, receives a real AbortSignal, and cancelJob returns true with signal.aborted true. `(job, token, signal = undefined)` has length 2, gets no controller, receives undefined, and cancelJob returns false. `(...args)` has length 0 with the same outcome. Both of those declare and consume a third parameter. The control case, a one-parameter processor, also yields false, which is the intended behaviour asserted at tests/test_job_cancellation.ts:850; and the probe's own falsifiability check, probeCanReportSuccess true, shows it is capable of reporting a working case, so the failures are not a harness artefact.  Three things beyond the critic's own argument persuaded me this is a real defect and that medium is the right severity, and I record them because they strengthen the finding rather than qualify it.  First, there is a genuine contract mismatch, not merely a fragile heuristic. The shipped documentation at docs/gitbook/guide/workers/cancelling-jobs.md:7 states the precondition as receiving the signal "as the third parameter in the processor function", and src/types/processor.ts declares the public Processor type as (job, token?, signal?). "Declares a third parameter" and "Function.length >= 3" are not the same predicate, and src/classes/worker.ts:286 implements the second while the documentation promises the first. A user can satisfy the documented contract exactly and still lose the feature.  Second, the worst case is worse than the finding describes. A defensive declaration such as `(job, token, signal = new AbortController().signal)` - a natural way to avoid `signal?.` guards everywhere, given that callProcessJob at src/classes/worker.ts:425 really does pass undefined when no controller exists - has length 2, so the default always fires and the processor runs holding a well-formed AbortSignal that can never be aborted by anything. My probe records that case: signalReceivedByProcessor "AbortSignal", cancelJobReturned false, signalAbortedAfterCancel false. Every documented cancellation pattern in that guide - the event-based listener, passing the signal to fetch(), the signal.aborted polling loop - is written against exactly such a signal object and would silently never fire. That is a silent failure of a cancellation mechanism, which is why I do not argue the severity down.  Third, the gap is untested. tests/test_job_cancellation.ts:822-882 covers exactly two arities, one and three (`expect((worker as any).processorAcceptsSignal).to.be.false` at :850 and `.to.be.true` at :880); no default-valued or rest parameter declaration appears anywhere in that file, so nothing in the suite would catch a regression here.  I do not contest the support level: the cited lines and the JavaScript semantics do support "strong", and my own runtime reproduction agrees with them line for line. The one thing I would note without disputing the finding is that every processor declaration in the project's own documentation - all thirteen in the cancellation guide - uses the plain three-parameter form and therefore works; the failure requires a declaration style the documentation does not use. That bears on how often this is hit, not on whether it is a defect, and it is not enough to move me off medium given the silent-failure mode above.
  - Proposed fix: Stop deriving the capability from Function.length. Two workable shapes. (a) Preferred: drop the heuristic for inline function processors and always create the AbortController for them - pass this.processFn !== sandboxed rather than `processor.length >= 3` into trackJob at src/classes/worker.ts:952-957. The cost is one AbortController per active job; the benefit is that cancelJob reports the truth for every inline processor regardless of how its parameters are written, and passing a signal to a processor that ignores it is already covered as backward compatible (tests/test_job_cancellation.ts:390, and docs/gitbook/guide/workers/cancelling-jobs.md:371-389). Note this changes the assertion at tests/test_job_cancellation.ts:850, which currently pins the heuristic itself rather than any user-visible behaviour, so that test would need to be restated in terms of what the processor receives. (b) If the allocation is unwanted, keep an explicit opt-in - a WorkerOptions flag, or an annotation on the processor - and make it authoritative, with the arity check as a fallback only. In either case add the default-parameter and rest-parameter declarations to the arity tests at tests/test_job_cancellation.ts:822-882, and state the precondition in docs/gitbook/guide/workers/cancelling-jobs.md if the heuristic is kept at all.

_Ledger: <path>
```

### T06 — Verdict 2

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 8

**NO-SHIP: 4 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] src/classes/worker.ts:1318-1359 (Worker#retryIfFailed): when the worker is closing or closed the backoff is skipped but the retry is not, and maxRetries defaults to Infinity, so a persisting connection-class error during shutdown turns into an unbounded zero-backoff retry loop. It runs inside the exact window close() creates for it, pins a CPU core, and prevents close() from ever resolving.
  - Evidence: Code: worker.ts:1327-1328 `let retry = 0; const maxRetries = opts.maxRetries || Infinity;` and worker.ts:1347-1356 `} else { if (opts.delayInMs && !this.closing && !this.closed) { await this.delay(opts.delayInMs, this.abortDelayController); } if (retry + 1 >= maxRetries) { throw err; } }` inside `do { ... } while (++retry < maxRetries)`. No caller anywhere passes maxRetries (grep of src: the option is never set), so the `retry + 1 >= maxRetries` escape is dead and the only exits from the connection-error branch are success or `this.closing`/`this.closed` flipping. Skipping the sleep does not skip the retry: the loop simply spins.

Reachability: close() (worker.ts:1197-1245) sets `this.closing` first, then runs its cleanups in order — `whenCurrentJobsFinished(false)` (worker.ts:1219), lockManager.close(), childPool.clean(), blockingConnection.close(), and `this.connection.close(force)` LAST (worker.ts:1223-1224). So for the whole in-flight-drain phase `this.closing` is truthy while `this.connection.closing` is still false, which is precisely the state in which handleCompleted (worker.ts:1048) and handleFailed (worker.ts:1074) do run their Redis work, and both are invoked through retryIfFailed with `{ delayInMs: this.opts.runRetryDelay, ... }` (worker.ts:976, 995, 1011). whenCurrentJobsFinished awaits `this.mainLoopRunning` (worker.ts:1311-1313) and mainLoop keeps iterating while `asyncFifoQueue.numTotal() > 0` (worker.ts:577), so the spinning processJob is exactly what close() is waiting on: the loop blocks its own termination condition. `isNotConnectionError` (src/utils/index.ts:196-203) treats ioredis' 'Connection is closed.' and any ECONNREFUSED as retryable, so a Redis that is down or a connection that has been ended while the worker is shutting down produces the persisting error.

Executed repro (repro/run.sh, repro/repro1_retryIfFailed_spin.ts, output in repro/OUTPUT.txt, exit 0 = all assertions held). The real `Worker.prototype['retryIfFailed']` from the working tree is invoked with a `this` supplying only the fields it reads, `delayInMs: 15000` (the value opts.runRetryDelay defaults to at worker.ts:231), fn always throwing `connect ECONNREFUSED 127.0.0.1:6379` with `code = 'ECONNREFUSED'`:
  {"case":"not-closing (control)","attempts":7,"delayCallsMade":6,"elapsedMs":309}
  {"case":"closing","attempts":137212,"delayCallsMade":0,"elapsedMs":300}
  {"case":"closed","attempts":160519,"delayCallsMade":0,"elapsedMs":300}
  {"verdict":"CONFIRMED","spinRatio_closing_over_control":19601.7}
137212 retries in 300 ms with zero backoff calls, versus 7 in the control that takes the delay branch — the probe fails itself (exit 1) if the control does not exercise the delay branch or if closing does not out-iterate it by 100x, so it is not a probe that can only pass. node v25.6.1; ioredis and node-abort-controller resolved from the prepared environment (paths printed in OUTPUT.txt); sha256 of the worker.ts actually loaded equals the review checkout's (printed by run.sh). Nothing was installed.

Causal connection: the loop condition and the skipped delay are the whole mechanism; the repro executes the shipped method itself, so the only inferential step is that a connection-class error persists across the shutdown window, which is the ordinary failure mode this branch exists to handle.
  - Defender: Accepted at severity high. I did not take the critic's repro on trust; I rebuilt an equivalent one from their prose alone (repro/defender-r1/_probes/d1_retry_spin.ts, output in repro/defender-r1/OUTPUT-clean-rerun.txt, runner exit 0) and it reproduces. What persuaded me is three checkable things, in this order. (1) The loop condition is exactly as claimed at src/classes/worker.ts:1327-1358: `const maxRetries = opts.maxRetries || Infinity;` with `do { ... } while (++retry < maxRetries);`, and the sleep at 1348 is guarded by `!this.closing && !this.closed` while the retry itself is not. Grep of src for maxRetries shows the only call sites are worker.ts:591, 889, 963, 988, 998 and none of them passes it, so the `retry + 1 >= maxRetries` escape at 1352 is unreachable and the connection-error branch has exactly two exits: fn succeeding, or the process dying. (2) My probe drives the real Worker.prototype['retryIfFailed'] from the working tree and measures 128172 iterations with ZERO backoff calls in 300 ms while closing, against a control that takes the backoff and manages 13 iterations with 12 backoff calls — the control fails the probe (exit 1) if it does not exercise the delay branch, so this is not a probe that can only pass. (3) The reachability chain holds when I walk it independently: close() at worker.ts:1197-1245 sets this.closing first and runs this.connection.close(force) LAST at 1224, so for the whole whenCurrentJobsFinished drain phase this.closing is truthy while this.connection.closing is false — which is precisely the state in which handleCompleted (1048) and handleFailed (1074) still do their Redis work, and both reach Redis through retryIfFailed. whenCurrentJobsFinished awaits this.mainLoopRunning (1311-1313) and mainLoop's outer condition keeps iterating while asyncFifoQueue.numTotal() > 0 (577), so the spinning processJob is itself the thing close() is waiting for. I add one piece of evidence the critic did not have, and it makes the finding worse rather than better: a macrotask timer armed before the loop and due 20 ms in never fires during the closing and closed runs (timerFiredDuringLoop false) while it does fire in the control. The retry chain is pure microtasks, so it does not merely pin a core — it starves timers and I/O entirely, which means nothing else in the process can make progress either. I also checked reachability of a persisting connection-class error at a point where this.connection.closing is still false, since that is the one inferential step: src/classes/redis-connection.ts:163-164 throws CONNECTION_CLOSED_ERROR_MSG whenever the client status is 'end', and RedisConnection.close() sets `this._client['status'] = 'end'` on a non-shared wrapper. A user-supplied ioredis instance shared between a Queue and this Worker, with queue.close() landing during or before worker.close(), puts the worker's own RedisConnection.closing at false while every command rejects with exactly the message isNotConnectionError (src/utils/index.ts:196-203) classifies as retryable. That is an ordinary shutdown ordering, not a contrived one.
  - Proposed fix: Make the shutdown state terminate the retry rather than only the sleep, and give the loop a real bound. Concretely, in the else branch at src/classes/worker.ts:1347-1356: if (this.closing || this.closed) { throw err; } before considering the delay, so that skipping the backoff during shutdown means giving up rather than spinning; and independently default maxRetries to a finite value (or pass an explicit bound from the five call sites) so that the retry + 1 >= maxRetries escape is live. A yield (await delay(0) or setImmediate) on every iteration would stop the macrotask starvation but would not fix the non-termination, so it is not a substitute.
- **R1-F2** [high/moderate] src/classes/worker.ts:952-957 and 1015: processJob registers and deregisters lock renewal purely by job id (`trackJob(job.id, token, ...)` / `untrackJob(job.id)` in a finally), while LockManager stores one entry per job id (src/classes/lock-manager.ts:56-59, 186-206). Two overlapping executions of the same job id on one worker — which is what happens after a job is declared stalled and re-queued while its first execution is still running — collapse into a single entry, so the first execution to finish silently deletes the live second execution's tracking: its lock stops being renewed and worker.cancelJob() reports it as not found.
  - Evidence: Code: lock-manager.ts:56-59 `protected trackedJobs = new Map<string, { token: string; ts: number; abortController?: AbortController }>();`; trackJob does `this.trackedJobs.set(jobId, { token, ts, abortController })` (lock-manager.ts:196) with no check for an existing entry; untrackJob does `this.trackedJobs.delete(jobId)` (lock-manager.ts:205) with no token check; the renewal timer iterates `this.trackedJobs.keys()` and renews with the stored token (lock-manager.ts:141-153, 94-102); cancelJob returns false when no entry exists (lock-manager.ts:228-235), which worker.cancelJob returns verbatim (worker.ts:458-460). worker.ts:1014-1015 runs `this.lockManager.untrackJob(job.id)` in processJob's finally unconditionally — it does not check that the entry still belongs to this execution's token.

Executed repro (repro/repro2_lockmanager_jobid_collision.ts, output in repro/OUTPUT.txt, exit 0 = all assertions held), driving the real LockManager from the working tree: after trackJob('42','tok-A') then trackJob('42','tok-B') the map holds one entry — {"afterBoth":{"trackedIds":["42"],"activeCount":1}}; a renewal during the overlap goes out under the second execution's token only — {"extendLocksCalledWith":[{"jobIds":["42"],"tokens":["tok-B"]}]}, so the first execution's lock (held under tok-A) is the one that is not renewed; then the first execution's finally runs untrackJob('42') and the still-running second execution is gone — {"afterFinallyOfFirst":{"trackedIds":[],"activeCount":0,"cancelJobReturns":false,"secondExecutionSignalAborted":false,"distinctControllers":true}}. The two AbortControllers are distinct objects, so the second execution's processor is holding a signal that can no longer be aborted by any public API.

Consequence chain, from the same tree: an untracked live job is never extended, so after opts.lockDuration (default 30000, worker.ts:226) it is picked up by moveStalledJobsToWait (worker.ts:1361-1383); with the default maxStalledCount of 1 (worker.ts:228) a second stall moves the job to failed while its processor is still running, and the eventual moveToCompleted then throws for a lost lock. Note also that lock renewal failure is only reported, never acted on: extendLocks emits 'lockRenewalFailed' and an error (lock-manager.ts:104-113) and nothing in src calls cancelJob internally (grep for cancelJob/cancelAllJobs in src matches only the definitions at worker.ts:458/468 and lock-manager.ts:228/241), so nothing interrupts the doomed execution.

Inference disclosed: the repro establishes the collision mechanism and its effects on the tracking structure; that the same worker can hold two concurrent executions of one job id is inferred from the stalled-recovery path (a stalled job returns to wait and any worker, including this one, may fetch it while the first execution is still in flight), not executed end-to-end, because the project suite needs a Redis server that is not available here.
  - Defender: Accepted at severity high. I rebuilt the experiment from the critic's prose against the real LockManager in the working tree (repro/defender-r1/_probes/d2_lock_collision.ts) and every step reproduces: trackJob('42','tok-A') then trackJob('42','tok-B') leaves one entry ({"trackedIds":["42"],"activeCount":1}) with two distinct AbortController objects; a renewal during the overlap goes out under the second token only ({"jobIds":["42"],"tokens":["tok-B"]}); the first execution's finally then runs untrackJob('42') and the still-live second execution is gone, with cancelJob returning false and its signal unaborted. A negative control with distinct job ids does not collide and cancelJob returns true, so the probe can distinguish the two cases. The code matches: lock-manager.ts:196 sets by jobId with no occupancy check, 205 deletes with no token check, and worker.ts:1014-1015 runs untrackJob(job.id) in processJob's finally unconditionally, with no test that the entry still belongs to this execution's token. I looked specifically for a reason the two-executions-of-one-job-id state cannot arise on a single worker, because that is the finding's only inferential step, and I could not find one — the fetch path has no per-worker dedup by job id (mainLoop at worker.ts:582-620 and _getNextJob at 676-706 filter on paused/closing/rate limit only), and the project's own documentation states the state outright: docs/gitbook/guide/workers/cancelling-jobs.md:322-323 says the stalled checker moves the job back to waiting and 'Another worker (or the same worker) will pick it up and retry', in a section whose whole premise is that the original processor is still running and must be told to stop. I will note that the harm runs the opposite way round from the critic's emphasis and is worse for it: by the time a second execution exists the first has usually already lost its lock, so 'the first execution's lock stops being renewed' is often moot — the material damage is that when the first execution finishes, its finally silently untracks the SECOND, legitimately locked, still-running execution, which then stops being renewed, stalls after opts.lockDuration (default 30000, worker.ts:226), and at the default maxStalledCount of 1 (worker.ts:228) is moved to failed while its processor is still executing. Nothing in src calls cancelJob internally, and after the untrack no caller can reach that execution's controller either. Severity high is right. I am not contesting the moderate support level: it is the honest level for a mechanism established directly plus a reachability step established by code reading and project documentation rather than by an end-to-end run, which no one here can perform without Redis.
  - Proposed fix: Key the tracking on the execution, not the job id. Either make trackedJobs a Map<string, Map<string, entry>> keyed by jobId then token, or keep one entry per job id but make untrackJob token-aware — untrackJob(jobId, token) that deletes only when the stored entry's token matches, with worker.ts:1015 passing the execution's own token. cancelJob/cancelAllJobs would then abort every live execution of the id rather than whichever one happens to be stored.
- **R1-F3** [medium/moderate] src/classes/worker.ts:1082-1092: the DelayedError / WaitingError / WaitingChildrenError branch of handleFailed calls `this.moveToActive(client, token, this.opts.name)` with no closing/paused guard, unlike every other fetch-next decision in the same file. A worker that has been paused or closed therefore still pulls a fresh job out of wait into active and runs it.
  - Evidence: Contrast inside the same function: handleFailed's normal path gates the fetch with `fetchNextCallback() && !(this.closing || this.paused)` (worker.ts:1094-1098) and handleCompleted does the same (worker.ts:1049-1053); _getNextJob returns early when paused or closing (worker.ts:682-688). The delayed/waiting branch at worker.ts:1082-1092 has neither guard — it only sits behind `if (!this.connection.closing)` (worker.ts:1074), which, per the cleanup ordering in close() (worker.ts:1217-1225, connection.close is the last step), is still false throughout the in-flight drain.

What happens to the job it pulls in: moveToActive -> nextJobFromJobData returns the Job, processJob returns it, mainLoop puts it in the fifo queue and its outer loop continues because `asyncFifoQueue.numTotal() > 0` (worker.ts:577), the inner fetch loop is skipped because paused/closing is set, and the job is then handed to `this.processJob(...)` unconditionally at worker.ts:629-637. So the job is not merely activated, it is executed after pause()/close() was requested. This is user-visible for flows: a parent job going to waiting-children during shutdown is exactly the trigger.

Worst case: with `pause(true)` (doNotWaitActive, worker.ts:1119-1141) or a later `close(true)` the force path skips whenCurrentJobsFinished (worker.ts:1218-1219) and closes the connections underneath that freshly activated job; its handleCompleted/handleFailed then hit `this.connection.closing` (worker.ts:1048, 1074) and return without recording anything, leaving the job in active with a dead worker's token until the stalled checker recovers it one lockDuration later — and after maxStalledCount (default 1) such a job is failed rather than retried.
  - Defender: Accepted at severity medium. I rebuilt the check from the critic's prose against the real worker code (repro/defender-r1/_probes/d3_delayed_branch_fetch.ts) and it reproduces, and I was able to close the one step the critic left as a static reading. With this.closing and this.paused both set and this.connection.closing still false — the state close() creates for its entire drain phase, given that this.connection.close(force) is the last cleanup at worker.ts:1224 — both the DelayedError and the WaitingChildrenError branches of handleFailed call moveToActive exactly once. In the identical state, handleCompleted passes fetchNext=false and handleFailed's ordinary path passes fetchNext=false, which shows the guard is deliberate on every other fetch-next decision in the file and simply absent from worker.ts:1082-1092. The sanity control (connection.closing true) leaves the branch inert, so the probe is measuring the right thing. The step the critic inferred, I executed: I drove the real Worker.prototype.mainLoop with a processJob that returns a freshly activated job at the moment pause() lands, and mainLoop PROCESSED that job with this.paused true ({"processedIds":["first","activated-after-pause"],"processedWhilePaused":["activated-after-pause"]}), while the negative control with no follow-on job processes nothing after the pause. So the job is not merely pulled from wait into active during shutdown, it is executed — which is what makes this a correctness problem rather than a cosmetic one, and it matches the reading of worker.ts:624-637, where the fetched job is handed to processJob with no paused/closing test. I accept the critic's worst case too: with pause(true) or close(true) the force path skips whenCurrentJobsFinished (worker.ts:1218-1219) and closes the connections under that freshly activated job, whose handleCompleted/handleFailed then return at the this.connection.closing gate having recorded nothing, leaving it in active until the stalled checker recovers it a lockDuration later — and at the default maxStalledCount of 1 that recovery fails the job rather than retrying it. Medium is the right severity: it needs a flow or delayed/waiting-children job in flight at the moment shutdown is requested, but that is a normal shape for parent jobs and the consequence is a job executed after the API said stop, or lost to a spurious failure.
  - Proposed fix: Apply the same guard the rest of the file uses. At src/classes/worker.ts:1082-1092, return without fetching when the worker is shutting down: if (this.closing || this.paused) { return; } before `const client = await this.client;`, so the delayed/waiting branch behaves like handleCompleted and handleFailed's ordinary path. If the intent is that the branch must still return a job when running normally, the guard belongs on the moveToActive call only, leaving the state transition the processor already performed untouched.
- **R1-F4** [medium/strong] src/classes/worker.ts:282-292 sets `processorAcceptsSignal = processor.length >= 3` only in the plain-function branch, so for sandboxed processors (file path or URL) it is permanently false, and for any function processor whose declared arity is under 3 — rest args or a defaulted signal parameter, i.e. ordinary wrapper/middleware shapes — it is false too. The documented public APIs worker.cancelJob() and worker.cancelAllJobs() (worker.ts:450-470) are then guaranteed no-ops, and cancelAllJobs returns void so a caller cannot even detect it.
  - Evidence: worker.ts:283-287 `if (typeof processor === 'function') { this.processFn = processor; this.processorAcceptsSignal = processor.length >= 3; } else { /* SANDBOXED */ ... }` — the sandboxed branch (worker.ts:287-336) never touches the flag, and it is initialised false at worker.ts:198. That flag is passed straight through as `shouldCreateController` (worker.ts:952-957) and LockManager only creates an AbortController when it is true (lock-manager.ts:186-199); cancelJob returns false whenever there is no controller (lock-manager.ts:228-235) and cancelAllJobs silently skips (lock-manager.ts:241-247). Nothing recomputes the flag afterwards: grep for processorAcceptsSignal in src matches only worker.ts:198, 286, 956, so a subclass overriding createSandbox (worker.ts:368-375) or otherwise assigning processFn also gets no signals.

Arity is the wrong oracle. Executed check (repro/ARITY.txt, node v25.6.1): `async (job, token, signal) => {}` -> length 3 (accepted); `async (job, token, signal = undefined) => {}` -> length 2 (rejected); `async (...args) => {}` -> length 0 (rejected); `async (job, token, ...rest) => {}` -> length 2 (rejected). A processor that genuinely reads the signal in any of the last three shapes is handed `undefined` at worker.ts:983-986 and, if it does the documented `signal.addEventListener('abort', ...)`, throws a TypeError on every job — a total processing outage for that worker rather than a degraded cancel.

The docstrings at worker.ts:451-457 and 462-467 state cancelJob 'Cancels a specific job currently being processed by this worker' returning 'true if the job was found and cancelled, false otherwise' and cancelAllJobs 'Cancels all jobs currently being processed by this worker', with no caveat that whole processor classes can never be cancelled. Callers using the documented pattern (the tree's own tests/test_job_cancellation.ts:469-518 cancels on the lockRenewalFailed event) get a silent false — for a sandboxed worker, the worker keeps a child process running on a job whose lock is already lost.
[R2 escalation] New, and checkable in the tree: src/types/processor.ts:6-10 types the signal as optional (`signal?: AbortSignal`), which is what defeats my outage escalation under the project's own typings; tests/test_job_cancellation.ts:935-936 is the single bare `signal.` use in the tests and it is guarded by `if (signal)`. Coverage check, by grep of the review checkout: case-insensitive 'sandbox' matches 0 lines in tests/test_job_cancellation.ts and 0 in tests/test_job_cancellation_advanced.ts; those two files hold all 41 cancelJob/cancelAllJobs call sites in tests/; all 24 `new Worker(` constructions in tests/test_job_cancellation.ts pass a function processor. New measurement, driving the real LockManager from the working tree: repro/critic-r2/_probes/c5_cancel_observability.ts, captured in repro/critic-r2/OUTPUT.txt, clean rerun of repro/critic-r2/run.sh exit status 0, node v25.6.1, node-abort-controller 3.1.1 from the prepared environment, lock-manager.ts sha256 c22818357c6524c59962a7fa3d2b0608dfd077b2e344941a307cf194dd6a5aef printed in the run. Measured: shouldCreateController=false -> {"controllersCreated":0,"trackedIds":["a","b","c"],"activeJobCount":3, "cancelJobReturns":[false,false,false],"cancelAllJobsReturnType":"undefined", "signalsAborted":0}; shouldCreateController=true -> {"controllersCreated":3,"trackedIds":["a","b","c"],"activeJobCount":3, "cancelJobReturns":[true,true,true],"cancelAllJobsReturnType":"undefined", "signalsAborted":3}. Unchanged code cites for the held components: worker.ts:198, 283-287, 952-957, 450-470; lock-manager.ts:186-199, 228-235, 241-247.
  - Defender: Accepted at severity medium. The critic has withdrawn the one component I rejected in round 1 (the 'total processing outage' escalation) and reopened with the scope I had already accepted, so there is nothing left between us on this finding; I re-verified every component of the narrowed claim rather than carrying my round-1 acceptance forward on memory. (1) The flag is set only in the function branch: src/classes/worker.ts:198 initialises processorAcceptsSignal false, 283-287 assigns it only when typeof processor === 'function', and the SANDBOXED branch at 287-336 never touches it. A fresh grep of src for processorAcceptsSignal matches exactly three lines - worker.ts:198, 286 and 956 - so nothing recomputes it and a subclass overriding createSandbox (368-375) gets no signals either. (2) The arity oracle produces false negatives for ordinary wrapper shapes; re-measured on this interpreter (node v25.6.1): (job, token, signal) is 3, (job, token, signal = undefined) is 2, (...args) is 0, (job, token, ...rest) is 2. (3) Driving the real LockManager from the working tree, shouldCreateController=false gives {controllersCreated:0, activeJobCount:3, cancelJobReturns:[false,false,false], cancelAllJobsReturnType:'undefined', signalsAborted:0} against the positive control shouldCreateController=true giving {controllersCreated:3, cancelJobReturns:[true,true,true], signalsAborted:3} - so the documented APIs are silent no-ops for the whole class of processors that never gets a controller, and cancelAllJobs returns nothing, so a caller cannot detect it. (4) The critic's new evidence is correct and it is what settles the component I disputed: src/types/processor.ts:6-10 declares signal?: AbortSignal, so under the project's own typings a processor that reads the signal must cope with undefined, and the only bare `signal.` use in tests/test_job_cancellation.ts (line 936) is guarded by `if (signal)`. That is exactly why the outage escalation does not stand and why the honest scope is a silent uncancellable job, which is what I accept. (5) I reproduced the coverage check independently: case-insensitive 'sandbox' matches 0 lines in tests/test_job_cancellation.ts and 0 in tests/test_job_cancellation_advanced.ts, and all 24 `new Worker(` constructions in tests/test_job_cancellation.ts pass a function processor (the processor argument at lines 52, 115, 168, 219, 239, 278, 345, 395, 426, 476, 527, 572, 589, 646, 685, 727, 768, 783, 828, 861, 890, 934, 981, 1027 is an inline async function in every case). One immaterial arithmetic difference, disclosed rather than disputed: I count 40 cancelJob/cancelAllJobs call sites in tests/ (19 in test_job_cancellation.ts, 21 in test_job_cancellation_advanced.ts), not 41; the critic's point - that they all live in two files neither of which constructs a sandboxed worker - is unaffected. Medium severity is right: a documented public API that is a guaranteed no-op for an entire supported processor class, with no way for the caller to detect it. I re-state the one caveat from round 1 because it constrains the fix rather than the finding: setting the flag for the sandboxed branch would make cancelJob return true while cancelling nothing, since the installed sandbox wrapper has arity 2 and neither ChildCommand nor ParentCommand carries an abort or cancel command.
  - Proposed fix: Two changes, and the order matters. First, stop inferring the contract from Function.length alone: add an explicit WorkerOptions flag (e.g. useAbortSignal) that forces controller creation when set, keeping processor.length >= 3 only as the default for unannotated function processors, so rest-arg and defaulted-parameter wrappers stop being silently denied a signal without changing behaviour for the shapes tests/test_job_cancellation.ts:822-883 already pins. Second, make the sandboxed case honest rather than silent - either document at worker.ts:450-470 and in docs/gitbook/guide/workers/cancelling-jobs.md that cancellation is unavailable for sandboxed processors and warn at construction time, or implement it by adding an abort command to the child protocol. Do NOT simply set processorAcceptsSignal in the sandboxed branch: the wrapper installed as processFn takes (job, token) and the child protocol carries no abort command, so that would turn an honest false into a lie. Independently, give cancelAllJobs a return value (count or ids) so a caller can detect a no-op.
- **R2-F1** [high/strong] src/classes/worker.ts:568-640 (mainLoop) treats every promise it hands to asyncFifoQueue.add() as one that can only settle. When one rejects instead, the worker deadlocks permanently and silently. worker.ts:569 constructs `new AsyncFifoQueue<...>()` with ignoreErrors left at its default false, and in that mode a rejection pushes NO placeholder into the queue (async-fifo-queue.ts:89-96), so the fetch at worker.ts:626 prints one console.error line and loops back onto a fresh nextPromise (async-fifo-queue.ts:136-151) that only a subsequent add() could settle — and mainLoop, blocked inside that fetch, is the only producer. The worker then makes no progress, burns no CPU, emits no event and throws nothing; because whenCurrentJobsFinished awaits this.mainLoopRunning (worker.ts:1310-1313) and close() awaits it at worker.ts:1219, close() and pause() also never resolve. Two ordinary routes produce the rejected promise: (A) `this.emit('active', job, 'waiting')` at worker.ts:948 sits OUTSIDE processJob's try/catch/finally, so any user 'active' listener that throws rejects processJob; (B) with no 'error' listener attached to the worker, `this.emit('error', err)` at worker.ts:1339 throws by EventEmitter contract, and because it runs BEFORE the `return` at worker.ts:1342-1343 the throw escapes the very onlyEmitError branch that exists to swallow it — reached by the shipped lost-lock error out of the finalisation call.
  - Evidence: CODE. worker.ts:569 `const asyncFifoQueue = new AsyncFifoQueue<void | Job<...>>();` — no argument, so ignoreErrors is false (async-fifo-queue.ts:73 `constructor(private ignoreErrors = false)`). async-fifo-queue.ts:89-96: the add() catch pushes `undefined` into the queue ONLY `if (this.ignoreErrors)`, then always calls rejectPromise(err), which rejects the current nextPromise and immediately replaces it (120-123). async-fifo-queue.ts:136-151: fetch() catches that rejection, `console.error('Unexpected Error in AsyncFifoQueue', err)`, and re-enters `while (this.queue.length === 0) { await this.wait(); }` on the replacement promise. worker.ts:625-627 is that fetch; worker.ts:631-637 is the add() of `this.processJob(...)`; worker.ts:948 is `this.emit('active', job, 'waiting')`, textually above processJob's `try {` at worker.ts:959 and therefore outside both its catch and its finally; worker.ts:1336-1345 is retryIfFailed's non-connection branch, emitting at 1339 before the `return` at 1343. worker.ts:1219 and 1310-1313 are close()'s await of whenCurrentJobsFinished and its await of this.mainLoopRunning.

EXECUTED REPRO. repro/critic-r2/run.sh, sources in repro/critic-r2/_probes/, captured output in repro/critic-r2/OUTPUT.txt, clean rerun exit status 0; node v25.6.1; deps resolved only from the prepared environment (ioredis 5.11.1, node-abort-controller 3.1.1, uuid 11.1.1, cron-parser 4.9.0, semver 7.8.5, ts-node 10.9.2, typescript 5.9.3) and the project's own import name resolves to NOTHING from it (printed as `bullmq resolves to: NOTHING (MODULE_NOT_FOUND)`); sha256 of every reviewed file printed from both the review checkout and the scratch copy and identical (worker.ts 70f5ce277f2056f281a7a241dc42362cd2e2a1d96323e16e838ddcf94b34dde2, async-fifo-queue.ts a622d35355ad4c03e7046ca4dd738f5e39acb2bb425d82304fb0781cbcf7f189). Nothing was installed.
  c1 (the real AsyncFifoQueue): {"default_ignoreErrors_fetchOutcome":"TIMEOUT", "default_numTotalAfter":0} — fetch() never settles although the queue is empty; controls {"ignoreErrorsTrue_fetchOutcome":"settled:undefined", "default_resolvedPromise_fetchOutcome":"settled:\"x\""} both settle, so the probe can distinguish the modes and fails itself (exit 1) if they do not.
  c2 (the real Worker.prototype['mainLoop'] and the real retryIfFailed, driven with a processJob that rejects and sets closing=true at the same instant): {"wedge_mainLoopOutcome":"TIMEOUT","wedge_closingFlagAtEnd":true} against the control that differs ONLY in processJob settling: {"control_mainLoopOutcome":"RETURNED","control_closingFlagAtEnd":true}. mainLoop does not return even though shutdown has been requested and numTotal() is 0 — this is close() hanging forever, not merely a worker that stops fetching.
  c3 (the real Worker.prototype.processJob, with the real retryIfFailed, callProcessJob, getUnrecoverableErrorMessage and handleFailed, and a real EventEmitter for emit): route A {"routeA_outcome":"REJECTED:user active listener threw","routeA_untrackJobCalled":0} — it rejects and the finally never even runs, so the job is not untracked; control {"routeA_control_outcome":"RESOLVED"}. Route B, no 'error' listener plus job.moveToFailed throwing the shipped 'Missing lock for job j1. moveToFinished': {"routeB_outcome":"REJECTED:Missing lock for job j1. moveToFinished"}; the control that differs only by attaching an 'error' listener gives {"routeB_control_outcome":"RESOLVED"}.

CAUSAL CONNECTION. c3 supplies exactly the input c2 shows to be fatal, and both run the shipped functions from the working tree, so the chain rejected-processJob -> parked fetch -> mainLoop never returns -> close() never resolves is executed end to end at the in-process level. The single inferential step is that one of the two routes occurs in a deployment: route A needs one throwing 'active' listener, route B needs only the ABSENCE of an 'error' listener plus a lost lock, which is the same lost-lock condition the tree's own cancellation documentation treats as routine. Note the failure is invisible: the sole diagnostic is one console.error line emitted from async-fifo-queue.ts:146, which does not go through the worker's 'error' event and so reaches no error handler, log pipeline or metric.

  - Proposed fix: Three changes, and only the first is strictly necessary. (1) Make the classifier total: give src/utils/index.ts:196-203 an existence guard - const errorMessage = (error as any)?.message; return typeof errorMessage !== "string" ? true : (errorMessage !== CONNECTION_CLOSED_ERROR_MSG && !errorMessage.includes("ECONNREFUSED") && (error as any)?.code !== "ECONNREFUSED") - so an arbitrary thrown value is classified as a non-connection error rather than making the classifier itself throw. Guard src/classes/worker.ts:1335 the same way, since opts.span?.recordException((<Error>err).message) dereferences before the classifier when telemetry is configured. (2) Normalise at the boundary: in processJob's catch at worker.ts:997, convert a non-Error rejection into an Error once (err instanceof Error ? err : new Error(String(err))) before it is handed to retryIfFailed and handleFailed, which also fixes R3-F2. (3) Make the loop tolerant rather than relying on nothing ever rejecting: construct the queue at worker.ts:569 as new AsyncFifoQueue<...>(true), so a rejection pushes a placeholder and fetch() cannot park; if the default must stay false, wrap the promise added at 631-637 so it can only settle. (3) is the change that makes the dispute about reachability moot, which is the practical reason to prefer taking it as well as (1).
- **R2-F2** [medium/strong] src/classes/worker.ts:1147-1163 (resume) is unsafe in two independent ways, both silent. (1) It is gated on `if (!this.running)`, and this.running stays true for the whole window in which the main loop is unwinding — precisely the window pause(true) (doNotWaitActive, worker.ts:1119-1141) returns inside. In that window resume() does nothing at all: it does not clear this.paused, does not call run(), emits no event, throws nothing and returns void, so the caller cannot detect it; when the loop finally exits, paused is still true and nothing restarts it, leaving the worker permanently idle. (2) At worker.ts:1158 it calls `this.run()` and discards the promise, unlike the constructor at worker.ts:340 which guards the identical call with `.catch(error => this.emit('error', error))`. A restart that fails is therefore an unhandled rejection, which under Node's default policy terminates the process — the same failure that the autorun path routes to the 'error' event kills the host process when it arrives via resume().
  - Evidence: CODE. worker.ts:1147-1163: `resume(): void { if (!this.running) { this.trace(..., span => { ...; this.paused = false; if (this.processFn) { this.run(); } this.emit('resumed'); }); } }` — the whole body is inside the `!this.running` gate, the trace callback is synchronous and its returned promise is discarded, and this.run() at 1158 has no catch. Contrast worker.ts:340 in the constructor: `this.run().catch(error => this.emit('error', error));`. run() is async and rejects rather than throws: worker.ts:515-547 awaits `this.client` (queue-base.ts:92-94 -> redis-connection.ts:210 returns this.initializing, which rejects at redis-connection.ts:163-164 and 183-184 when the client ends or never becomes ready), `this.blockingConnection.client`, and `this.mainLoopRunning`. pause(true) at worker.ts:1119-1141 skips whenCurrentJobsFinished, so it returns while mainLoop is still draining and this.running is still true.

  - Defender: I hold the round-2 split, and I answer the escalation on its own terms rather than by repeating myself. ACCEPTED COMPONENTS - the behaviour, both halves, re-measured this round on the real Worker.prototype.resume rather than carried forward on memory. With running true and paused true, resume() returns undefined, leaves paused true, calls run() zero times and emits nothing: {"pausedAfter":true,"runCalls":0,"events":[]}. The control with running false clears paused, calls run() once and emits "resumed": {"pausedAfter":false,"runCalls":1,"events":["resumed"]}, and the probe exits 1 if that control does not actually resume. The window is real: pause(true) at worker.ts:1131-1135 skips whenCurrentJobsFinished and returns while mainLoop is still draining, and run()'s finally at worker.ts:544-546 is the only writer that clears this.running. And resume() drops the promise from this.run() at worker.ts:1158, unlike the constructor at worker.ts:340 which writes this.run().catch(error => this.emit("error", error)); round 2 established by child process that the resulting crash is the dropped promise and not a synchronous throw a caller could catch, and nothing this round disturbs that. REJECTED COMPONENT - that this is a finding against the change under review. The critic concedes no hunk covers resume() and argues instead that the added code makes the pre-existing gate newly reachable, by leaving this.running true forever. I grant the premise and it still does not carry, for two reasons that are checkable rather than argumentative. (i) THE CHANGE ADDS NO WRITER OF this.running. The one added line the critic points at, worker.ts:208 protected running = false;, is a field DECLARATION inside the reconstructed property block - it is the initialiser, not a mutation. A grep of the working tree for assignments to this.running returns worker.ts:525 and worker.ts:546, both inside run(), and run() is byte-identical to HEAD: diff of HEAD 275-307 against the working tree 515-547 is empty. pause() and resume() are likewise byte-identical (HEAD 720-736 against 1147-1163 is empty). So the change neither authored the gate nor moved the flag. (ii) IN THE WEDGED STATE, resume()'s NO-OP IS NOT A DISTINCT HARM. I re-measured the wedge myself this round (real mainLoop, real AsyncFifoQueue: outcome TIMEOUT against a settling control) and I have conceded it under R2-F1 at high severity, including that close() and pause() then never resolve. Once that has happened, the worker is already unrecoverable by any API; recording "and resume() would also not have helped" as a second finding attributes to the reconstruction a pre-existing gate whose only new reachability is the defect already on the ledger. The escalation is R2-F1's reachability argument re-used, and it should be priced once. (iii) Route 2 - the dropped run() promise at worker.ts:1158 - is untouched by the escalation entirely. It requires resume() to be called with this.running FALSE and the restart to fail, which is the opposite of the wedged state the escalation constructs. Nothing in the added region calls resume(), assigns this.running, or alters when pause(true) returns. So the honest disposition is unchanged: the critic has found two real defects, both worth fixing, in code this change does not touch. If the review's scope were the reconstructed worker as a whole rather than the working-tree diff, I would accept this finding outright at medium; on the frozen review window, which is the diff, the attribution does not hold. SUPPORT LEVEL: not contested. The critic's evidence does strongly support the behaviour it describes; my objection is to attribution, not to strength. SEVERITY: not contested either - medium is right for the behaviour, and I am not arguing it down to make the attribution point.
  - Proposed fix: Unchanged from round 2, and to be taken as separate work against the pre-existing code rather than as a change to the region under review. (1) Gate resume() on the paused state rather than on this.running, and make it restart the loop once the previous one has unwound: clear this.paused and emit "resumed" unconditionally, and start run() only when the loop is not currently running - resume(): void { this.paused = false; this.emit("resumed"); if (!this.running && this.processFn) { this.run().catch(error => this.emit("error", error)); } } - with the drain case handled by having run()'s finally re-enter the loop when paused has been cleared meanwhile. Give resume() a boolean return or a throw when it cannot act, so the no-op is detectable. (2) Whatever the gate becomes, the call at worker.ts:1158 must carry the same handler the constructor uses at worker.ts:340, so a failed restart reaches the "error" event instead of killing the host process.
- **R3-F1** [high/strong] src/classes/worker.ts:959-1013 - processJob wraps TWO different things in one try and hands both to one catch: the processor call at 981-987, and the completion bookkeeping it performs itself at 988-996. Because retryIfFailed rethrows any non-connection error immediately and without ever retrying (worker.ts:1336, 1345 - the delay/loop at 1347-1356 is reached only for connection errors), ANY failure of the completion write is caught at 997 and routed into handleFailed at 1004-1010, i.e. into job.moveToFailed. A job whose processor already ran to completion - whose irreversible side effects have already happened - is therefore recorded as FAILED with an internal error as its failedReason, its result is discarded and never written anywhere, and with attempts remaining the job is put back for another attempt, so the processor runs a second time. There is no compensating path: the result exists only in the local variable at 981 and nothing retries the completion write.
  - Evidence: CODE. worker.ts:981-987 `const result = await this.callProcessJob(job, token, ...)`; 988-996 `return await this.retryIfFailed(() => this.handleCompleted(result, job, token, fetchNextCallback, span), { delayInMs: this.opts.runRetryDelay, span })` - note `return await`, so a rejection here is caught by processJob's OWN catch at 997; 997-1013 that catch calls handleFailed with the same shape it uses for a processor failure, with no test of where the error came from; 1041-1065 handleCompleted (the completion write, and the emit of 'completed' at 1054 which is therefore never reached); 1094-1100 handleFailed's moveToFailed plus the unconditional emit('failed') at 1100; 1336/1345 retryIfFailed - `if (isNotConnectionError(err)) { ...; throw err; }` with no retry and no delay for this class of error, so the name retryIfFailed is misleading here: the completion write is attempted exactly once. Two shipped triggers, neither needing telemetry: (a) src/classes/job.ts:733-737, moveToCompleted does `tryCatch(JSON.stringify, JSON, [returnValue])` and `throw errorObject.value` when the processor's return value is not JSON-serialisable - an ordinary JS value such as a BigInt or a self-referential object; (b) the completion write failing for a lost lock, which src/classes/job.ts:749 reaches through scripts.moveToFinished and which the project's own documentation treats as routine (this is the same 'Missing lock for job <id>. moveToFinished' condition already on this ledger). isNotConnectionError (src/utils/index.ts:196-203) classifies both as non-connection errors, measured: {"Error(\"Missing lock for job j1. moveToFinished\")":"returned:true"}.

Only the Redis script layer is stubbed, and it is instrumented so the branch actually taken is on the record.

  BigInt in the result: {"processorRuns":1,"sideEffectsPerformed":1,"events":["error:Do not know how to serialize a BigInt","failed:Do not know how to serialize a BigInt"],"moveToCompleted_entered":1,"permanentlyFailed_moveToFailedArgs":0,"requeuedForAnotherAttempt":1,"failedReasonRecordedOnJob":"Do not know how to serialize a BigInt"} - no 'completed', a 'failed' event carrying an internal serialisation error, and the job requeued for another attempt, i.e. the processor runs again.
  Circular result: same shape, {"requeuedForAnotherAttempt":1,"events":["error:Converting circular structure to JSON...","failed:Converting circular structure to JSON..."]}.
  Same trigger with attempts=1: {"permanentlyFailed_moveToFailedArgs":1,"requeuedForAnotherAttempt":0} - the successfully processed job is failed permanently and the result is gone.

The probe asserts the control completes and is neither failed nor requeued, so it cannot pass on an inert path.

CAUSAL CONNECTION. The measured behaviour is produced by the two cited lines and nothing else: the `return await` at 988 puts the completion write inside the processor's catch, and the immediate rethrow at 1345 means one transient failure is enough. Change either and the conversion disappears. The attack-surface consequence is duplication and loss: the work is done, the result is discarded, the job is announced failed with an error the caller never raised, and on a retry the processor's side effects happen twice.
```
