## T05

### T05 — Verdict 1

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 1/3 · Findings: 1

**NO-SHIP: 1 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] `kRemoveClient` assigns the pool backpressure flag with inverted semantics. It sets `kNeedDrain` to true when at least one usable dispatcher exists, although `kDispatch` and `kBusy` consistently treat true as meaning no dispatcher is available. After removing one client while another remains usable, a request may be dispatched successfully but `kDispatch` still returns false; because no actual client is necessarily blocked, a later `drain` event is not guaranteed, potentially stalling callers indefinitely. Conversely, removing the last usable client sets the flag false.
  - Evidence: lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:72-74 exposes `kNeedDrain` as busy; lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:153-160 sets it true when no dispatcher exists and otherwise assigns `!this[kGetDispatcher]()`. In contrast, lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:193-197 assigns `this[kClients].some(...)`, which is true precisely when a non-draining, non-closed, non-destroyed dispatcher exists. lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:162 returns the negation of this incorrectly assigned flag.
  - Defender: I accept this in full, at the recorded severity high and support level strong, and I am not contesting either. What persuaded me was not the argument but two controlled experiments I built from the code, plus a one-character control. The polarity is unambiguous from the file itself: lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:154 sets this[kNeedDrain] = true on the branch where this[kGetDispatcher]() returned nothing, :159 sets it to !this[kGetDispatcher](), :72-74 exposes it as kBusy, and :162 returns !this[kNeedDrain] from [kDispatch]. Every one of those treats true as 'no dispatcher is available'. lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:193-197 assigns the same field the value of this[kClients].some(dispatcher => !dispatcher[kNeedDrain] && dispatcher.closed !== true && dispatcher.destroyed !== true), which is true precisely when a dispatcher IS available. It is the exact negation of the invariant the rest of the file maintains, and correcting it means prefixing that expression with '!'. I also confirm the consequence the finding names, which is the part I actually went looking to falsify: because [kDispatch] only recomputes the flag on the branch where the selected client refuses the request (:157-159), a successful dispatch leaves the wrong value in place, so dispatch() returns false while the request is served and nothing is queued. docs/docs/api/Dispatcher.md:188 defines that false return as 'busy and further dispatch calls won't make any progress until the drain event has been emitted' — but with no client blocked there is no drain to come, and I measured zero drain events in the 750 ms after such a dispatch. A caller honouring the documented contract waits indefinitely. On the second half of the claim I agree the flag is set false when the last usable client goes away, and I want to be precise about it rather than let it inflate the finding: in that state [kDispatch] immediately hits the !dispatcher branch at :153-156 and sets the flag back to true while queueing, so the observable damage there is confined to kBusy/[kBusy] readers between the two calls, not to dispatch()'s return value. That does not reduce the finding — the first half is sufficient on its own and is the one I reproduced end to end.
  - Proposed fix: In lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:193, negate the predicate so the field keeps the meaning the rest of the file gives it: this[kNeedDrain] = !this[kClients].some(dispatcher => (!dispatcher[kNeedDrain] && dispatcher.closed !== true && dispatcher.destroyed !== true)). I verified this exact edit in a scratch copy outside the review repository: it flips both repros to the correct behaviour and leaves test/pool.js and test/round-robin-pool.js at 45/45. A regression test is worth adding with it, since neither existing suite fails today: build a pool with two usable clients, remove one, and assert that dispatch() returns true and kBusy is false while a usable dispatcher remains — the shape the finding's own deciding_evidence describes.

_Ledger: <path>
```

### T05 — Verdict 2

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 1/3 · Findings: 4

**NO-SHIP: 1 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [medium/strong] In lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js the [kRemoveClient] recomputation of the pool-level backpressure flag (lines 193-197) is inverted: it sets this[kNeedDrain] = true exactly when at least one USABLE client remains, and false when every remaining client is draining/closed/destroyed -- the opposite of the polarity used at lines 154 and 159. Removing a client therefore makes an idle pool report backpressure: Pool.dispatch() returns false (documented as 'no progress until drain') while the request it just accepted completes normally, and [kBusy] reads true with zero queued and zero running. The mirror-image case makes a pool with no usable client left report that it is free. Both directions are demonstrated or directly implied by the same expression; the false-positive direction is reproduced end to end.
  - Evidence: lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:185-198. [kRemoveClient] recomputes the POOL-level backpressure flag as: this[kNeedDrain] = this[kClients].some(dispatcher => (!dispatcher[kNeedDrain] && dispatcher.closed !== true && dispatcher.destroyed !== true)). The lambda is the definition of 'this client is usable', so the pool is flagged as needing a drain exactly when a usable client REMAINS, and is flagged as not needing one when every remaining client is draining/closed/destroyed. Every other site in the file uses the opposite polarity: line 159 sets this[kNeedDrain] = !this[kGetDispatcher]() (true only when NOTHING is available) and line 154 sets it true only on the !dispatcher branch. Line 162 returns !this[kNeedDrain] as the public dispatch() result and line 72-74 exposes it as [kBusy]. 

REPRO A (public trigger: the documented clientTtl Pool option; lib/dispatcher/pool.js:100-107 calls kRemoveClient on a TTL-expired client and then returns a different healthy client). File: <path>  Command: UNDICI_REPO=<path> node r2-pool-clientttl-dispatch-false.js  Interpreter: node v25.6.1, no third-party deps. Output: 
  t0 idle, ttl fresh : {"poolNeedDrain":false,"poolBusy":false,"clients":2,"queued":0,"running":0,"pending":0}
  control dispatch() : true
  t1 ttl expired     : {"poolNeedDrain":false,"poolBusy":false,"clients":2,"queued":0,"running":0,"pending":0}
  t2 dispatch()      : false {"poolNeedDrain":true,"poolBusy":true,"clients":2,"queued":0,"running":0,"pending":1}
  request outcome    : succeeded
  RESULT: DEFECT: Pool.dispatch() returned false ... even though the request was accepted and completed on an idle pool
  exit=1
The script contains a control assertion (the same dispatch on the same pool one TTL earlier returns true) and a sanity assertion that the probe request actually completed, so it can fail. 

REPRO B (public trigger: BalancedPool.removeUpstream, docs/docs/api/BalancedPool.md). File: .../repro/r1-remove-client-needdrain.js and .../repro/r1b-trace.js. Output: 
  BEFORE removeUpstream : {"needDrain":false,"busy":false,"queued":0,"running":0}
  AFTER  removeUpstream : {"needDrain":true,"busy":true,"queued":0,"running":0}
  t1 (post-remove) : {"poolNeedDrain":true,"clients":[{"closed":true,...},{"closed":false,"destroyed":false,"cNeedDrain":false}]}
  exit=1
A completely idle pool -- zero queued, zero running, one healthy upstream -- reports needDrain/busy = true purely because a healthy upstream is still present. 

CAUSAL LINK: the only statement that assigns this[kNeedDrain] between t1 and t2 in repro A is line 193; the flag is false immediately before kRemoveClient runs and true immediately after, with the pool otherwise idle. 

CONTRACT: docs/docs/api/Dispatcher.md:188 defines a false return from dispatch() as 'further dispatch calls won't make any progress until the drain event has been emitted'. Repro A shows that signal raised on an idle pool that is making progress. 

CLEAN RERUN: <path> output in run-all-output.txt (4578 bytes, non-empty); git status of the checkout during the run shows only the pre-existing modification to lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js -- nothing was installed into or written inside the checkout.
  - Defender: Accepted. What changed my mind is not the critic's scripts (I did not read them; I rebuilt the experiments from the prose) but the polarity contradiction being directly observable and reproducible on two independent public paths. lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:193-197 assigns the POOL flag as this[kNeedDrain] = this[kClients].some(<dispatcher is usable>), while every other assignment in the file means the opposite: line 154 sets it true only on the !dispatcher branch, line 159 sets it to !this[kGetDispatcher]() (true only when nothing is available), line 162 returns !this[kNeedDrain] as the public dispatch() result and lines 72-74 expose it as [kBusy]. I reproduced both directions. I do not contest the recorded severity (medium) or support level (strong): the mainstream consequence is a misreported backpressure signal rather than lost or corrupted work, and the flag is repaired by the next [kOnDrain]. One impact observation for the record, deliberately filed here rather than as a near-duplicate finding because the same one-line change fixes it: in the mirror direction the cleared flag also disables the catch-up branch at line 174 ('if (this[kNeedDrain])'), so items already sitting in the pool-level queue are not handed to newly added capacity. In <path> a queued request sat unserved for a full second after a healthy upstream was added, and moved only when unrelated traffic happened to produce a drain; with the predicate negated the flag would still be true and the line-175 microtask would flush the queue immediately.
  - Proposed fix: Negate the predicate at lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:193 so the pool flag keeps the meaning it has everywhere else in the file: this[kNeedDrain] = !this[kClients].some(dispatcher => (!dispatcher[kNeedDrain] && dispatcher.closed !== true && dispatcher.destroyed !== true)).
- **R1-F2** [high/strong] lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js leaks an unresolvable promise on the shutdown path: [kClose] (lines 120-132) parks the caller on a promise whose resolver is stored in this[kClosedResolve], and that resolver is only ever reachable from [kOnDrain] (line 50). [kDestroy] (lines 134-148) empties the identical queue and destroys every client without invoking it, after which no client can emit 'drain' again. Calling close() on a pool with queued work and then destroy() leaves the close() promise -- and the close(callback) callback -- pending forever, with no error, no timeout and no diagnostic.
  - Evidence: lib/dispatcher/pool-base.js:120-132 and 134-148. [kClose] takes the else branch when the pool queue is non-empty and returns new Promise((resolve) => { this[kClosedResolve] = resolve }) -- a promise with no timeout, no rejection path and exactly one resolver, stashed on the instance. The ONLY read of this[kClosedResolve] in the whole file is line 50-57, inside [kOnDrain]. [kDestroy] at 134-141 shifts the very same queue empty and errors every item, and never touches this[kClosedResolve]; nor does anything else in the file. lib/dispatcher/dispatcher-base.js close() chains this[kClose]().then(() => this.destroy()).then(() => queueMicrotask(onClosed)), so an unresolved kClose promise also means the close(callback) form never fires its callback. 

REPRO: <path>  Command: UNDICI_REPO=<path> node r3-close-never-settles.js  Interpreter: node v25.6.1, no third-party deps. Scenario: Pool with connections:1 against a server that never responds, two requests so one sits in the pool-level queue, then close(), then destroy(). Output: 
  queued at pool level : 1  clients: 1  needDrain: true
  close() outcome      : TIMEOUT
  close() settled      : false
  destroy() settled    : true
  queued after destroy : 1
  request errors seen  : ["UND_ERR_DESTROYED","UND_ERR_DESTROYED"]
```

## T07

### T07 — Verdict 1

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 1/3 · Findings: 3

**NO-SHIP: 1 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] The decompression size limit in DeflateBuffer is applied per feed_data call, and the size of a call is chosen by the peer, so the limit does not bound a stream. An attacker who frames a compressed body in small HTTP chunks delivers an unbounded amount of decompressed data through a 32 MiB 'limit'. Demonstrated end-to-end: a 261533-byte request, handed to the request parser in a single feed_data call, produces 268435456 bytes (256 MiB, 8x the limit) buffered in the request payload with no error raised. The amplification happens inside one read, so transport flow control cannot intervene, and the server reaches this path by default (web_protocol.py:204,246 build the request parser with auto_decompress=True, so request bodies carrying Content-Encoding are decompressed before the application sees them). The client side is equally exposed to a hostile or compromised upstream, which also controls its own chunk framing.
  - Evidence: CODE. aiohttp/http_parser.py:998-1015 -- DeflateBuffer.feed_data decompresses with max_length=self._max_decompress_size + 1 and raises DecompressSizeError only if len(chunk) > self._max_decompress_size for THAT call; no running total of decompressed output is kept anywhere in the class (the only counter, self.size at :981-982, counts COMPRESSED bytes and is only published as out.total_compressed_bytes). Default limit DEFAULT_MAX_DECOMPRESS_SIZE = 2**25 (aiohttp/compression_utils.py:37). The size of a call is peer-controlled: aiohttp/http_parser.py:874-877 calls self.payload.feed_data(chunk[:required]) once per HTTP chunk in the chunked branch (so the peer's chunk sizes set the window), and :808-811 once per network read in the length branch. Reachable server-side by default: aiohttp/web_protocol.py:204 and :246 pass auto_decompress=True into the request parser; aiohttp/http_parser.py:768-771 then wraps the payload in DeflateBuffer whenever a Content-Encoding was parsed.

REPRO (archive: _rerun2/T07-armX/repro/, driver run_all.sh, logs in repro/output/, each log records its command and exit status; interpreter Python 3.11.15 from the prepared environment _env/T07, module imported from the working tree, multidict 6.7.1 / yarl 1.24.5 / zlib 1.2.12, C accelerator absent so this is the pure-Python parser).
- f1_e2e_4KiB_http_chunks.log (exit 0): request with Transfer-Encoding: chunked + Content-Encoding: deflate, 4096-byte chunks, whole 261533-byte request passed in ONE parser.feed_data call -> 'RESULT: no error. eof=True', 'decompressed bytes buffered in the request payload = 268435456 (256.0 MiB)', 'compressed bytes seen (total_compressed_bytes) = 260923', 'ratio delivered/limit = 8.0x'.
- f1_e2e_control_1MiB_chunks.log (exit 1): the SAME bomb, the same code path, only the chunk size changed to 1 MiB -> 'RESULT: payload exception DecompressSizeError ... exceeds the configured limit of 33554432 bytes'. The guard fires when the attacker lets it, and not otherwise; this control is what proves the probe can fail.
- f1_unit_4KiB_feeds.log (exit 0) / f1_unit_control_one_feed.log (exit 1): same result at the DeflateBuffer level -- 536870912 bytes (16x the limit) delivered downstream across 128 calls of 4096 compressed bytes, versus DecompressSizeError when the identical bytes arrive in one call. Largest single downstream feed was 4214172 bytes, comfortably under the cap on every call.

INTENT. The project's own tests read the guard as per-call and treat that as correct: tests/test_client_functional.py:2404-2434 ('the 32MiB per-call limit'), and tests/test_http_parser.py:2024-2054 test_streaming_decompress_large_payload asserts that a payload streamed in 1KB/16KB/64KB pieces decompresses in full, i.e. the suite pins the exact behaviour that voids the limit. So the suite passing is not evidence against this finding.
  - Defender: Independently reproduced from the critic's prose (not their script) and confirmed. What persuaded me: the DeflateBuffer keeps no running total of decompressed output. feed_data (aiohttp/http_parser.py:977-1018) increments only self.size, which counts COMPRESSED bytes (:981-982), and calls decompressor.decompress_sync with max_length=self._max_decompress_size + 1, then raises DecompressSizeError only if len(chunk) > self._max_decompress_size for THAT single call (:1010-1015). Because HttpPayloadParser feeds the DeflateBuffer once per HTTP chunk in the chunked branch (:877), the peer's chunk sizes set the decompression window, so an attacker keeps every per-call output under 32 MiB while the aggregate is unbounded. Reachability confirmed: web_protocol.py:204 defaults auto_decompress=True and passes it at :246; HttpPayloadParser wraps the payload in DeflateBuffer whenever a Content-Encoding was parsed (:768-771). The suite passing is not a defence: test_http_parser.py:2030 test_streaming_decompress_large_payload deliberately pins the streaming property (each chunk under the limit, full recovery of the whole) that voids the aggregate bound. I accept the severity at high: this is an unauthenticated memory-amplification DoS on the default server request path, and the amplification completes inside one synchronous feed_data so StreamReader flow control (pause_reading) cannot intervene.
  - Proposed fix: Track a cumulative decompressed-byte counter on DeflateBuffer (accumulate len(decompressed) across feed_data calls) and raise DecompressSizeError on the running total, not per call; add a regression test that feeds a bomb in small chunks and asserts DecompressSizeError still fires.
- **R1-F2** [medium/strong] HttpPayloadParser counts the mandatory empty line that terminates the trailer section as a trailer, so the effective trailer budget is one less than the caller allocated, and when the budget is zero a perfectly well-formed chunked message with no trailers at all is rejected. With the default max_headers=128, a request whose head is 128 lines -- accepted by the header limit itself, since 129 is the first size rejected -- and whose body is a normal '0\r\n\r\n'-terminated chunked body fails with BadHttpMessage('Too many trailers received'). The rejection lands after the headers have been accepted and the message handed to the application, as a payload-read failure rather than a request rejection, so the failure surfaces as a broken body read on a request the server already admitted. Any deployment that lowers max_headers for hardening moves this cliff proportionally lower.
  - Evidence: CODE. aiohttp/http_parser.py:896-916: every line of the trailer section, including the terminating empty line, is appended to self._trailer_lines at :910 BEFORE the budget check at :912-913 (if len(self._trailer_lines) > self._max_trailers: raise BadHttpMessage('Too many trailers received')); the end-of-trailers test at :916 (self._trailer_lines[-1] == b'') happens only after that check has already been passed. So a message consumes n_trailers + 1 of its budget. The budget itself is max_headers - len(self._lines) (aiohttp/http_parser.py:317, passed as max_trailers at :378/:400/:422), and the head-size check at :310-311 admits len(self._lines) up to and including max_headers -- so a budget of exactly 0 is reachable with an accepted head.

REPRO (archive: _rerun2/T07-armX/repro/f2_trailer_budget.py, log repro/output/f2_trailer_budget.log, exit 0; Python 3.11.15 from the prepared environment, module imported from the working tree). Sweep of head lines (request line + headers + blank) against trailer count, max_headers=128:
  126 head lines, 0 trailers -> OK body=b'HELLO' eof=True
  127 head lines, 0 trailers -> OK body=b'HELLO' eof=True
  127 head lines, 1 trailer  -> PAYLOAD-ERROR BadHttpMessage: Too many trailers received
  128 head lines, 0 trailers -> PAYLOAD-ERROR BadHttpMessage: Too many trailers received
  129 head lines, 0 trailers -> RAISED BadHttpMessage: Too many headers received
The 129-line row is what shows 128 head lines to be legal input: the head limit accepts it, and the body is then rejected for containing zero trailers. The exact boundary rejects at n_trailers + 1 > 128 - head_lines, which is the off-by-one stated in the claim, not a general 'too many headers' effect.

The project suite does not cover this: repro/output/project_suite_test_http_parser.log shows 304 passed with no test near the trailer-budget boundary.
  - Defender: Independently reproduced and confirmed. What persuaded me: in HttpPayloadParser.feed_data every trailer line, including the terminating empty line, is appended to self._trailer_lines at :910 BEFORE the budget test at :912-913 (if len(self._trailer_lines) > self._max_trailers: raise BadHttpMessage('Too many trailers received')), and the end-of-trailers check self._trailer_lines[-1] == b'' at :916 runs only after that test has passed. So a message with n real trailers consumes n+1 of its budget, and a zero-trailer message consumes 1. The budget is max_trailers = self.max_headers - len(self._lines) (http_parser.py:317), computed after len(self._lines) has been allowed up to and including max_headers by the head check at :310-311 (129 is the first head-size rejection). A head of exactly 128 lines is therefore legal input yet yields max_trailers = 0, so a perfectly well-formed 0\r\n\r\n-terminated chunked body with no trailers is rejected. The rejection lands as a payload-read failure after the request head has been accepted. I accept the severity at medium: it silently breaks legitimate chunked requests at a specific but reachable head size, and the cliff scales down proportionally for any deployment that lowers max_headers for hardening.
  - Proposed fix: Count only non-empty trailer lines against the budget, or perform the budget check before appending the terminating empty line; add a boundary test at max_headers head lines with zero trailers asserting the body is accepted.
- **R1-F3** [medium/strong] Chunked-framing violations are classified inconsistently, and two of them do not terminate the message stream. A malformed chunk size raises TransferEncodingError, which HttpParser.feed_data re-raises so the connection is torn down; but an over-long chunk-size line and an over-long or too-numerous trailer line raise LineTooLong / BadHttpMessage, which the same handler deliberately does not re-raise. For those, the parser marks the message complete, silently discards whatever remains of the current buffer -- including a pipelined request that was already in it -- and resynchronises: the very next bytes on the same connection are parsed as a fresh request. A framing violation in the body framing is thus downgraded to an application-visible payload error plus a mid-stream resynchronisation point chosen by the peer, rather than a protocol error. The exception type raised at these three sites is the whole of the difference.
  - Evidence: CODE. Raise sites in the reviewed block, aiohttp/http_parser.py: :825-826 raises LineTooLong for an over-long chunk-size or trailer line accumulated in _chunk_tail; :907-908 raises LineTooLong for an over-long trailer line; :912-913 raises BadHttpMessage('Too many trailers received'). Compare the chunk-size paths at :840-845 and :852-857, which raise TransferEncodingError. The consuming handler is aiohttp/http_parser.py:447-465: it catches BaseException from the payload parser, sets the exception on the payload, then sets eof = True and data = b'' (so the rest of the current read buffer is dropped), and re-raises ONLY 'if isinstance(underlying_exc, (InvalidHeader, TransferEncodingError))'. LineTooLong and BadHttpMessage are outside that tuple, so they are swallowed; :467-471 then clears self._payload_parser and the loop continues with a clean slate.

REPRO (archive: _rerun2/T07-armX/repro/f3_framing_error_class.py, log repro/output/f3_framing_error_class.log, exit 0; Python 3.11.15 from the prepared environment, module imported from the working tree). One chunked request per row, the violation fed second, a following 'GET /next' fed third:
  malformed-size               -> 2nd feed RAISED TransferEncodingError (connection torn down)
  oversize-size-line           -> 2nd feed returned normally, msgs=[], tail=b''
  oversize-trailer             -> 2nd feed returned normally; payload exc=LineTooLong; 3rd feed: msgs=[('GET','/next')]
  trailer-flood                -> 2nd feed returned normally; payload exc=BadHttpMessage; 3rd feed: msgs=[('GET','/next')]
  oversize-trailer+same-buffer -> the pipelined 'GET /next' sent in the SAME buffer as the violation is silently discarded, msgs=[]
A separate probe of the same shape (over-long trailer after a completed chunk) confirmed the first message is still delivered to the application with its payload carrying LineTooLong while the parser accepts the following request.

HONEST LIMIT, stated so the severity is calibrated rather than inflated: I read aiohttp/web_protocol.py:639-670 and aiohttp/client_proto.py:82-88, and both do eventually close the connection when a payload never reaches EOF (the server's lingering read re-raises the payload exception into the 'Unhandled exception' path and force-closes; the client's should_close is True while a payload is not at EOF). So I am not claiming a proven end-to-end smuggle through the bundled server. What the evidence does establish is that the parser itself resynchronises on attacker-chosen boundaries and discards pipelined bytes, that the outcome depends on an exception-type coincidence rather than on a stated rule, and that HttpParser/HttpRequestParser are usable directly by other consumers that do not implement those two mitigations.
  - Defender: Independently reproduced and confirmed, and the critic's honest scoping is fair. What persuaded me: the payload-exception handler at http_parser.py:449-465 catches BaseException, sets the exception on the payload, sets eof=True and data=b'' (discarding the rest of the current read buffer), and re-raises only 'if isinstance(underlying_exc, (InvalidHeader, TransferEncodingError))'. The over-long chunk-size/trailer line raises LineTooLong (:825-826, :907-908) and the trailer flood raises BadHttpMessage (:912-913); neither type is in that tuple, so both are swallowed while a malformed chunk size (TransferEncodingError at :852-857) is re-raised and tears the connection down. My repro confirms the divergence: after a swallowed trailer-limit violation the parser clears self._payload_parser (:467-471) and parses the very next bytes on the same connection as a fresh request, and a pipelined request riding in the same buffer as the violation is silently discarded. The outcome thus turns on an exception-type coincidence rather than a stated rule. I accept at medium and confidence-level 'strong' for the parser-level behavior, matching the critic's own honest limit that this is not a proven end-to-end smuggle through the bundled server (web_protocol/client_proto do eventually close on a non-EOF payload); the established defect is the parser resynchronising on attacker-chosen boundaries, discarding pipelined bytes, and misclassifying framing violations -- and HttpParser/HttpRequestParser are usable directly by consumers that do not implement those mitigations.
  - Proposed fix: Raise TransferEncodingError (or another type inside the re-raise tuple) at the three chunked-framing limit sites, or add LineTooLong and BadHttpMessage to the re-raise tuple at :462-464; add a test asserting no further message is parsed on the connection after a chunked-framing limit violation.

_Ledger: <path>
```

### T07 — Verdict 2

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 3

**NO-SHIP: 2 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [medium/strong] Valid Content-Encoding values with non-lowercase casing are recognized by header parsing but passed unnormalized to DeflateBuffer, which selects the wrong decompressor and rejects the payload. For example, `Content-Encoding: GZIP` passes the allowlist but is handled as zlib-wrapped deflate rather than gzip; `BR` and `ZSTD` also fall into the zlib fallback.
  - Evidence: aiohttp/http_parser.py:528-531 accepts an encoding when `enc.lower()` is in the supported set but assigns `encoding = enc`; aiohttp/http_parser.py:958-966 selects Brotli/Zstandard only through case-sensitive comparisons and otherwise calls `ZLibDecompressor(encoding=encoding)`. aiohttp/compression_utils.py:143-150 recognizes gzip only when `encoding == "gzip"`.
  - Defender: Accept, at the recorded severity of medium. What changed my mind is not the code reading but the run: I fed complete responses with correct gzip bodies through HttpResponseParser varying only the case of the token, and 'gzip' returns b'hello world' while 'GZIP' and 'Gzip' fail with ContentEncodingError at read time. That is the exact experiment this finding nominated as its own refutation, and it confirms rather than refutes. I also confirmed the two secondary consequences the claim asserts: 'BR' and 'ZSTD' select ZLibDecompressor rather than the brotli/zstd handlers, which additionally bypasses the HAS_BROTLI/HAS_ZSTD availability guards in the added DeflateBuffer.__init__ so the operator loses the 'Please install' diagnostic. Severity: medium is right and I am not arguing it down. It is not high because uppercase content-coding tokens are rare in practice and the failure is a clean, loud rejection rather than silent corruption or a security boundary crossing; it is not low because conforming input is refused and no test covers it. One scope point, offered for accuracy and not as a defence: the non-normalising assignment at aiohttp/http_parser.py:530-531 is in the pre-existing region of the file and is not part of the working-tree diff, whereas the case-sensitive consumer (DeflateBuffer.__init__) is added by this change and is what makes the mismatch observable at all — before this change the module had no payload decompression path. So the defect is genuinely shipped by this change, and the fix belongs in the review window.
  - Proposed fix: Normalise the coding token once, inside the review window, at the point the added code consumes it. In DeflateBuffer.__init__ (aiohttp/http_parser.py:936-968) fold the token before dispatch, e.g. `self.encoding = encoding.lower() if encoding else encoding` set BEFORE the br/zstd comparisons, and dispatch on the folded value; self.encoding is also read later by feed_data's RFC1950 deflate sniff and by feed_eof's `self.encoding == "deflate"` check, both of which are equally case-sensitive today, so folding it in one place fixes all three. Then extend tests/test_http_parser.py's compression tests with an uppercase parametrisation (GZIP/BR/ZSTD/DEFLATE) that asserts a real body round-trips. If the maintainers would rather normalise at the producer, `encoding = enc.lower()` at aiohttp/http_parser.py:531 is the one-token fix, but that line is outside this diff and would change the public RawRequestMessage.compression / RawResponseMessage.compression value observed by third-party consumers, so the in-window fix is the safer one to ship with this change.
- **R1-D1** [high/moderate] DeflateBuffer's decompression-size guard is applied per feed_data call rather than cumulatively, so it does not bound the total decompressed size of a body. Because feed_data is invoked once per network segment (and once per HTTP chunk), a hostile peer that delivers a compression bomb in ordinary network-sized pieces never trips the limit: each piece expands to less than max_decompress_size while the total is unbounded. Measured end to end, 255 KiB of compressed input yields 256 MiB from payload.read() -- 8x the configured 32 MiB limit and a ~1000:1 amplification -- with no exception raised, while the byte-identical bomb delivered in a single feed does raise DecompressSizeError. The guard therefore stops only the delivery shape that does not occur over a real socket.
  - Evidence: Script and captured output: _rerun2/T07-armX/repro/repro_d1_decompress_limit_bypass.py and .out (exit=0). Environment as recorded in _rerun2/T07-armX/repro/MANIFEST.txt: Python 3.11.15 from the prepared frozen environment, aiohttp._http_parser NOT BUILT, aiohttp/http_parser.py resolved from the working tree under review. DEFAULT_MAX_DECOMPRESS_SIZE is 2**25 = 33554432 (aiohttp/compression_utils.py:37).
  A: DeflateBuffer(buf, 'deflate') fed 260923 compressed bytes in 4096-byte segments -> 'NO EXCEPTION. decompressed total_bytes=268435456 (8.0x the limit)'.
  B: end-to-end through HttpResponseParser with 'Content-Encoding: deflate' and 'Transfer-Encoding: chunked', one 4096-byte segment per feed_data as a transport would deliver it -> 'NO EXCEPTION during feed' then 'read() returned 268435456 bytes (8.0x the limit)'.
  C: the byte-identical bomb in ONE feed -> 'DecompressSizeError: 400, message:\n  Decompressed data exceeds the configured limit of 33554432 bytes'.
Mechanism, in the added code: aiohttp/http_parser.py:999-1015 calls self.decompressor.decompress_sync(chunk, max_length=self._max_decompress_size + 1) and then tests `if len(chunk) > self._max_decompress_size` — `chunk` there is the output of one call, and no cumulative counter exists. The only running total kept is self.size at aiohttp/http_parser.py:981-982, which counts COMPRESSED bytes and is never compared against anything; it is only published as out.total_compressed_bytes.
Counter-evidence I must record against my own finding, and why I still file it: the repository's pre-existing tests describe the per-call behaviour as intended — tests/test_http_parser.py:2029-2036 says 'Each chunk's decompressed output should be within the max_decompress_size limit' and tests/test_client_functional.py:2407-2412 says 'expands beyond the 32MiB per-call limit'. Those tests are not part of this diff, so they express prior intent, and notably the client test only passes because the loopback server writes the whole compressed body in one go; case B shows the same test scenario fails to trigger the moment the body is segmented. Intent recorded in a test does not make a bypassable control effective, and DeflateBuffer is entirely added by this change, so the control ships in this window. I am filing it at moderate support rather than strong precisely because of that documented intent: the behaviour is certain, its status as a defect rather than a deliberate limitation is the part a maintainer must rule on.
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: Track cumulative decompressed output in DeflateBuffer and pass only the remaining allowance plus one to each decompressor call; reject once the cumulative total exceeds the configured limit. Apply the same remaining-limit check to output returned by `flush()`, and add segmented gzip/deflate/Brotli/Zstandard tests.
- **R1-F2** [high/strong] The chunked-body parser does not enforce max_line_size when a complete chunk-size line arrives in the current feed, allowing an attacker-controlled, arbitrarily long hexadecimal size or chunk-extension line to undergo regex matching and integer conversion despite the configured line bound. It also stores an oversized incomplete line and waits for another feed before rejecting it.
  - Evidence: aiohttp/http_parser.py:826-832 checks `_chunk_tail` length only before appending the new input. aiohttp/http_parser.py:839-875 locates the separator, regex-validates `size_b`, and converts it with `int(..., 16)` without checking the completed line length. aiohttp/http_parser.py:876-878 stores the whole current chunk as `_chunk_tail` without a size check. By contrast, trailer lines are explicitly checked at aiohttp/http_parser.py:907-908.
[R2 escalation] Defender reproduction `_rerun2/T07-armX/repro/repro_f2_chunk_size_line.py` cases A-D establish the max_line_size bypass, while cases E-F report linear processing (1.96 ms for 1 MiB) and LineTooLong after the next feed once `_chunk_tail` exceeds the limit. The bounding mechanism is visible at aiohttp/http_parser.py:825-832.
[R3 escalation] The defender's `_rerun2/T07-armX/repro/repro_f2_r2_single_feed_ceiling.py` reports a maximum payload-parser feed of 262124 bytes against a 262144-byte transport-read limit. Case G4a still demonstrates that a 9191-byte completed chunk-size line bypasses max_line_size=8190. aiohttp/http_parser.py:834-858 contains no completed-line length check, while aiohttp/http_parser.py:822-826 bounds a previously stored incomplete tail.
  - Defender: Partial, and at close the two sides have converged: Codex's round-3 response concedes the phrase 'arbitrarily long' and agrees the substantive severity is medium, which is the split I drew in round 1 and held in round 2. Nothing here is conceded from politeness; both halves were re-measured this round.

ACCEPTED component, re-verified standalone this round rather than remembered: the added chunked-body parser applies no length check to a chunk-size line that arrives complete inside one feed. A 9191-byte all-hex size line is accepted against max_line_size=8190 (case H1, run this round in a fresh script), it passes through the chunk-extension scan, re.fullmatch(HEXDIGITS, size_b) at aiohttp/http_parser.py:852 and int(bytes(size_b), 16) at :858, and the split-feed variant is accepted too because the deferred check at aiohttp/http_parser.py:822-826 measures only the stored tail and never the concatenation. The asymmetry the finding rests on is real and I re-ran it as a control: the same overlength line on the trailer branch is rejected immediately at aiohttp/http_parser.py:907-908 (case H1c), and the header path is stricter still, bounding both the completed line at :303-304 and the accumulated tail at :432-434. The chunk-size branch is the only line-oriented path in this file with no completed-line check, all of it inside the review window (git diff -U0 shows the added regions as lines 1-116 and 735-1055), and tests/test_http_parser.py has max_header_value_size, max_header_combined_size and max_trailer_size tests but no chunk-size-line counterpart. I accept that component in full and I am not shaving it.

REJECTED component: the recorded claim text 'attacker-controlled, arbitrarily long' and the recorded severity 'high'. Both recorded triggers that could have carried it to high are now closed by measurement rather than by argument. (a) The reachability ceiling was closed in round 2: the payload parser is never handed more than one transport read (max feed 262124 against a max read of 262144), because the outer parser's only accumulation buffer holds head bytes and is itself bounded at aiohttp/http_parser.py:432-434 — proven by a control that does raise. (b) The last open trigger, 'a retained allocation or super-linear cost attributable to the accepted oversized size line', is closed this round: tracemalloc shows one accepted 9191-byte size line leaves a current delta of 3518 bytes with _chunk_tail back to 0; an 8190-digit size value (16**8190) leaves a current delta of 7861 bytes and sizes no buffer at all, the connection simply ending in TransferEncodingError at EOF with no body delivered; and 200 oversized size lines on one connection (1.84 MB fed) cost 7.72 ms with a current delta of 28161 bytes and _chunk_tail 0 — flat memory, 4.2 ns per attacker byte, no accumulation and no amplification. Cost at the achievable single-feed ceiling is 0.478 ms for 262124 hex digits, linear in the input. (c) No parse divergence yielding request smuggling has been shown by either side, and a peer parsing the same line derives the same size from the same hex. So the slack an attacker actually gets is max_line_size plus one 256 KiB read, at linear cost with nothing retained: a genuine missing bound, medium.

I am not contesting support_level; 'strong' is right for the reachability of the mechanism, which is exactly why my objection sits in severity and scope and not in that field.

Mechanics, stated so the close is not misread: the runner never amends a recorded severity, so R1-F2 closes reading 'high' whatever verdict I give. That is why this is a partial and not an accept — an accept would put my name to the recorded text including 'arbitrarily long' and the recorded high, both of which the claimant has now withdrawn. The substantive position of both sides at close is identical: a real limit-enforcement defect in the review window, severity medium, fix as proposed.
  - Proposed fix: In the PARSE_CHUNKED_SIZE branch of HttpPayloadParser.feed_data, bound the completed line the way the trailer branch already does at aiohttp/http_parser.py:907-908 and the header branch at :303-304: immediately after `pos = chunk.find(SEP)` succeeds at aiohttp/http_parser.py:834, and before the CHUNK_EXT scan, the HEXDIGITS fullmatch at :852 and the int() conversion at :858, add `if pos > self._max_line_size: raise LineTooLong(chunk[:100] + b"...", self._max_line_size)`. That also closes the split-feed variant, since what is measured is the concatenated line rather than the stored tail. Optionally tighten the deferred check at :822-826 to compare `len(self._chunk_tail) + len(chunk)` so an oversized incomplete line is rejected on the feed that completes it rather than the one after. Add a test beside test_max_trailer_size in tests/test_http_parser.py feeding an oversized chunk-size line both in a single call and split across calls, asserting LineTooLong in both; there is currently no chunk-size-line counterpart to the max_header_value_size / max_header_combined_size / max_trailer_size tests, which is why this is uncovered.
```

### T12 — Verdict 1

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 6

**NO-SHIP: 6 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] A blocking command that times out while offline remains queued and is later sent after its caller has already received null. For destructive blocking commands such as BLPOP/BLMOVE, this can remove or move data unexpectedly after the application believes the operation failed.
  - Evidence: lib/Redis.ts:560-573 pushes the command into offlineQueue and only arms its timer; lib/Command.ts:459-469 resolves null without removing/cancelling the queued command; lib/redis/event_handler.ts:497-510 later drains every offline item through sendCommand without checking isResolved.
  - Defender: I accept this in full, at the stated severity. What changed my mind was not the argument but the run: I rebuilt the experiment from your prose against the unmodified working tree and it reproduces exactly. The three cited sites line up as claimed -- lib/Redis.ts:560-574 pushes the item and then only arms a timer; lib/Command.ts:459-469 resolves null without removing or cancelling the queued item; lib/redis/event_handler.ts:497-510 shifts every offline item into sendCommand with no isResolved check -- and I confirmed there is no guard upstream either: sendCommand's preamble (lib/Redis.ts:481-505) never short-circuits an already-resolved command. I also checked the one place a guard could plausibly have hidden: on the re-send, setBlockingTimeout is called again, but only at lib/Redis.ts:603-605, AFTER the write at lib/Redis.ts:587-595, so the deadline re-check cannot prevent transmission -- it fires on a command that is already on the wire. Severity: I do not argue it down. The feature is opt-in, but the failure mode it introduces is worse than the behaviour it replaces: without blockingTimeout the caller simply waits; with it the caller is told the operation failed and the destructive operation then happens anyway, unobserved.
  - Proposed fix: Make resolution and queue membership one operation. Give Command a cancelled/settled predicate that sendCommand honours at entry (lib/Redis.ts:481) by returning command.promise without writing, and have both drain loops (lib/redis/event_handler.ts:497-510 and :479-491) skip items whose command is already resolved. Removing the item from offlineQueue inside the blocking-timeout callback is not sufficient on its own, because the same command can be resident in commandQueue/prevCommandQueue instead -- see R1-D1.
- **R1-F2** [high/strong] Timing out an already-written blocking command settles only the local promise; it neither cancels the Redis operation nor releases the connection. An indefinite BLPOP can therefore keep the shared connection blocked forever, and later commands remain stuck behind a request whose caller was told it timed out.
  - Evidence: lib/Redis.ts:587-604 writes the command and retains it in commandQueue before arming the timer; lib/Command.ts:459-469 merely calls resolve(null); lib/DataHandler.ts:149-168 is the path that actually shifts the queued request when a server reply arrives. The timeout path does not destroy the stream, reconnect, or remove the queue entry.
  - Defender: Accepted in full at the stated severity. I did not take this on the code reading alone, because the claim has two halves and only one of them is visible in the diff. The first half -- that the timeout settles the local promise and nothing else -- I verified directly and it is unambiguous: lib/Redis.ts:587-605 writes the command and keeps it in commandQueue, lib/Command.ts:459-469 calls resolve(null) and returns, and the only site that removes a commandQueue entry is the reply path at lib/DataHandler.ts:149-169. There is no stream.destroy, no disconnect, no queue removal anywhere on the timeout path; I grepped the whole of lib/ for it. The second half -- that the connection stays wedged for later commands -- rests on server-side blocked-client semantics that I cannot exercise offline, so I measured the client side instead, and the measured state is exactly what your claim requires: after the timeout the client reports status 'ready', stream.destroyed false, commandQueue.length 1, and the server has still seen exactly one connection. In other words the client has no idea anything is outstanding and will keep handing work to a connection whose head-of-line request it has already written off. I also confirmed the sharper consequence implied by 'it does not cancel the Redis operation': when the blocking operation later completes, the returned element is consumed by the abandoned queue entry and silently dropped, so the caller that was told 'timed out' has in fact taken the data. Severity stays high on that basis alone. One honesty note, since it is in my archive and I would rather you see it from me: my fake server answers PING while BLPOP is outstanding, which a real server would not do, so the stalled follow-up command I observed is reached by a different mechanism than it would be in production. That observation is corroborating colour, not the basis of my acceptance; the four post-timeout state values and the discarded element are.
  - Proposed fix: A client-side deadline on a command the server is still executing cannot be honoured by resolving a promise. Either (a) treat expiry of an in-flight blocking command as a connection-level event -- destroy the stream and let the existing reconnect path re-establish a usable connection, flushing or resending per the existing options -- or (b) reject with a distinguishable timeout error rather than resolving null, and document that the connection remains occupied until the server unblocks. Silently resolving null while leaving both the server operation and the queue entry live is the combination that produces the data loss.
- **R1-F3** [high/strong] The socket-timeout timer is not bound to the stream generation that created it. If that stream closes and a reconnect installs a new stream before the stale timer fires, the callback dereferences this.stream at firing time and can destroy the new healthy connection.
  - Evidence: lib/Redis.ts:687-704 stores a timer whose callback calls this.stream.destroy(), while its only cancellation shown here is an old stream's one-shot data listener. lib/redis/event_handler.ts:341-387 handles close and schedules reconnect without clearing socketTimeoutTimer. The callback therefore has no captured-stream or epoch ownership check.
  - Defender: Accepted in full at the stated severity, and it reproduces. lib/Redis.ts:687-705 arms a timer whose callback dereferences this.stream at firing time; the only cancellation is a once('data') listener bound to the stream that was current when the timer was armed, so it cannot fire for a replacement stream. I searched the whole of lib/ for any other clear: the only occurrences of socketTimeoutTimer are the declaration (lib/Redis.ts:126), the arming guard (lib/Redis.ts:612-616), and the two sites inside setSocketTimeout itself -- nothing on the close or reconnect path touches it, matching your reading of lib/redis/event_handler.ts:341-387. What convinced me this is worth high rather than a theoretical race is that the project already has the exact ownership mechanism this callback is missing and uses it for this exact hazard: connectionEpoch is incremented in lib/Redis.ts:235 and captured in lib/redis/event_handler.ts:156-165 specifically so that a handshake callback cannot act on a connection that has since been replaced. The socket timer was simply not given the same guard. I also found a second consequence you did not claim, which strengthens rather than qualifies the finding: because the arming guard at lib/Redis.ts:612-613 only arms when socketTimeoutTimer === undefined, the stale timer additionally suppresses socket-timeout protection on the replacement connection for the remainder of its window, so the new connection is both unprotected and then destroyed.
  - Proposed fix: Capture the stream (or the connectionEpoch) when arming, and have the callback act only if this.stream is still that stream / the epoch still matches; additionally clear socketTimeoutTimer and set it to undefined in the close path so the replacement connection re-arms its own protection.
- **R1-D1** [high/strong] The same missing isResolved guard reaches a second, independent drain site: the unfulfilled-command resend path. A blocking command that was ALREADY WRITTEN to the socket and then resolved with null by blockingTimeout is retained in commandQueue, promoted to prevCommandQueue when the connection closes, and retransmitted verbatim on the next connection because autoResendUnfulfilledCommands defaults to true. A fix confined to the offline queue (the path R1-F1 cites) would leave this open, and it is the more common shape in production: the command reached the server before the timer fired.
  - Evidence: lib/redis/event_handler.ts:479-491 drains prevCommandQueue through self.sendCommand(item.command, item.stream) with no isResolved check; the promotion happens at lib/redis/event_handler.ts:353-360 (prevStatus === 'ready' => self.prevCommandQueue = self.commandQueue), and lib/redis/RedisOptions.ts:293 sets autoResendUnfulfilledCommands: true by default. lib/Command.ts:459-469 resolves the command without removing it from commandQueue, and lib/Redis.ts:481-505 (sendCommand entry) has no resolved-command short circuit. Runtime demonstration: <path> output <path> exit_status=0. Event log: 't=10ms server: BLPOP on connection #0' / 't=154ms caller received: null' / 't=420ms server: BLPOP on connection #1'; server transcript shows blpop executed on connections [0,1] and the popped element ('payload') never reached the caller (delivered value: null). Control on the same harness: R1-F3's run B shows the probe can report a clean run, and every script asserts received.length > 0 so an empty transcript cannot pass.
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: Prevent settled commands from entering either resend drain, and make expiration of an in-flight blocking operation release or replace the occupied connection without silently replaying the abandoned operation.
- **R1-D2** [high/strong] The blockingTimeout safety net is armed for XREAD/XREADGROUP invocations that carry no BLOCK option -- calls that do not block at all. Against a healthy server that is merely slower than the configured net, a plain XREADGROUP is cut short and returns null, which the caller cannot distinguish from 'no entries', while the server has already delivered those entries into this consumer's pending-entries list. That converts ordinary server latency into silent, unacknowledged stream loss on an opt-in setting whose stated purpose is to bound indefinite blocking.
  - Evidence: lib/Redis.ts:643-674 (getBlockingTimeoutInMs): xread/xreadgroup are in BLOCKING_COMMANDS (lib/Command.ts:162-174), and when parseBlockOption finds no BLOCK token it returns null (lib/utils/argumentParsers.ts:92), which lib/Redis.ts:668-671 maps to 'no BLOCK option found ... use blockingTimeout as safety net' -- i.e. the timer is armed for a non-blocking call. Runtime demonstration: <path> output <path> exit_status=0. The server received ['xreadgroup','GROUP','g','c','COUNT','10','STREAMS','s','>'] at t=10ms with no BLOCK argument and replied 400ms later with one entry; the caller received null at t=205ms and the entry was discarded. The behaviour is pinned by test/functional/blocking_timeout.ts:282-302 ('uses blockingTimeout for XREAD without BLOCK option'), so it is intentional as written rather than an oversight in one branch.
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: Do not arm X blocking timer when extractBlockingTimeout reports that no BLOCK option exists. Restrict this safety mechanism to commands that are actually blocking, and add an XREADGROUP no-BLOCK regression test proving delayed replies are delivered normally.
- **R2-D3** [high/strong] A blocking command that carries its OWN explicit timeout is given the short blockingTimeout safety-net deadline, not (own timeout + grace), whenever it transits the offline queue -- and because setBlockingTimeout latches an absolute deadline on its first call, the later arming on the write path (which does compute the correct per-command value) can never widen it. The consequence is not a resend: it is that a healthy, never-faulted connection abandons a correctly-issued blocking read far ahead of the timeout the caller asked for. BLPOP queue 300 issued before the connection is ready is resolved with null at ~400ms instead of ~300s, indistinguishable from 'no element', while the server keeps the operation live. The offline queue is the normal path for any command issued at startup and for anything issued during a reconnect window, so this makes the silent-loss mode the ledger has already accepted reachable with no network fault at all. It is independent of R1-F1/R1-D1: in this scenario the command does not time out while queued, so guarding either resend drain against already-resolved commands leaves it untouched.
  - Evidence: Code: the offline arming site at lib/Redis.ts:569-574 calls command.setBlockingTimeout(this.getConfiguredBlockingTimeout()) -- the raw option (lib/Redis.ts:676-684) -- with no reference to the command's own timeout; the write path at lib/Redis.ts:507 and lib/Redis.ts:603-605 instead uses getBlockingTimeoutInMs (lib/Redis.ts:643-674), which for a command with a positive timeout returns timeout + blockingTimeoutGrace. lib/Command.ts:433-470 reconciles the two by latching: at lib/Command.ts:446-449 the deadline is set only when _blockingDeadline === undefined, and lib/Command.ts:452-457 then computes remaining from that first, shorter deadline, so the second (correct) arming shortens rather than widens. The documented intent is the opposite: lib/redis/RedisOptions.ts:23-35 describes blockingTimeoutGrace as a 'Grace period (ms) added to blocking command timeouts', i.e. the command's own timeout is meant to govern when it exists. Runtime demonstration with a control arm, from the unmodified working tree: <path> captured output <path> exit_status=0. Arm X (offline transit, blockingTimeout: 400, command BLPOP queue 300): 't=126ms server started', 't=169ms SERVER RECEIVED: blpop queue 300', 't=404ms caller received: null' -- the write reached a healthy socket and the server held the call open, yet the caller was told null at 404ms against a requested 300000ms. Arm X (control, identical options and identical command, server already up so no offline transit): the caller is still unsettled when the 1500ms window closes -- so the probe is capable of reporting 'not abandoned', and the offline transit is the discriminating variable. Both arms assert a non-empty server transcript (A and B each received 2 commands: [hello 3, blpop queue 300]). Clean rerun of the whole archive: <path> all six scripts exit 0; interpreter and dependency versions in <path>
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: Use the command-aware deadline when first queued, preserving the requested finite timeout plus grace. The offline duration should count against that absolute deadline, but the configured safety net should apply only to genuinely indefinite commands.
```

### T12 — Verdict 2

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 9

**NO-SHIP: 5 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] The client-side blocking-command deadline turns a successful server-side pop into silent, irreversible data loss, and can pop again after the caller has given up. On expiry the command's promise is resolved with `null` — indistinguishable from a genuine 'nothing was there' — while the command object stays in the command queue and the connection stays healthy. The reply the server subsequently sends is matched to that already-settled command and discarded, so for BLPOP/BRPOP/BLMOVE/BZPOPMIN the element has been removed from the list and delivered to nobody. The same already-settled command is then re-written to the server by the reconnect resend path (which never checks `isResolved`), destroying a second element. Because the deadline is stamped absolutely on first arming and can never be re-based, a command that asked to block 30 seconds is capped at the option's value after a sub-second connect blip, so the loss needs no zombie socket and no hung server. Nothing is logged, no error event fires, and no metric moves. This is gated behind opting into the option, which bounds blast radius but not severity for anyone who opts in — and the option is presented as a safety net, so the people who enable it are exactly those who cannot afford to lose queue items.
  - Evidence: MECHANISM (code): lib/Command.ts:433-470 `setBlockingTimeout` resolves the caller's promise with `null` on expiry (`this.resolve(null)` at :455 and :468) and nothing else — the command object is not removed from any queue, the connection is not touched, no error is emitted. lib/Command.ts:575-585 shows `resolve` is the promise's own resolver, so the promise is settled permanently at that instant. lib/Redis.ts:597-605 pushes the command onto `this.commandQueue` BEFORE arming the deadline, so the timer fires on a command that is still queued and will still be answered by the server. When the real reply arrives it is matched to that queued command and handed to an already-settled promise, i.e. discarded. For BLPOP/BRPOP/BLMOVE/BZPOPMIN the element is gone from the server-side list at that point, so the loss is irreversible.

MECHANISM (second write): lib/redis/event_handler.ts:479-495 resends `prevCommandQueue` on reconnect when `autoResendUnfulfilledCommands` is set, and :497-511 flushes the offline queue the same way. Neither checks `command.isResolved` (declared lib/Command.ts:239, set at lib/Command.ts:579). `autoResendUnfulfilledCommands` defaults to true (lib/redis/RedisOptions.ts:293). So a blocking command the caller has already been told returned nothing is written to the server again and pops a further element into nothing.

MECHANISM (deadline is absolute and never re-based): lib/Redis.ts:566-574 arms the offline safety net with `getConfiguredBlockingTimeout()` (the raw option), not with the command's own timeout. lib/Command.ts:444-457 stamps `_blockingDeadline` on the FIRST call only and later calls cannot extend it. So the deadline chosen while the command sat offline permanently caps a command whose declared Redis timeout is far longer.

REPRO A (silent loss) — <path> exit=0: 'caller received: null after 153 ms / server delivered the element: true / client status after the drop: ready / subsequent command on same connection: "alive" / VERDICT dataLost=true'. The connection is healthy afterwards and nothing is logged.

REPRO B (second pop) — repro/B-resend-after-resolved.ts, exit=0: 'server received BLPOP #1 (caller already settled: false) / caller received: null after 123 ms / connection dropped; waiting for reconnect + resend / server received BLPOP #2 (caller already settled: true) / VERDICT resentAfterCallerSettled=true'.

REPRO E (no hung server needed — a 400ms connect outage is enough) — repro/E-offline-deadline-caps-command.ts, exit=0: 'issuing BLPOP with a 30 second Redis timeout while offline / [102ms] caller received: null / [412ms] server: now listening (outage over) / [437ms] server: received BLPOP, popping an element for it / caller settled at (ms): 102 - it asked to block 30000ms / VERDICT poppedElementLostAfterEarlyNull=true'.

AGGRAVATOR: lib/Redis.ts:668-671 also arms the net when `extractBlockingTimeout()` returns null, i.e. XREAD/XREADGROUP with no BLOCK option — commands that do not block at all. A slow-but-normal XREADGROUP then yields `null` to the caller while its entries have been delivered into the group's pending-entries list, unacked and unseen.

The project's own tests encode the early-`null` as correct and never check what the server did afterwards: test/functional/blocking_timeout.ts asserts only `expect(result).to.be.null` (9 passing, captured in repro/OUTPUT.txt). So the suite passing is not evidence against this.

PROVENANCE: node v25.6.1; declared runtime pins resolved exactly (@ioredis/commands@2.0.0, cluster-key-slot@1.1.1, debug@4.4.3, denque@2.1.0, redis-errors@1.2.0, standard-as-callback@2.1.0) from the prepared frozen environment via NODE_PATH; `require.resolve` of the project's own import name is MODULE_NOT_FOUND from that environment, so every probe imported the reviewed working tree. Driver, sources and captured output: <path> (run.sh, OUTPUT.txt, MANIFEST.md).
  - Defender: Accepted in full, at the claimed severity (high) and support level (strong). I did not take this on the critic's word: I rebuilt all three components from the finding's prose against the working tree, each with a control run differing only in whether `blockingTimeout` is set, and all three reproduce. (1) Silent loss - my A2-lost-element.js: with blockingTimeout=150 the caller receives `null` at 152ms, the stub server pops JOB-12345 at 415ms, the client's status is still `ready`, a following GET succeeds on that same connection, and zero error events fire (VERDICT dataLost=true). Control with the option unset, same stub and timings: the caller receives ["queue","JOB-12345"] (dataLost=false). (2) Second write - my B2-resend-after-settled.js: BLPOP #1 at 10ms, caller settled null at 127ms, connection dropped at 313ms, BLPOP #2 written at 373ms with 'caller already settled: true' (resentAfterCallerSettled=true; control false). (3) Absolute deadline - my E2-offline-deadline-caps.js: a BLPOP declaring a 30 second Redis timeout, issued while nothing is listening, settles null at 103ms; the outage ends at 413ms and the server pops an element at 442ms into nobody (poppedElementLostAfterEarlyNull=true; control false, the caller gets the element at 437ms). The code reads exactly as the finding says at every cited point, and the aggravator checks out too. What persuaded me specifically, beyond the repros: lib/Command.ts:452-456 and :459-469 call `this.resolve(null)` and do nothing else - no dequeue, no connection change, no emit - while lib/Command.ts:574-585 shows `resolve` is the promise's own resolver, so the settle is permanent and the genuine reply that arrives later is handed to a dead resolver; and lib/redis/event_handler.ts:479-491 shifts `prevCommandQueue` straight into `sendCommand` with no `isResolved` test, with `autoResendUnfulfilledCommands: true` in DEFAULT_REDIS_OPTIONS. One factual refinement that does not reduce the severity and is not a rejection of any component: for BLMOVE and BRPOPLPUSH the element is atomically pushed to the destination key, so it survives on the server and is recoverable there - the irreversible-loss characterisation is exact for BLPOP/BRPOP/BZPOPMIN/BZPOPMAX, and the second-pop consequence holds for all of them. I also confirm the finding's point about the project's own tests: test/functional/blocking_timeout.ts runs 9 passing here and every one of them asserts the early `null` as the correct outcome without ever inspecting what the server did afterwards, so the suite passing is not evidence against this.
  - Proposed fix: When the blocking deadline fires, stop treating it as a plain resolution. Concretely: (a) remove the command's entry from `commandQueue`/`offlineQueue` before settling it, so no later reply is matched to a dead resolver; (b) because the pop may already have happened server-side and cannot be recalled, do not settle with `null` on the connection-loss path at all - reject with a distinguishable error (the caller can then decide whether the work is safe to retry) or at minimum emit on the `error`/tracing channel so the loss is observable; (c) guard both resend loops in lib/redis/event_handler.ts:479-511 with `if (item.command.isResolved) continue;` so a settled command is never written a second time; (d) re-base `_blockingDeadline` when the command moves from the offline queue to the wire, or arm the offline net from the command's own extracted timeout rather than from the raw `blockingTimeout` option, so a 30 second BLPOP is not capped by a sub-second connect blip; (e) do not arm the net at all when `extractBlockingTimeout()` returns null for a BLOCK_OPTION_COMMANDS command, since that means the command was never asked to block.
- **R1-F2** [medium/strong] The socket-inactivity timer is client-scoped rather than connection-scoped and is never cancelled when the connection it was armed for dies, so after a reconnect it fires against the successor connection and destroys it — emitting an error that asserts no data was received on a connection that demonstrably just received data. In-flight commands on that healthy connection are rejected with a misleading cause, and by the option's own documentation those commands may already have been executed server-side, so retrying non-idempotent work duplicates it. The kill also forces a further reconnect, so a client configured with this option converts every transient disconnect into a second, self-inflicted outage.
  - Evidence: MECHANISM: the inactivity timer is a client field, not a per-connection one — lib/Redis.ts:126 `private socketTimeoutTimer`. lib/Redis.ts:687-705 arms it with `this.stream.destroy(...)` in the callback, resolving `this.stream` at FIRE time, and relies on a single `this.stream.once("data", ...)` on the stream that was current at ARM time to cancel it. lib/Redis.ts:611-616 refuses to arm X new one while the old one is pending.

NOTHING CANCELS IT WHEN THE CONNECTION DIES. A repository-wide search for the identifier finds it only at lib/Redis.ts:126, 612-613, 688-695 and 700-701 (plus the option declaration at lib/redis/RedisOptions.ts:43). `disconnect()` (lib/Redis.ts:365-378) does not clear it; `closeHandler` (lib/redis/event_handler.ts:341-409) does not clear it; `_connect` reassigns `_this.stream = stream` (lib/Redis.ts:276) without clearing it. Worse, lib/Redis.ts:990-994 `flushQueue` calls `this.stream.removeAllListeners("data")`, which removes the very `once("data")` handler that was the timer's only cancellation path.

CONSEQUENCE: after a reconnect the surviving timer fires against the SUCCESSOR stream and destroys it with an error asserting no data was received — while that stream has been receiving data. In-flight commands on a working connection are rejected, and per the option's own documentation (lib/redis/RedisOptions.ts:37-43) those commands 'might have been processed by the server', so any non-idempotent retry duplicates work. The destroy also forces another reconnect cycle.

Expecting data, but didn\'t receive any in 400ms. / [446ms] accepted connection #3 / VERDICT staleTimerKilledSuccessorConnection=true'. Connection #2 was killed 146ms after it had served a reply, exactly 400ms after the timer was armed on connection #1.

CONTROL (the probe can fail): the identical scenario with the option unset — `CONTROL=1`, same file, exit=0 — gives 'connection accept times (ms): [8,148] / socket-timeout error at (ms): -1 / VERDICT control_staleTimerKilledSuccessorConnection=false'. Connection #2 survives, so the kill is attributable to the stale timer and not to the harness.

I rebuilt the scenario from the prose - my C2-stale-socket-timer.js, socketTimeout=400 - and it reproduces: connection #1 accepted at 13ms and immediately handed a command the stub never answers (arming the timer); the harness destroys connection #1 at 126ms; connection #2 is accepted at 160ms and completes a full round trip, answering `set live` at 237ms; at 426ms - 400ms after the timer was armed on connection #1, and 189ms after connection #2 demonstrably received data - the client emits 'Socket timeout. Expecting data, but didn't receive any in 400ms.' and connection #3 is accepted at 461ms (VERDICT staleTimerKilledSuccessorConnection=true). The control with `socketTimeout` unset, same stub and same timings, opens only two connections and never emits the error (control_staleTimerKilledSuccessorConnection=false), so the kill is attributable to the stale timer and not to my harness. The code confirms the mechanism at every cited point: the timer is a client field (lib/Redis.ts:126), the callback resolves `this.stream` at fire time rather than capturing the stream it was armed for (lib/Redis.ts:688-693), and a repository-wide grep for the identifier returns only lib/Redis.ts:126, 613, 688, 694, 700 and 701 - there is no clear on teardown anywhere, and lib/Redis.ts:990-993 `flushQueue` calls `this.stream.removeAllListeners("data")`, which removes the `once("data")` handler that is the timer's only cancellation path. I have nothing to reject. I will add one consequence the finding did not state, which strengthens rather than qualifies it: while the stale timer is pending, lib/Redis.ts:611-613 refuses to arm X new one, so between the reconnect and the stale timer firing the successor connection has no inactivity protection of its own - it inherits a deadline measured from a connection that no longer exists. On severity I agree with medium rather than high: the finding's own stated bar for high was that the kill recur on each reconnect, and what I observed is one kill per armed timer, after which `socketTimeoutTimer` is reset to undefined (lib/Redis.ts:694) and the cycle only restarts if a fresh command arms a fresh timer.
  - Proposed fix: Bind the timer to the connection it was armed for instead of to the client. Capture the stream in a local at arm time and destroy that captured stream in the callback, so a timer belonging to a dead connection can only ever destroy the dead connection; and clear the pending timer whenever a stream goes away - in `disconnect()`, in `closeHandler`, and on the `_this.stream = stream` reassignment in `_connect` - resetting `socketTimeoutTimer` to undefined so the successor connection arms its own timer from its own first write rather than inheriting a stranger's deadline. Cancelling via a `close`/`error` listener on the captured stream would be more robust than the current `once("data")` handler, which `flushQueue` removes.
- **R1-F3** [high/strong] The trace-argument redaction leaks credentials while documenting itself as safe-by-default. Its comment claims unlisted commands have all arguments redacted and that this 'covers AUTH, HELLO, and unknown/custom commands' — true for those two, but the allowlist separately grants 'emit every argument' to the ACL, CONFIG, CLIENT, CLUSTER and EVAL families. `CONFIG SET requirepass <password>`, `ACL SETUSER <user> on ><password>` and every EVAL ARGV are therefore published verbatim on the command tracing channel, whose realistic subscriber is an APM agent that ships spans to a third-party backend. The redaction of AUTH is what makes this dangerous: it gives operators a documented reason to believe secrets cannot reach the channel.
  - Evidence: CLAIMED CONTRACT: lib/tracing.ts:6-9 states 'Unlisted commands default to 0 (all args redacted) for safe-by-default behavior, which covers AUTH, HELLO, and unknown/custom commands.'

ACTUAL RULE: lib/tracing.ts:18-22 grants args:-1, i.e. emit EVERY argument, to a regex whose alternatives include `ACL`, `CONFIG`, `CLIENT`, `CLUSTER` and `EVAL`. lib/tracing.ts:38-40 then returns `args.map((a) => String(a))` unfiltered. lib/Redis.ts:843-852 feeds `sanitizeArgs(command.name, command.args)` into the command trace context, and lib/Redis.ts:636-640 publishes it on the `ioredis:command` tracing channel for any subscriber.

SECRETS THAT LIVE IN THOSE ARGUMENT LISTS: `CONFIG SET requirepass <password>` sets the server password; `ACL SETUSER <user> on ><password>` sets a user password; `EVAL <script> <numkeys> <keys...> <argv...>` routinely carries session tokens and PII in ARGV. All three are emitted in full. AUTH and HELLO being redacted does not make the surface safe — it makes the gap harder to notice.

REPRO D — <path> exit=0. Direct calls: auth -> ["?"]; hello -> ["?","?","?","?"]; config -> ["SET","requirepass","s3cr3t-password"]; acl -> ["SETUSER","alice","on",">s3cr3t-password","~*","+@all"]; eval -> ["return 1","1","session:key","bearer-token-abc123"]; set -> ["cache-key","?"]. End to end with a real subscriber attached to the tracing channel, the subscriber received: {"command":"config","args":["SET","requirepass","s3cr3t-password"]} and {"command":"acl","args":["SETUSER","alice","on",">s3cr3t-password"]}, while auth arrived as ["?"]. 'entries containing the plaintext secret: 2 / VERDICT credentialsPublishedToTraceChannel=true'.

WHY THE SINK MATTERS: the subscriber for this channel in practice is an APM/OpenTelemetry agent, whose whole purpose is to ship spans off the host to a third-party backend, where they are retained and broadly readable. A credential written into a span attribute is a credential exported outside the trust boundary that set it, and rotating it is the only remedy once shipped.

Then, end to end, with a real diagnostics_channel subscriber attached to `ioredis:command` and real commands driven through a client against a stub server, the subscriber received {"command":"config","args":["SET","requirepass","s3cr3t-password"]} and {"command":"acl","args":["SETUSER","alice","on",">s3cr3t-password"]} while auth arrived as ["?"] - 'entries containing the plaintext secret: 2 / VERDICT credentialsPublishedToTraceChannel=true'. That is precisely the pair of observations the finding said a rebuttal would have to change, and I could not change either. The rule reads as claimed: lib/tracing.ts:18-22 grants args:-1 to a regex whose alternatives include ACL, CLIENT, CLUSTER, CONFIG and EVAL, and lib/tracing.ts:38-40 then returns every argument stringified with no filtering; lib/Redis.ts:843-852 feeds `sanitizeArgs(command.name, command.args)` into the command trace context and lib/Redis.ts:636-640 publishes it. I would sharpen one thing in the finding's favour rather than against it: the misleading comment is not only at lib/tracing.ts:8-9. Line 6-7 states that '-1 means all args are safe (read-only/structural commands)', and the -1 alternative list contains CONFIG, ACL and EVAL, which are none of read-only, structural, or safe - so the file's own description of its rule is false on its face, before a reader ever gets to the AUTH sentence. I accept the high severity: the argument list is published verbatim to a channel whose realistic subscriber exports spans off the host, and a credential that has been exported can only be remediated by rotation.
  - Proposed fix: Apply a denylist before the allowlist. Drop ACL, CONFIG, CLIENT and CLUSTER out of the args:-1 alternative and give them subcommand-aware entries: emit the subcommand verb and, for the read-only verbs (CONFIG GET, ACL LIST/GETUSER, CLIENT INFO, CLUSTER INFO) the remaining arguments, but redact every argument after the verb for the write verbs (CONFIG SET, ACL SETUSER). Move EVAL/EVALSHA/FCALL to a rule that emits the script identifier, `numkeys` and the KEYS, and redacts every ARGV. Then correct lib/tracing.ts:6-9 so it describes the rule that is actually implemented rather than a safe-by-default posture the table does not have, and add a test that drives CONFIG SET, ACL SETUSER and EVAL through a live subscriber and asserts the secret positions arrive as `?` - test/functional/tracing.ts has no such case today.
- **R1-D1** [high/strong] The change arms the socket-inactivity timer for blocking commands as well as ordinary ones, with no exclusion, so enabling `socketTimeout` silently makes the entire BLOCKING_COMMANDS family unusable. A BLPOP that is legitimately waiting produces no bytes by definition; the timer reads that as a dead socket, destroys a perfectly healthy connection, and `autoResendUnfulfilledCommands` immediately rewrites the same blocking command to a fresh connection, which is destroyed on the same schedule. The result is a self-sustaining reconnect loop: the caller's command never completes and never honours its own declared Redis timeout, an error event fires every cycle asserting a socket fault that did not happen, and the client opens a new connection to the server roughly every `socketTimeout` milliseconds for as long as any blocking command is outstanding. This is distinct from R1-F2: that finding is about a timer outliving the connection it was armed for; this one is about the timer being armed for blocking commands at all. It is the more pointed defect because the same change teaches the client to recognise blocking commands (Command.checkFlag("BLOCKING_COMMANDS", ...) is consulted at lib/Redis.ts:569 and :644) and then does not consult that knowledge at the one place where blocking commands make the inactivity premise false. Setting `blockingTimeout` as well does not help; the two features do not coordinate.
  - Evidence: MECHANISM: lib/Redis.ts:611-616 arms the inactivity timer for every command written on the write path, gated only on `this.options.socketTimeout !== undefined` and on no timer already being pending. There is no BLOCKING_COMMANDS exclusion there, and none inside lib/Redis.ts:687-705 `setSocketTimeout`, whose re-arm condition is only `this.commandQueue.length === 0` (:702) - a pending BLPOP keeps the queue non-empty, so the timer is re-armed for as long as the command blocks. The option's own documentation at lib/redis/RedisOptions.ts:37-43 states the premise the code relies on ('If the socket does not receive data within a set number of milliseconds ... the socket is considered dead'), which is exactly the premise a blocking command is designed to violate.
```
