Expecting data, but didn't receive any in 300ms. / [370ms] accepted connection #2 / [371ms] conn#2: BLPOP #2 / [671ms] error / [716ms] accepted connection #3 ... / connections opened: 6 / BLPOPs written to the server: 6 / socket-timeout error events: 5 at [323,671,1017,1364,1712] / caller outcome: PENDING / VERDICT healthyBlockingConnectionKilled=true'. Six connections and six writes of the same BLPOP in 1.8 seconds, and the caller has learned nothing.

The single option is the whole difference.

THE TWO FEATURES DO NOT COORDINATE: <path> is the same probe with `blockingTimeout: 5000` added alongside `socketTimeout: 300`; the loop is unchanged - 'connections opened: 6 / socket-timeout error events: 5 at [324,673,1020,1368,1717] / caller outcome: PENDING / VERDICT healthyBlockingConnectionKilled=true'.

That file's three tests all require a live Redis on 127.0.0.1:6379 and could not be collected here (0 passing, 3 failing), so the option's only project-owned coverage did not execute at all in this environment.

I would add (d), which my measurement above makes necessary and which none of (a)-(c) covers: retryAttempts must not be reset on a connection that never completed any useful work, or the maxRetriesPerRequest bound must be evaluated per outstanding command rather than per consecutive-failed-connect streak. - **R2-F1** [high/strong] A SELECT that the server rejects during connection setup is swallowed, and the client still reaches `ready`. From that moment every command the application issues executes against whatever database the server is actually on (0), while the client's own `condition.select`, the value it re-derives on each reconnect, and the `database` field it publishes on the command tracing channel all keep reporting the database the application asked for. Nothing re-issues SELECT and nothing re-checks it, so the divergence is permanent for the life of the client and survives every reconnect: writes land in the wrong database and reads miss. The only signal is one generic `error` event carrying the server's raw message, indistinguishable from the routine transient connection errors this client emits during any reconnect, and it says nothing about the client having carried on regardless. There is no API by which an application can ask which database the connection is really on. This is a silent wrong-target-write, not a connection failure: the client reports itself healthy and the application's data goes somewhere it did not choose.
  - Evidence: MECHANISM: lib/redis/event_handler.ts:49-55 registers the setup SELECT with `errorHandler: (err) => self.silentEmit("error", err)`. lib/redis/event_handler.ts:126-136 (the RESP3 path, after `Promise.allSettled` at :115) and lib/redis/event_handler.ts:104-111 (the RESP2 path, `send().catch(errorHandler)` under `Promise.all`) both treat 'this command has an errorHandler' as 'continue' — only a command WITHOUT an errorHandler rethrows. `handleAuthError` (lib/redis/event_handler.ts:251-274) rethrows for genuine auth failures, which is why an auth failure is fatal; the SELECT handler does not, so a rejected SELECT is absorbed. connectHandler then proceeds normally to `_readyCheck` and `readyHandler`, so the client is marked `ready` on a connection sitting on the wrong database.

NOTHING CORRECTS IT: `self.condition.select` is never set back. lib/redis/event_handler.ts:445-447 computes `finalSelect` from `prevCondition`/`condition`, and :513-516 re-issues SELECT only `if (self.condition.select !== finalSelect)` — both sides are the requested db, so the branch never fires. The per-item guards in the two resend loops (lib/redis/event_handler.ts:484-489 and :503-508) compare `item.select` against the same wrong `condition.select`, so they never issue a corrective SELECT either. lib/Redis.ts:619-626 only ever moves `condition.select` forward on a locally issued `select`.

OBSERVABILITY MAKES IT WORSE, NOT BETTER: lib/Redis.ts:843-852 `_buildCommandContext` reports `database: this.condition?.select ?? this.options.db ?? 0`, i.e. the requested database — so the command trace published at lib/Redis.ts:636-640 attributes every one of these commands to a database they did not run in. An operator reading traces sees exactly the database they expected.

I re-read every cited line in the working tree again this round and each reads as claimed: the setup SELECT is registered with an errorHandler that only silentEmits (lib/redis/event_handler.ts:49-55); both handshake paths treat 'has an errorHandler' as 'continue' (:126-136 RESP3, :104-111 RESP2) while handleAuthError rethrows (:251-274); nothing moves condition.select back, so the corrective branch at :513-516 and the per-item guards at :484-489 and :503-508 compare the wrong value against itself and never fire. THE AGGRAVATOR INSIDE THE REVIEWED WINDOW: lib/Redis.ts:843-852 builds the command trace context with 'database: this.condition?.select ?? this.options.db ?? 0' - the requested database, not the observed one - and this function is an added line of the frozen review window, so the change's own new observability surface positively attributes these commands to a database they did not run in. My round-3 probe confirms the mechanism of that field independently: the published span carries a 'database' member taken from condition.select at publish time. ONE CORRECTION TO THIS SEAT'S EARLIER FRAMING: round 2 recorded a scope observation that 'git diff --name-only' returns lib/Redis.ts alone, so the swallow mechanism sits in an unmodified file. I withdraw any weight from that. The debate's own collection guidance states the diff is a review window and not a patch history, that the pre-diff baseline is synthetic and was never executed, and that the code is to be reviewed as it stands; and the recorded pre-image is in fact a 605-line fragment that already contains sendCommand and the trace call verbatim (git show HEAD:lib/Redis.ts, lines 497-509), which is exactly why diff-based attribution is worthless here in either direction. I accept the finding on the code as it stands. SEVERITY: high is right and I am not negotiating it. A silent wrong-database write that survives every reconnect, with no API to ask which database the connection is really on and a trace channel that actively reports the wrong answer, is the worst shape a defect of this kind can take.
  - Proposed fix: Two changes, and I agree with the critic about which one this review can actually require. (1) Make a rejected setup SELECT non-silent. The minimal honest version is to clear self.condition.select when the setup SELECT rejects, so the corrective branch at lib/redis/event_handler.ts:513-516 re-issues it on the next connection instead of comparing the requested value against itself; the stronger version is to rethrow the way handleAuthError does at :251-274 and fail the connection. Either way, expose the database the connection is known to be on so an application can assert it, and rewrite test/functional/connection.ts:569-586 - which currently asserts only that an error is emitted - to assert where a subsequent write lands. (2) Independently, and squarely inside the reviewed window: lib/Redis.ts:843-852 must not report a requested value as an observed one. Either publish the database the connection is known to be on, or omit the member when the setup SELECT did not succeed. Publishing 'database: 5' for a command that ran on db 0 makes the change's new observability surface an active source of wrong answers, which is worse than having no member at all.
- **R2-F2** [medium/strong] The BLOCK-option parser for XREAD/XREADGROUP scans every argument for a token spelled 'block' (case-insensitively) and takes the argument after it as the block duration, with no awareness of where that option may legally appear. XREADGROUP places user-controlled names — the consumer group and the consumer — before the option list, and XREAD's STREAMS keys are user-controlled too, so any of those named 'block' hijacks the parse. Two outcomes follow, both wrong. When the neighbouring argument is not numeric the extractor returns `undefined` and the client-side blocking protection is silently switched off for that command even though it declared a BLOCK duration — a safety feature that disables itself on the basis of a data value, exactly where the operator believes it is armed. When the neighbouring argument is numeric, that number becomes the client's deadline: a command declaring BLOCK 30000 is capped at 2ms plus grace. The caller is then settled with `null` while the server is still holding the command, and for XREADGROUP the entries the server subsequently delivers land in the consumer group's pending-entries list, unacked, attributed to a consumer that has been told there was nothing to read. In a multi-tenant deployment the hijacking value is supplied by whoever names the stream, group or consumer.
  - Evidence: MECHANISM: lib/utils/argumentParsers.ts:73-93 `parseBlockOption` iterates the entire argument array (`for (let i = 0; i < args.length; i++)`), matches `token.toLowerCase() === "block"` against every element, and returns `parseNumberArgument(args[i + 1])` for the FIRST such element — returning `undefined` when that neighbour is not a finite number (:80-82). It has no notion of the option region ending at STREAMS, and no notion of XREADGROUP's `GROUP <group> <consumer>` prefix preceding the options. lib/Command.ts:500-502 routes xread/xreadgroup to it, and lib/Redis.ts:654-673 converts the result into the client-side deadline: a positive number becomes `timeout + blockingTimeoutGrace` (:656-662, grace defaults to 100 at lib/redis/RedisOptions.ts:306), while `undefined` falls through to `return undefined` at :673, i.e. no timer at all. The settle-with-null consequence is the mechanism at lib/Command.ts:459-469 that is already established on this ledger.

REPRO H, part 1 (direct calls on the working tree) — <path> exit=0: 'honest case: parseBlockOption=30000 extractBlockingTimeout=30000 / consumer named "block": parseBlockOption=undefined extractBlockingTimeout=undefined / group "BLOCK", consumer "2": parseBlockOption=2 extractBlockingTimeout=2 / stream key named "block", no BLOCK option: parseBlockOption=undefined extractBlockingTimeout=undefined / stream key named "block", id 0: parseBlockOption=0 extractBlockingTimeout=0'. Four different answers for four argument lists that differ only in user-supplied names.

REPRO H, part 2 (end to end, same file, exit=0): a client with `blockingTimeout: 30000` issues `xreadgroup GROUP BLOCK 2 BLOCK 30000 STREAMS s >` against a stub that blocks like a real server. Captured: '[9ms] server: received xreadgroup GROUP BLOCK 2 BLOCK 30000 STREAMS s > - blocking, as a real server would / caller settled at (ms): 102 with: null / the command declared BLOCK 30000 (30 seconds); observation window was 1500ms / VERDICT declaredBlockIgnored=true'.

CONTROL (the probe can fail): `CONTROL=1`, same file, identical except the group and consumer are named `orders` and `worker-1` — 'caller settled at (ms): -1 with: PENDING / VERDICT control_declaredBlockIgnored=false'. The names are the whole difference.

NO TEST COVERS IT, AND THE TESTS ENCODE THE SCAN AS CORRECT: a repository-wide search of test/ for `parseBlockOption` or `argumentParsers` returns nothing, and the only test that uses a lowercase `block` token, test/unit/command.ts:443, uses it as the option keyword (`["block", 3000, "STREAMS", "stream", "0"]`) — so the case-insensitive whole-array scan is asserted as intended behaviour and no case exists anywhere for a key, group or consumer carrying that name.

  - Defender: Accepted in full at the claimed severity (medium) and support level (strong). What persuaded me was not the reasoning but the two observations the finding said a rebuttal would have to change, both of which I reproduced and neither of which I could change. First, calling the parser and the extractor directly on the working tree: the honest list ['GROUP','orders','worker-1','BLOCK','30000','STREAMS','s','>'] gives 30000; renaming only the consumer to 'block' gives undefined; renaming only the group to 'BLOCK' and the consumer to '2' gives 2. Three argument lists that all declare BLOCK 30000 and differ only in user-supplied names, three different answers. I then asked the client itself what deadline it would arm with blockingTimeout: 30000, which is the step that turns a parse quirk into behaviour: honest=30100, consumer named 'block'=undefined (no timer armed at all - the safety net silently switches itself off on the basis of a data value), group 'BLOCK' with consumer '2'=102 (2ms plus the 100ms grace). Second, end to end: a client with blockingTimeout 30000 issuing 'xreadgroup GROUP BLOCK 2 BLOCK 30000 STREAMS s >' against a stub that blocks like a real server has its caller settled with null at 115ms, while the control - identical in every respect except that the group and consumer are named 'orders' and 'worker-1' - is still pending at the end of the 1500ms window. The code reads as claimed: lib/utils/argumentParsers.ts:73-93 scans the entire argument array, matches token.toLowerCase() === 'block' anywhere in it, returns parseNumberArgument(args[i+1]) and undefined when that neighbour is not finite; it has no notion of the option region ending at STREAMS and none of XREADGROUP's GROUP <group> <consumer> prefix. lib/Command.ts:500-502 routes xread/xreadgroup to it and lib/Redis.ts:654-673 turns the result into the deadline or into no timer at all. I add one refinement in the finding's favour rather than against it, because it narrows where the numeric-hijack fires and I would rather it be on the record than discovered later: the hijack takes effect on the ready path, where the extracted value arms the deadline directly. If the same command is issued before the connection is ready it goes through the offline queue, where lib/Redis.ts:569-574 arms from the raw blockingTimeout option instead, and lib/Command.ts:447-449 stamps that deadline first and never re-bases it - so the hijacked value is masked and the caller stays pending. My MODE=offline run confirms that. This does not reduce the finding: the ready path is the normal path for a worker looping on XREADGROUP, and the 'protection silently disabled' branch fires on both paths. I agree with medium: the numeric-neighbour case needs a group or key named 'block' followed by a numeric name, which is reachable but not ordinary, while the more likely branch turns the safety net off rather than firing it early.
- **R2-F3** [high/strong] A command that has already been rejected with `Command timed out` is left in the command queue and is rewritten to the server by the reconnect resend path, so a non-idempotent write the caller was explicitly told had FAILED is applied a second time on the successor connection — with no caller left to observe it, because the promise is already settled. The `commandTimeout` option is the one a caller reaches for precisely to get a definite answer about a command's fate; here the definite answer is wrong in the most expensive direction, reporting failure for work that the client then goes on to perform again. This needs no new option to be enabled: `commandTimeout` plus the default `autoResendUnfulfilledCommands` plus an ordinary transient disconnect. Separately and compounding it, `isResolved` is set only inside the resolve path and never by the reject path, so it is `false` for every rejected command; the `isResolved` guard proposed for the two resend loops therefore does not stop this class of double-write at all.
  - Evidence: MECHANISM (the reject leaves the command queued and unmarked): lib/Redis.ts:503-505 arms `command.setTimeout(this.options.commandTimeout)`. lib/Command.ts:417-425 fires `this.reject(new Error("Command timed out"))` and does nothing else — no dequeue from `commandQueue`, no connection change. lib/Command.ts:536-543 `reject` clears the timers and rejects the promise; it never touches `isResolved`. lib/Command.ts:574-585 shows `isResolved = true` is executed only on the resolve path, after `resolve(this.transformReply(value))` — so `isResolved` is `false` for every rejected command, including this one. lib/Redis.ts:597-601 had already pushed the command onto `commandQueue`, and nothing removes it.

MECHANISM (the second write): lib/redis/event_handler.ts:353-360 moves the still-populated `commandQueue` into `prevCommandQueue` on close, and :479-491 shifts it straight back into `sendCommand` when `autoResendUnfulfilledCommands` is set — which it is by default (DEFAULT_REDIS_OPTIONS in lib/redis/RedisOptions.ts). Neither loop tests `isResolved`, and even if it did, the flag is false here.

REPRO J — <path> exit=0. `commandTimeout: 200`; the stub applies INCR and withholds the first reply; the harness then drops the connection, an ordinary transient outage. Captured: '[8ms] server: INCR #1 applied - counter is now 1 / caller outcome after 400ms: REJECTED Command timed out at 201ms / harness: dropping the connection (ordinary transient outage) / [517ms] server: accepted connection #2 / [520ms] server: INCR #2 applied - counter is now 2 / INCRs the server applied in total: 2 (counter=2) / INCRs applied AFTER the caller was told the command failed: 1 / Command.isResolved after reject(): false / VERDICT nonIdempotentWriteReappliedAfterReportedFailure=true / VERDICT isResolvedAfterReject=false / VERDICT serverAppliedIncrs=2'.

CONTROL (the probe can fail): `CONTROL=1`, same file, changing only `autoResendUnfulfilledCommands` to false — 'INCRs the server applied in total: 1 (counter=1) / INCRs applied AFTER the caller was told the command failed: 0 / VERDICT control_nonIdempotentWriteReappliedAfterReportedFailure=false'. The caller is rejected identically in both runs, so the extra write is attributable to the resend path and not to the harness.

WHY THIS IS NOT COVERED BY THE FIX ALREADY ON THE LEDGER: the fix proposed for the accepted blocking-timeout finding guards the two resend loops with `command.isResolved`. Repro J prints `Command.isResolved after reject(): false` for a freshly rejected command, so that guard is a no-op for every rejected command and this double-write survives it. The guard has to be on 'settled', not on 'resolved'.

NO TEST COVERS IT: the project's commandTimeout tests and its autoResendUnfulfilledCommands tests are in different files and neither combines the two; no test asserts what the server received after a command was rejected by `commandTimeout`.

  - Defender: Accepted in full at the claimed severity (high) and support level (strong). The finding named the two observations a rebuttal would have to change - the server-side INCR count and the flag itself - and I could change neither. My probe, rebuilt from the prose: commandTimeout 200, a stub that applies INCR and withholds the first reply, then an ordinary transient disconnect. '[11ms] server: INCR #1 applied - counter is now 1 / caller outcome: REJECTED Command timed out at 212ms / harness: dropping the connection (ordinary transient outage) / [483ms] server: accepted connection #2 / [484ms] server: INCR #2 applied - counter is now 2 <-- AFTER the caller was told the command failed'. The control changes exactly one thing, autoResendUnfulfilledCommands to false, and the caller is rejected identically at 214ms while the server applies one INCR and only one - so the second write is attributable to the resend path and not to my harness. On the compounding point, which is the part that matters most for this review: I constructed a command, rejected it with the same error the timeout path uses, and read the flag - 'Command.isResolved after reject(): false'. The code says why: lib/Command.ts:417-425 fires this.reject(new Error('Command timed out')) and does nothing else, lib/Command.ts:536-543 clears the timers and rejects the promise without touching isResolved, and lib/Command.ts:574-585 sets isResolved = true only on the resolve path. So the isResolved guard proposed for the two resend loops in the fix for the already-accepted blocking-timeout finding is a no-op for every rejected command, and this double-write survives it - the guard has to be on 'settled', as the finding says. I checked the coverage claim and it holds: commandTimeout is exercised only in test/functional/commandTimeout.ts (2 passing here) and autoResendUnfulfilledCommands only in test/functional/connection.ts:413 and test/functional/tracing.ts:296; no test combines them and none asserts what the server received after a commandTimeout rejection. I accept the high severity without argument: commandTimeout is the option a caller reaches for to get a definite answer about a command's fate, the answer here is 'it failed', and the client then performs the work again with nobody left to observe it - the caller's only safe response to a reported failure, retrying, then applies the work a third time.
- **R3-F1** [medium/strong] The observability surface this change adds cannot report failure, and is asserted by the project's own tests not to. `ioredis:command` is published on the write path only, so a command that fails BEFORE it is written emits no span at all: rejected because the client is `end`, rejected because the stream is not writable while `enableOfflineQueue` is false, or queued offline and then rejected when the queue is flushed on close (or on `maxRetriesPerRequest`). During an outage an APM subscribed to this channel therefore sees Redis span volume fall to zero rather than seeing errors - indistinguishable from an application that stopped calling Redis, and the opposite of what an operator installs the channel for. Second, `isTraced` is latched on the first write and never cleared, so a command the client writes to the server more than once is represented by exactly one span: the duplicate non-idempotent write that this ledger has already accepted as real (a `commandTimeout`-rejected INCR applied twice, a settled BLPOP popping a second element) is invisible on the very channel that exists to observe it, and the project's own tracing test asserts that it must be. Third, a blocking command settled with the client-fabricated `null` is published as a SUCCESS whose result is null, so the already-accepted silent-data-loss path is positively recorded as a healthy command. The channel does report post-write server errors correctly, and that is what makes the gap dangerous rather than obvious: it looks like a surface that reports failures, so its silence reads as health.
  - Evidence: MECHANISM (pre-write failures are never traced): lib/Redis.ts:628-630 returns `command.promise` untraced whenever `!writable`, and the four early returns above it are all failure or shortcut paths that never reach the trace call at all - lib/Redis.ts:487-490 rejects with CONNECTION_CLOSED_ERROR_MSG when the status is `end`; :491-501 rejects a non-subscriber command in RESP2 subscriber mode; :533-541 rejects with "Stream isn't writeable and enableOfflineQueue options is false"; :543-547 resolves the `quit` shortcut. A command that does reach the offline queue is only traced if it is later flushed through the write path; if it is instead rejected by lib/Redis.ts:977-1001 `flushQueue` - which the close path and the maxRetriesPerRequest branch at lib/redis/event_handler.ts:389-402 both call - it emits nothing. The publish itself is at lib/Redis.ts:636-640 (`command.isTraced = true; return traceCommand(...)`), and lib/tracing.ts:126-139 shows the span is created only when the channel has subscribers.

MECHANISM (one span per command, however many times it is written): `isTraced` is declared `false` once per Command object at lib/Command.ts:237 and is never reset; lib/Redis.ts:628 short-circuits on it. The comment at lib/Redis.ts:632-635 states this is deliberate - "Commands may pass through sendCommand multiple times (offline queue flush, prevCommandQueue resend after reconnect). The isTraced flag ensures we don't emit duplicate trace events." The resend loops that write the command again are lib/redis/event_handler.ts:479-491 and :497-511.

REPRO L3 - <path> exit 0, five phases in one process with a real diagnostics_channel subscriber on `ioredis:command`. Captured: 'A: caller rejected with "Stream isn't writeable and enableOfflineQueue options is false" -> spans for that command: 0 / B: caller rejected with "Connection is closed." -> spans for that command: 0 / C: caller rejected with "ERR boom" -> spans for that command: 1 (error:ERR boom) / D: caller received null while the server still holds the command -> spans: 1 (success:null) / E: caller rejected Command timed out; INCRs the server actually applied: 2; spans for that command: 1 (error:Command timed out) / VERDICT preWriteFailureEmitsNoSpan=true / VERDICT serverWritesInvisibleToTrace=true / VERDICT postWriteFailureEmitsErrorSpan=true / VERDICT fabricatedNullEmitsSuccessSpan=true'. Phase C is the built-in control: the same subscriber, in the same process, does capture a failure once the command has been written, so the zeros in A and B are attributable to the pre-write gap and not to the probe. The full per-span log is printed in the archive, and in phases A and B it is empty for the whole client lifetime.

THE PROJECT'S OWN TESTS ENCODE THE SECOND COMPONENT AS CORRECT: test/functional/tracing.ts:278-322 is `it("should not produce duplicate trace events for resent unfulfilled commands")`, which drives a command through the reconnect resend path and asserts `expect(setEvents.length).to.eql(1)` at :321 while never asserting what the server received. The only failure the tracing suite exercises is a post-write server error - :327 `it("should trace errors on failed commands")`, a WRONGTYPE reply - which is exactly the case that works. `grep -rln 'ioredis:command|ioredis:batch|ioredis:connect' test/` returns test/functional/tracing.ts and nothing else, and no test in it constructs a client with `enableOfflineQueue: false` or asserts anything about spans for a command rejected before the write. (Environment limit, recorded and not used as evidence either way: that file is 31 passing / 11 failing here, every failure being `Connection is closed.` because it needs a live server on 127.0.0.1:6379.)

PROVENANCE: node v25.6.1; the six declared runtime pins resolved exactly (@ioredis/commands 2.0.0, cluster-key-slot 1.1.1, debug 4.4.3, denque 2.1.0, redis-errors 1.2.0, standard-as-callback 2.1.0) plus ts-node 10.9.2, typescript 5.4.5, mocha 11.8.0 from the prepared frozen environment via NODE_PATH; `require.resolve` of the project's own import name is MODULE_NOT_FOUND from that environment, so the probe imported the reviewed working tree and nothing else; the scratch tree's lib/ is asserted byte-identical to the review repo's by `diff -r` inside the driver, and `tsc --noEmit` over it exits 0. Driver, sources, manifest and a clean rerun: <path> (run.sh exit 0, OUTPUT.txt 9125 bytes, 17 VERDICT lines, MANIFEST.md). Nothing was installed, downloaded, vendored or extracted, and no published copy of the project under review was read in any form.
  - Defender: Partial. I accept three components on my own measurements and reject two, and I contest the support level for the claim as worded. I am not contesting the severity. === ACCEPTED (1) PRE-WRITE FAILURES PRODUCE NO COMMAND SPAN. Rebuilt from the finding's prose only, with one subscriber attached to ioredis:command and ioredis:connect over the same window. A client with enableOfflineQueue:false against a port nothing listens on: caller rejected with "Stream isn't writeable and enableOfflineQueue options is false", command spans 0. An offline-queued command flushed when retryStrategy gives up: caller rejected with 'Connection is closed.', command spans 0. In-process control, a stub that answers a written command with '-ERR boom': 5 command spans, 1 of them an error span. The same counts the critic reports. The code is as cited: lib/Redis.ts:487-490, :491-501, :533-541 and :543-547 all return command.promise without reaching the trace call; :628-630 returns early whenever !writable; flushQueue at :977-1001 rejects with no span, and lib/redis/event_handler.ts:389-402 is one of its callers. === ACCEPTED (2) THE FABRICATED NULL IS PUBLISHED AS A SUCCESS, and this is the most serious of the three. blockingTimeout 300 against a stub that holds the BLPOP and answers at 900ms: the caller received null at 303ms; exactly one blpop span; its asyncStart carries result null; zero failure spans; and the stub then delivered element-1 to nobody. The data-loss path this ledger already accepted (R1-F1) is recorded on the new channel as a healthy command that returned 'queue empty'. An operator auditing the channel after losing queue items would find a clean success. === ACCEPTED (3) THE RESEND TEST ASSERTS THE SPAN COUNT AND NOT THE SERVER. test/functional/tracing.ts:278-322 drives a command through the reconnect resend and asserts expect(setEvents.length).to.eql(1) at :321 while asserting nothing about what the server received. Read and confirmed. === REJECTED (A) THE CENTRAL INFERENCE ABOUT AN OUTAGE. The claim states that an APM subscribed to this channel 'sees Redis span volume fall to zero rather than seeing errors - indistinguishable from an application that stopped calling Redis'. Measured against the tracing surface this change adds, that is wrong. In the very windows where command spans were 0, ioredis:connect carried 24 failure spans in phase A and 6 in phase B. Every automatic reconnect runs through self.connect() at lib/redis/event_handler.ts:386, and connect() is wrapped by traceConnect at lib/Redis.ts:208-217 via lib/tracing.ts:152-159, so a failing connection publishes a start and an error on that channel for every attempt, indefinitely. An application that stopped calling Redis produces neither. The real gap is narrower and worth stating accurately: what is missing is per-command attribution during an outage - which callers died and on which commands - not failure reporting as such. === REJECTED (B) THE SUITE-COVERAGE SENTENCE. 'The only failure the tracing suite exercises is a post-write server error - :327' is false. test/functional/tracing.ts:677-723 is it('should trace connection failure'): it subscribes to ioredis:connect, builds a client on port 1 with retryStrategy: () => null and connectTimeout 1000, and asserts that errorEvents.length is greater than 0. Against the working tree, in isolation, it passes: 'tracing / ioredis:connect / should trace connection failure ... 1 passing (9ms)'. It fails only when the whole file is collected in this environment, and the assertion that fails there is startEvents.length === 1 with an actual of 2, because earlier cases in the same file need a live server on 127.0.0.1:6379 and their background reconnects publish a second start on the same channel. That is an environment artefact; I record it and rest nothing on it. === REJECTED (C) THE RESENT-WRITE COMPONENT AS A DEFECT OF THIS SURFACE. I do not dispute the count. One span for a command the client writes twice is exactly what happens and I am not asking the critic to change that number. I reject the attribution. traceCommand wraps command.promise with tracingChannel.tracePromise (lib/tracing.ts:126-139); a Command carries exactly one promise, created once and settled once (lib/Command.ts:238-242, isResolved at :238). A second span for the same command therefore has no promise to close, and would double-count one caller-visible operation's latency. The duplicate server-side write is a defect of the resend path, and this ledger has already accepted it twice - R1-F1 and R1-D1 - where the remedy belongs: stop writing a settled command again. Publishing a second span would not prevent one byte of the second write; it would only make the span count wrong as well. Nor does the test 'assert that it must be' invisible: it asserts that one caller-visible operation yields one span, which is correct span semantics, and it is silent about the server. The accurate criticism, which I accept and have recorded above as component (3), is that the tracing suite asserts signals and never asserts consequences - the same shape this ledger already records for the blocking-timeout tests. === SUPPORT LEVEL. Contested strong -> moderate, for the claim as worded and not for the corrected scope. The headline sentence is 'The observability surface this change adds cannot report failure'; the critic's own phases C and E emit error spans, my phase C does, and connection failure is both traced and covered by a passing test. Evidence that strongly supports 'this surface cannot attribute a pre-write failure to a command, and records a fabricated success' does not support 'cannot report failure'. === SEVERITY. Medium stands. I am not negotiating it downward and, if anything, component (2) alone would justify the top of that band, because it makes an already-accepted data-loss path read as healthy.
  - Proposed fix: For the components I accept, two changes that are small and local. (1) Publish a span for commands that fail before the write: the four early returns at lib/Redis.ts:487-490, :491-501, :533-541 and the flushQueue rejection at :977-1001 should go through the same trace call, so a failed command is attributable to a command rather than only to a connection. (2) Do not publish a client-fabricated result as a success. When setBlockingTimeout resolves the caller with null, the span for that command should carry an explicit marker - an error, or at minimum a context member recording that the value was synthesised by the client and not returned by the server - so that the data-loss path accepted as R1-F1 is not recorded as a healthy empty pop. I do not propose a second span for a resent command, for the reason given in my response.

## Unanswered at close — never adjudicated

- **R3-D1** [medium/strong] (claimant: claude) The argument sanitiser behind the change's new command-trace context redacts the wrong set. It advertises safe-by-default redaction and does redact AUTH and HELLO, but it positively allow-lists ACL, CONFIG and EVAL at 'all arguments verbatim', so the three command families that most reliably carry a secret or a payload are published in cleartext on a process-wide diagnostics channel to every subscriber - in practice an APM or log pipeline, i.e. storage that is longer-retained and lower-trust than the application itself. CONFIG SET requirepass <password> and CONFIG SET masterauth <password> publish the new Redis password; ACL SETUSER <user> on ><password> publishes the user's password; EVAL and EVALSHA publish the whole script plus every KEYS and ARGV member, so the same value the sanitiser carefully redacts when written with SET is published verbatim when the identical write is done from a Lua script - the shape used by essentially every rate-limiter, lock and idempotency helper. The internal inconsistency is what makes this worse than an ordinary logging gap: the surface looks credential-aware, so an operator who checks that AUTH is redacted will reasonably conclude the channel is safe to ship to a vendor. This is the mirror image of the open finding about what the channel fails to report, and it is deliberately not a restatement of it: that one is about silence, this one is about the channel emitting material it should never emit.
  - Evidence: The publish path is inside the frozen review window: lib/Redis.ts:843-852 _buildCommandContext returns 'args: sanitizeArgs(command.name, command.args)' and is among the added lines of the single modified file ('git diff' in the review repository); it is handed to traceCommand at lib/Redis.ts:636-640, which passes it to commandChannel.tracePromise at lib/tracing.ts:126-139. The rule table is lib/tracing.ts:10-23: the args:-1 subset regex is /^(ACL|BIT|B[LRZ]|CLIENT|CLUSTER|CONFIG|COMMAND|DECR|DEL|EVAL|EX|FUNCTION|GEO|GET|HINCR|HMGET|HSCAN|INCR|L[TRLM]|MEMORY|P[EFISTU]|RPOP|S[CDIMORSU]|XACK|X[CDGILPRT]|Z[CDILMPRS])/i and lib/tracing.ts:38-40 returns 'args.map((a) => String(a))' for it; the comment at lib/tracing.ts:7-9 states the default is 0 'for safe-by-default behavior, which covers AUTH, HELLO, and unknown/custom commands'. DIRECT MEASUREMENT (probe P1 phase D, sanitizeArgs called exactly as the trace context calls it): 'auth ["s3cr3t-password"] -> ["?"]  secretInSpan=false'; 'hello ["3","AUTH","default","s3cr3t-password"] -> ["?","?","?","?"]  secretInSpan=false'; 'config ["SET","requirepass","s3cr3t-password"] -> ["SET","requirepass","s3cr3t-password"]  secretInSpan=true'; 'config ["SET","masterauth","s3cr3t-password"] -> ["SET","masterauth","s3cr3t-password"]  secretInSpan=true'; 'acl ["SETUSER","alice","on",">s3cr3t-password","~*","+@all"] -> ["SETUSER","alice","on",">s3cr3t-password","~*","+@all"]  secretInSpan=true'; 'set ["k","sensitive-value"] -> ["k","?"]  secretInSpan=false' - VERDICT D_commandsLeakingCredentialVerbatim=3. The EVAL asymmetry, same call, separately measured: set ["user:1:ssn","123-45-6789"] -> ["user:1:ssn","?"] while eval ["return redis.call(\"set\",KEYS[1],ARGV[1])","1","user:1:ssn","123-45-6789"] -> the same four members verbatim, including 123-45-6789; and migrate with a trailing AUTH is fully redacted, which shows the table is not applying any credential-aware rule, only a prefix allow-list. END TO END, through a real subscriber rather than a direct call (probe P2 phase E): a client against a stub server, with one diagnostics_channel subscriber on ioredis:command, issuing config and acl through the public API produced the spans 'E: span command=config args=["SET","requirepass","s3cr3t-password"]' and 'E: span command=acl args=["SETUSER","alice","on",">s3cr3t-password","~*","+@all"]', against 'E: span command=auth args=["?"]' and 'E: span command=hello args=["?"]' - VERDICT E_spansCarryingCredentialVerbatim=2, VERDICT E_leakingCommandNames=["config","acl"]. So the secret reaches a subscriber, not merely a helper's return value. NO TEST COVERS IT: no case in test/functional/tracing.ts asserts anything about redaction of config, acl, eval or evalsha arguments; the file's only redaction assertions concern the commands that are already safe. Archive, clean rerun, driver exit 0: <path> (run.sh, P1-trace-gaps.js, P2-span-content.js, OUTPUT.txt 3991 bytes / 31 VERDICT lines, MANIFEST.md). Provenance: node v25.6.1; the six declared runtime pins plus ts-node 10.9.2, typescript 5.4.5 and mocha 11.8.0 resolved from the prepared frozen environment; require.resolve of the project's own import name is MODULE_NOT_FOUND from that environment; scratch lib/ byte-identical to the review repository's by diff -r; tsc --noEmit exits 0; the review repository was not modified. Nothing was installed, downloaded, vendored or extracted, and no published copy of the project under review was read in any form.
