### T11 — Verdict 1

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 2/3 · Findings: 3

**NO-SHIP: 1 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [medium/strong] The public `retryAfter` option is ignored. Although it is read from `retryOptions`, it is never copied into `this.retryOpts`, and the default retry policy unconditionally honors a response's `Retry-After` header. Consequently, callers setting `retryAfter: false` still receive header-directed delays (up to `maxTimeout`).
  - Evidence: lib/handler/retry-handler.js:61-75 destructures `retryAfter`; lib/handler/retry-handler.js:82-106 constructs `this.retryOpts` without a `retryAfter` property; lib/handler/retry-handler.js:216-231 unconditionally reads and applies `headers?.['retry-after']`.
[R2 escalation] lib/handler/retry-handler.js:84 contains `retryAfter: retryAfter ?? true`; lib/handler/retry-handler.js:186-194 omits `retryAfter` from the default policy’s destructuring; lib/handler/retry-handler.js:225 reads the response header unconditionally. The defender’s reproduced timings show `{ retryAfter: false }` with the header waited about 3 seconds, versus about 0.5 seconds without it.
  - Defender: Accepted in full, at severity medium and support strong, with no component of it still disputed. In round 1 I accepted the operative conclusion (retryAfter: false does not suppress Retry-After-directed delays) and rejected exactly one thing: the claim's mechanism sentence, which said the option 'is never copied into this.retryOpts' and cited lib/handler/retry-handler.js:82-106 as the construction that omits it. Codex's round-2 escalation withdrew that component explicitly and re-cited the mechanism correctly: lib/handler/retry-handler.js:84 holds 'retryAfter: retryAfter ?? true', the built-in policy's destructuring at :186-194 omits retryAfter, and the header read at :225 is unconditional. That is precisely the correction I asked for and it is what changed the verdict from partial to accept — not repetition, but the citation moving to the line that actually carries the defect. I re-verified all three citations in the working tree this round rather than relying on my round-1 notes, and re-ran the timing probe from scratch: the behaviour is unchanged. I am not arguing severity down: a server-supplied header can impose a wait bounded only by maxTimeout (30s by default) on a caller who explicitly opted out, and the documented contract (docs/docs/api/RetryHandler.md:85, docs/docs/api/RetryAgent.md:60, types/retry-handler.d.ts:95) promises the opposite. I am not contesting support level either; with :84 set aside the remaining citations plus two independent runs support 'strong'. One scope fact I record for the fixer, not as any narrowing of the accepted finding: because the value genuinely is in this.retryOpts, it is forwarded to a caller-supplied retry function via opts.retryOptions at :172-179 and :460-467, so a custom policy can honour it; the callers actually harmed are those on the default policy, which is the default.
  - Proposed fix: Add retryAfter to the destructuring of retryOptions in RetryHandler[kRetryHandlerDefaultRetry] at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:186-194, then gate the read at :225 — e.g. 'let retryAfterHeader = retryAfter === false ? undefined : headers?.['retry-after']' — leaving the existing 'retryAfterHeader === 0' and '> 0' branches untouched so that with the option off the delay falls through to Math.min(minTimeout * timeoutFactor ** (counter - 1), maxTimeout). Guarding at :225 rather than at the ternary keeps the malformed-date path (calculateRetryAfterHeader) out of play too. Regression test: dispatch through RetryAgent against a 503 carrying 'Retry-After: 3' under { retryAfter: false, minTimeout: 50, timeoutFactor: 1 }, assert a second attempt occurred, and assert the inter-attempt gap is well under 1000 ms; the existing test at test/issue-5137.js only passes retryAfter: true, so the false case is uncovered today.
- **R1-F2** [high/moderate] Consumer abort state is not sticky across a delayed retry, so aborting while the retry policy/backoff is pending can still launch a new request. The proxy forwards `abort()` only to its current (already failed or paused) controller; the delayed callback subsequently calls `retry()` without checking abort state, and the next dispatch replaces the proxy target with a fresh, non-aborted controller. This can continue network activity after user cancellation and potentially deliver callbacks after abort.
  - Evidence: lib/handler/retry-handler.js:41-55 stores no proxy-level aborted state and implements `abort(reason) { this.target?.abort(reason) }`; lib/handler/retry-handler.js:162-172 replaces `controllerProxy.target` on every dispatch; lib/handler/retry-handler.js:413-424 dispatches unconditionally from `retry()`; lib/handler/retry-handler.js:426-440 checks abort only when the original `onResponseError` occurs, while the later `shouldRetry` callback calls `this.retry()` without rechecking; lib/handler/retry-handler.js:216-231 schedules that callback with `setTimeout`.
  - Defender: Accepted as filed, at the high severity claimed. What changed my mind was not the argument but rebuilding the experiment from the finding's prose and watching it happen: I did not read anything of Codex's beyond the ledger text. A connection reset mid-body schedules a 2000 ms backoff; the consumer aborts at t=465 ms; at t=2170 ms the server receives a fresh Range request and streams 3,145,728 further bytes that the consumer never sees. With a downstream handler aborting through the stable controller it was handed, that handler then receives 49 further onResponseData callbacks and a successful onResponseEnd after its own abort — i.e. the request is reported as having completed successfully after cancellation, which is worse than the 'potentially deliver callbacks after abort' the finding hedges at. High is right for a client library: abort must stop network activity, the transferred volume is bounded only by the resource size, and the connection cannot be reused until it drains. Two scope facts I offer for the fix rather than as any narrowing of the finding: (a) I instrumented retry() and it fires after the abort in the pre-headers case too, but no request reaches the socket there, because that path re-enters the downstream onRequestStart at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:174 and api-request re-aborts on its stored reason at lib/api/api-request.js:84-87; (b) the damage is therefore concentrated on the resume path, where headersSent is true and that re-entry is deliberately skipped. The root cause is exactly as the finding states: RetryController at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:44-57 keeps no state of its own, so abort(reason) at :51 lands on whichever controller happens to be current — during a backoff that is the dead one — and nothing between the timer firing and this.dispatch at :426 consults abort state.
  - Proposed fix: Give RetryController its own terminal state: in abort(reason) record this.aborted = true and this.reason = reason before forwarding to this.target, and have the aborted/reason getters prefer the stored value over the target's. Then bail out on it in the two places that resume the retry machinery — at the top of retry() (lib/handler/retry-handler.js:406) and in the shouldRetry callback at :440 — propagating this.handler.onResponseError(this.controllerProxy, this.controllerProxy.reason) instead of dispatching, and clear any pending timer. Note the default policy's setTimeout at :240 is not cancellable from the handler today, so either the bail-out has to happen when the callback returns or the retry contract needs a cancellation hook; the former is enough to stop the dispatch. Tests: the two scenarios in repro/f2-abort-during-backoff.js and repro/f2b-callbacks-after-abort.js are written as bounded observations and can be lifted into test/ directly.
- **R1-D1** [medium/strong] The body-length reconciliation added to onResponseEnd turns every successful HEAD response that carries a non-zero Content-Length into a failure. onResponseStart derives an end checkpoint from Content-Length (lib/handler/retry-handler.js:348-351), and onResponseEnd then requires this.start === this.end + 1 (lib/handler/retry-handler.js:391-398). A HEAD response legitimately advertises the entity's Content-Length while sending zero body bytes, so this.start stays 0 and the check can never be satisfied. The thrown RequestRetryError carries the 200 status, so the default policy's status-code gate at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:210-217 declines to retry and propagates it, and the consumer receives RequestRetryError(UND_ERR_REQ_RETRY, 'Content-Range mismatch') on the response body after having already been handed statusCode 200. HEAD is in the default retryable methods list (lib/handler/retry-handler.js:105), so this is a first-class supported path, and no test in test/retry-handler.js, test/retry-handler2.js, test/retry-agent.js or test/retry-handler-controller-proxy.js exercises HEAD. Severity medium rather than high: undici.request consumers that consume the body (body.text(), piping) get a hard rejection, but fetch() is unaffected because it discards the body for HEAD, body.dump() swallows the error, and consumers that never touch the body see no uncaught exception; there is no data corruption, only a supported method converted from success into an error.
  - Evidence: Probe <path> output d1-output.txt (exit 1), clean rerun d1-head-content-length-rerun.txt (exit 1), node v25.6.1, module resolved at runtime to <path> Against one server: 'GET / via RetryAgent (control) -> OK status=200 bodyBytes=12'; 'HEAD /empty via RetryAgent (control) -> OK status=200 bodyBytes=0'; 'HEAD / via plain Agent (control) -> OK status=200 bodyBytes=0'; 'HEAD / via RetryAgent -> THREW RequestRetryError code=UND_ERR_REQ_RETRY message="Content-Range mismatch"'. Interposing on the handler shows onResponseEnd entered with start=0 end=11 for a HEAD whose Content-Length was 12, i.e. exactly the 391-398 comparison failing. Note on probe design, because it produced a false negative first: body.dump() swallows the error, so the body must actually be consumed or its 'error'/'end' events observed — with dump() the same HEAD reports OK status=200.
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: Skip received-body length reconciliation for HEAD requests, while retaining the check for methods that carry response bodies. Add a regression test using RetryAgent with a successful HEAD response whose Content-Length is nonzero and consume the response body with `body.text()` so the error cannot be swallowed by `dump()`.

_Ledger: <path>
```

### T11 — Verdict 2

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 2/3 · Findings: 7

**NO-SHIP: 5 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [critical/strong] The RetryController proxy that RetryHandler hands to every downstream callback exposes rawHeaders and rawTrailers as getter-ONLY accessors, while the controller it stands in for declares them as writable data properties that undici itself assigns to. Any handler placed downstream of RetryHandler that writes controller.rawHeaders therefore throws a TypeError in strict mode. undici ships such a handler: the decompress interceptor. Composing it so that it sits outside the retry interceptor turns an ordinary gzip 200 into an UNCAUGHT exception that terminates the Node process. This is a crash-class regression reachable from a two-line, documented composition, and it is not covered by any test in the tree.
  - Evidence: CODE. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:44-57 defines `class RetryController` with `get rawHeaders () { return this.target?.rawHeaders ?? null }` (line 55) and `get rawTrailers () { ... }` (line 56) and no corresponding setters. Line 115 constructs it once (`this.controllerProxy = new RetryController()`) and lines 123, 133, 140, 175, 180, 311, 360, 380, 400, 428, 436 and 446 pass that proxy — never the real controller — to every downstream handler callback.

CONTRACT IT REPLACES. lib/core/request.js:50-95 is the real per-dispatch `RequestController`; lines 58-59 declare `rawHeaders = null` and `rawTrailers = null` as ordinary WRITABLE class fields, and undici assigns to them on the hot path at lib/core/request.js:339 (`controller.rawHeaders = headers`), :377 (upgrade) and :398 (`controller.rawTrailers = trailers`). The proxy is therefore not a faithful stand-in: it silently drops half the property contract.

SHIPPED WRITERS. `grep -rn "\.rawHeaders = \|\.rawTrailers = " lib` returns, besides core, lib/interceptor/decompress.js:201 (`controller.rawHeaders = filteredHeaders`, inside DecompressHandler.onResponseStart, guarded only by `if (controller?.rawHeaders)` — and the proxy getter DOES return the live array, so the guard passes and the assignment is reached) and lib/interceptor/cache.js:357, plus lib/mock/mock-utils.js:425-426. Third-party handlers have the same licence, since the writable form is what lib/core/request.js publishes.

UNIT PROBE (repro/proxy-setter-unit.js, run against the unmodified working tree). Output: {"rawHeaders":"TypeError: Cannot set property rawHeaders of #<RetryController> which has only a getter","rawTrailers":"TypeError: Cannot set property rawTrailers of #<RetryController> which has only a getter","rawHeadersDescriptor":{"get":"function","set":"undefined"},"rawTrailersDescriptor":{"get":"function","set":"undefined"}}; exit status 1.

END-TO-END, FIRST ERROR (repro/decompress-over-retry.js subject-noretry): `Client(...).compose(interceptors.retry({maxRetries:0,...}), interceptors.decompress())` against a local origin serving a gzip 200 with content-length. Output: {"phase":"subject-noretry","uncaughtException":{"name":"TypeError","message":"Cannot set property rawHeaders of #<RetryController> which has only a getter"}} — an UNCAUGHT exception, i.e. the process dies.

END-TO-END, DEFAULT RETRIES (repro/decompress-over-retry.js subject, maxRetries:1): the TypeError is swallowed and treated as a connection failure, RetryHandler re-dispatches, and the second 200 walks the headersSent resume path where the assertion at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:298 fires. Output: {"phase":"subject","uncaughtException":{"name":"AssertionError","code":"ERR_ASSERTION","message":"content-range mismatch"}} — again UNCAUGHT. Stack (captured before the JSON handler was added): AssertionError at RetryHandler.onResponseStart (lib/handler/retry-handler.js:298) -> Request.onResponseStart (lib/core/request.js:345) -> Parser.onHeadersComplete (lib/dispatcher/client-h1.js:702), re-emitted as an unhandled "error" event on the Gunzip instance.

CONTROL. repro/decompress-over-retry.js control — the decompress interceptor ALONE (so its handler receives the real RequestController) succeeds: {"phase":"control","result":{"ok":true,"statusCode":200,"body":"{\"hello\":\"world\",\"n\":42}"}}, exit status 0. The only variable changed is whether the retry proxy is interposed.

BLAST RADIUS. repro/compose-order-matrix.js runs five composition orders, each in its own child process: decompress-only OK(0), retry-then-decompress UNCAUGHT AssertionError (child exit 2), decompress-then-retry OK(0), retry-then-cache OK(0), cache-then-retry OK(0). Exactly the order that puts an assigning handler downstream of the proxy fails.

COUNTERFACTUAL. In a scratch copy derived from the working tree (tar copy, .git excluded; the review repo itself was never modified — `git status --porcelain` afterwards still shows only the frozen review window), adding `set rawHeaders (v) { if (this.target) this.target.rawHeaders = v }` and the matching rawTrailers setter to RetryController — and changing nothing else — makes the failing composition succeed: {"phase":"subject","result":{"ok":true,"statusCode":200,"body":"{\"hello\":\"world\",\"n\":42}"}}.

TEST COVERAGE. test/retry-handler-controller-proxy.js exercises the proxy but only READS rawHeaders/rawTrailers (lines 93-94); nothing in the tree writes them through the proxy, which is why 63/63 retry-related tests pass while this crashes.

ENVIRONMENT. node v25.6.1; project version 8.9.0 from the working tree; dependencies from the prepared frozen environment via NODE_PATH; `require.resolve("undici")` from that environment alone is NOT-FOUND (MODULE_NOT_FOUND). Full driver transcript with exit statuses: <path> manifest: <path>
  - Defender: I accept this in full, at critical, and I am not shading the severity. What persuaded me was not Codex's transcript -- I did not read it -- but my own re-derivation from its prose, which reproduced every step. (1) Unit level: constructing a RetryHandler from the working tree and assigning to its controllerProxy throws, and the prototype descriptors show set: undefined for both properties. My probe was written so it can fail -- it prints NO-DEFECT and exits 0 if both are writable -- and it did not. (2) Contract level: lib/core/request.js:58-59 declares rawHeaders/rawTrailers as plain writable class fields and undici assigns to them at lib/core/request.js:339, :377 and :398, so a stand-in that exposes them getter-only is not a faithful substitute. (3) Shipped writer: lib/interceptor/decompress.js:185-201 reads controller.rawHeaders, finds the live array through the getter so the `if (controller?.rawHeaders)` guard passes, and then assigns -- the guard cannot save it. (4) End to end: the process dies. And I found the reachability argument to be STRONGER than Codex stated, which is why I am not contesting the support level downward. Codex characterised the failing composition as merely 'documented' in the sense of being legal. It is worse than that: docs/docs/api/Interceptors.md:398-405 sets out retry -> redirect -> decompress -> responseError as THE recommended stack, and docs/docs/api/Interceptors.md:14-18 repeats retry-then-decompress in the very first example on the page. I ran that documented stack verbatim and it kills the process on an ordinary gzip 200. So this is not an unlucky ordering a user might stumble into; it is the ordering the project tells them to use. proposed_fix is a concrete two-setter change; Review mode does not apply it.
  - Proposed fix: Give RetryController write-through setters so it is a faithful stand-in for RequestController: `set rawHeaders (v) { if (this.target) this.target.rawHeaders = v }` and `set rawTrailers (v) { if (this.target) this.target.rawTrailers = v }` alongside the existing getters at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:55-56. Add a regression test that composes retry outermost with decompress inner over a gzip 200 -- i.e. the composition docs/docs/api/Interceptors.md:398-405 recommends -- since test/retry-handler-controller-proxy.js currently only reads these properties through the proxy and never writes them.
- **R1-F2** [high/strong] The body-length assertion in onResponseEnd compares the count of BODY bytes seen against Content-Length-1, so it can never hold for a HEAD response — which carries an entity Content-Length but no body. HEAD is in the default retryable method list, so every HEAD request made through the retry interceptor or RetryAgent against an origin that sends Content-Length now fails with RequestRetryError("Content-Range mismatch") on a perfectly good 200. HEAD-based existence/size probes and health checks break silently on adoption, and the failure is reported as a retry error on a 2xx, which is actively misleading.
  - Evidence: CODE. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:383-404, onResponseEnd: `if (this.end != null && Number.isFinite(this.end)) { if (this.start !== this.end + 1) { throw new RequestRetryError("Content-Range mismatch", this.statusCode, { headers: this.headers, data: { count: this.retryCount } }) } }` (comparison at line 392). `this.start` is a BODY-byte counter — it is only advanced in onResponseData (line 378: `this.start += chunk.length`). `this.end` is set from the header at line 335: `this.end = contentLength != null ? Number(contentLength) - 1 : null`. For HEAD, onResponseData is never called, so this.start stays 0 while this.end is contentLength-1; the invariant is unsatisfiable by construction.

DEFAULT METHOD LIST. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:90 sets `methods: methods ?? ["GET", "HEAD", "OPTIONS", "PUT", "DELETE", "TRACE", "QUERY"]` — HEAD is retryable by default, so it goes through this handler with no opt-in.

EXECUTED REPRO (repro/head-request.js, unmodified working tree, node v25.6.1). A local origin sets content-length: 1000 and returns the body for GET, nothing for HEAD — i.e. a correct HTTP/1.1 origin. Through `Client.compose(interceptors.retry({maxRetries:1,minTimeout:10,maxTimeout:10}))` the output is: {"GET":{"ok":true,"statusCode":200,"bodyBytes":1000},"HEAD":{"ok":false,"name":"RequestRetryError","code":"UND_ERR_REQ_RETRY","message":"Content-Range mismatch","statusCode":200}}; exit status 1. Note statusCode 200 on the error object — the response was fine.

COUNTERFACTUAL. In a scratch copy derived from the working tree, deleting exactly that block (452 bytes, nothing else changed) flips the result to {"GET":{"ok":true,...},"HEAD":{"ok":true,"statusCode":200,"bodyBytes":0}}, exit status 0. That establishes the block as the cause, not a coincidence.

THE CHECK IS ALSO UNTESTED. Against the same mutated copy the project's own retry suites still pass 63/63 (test/retry-handler.js, test/retry-handler2.js, test/retry-handler-controller-proxy.js, test/client-retry-resume-backpressure.js, test/retry-agent.js, test/interceptors/retry.js) — including "Should throw RequestRetryError when Content-Range mismatch", which is satisfied by a different code path. So the block buys no covered behaviour and costs all of HEAD.

ADJACENT EXPOSURE (inference, stated as such). The same unsatisfiable comparison fires for any 2xx whose declared Content-Length legitimately differs from the number of bytes the handler counts. I demonstrated HEAD; I did not demonstrate the others and do not claim them.

Full transcript with exit statuses: <path> (section "head-request.js" and the tree-copy counterfactual).
  - Defender: Accepted in full at high. I reproduced it independently from the prose and added a baseline Codex did not record, which removes the last alternative explanation. The mechanism is exactly as claimed and is visible in the source without running anything: this.start is advanced only in onResponseData (lib/handler/retry-handler.js:378), this.end is set from Content-Length at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:335, and onResponseEnd requires this.start === this.end + 1 at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:392. A HEAD response carries the entity Content-Length and no body, so onResponseData never fires and the invariant is unsatisfiable by construction -- there is no origin behaviour that can satisfy it. HEAD is in the shipped default method list at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:90, so this needs no opt-in. Severity high is right and I am not arguing it down: it is silent-on-adoption (the code compiles, the tests pass, HEAD probes simply start failing), and the error surfaces as a retry error carrying statusCode 200, which will send whoever debugs it looking at their origin rather than at the interceptor.
  - Proposed fix: Skip the onResponseEnd length check for responses that cannot carry a body: guard it on the request method not being HEAD and on the status not being 204/304, e.g. capture the method in the constructor and require `this.opts.method !== 'HEAD' && this.statusCode !== 204 && this.statusCode !== 304` before comparing this.start to this.end + 1. Add a HEAD-through-retry test, since the existing 'Should throw RequestRetryError when Content-Range mismatch' case is satisfied by a different code path and leaves this block uncovered.
- **R1-F3** [high/strong] The resume path in onResponseStart raises a bare node:assert AssertionError, not a RequestRetryError, whenever a connection drops after the response headers were forwarded downstream but before any body byte arrived. Because this.start is still 0, retry() sends no Range header, the origin answers a plain 200 with no Content-Range, parseRangeHeader(undefined) returns {start:0,end:null,size:null} rather than null so the Content-Range guard passes, and assert(this.end == null || this.end === end) then compares a number against null and fails. A mid-flight connection drop is the single most ordinary thing this handler exists to absorb, and here it converts a successful retry into an internal assertion leaked to the caller.
  - Evidence: CODE PATH. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:243 onResponseStart; the headersSent branch begins at line 262. Line 267 `if (statusCode !== 206 && (this.start > 0 || statusCode !== 200))` does NOT throw for a 200 with this.start === 0, so execution continues. Line 274 `const contentRange = parseRangeHeader(headers["content-range"])`; line 276 `if (!contentRange)` does not fire — see the next item. Line 295 destructures `const { start, size, end = size ? size - 1 : null } = contentRange`, and because `end` is explicitly null (not undefined) the default does not apply, so end === null. Line 297 `assert(this.start === start)` passes (0 === 0); line 298 `assert(this.end == null || this.end === end, "content-range mismatch")` fails, because this.end was set to Content-Length-1 at line 335 on the first response.

WHY THE GUARD DOES NOT CATCH IT. lib/core/util.js:798-810: `function parseRangeHeader (range) { if (range == null || range === "") return { start: 0, end: null, size: null } ... }`. A MISSING content-range header yields a truthy object, not null, so the `if (!contentRange)` guard at line 276 is bypassed and the code falls through to the asserts. The comment above line 276 ("We always throw here as we want to indicate that we entred unexpected path") shows the intent was to throw a RequestRetryError in exactly this situation; the parse helper defeats it.

EXECUTED REPRO (repro/resume-zero-bytes.js, unmodified working tree). A raw net server answers connection #1 with headers only (`HTTP/1.1 200 OK`, `content-length: 10`) and destroys the socket 50 ms later with zero body bytes; connection #2 serves the complete response. Through `Client.compose(interceptors.retry({maxRetries:2,minTimeout:10,maxTimeout:10}))` the output is: {"connectionsSeen":2,"result":{"outcome":"REJECTED","name":"AssertionError","code":"ERR_ASSERTION","message":"content-range mismatch"}}; exit status 1. The retry DID reach the origin and the origin DID serve the body — the handler threw it away.

ERROR TYPE MATTERS. The caller receives ERR_ASSERTION, not UND_ERR_REQ_RETRY. Code that discriminates on err.code (the pattern the rest of this file establishes — every other failure in onResponseStart throws RequestRetryError — lines 268, 278 and 287) will not classify it, and an AssertionError escaping a library callback is a crash-class signal rather than a request-scoped error.

SAME ASSERT IS THE SECOND-STAGE KILLER IN R1-F1. repro/decompress-over-retry.js subject reaches line 298 by a different route and there the AssertionError escapes as an UNCAUGHT exception that terminates the process, which is the practical severity ceiling for this line.

Full transcript with exit statuses: <path> (section "resume-zero-bytes.js").
  - Defender: Accepted in full at high. Two things persuaded me, both checked directly rather than taken on Codex's word. First, the pivot of the whole finding is falsifiable in one call, so I made it: lib/core/util.js:799 begins `if (range == null || range === '') return { start: 0, end: null, size: null }`, and calling it confirms parseRangeHeader(undefined) -> {"start":0,"end":null,"size":null} while parseRangeHeader('nonsense') -> null. A MISSING content-range header therefore yields a TRUTHY object, so the `if (!contentRange)` guard at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:276 -- whose own comment says 'We always throw here as we want to indicate that we entred unexpected path' -- cannot fire, and control falls through to the asserts. The guard that was written to catch precisely this case is defeated by the helper it calls. Second, I reproduced the end-to-end failure and captured something the ledger only asserts: the request lines actually sent. Neither dispatch carried a range header, which forecloses the obvious rebuttal that the origin misbehaved by ignoring a Range request -- none was ever made, because retry() at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:406-430 only adds one when this.start !== 0. The error type matters as much as the outcome and I agree with Codex on it: every other failure in onResponseStart throws RequestRetryError (lines 268, 278, 287), so a caller discriminating on err.code gets ERR_ASSERTION from a path that should have produced UND_ERR_REQ_RETRY. High is the right severity: a mid-flight drop before the first body byte is the single most ordinary event this handler exists to absorb, and here a successful retry is converted into an internal assertion leaked to the caller.
  - Proposed fix: Make the missing-content-range case reachable again and keep the error type request-scoped. Either have the resume path test the header itself before parsing -- `const raw = headers['content-range']; if (raw == null || raw === '') throw new RequestRetryError('Content-Range mismatch', statusCode, { headers, data: { count: this.retryCount } })` ahead of lib/handler/retry-handler.js:274 -- or, better for the zero-bytes case specifically, treat this.start === 0 as 'nothing was forwarded downstream yet, restart cleanly' rather than as a resume, since a plain 200 that repeats the whole body is a correct answer to a request that carried no Range header. Whichever route, replace the bare node:assert calls at :297-298 with RequestRetryError so callers discriminating on err.code are not handed ERR_ASSERTION from a library callback.
- **R1-F4** [medium/moderate] A caller abort that arrives while the retry policy is waiting out its backoff is not honoured until the backoff timer expires. RetryController.abort forwards to this.target, which during the wait is the controller of the connection that already failed, and the proxy keeps no local aborted latch; the default retry policy arms a plain setTimeout with no cancellation path; and retry() re-dispatches without consulting any abort state. Cancellation latency is therefore bounded only by the backoff, which defaults to maxTimeout = 30 s and which a remote origin can drive up to that cap with a Retry-After header.
  - Evidence: CODE. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:51 `abort (reason) { this.target?.abort(reason) }` — a bare forward, with no local flag; lines 44-57 show the proxy stores nothing of its own besides `target`. Line 53 `get aborted () { return this.target?.aborted ?? false }` reads through to the target, so once onRequestStart (line 173, `this.controllerProxy.target = controller`) re-points the proxy at a fresh connection the observable aborted state resets to false.

NO CANCELLATION. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:240 `setTimeout(() => cb(null), retryTimeout)` in the default retry policy: the handle is not stored, not cleared and not unref'd, so nothing can shorten or cancel the wait, and the timer keeps the event loop alive. retry() at lines 406-430 dispatches unconditionally — it inspects this.start and this.etag only, never any abort state.

HOW THE ABORT ARRIVES. lib/api/api-request.js:76-77 — when the AbortSignal fires and no response body exists yet, RequestHandler calls `this.abort(this.reason)`, and this.abort was bound at lib/api/api-request.js:92 to `(reason) => controller.abort(reason)` where `controller` is the object delivered to onRequestStart — i.e. the RetryController proxy. So user aborts land on the proxy, which forwards them to a dead connection.

HOW THE BACKOFF IS SET. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:232-238: retryTimeout is `Math.min(retryAfterHeader, maxTimeout)` when the origin sent Retry-After, else `Math.min(minTimeout * timeoutFactor ** (counter - 1), maxTimeout)`; line 85 defaults maxTimeout to 30 * 1000. An origin returning a retryable status with `Retry-After: 300` therefore pins a cancelled caller for the full 30 s.

MEASURED (repro/abort-during-backoff.js, unmodified working tree). Origin destroys the first connection to force a network error; retry configured minTimeout=maxTimeout=3000, timeoutFactor=1; the caller aborts 500 ms in. Output: {"raceResult":"SETTLED","settled":{"ok":false,"name":"Error","message":"caller cancelled","msAfterAbort":2516},"requestsSeenByServer":1,"abortSignalAborted":true}. The rejection lands 2516 ms after the abort — exactly the remainder of the backoff.

SCOPE, STATED HONESTLY. In this configuration no extra request reached the wire: the abort is finally acted on at the START of the next dispatch, because lib/api/api-request.js:83-85 re-checks this.reason in onRequestStart and aborts the new controller there. So the defect I am claiming is deferred cancellation and a resource held open for the whole backoff, not a duplicate request. That mitigation lives in the API layer, not in the code under review, and it does not apply to a downstream handler that is not RequestHandler.

Full transcript with exit statuses: <path> (section "abort-during-backoff.js"). Note the probe prints "NOT REPRODUCED" for its duplicate-request hypothesis, which I am NOT claiming; the timing figure is the claim.
  - Defender: Accepted at medium with support moderate -- the levels Codex assigned, which I checked rather than waved through, and which I think are calibrated correctly. I reproduced the timing independently and got 2509 ms against Codex's 2516 ms on a 3000 ms backoff with the abort fired 500 ms in; the residual is the remainder of the timer, not noise. I also reproduced the honest negative Codex flagged: requestsSeenByServer was 1, so no duplicate request reached the wire, and Codex explicitly disclaims that hypothesis. The mechanism is plain in the source: lib/handler/retry-handler.js:51 `abort (reason) { this.target?.abort(reason) }` is a bare forward with no local latch, and during the wait this.target is the controller of the connection that already failed; lib/handler/retry-handler.js:240 `setTimeout(() => cb(null), retryTimeout)` stores no handle, so nothing can clear, shorten or unref it; retry() at :406-430 consults this.start and this.etag and no abort state. The ceiling is real too: :232-238 clamps a Retry-After to maxTimeout, which :85 defaults to 30 s, so a remote origin can choose the cancellation latency up to that cap. I am not inflating this above medium. The mitigation Codex names is genuine -- lib/api/api-request.js:83-85 re-checks this.reason at the start of the next dispatch -- so a caller using the standard request() API gets a deferred rejection rather than an extra request, and the practical cost is a held socket, a live timer keeping the event loop alive, and a cancellation that arrives up to 30 s late. That is a real defect worth fixing and not a critical one.
  - Proposed fix: Give the wait a cancellation path and consult abort state before re-dispatching. Concretely: keep a local aborted latch on RetryController so `abort()` records the reason and `get aborted ()` returns true even across a target re-point (`abort (reason) { this._aborted = true; this._reason = reason; this.target?.abort(reason) }`); have the default retry policy at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:240 store the timer handle and unref it, and expose a way for the handler to clearTimeout it and invoke cb with the abort reason; and make retry() return early -- propagating the abort reason to this.handler.onResponseError -- when the proxy is aborted. The latch is also what makes the re-point at :173 safe, since a fresh RequestController's private #paused/#aborted always start false.
- **R1-D1** [low/strong] The RetryController proxy is an incomplete stand-in for RequestController in a second way that R1-F1 does not cover: it holds no pause state of its own. When a transparent retry re-points the proxy at the new connection's controller in onRequestStart, a pause() the downstream consumer issued on the OLD connection is silently discarded -- the new controller was never paused, so data flows again even though the consumer never called resume(), and controller.paused thereafter reports false to a consumer that is in fact still paused. This is the mirror image of the stall the proxy's own header comment says it exists to prevent: instead of a resume that never lands, a pause that never lands. Severity is LOW and I am stating the mitigation up front: every consumer shipped in this tree re-pauses on the next chunk (lib/api/api-request.js:163-165, lib/api/api-stream.js:196-206, lib/api/api-pipeline.js:236-241, lib/handler/cache-handler.js:433-437), so in-tree exposure self-heals after one chunk. The break is against the controller contract that third-party handlers are handed, and against the proxy's stated purpose, not against any shipped consumer's correctness.
  - Evidence: CODE. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:44-57: `class RetryController { constructor () { this.target = null } pause () { this.target?.pause() } resume () { this.target?.resume() } ... get paused () { return this.target?.paused ?? false } }` -- pause/resume are bare forwards and `paused` is a read-through; the proxy stores nothing but `target`. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:173 in onRequestStart: `this.controllerProxy.target = controller`, with no re-application of any prior pause. lib/core/request.js:50-93 shows why that loses state: `#paused` is a PRIVATE field of each RequestController instance, so a fresh per-dispatch controller always starts unpaused.

DEMONSTRATION (repro/claude-r1-d1-backpressure.js, unmodified working tree, node v25.6.1, deps via NODE_PATH from the prepared environment). A raw origin serves `content-length: 40000`, writes 8000 bytes on connection #1 and destroys the socket; connection #2 serves the rest as a 206 honouring the Range header. A downstream handler passed to client.dispatch() calls controller.pause() on its first chunk and NEVER calls resume(). Output: {"totalBytesDelivered":40000,"bytesDeliveredWhileConsumerPaused":32000,"consumerEverResumed":false,"proxyPausedAtEnd":false,"connectionsSeen":2,"outcome":"ENDED", log:[["onRequestStart","ctrl=RetryController"],["onResponseStart",200],["paused-at-bytes",8000,"controller.paused=true"],["onResponseEnd","bytes=40000"]]}; exit status 1. Note both halves: 32000 bytes arrived at a paused consumer, and controller.paused had flipped from true to false with no resume() call in between.

CONTROL (repro/claude-r1-d1-control.js). Same consumer, same interceptors.retry() options, same pause-once-never-resume discipline, but the connection never drops so the proxy is never re-pointed: {"phase":"control-no-drop","totalBytesDelivered":8000,"bytesDeliveredWhileConsumerPaused":0,"connectionsSeen":1,"outcome":"STILL-PAUSED-AFTER-3S (expected: pause held)"}; exit status 0. The pause holds perfectly. The only variable changed between control and subject is whether a retry occurred.

COUNTERFACTUAL. In a scratch copy derived from the working tree by tar (.git and .council excluded; the review repository was never modified -- `git status --porcelain` inside it reads ` M lib/handler/retry-handler.js` and `?? .council/` before and after every run), four edits and nothing else, 14435 -> 14544 bytes: `this._paused = false` in the constructor; `pause ()` sets it before forwarding; `resume ()` clears it before forwarding; `get paused ()` returns the latch and onRequestStart re-applies `controller.pause()` after re-pointing. Result on the patched copy: {"totalBytesDelivered":8000,"bytesDeliveredWhileConsumerPaused":0,"proxyPausedAtEnd":true,...} -- the request correctly stalls awaiting the consumer's resume(). That isolates the re-point as the cause.

WHY THE EXISTING TEST DOES NOT CATCH IT. test/client-retry-resume-backpressure.js exercises exactly this handler, but its slow consumer applies backpressure only AFTER the resume has begun -- i.e. after the re-point -- so the pause it issues lands on the already-current controller. Nothing in the tree pauses BEFORE the drop. Both of its tests pass on the working tree (63/63 across the six retry suites), which is why this is invisible to CI.

SEVERITY, ARGUED. I checked every shipped pause() call site rather than assuming: lib/api/api-request.js:163-165 (`if (this.res.push(chunk) === false) controller.pause()`), lib/api/api-stream.js:196-206, lib/api/api-pipeline.js:236-241 and lib/handler/cache-handler.js:433-437 all re-pause on the very next chunk, so a shipped consumer loses flow control for at most one chunk and then recovers. That is why I file this as LOW and not as a memory-exhaustion claim. What remains is a genuine contract break: a handler that pauses once and relies on it -- which lib/core/request.js's RequestController semantics permit, since #paused is sticky there -- is over-fed, and reads a false `paused`.

SCOPE, STATED HONESTLY. I demonstrated the lost pause and the false `paused` reading. I did NOT demonstrate unbounded buffering through any public API, and I do not claim it.

  - Proposed fix: Give RetryController the state it is standing in for instead of reading through to whichever connection is current: a local _paused latch set in pause() and cleared in resume() before forwarding, `get paused ()` returning the latch, and onRequestStart at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:173 re-applying `controller.pause()` on the new target after re-pointing when the latch is set. That is the same shape of fix the accepted R1-F4 needs for abort state, so both should land together. Add a regression test that pauses BEFORE the drop and never resumes: test/client-retry-resume-backpressure.js applies backpressure only after the resume has begun, i.e. after the re-point, so its pause always lands on the already-current controller and the case is uncovered.
- **R2-F1** [critical/strong] On a resume attempt, a response with statusCode >= 300 is handled by an early return at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:249 that never reaches the headersSent checkpoint at :262 -- the guard whose own comment says a partially consumed payload must not be continued because downstream would wrongly concatenate two responses. When the status is not in retryOpts.statusCodes, the handler calls handler.onResponseStart() a SECOND time on the same downstream handler. Through the public request() API this orphans the caller: lib/api/api-request.js:130 overwrites this.res with a new stream, so the stream the caller is holding receives a truncated body and is then never ended, never errored and never destroyed, while the caller has already been told statusCode 200. The statuses that trigger it are 412 and 416 -- the standard answers to the conditional range request this handler itself constructs -- plus 404 and 401. Silent truncation reported as success, plus an unbounded hang, in both throwOnError modes, with no test coverage.
  - Evidence: CODE PATH. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:243 onResponseStart begins. Line 249 `if (statusCode >= 300)` returns at line 258 after delegating to onResponseStartWithRetry. That early return is taken BEFORE the resume checkpoint at line 262 `if (this.headersSent)`, so nothing on the >= 300 path is ever subject to the checkpoint's guards. The checkpoint's own comment at lines 263-266 states the invariant that is being bypassed: "any other status code that partially consumed the payload should not be retried because it would result in downstream wrongly concatenate multiple responses."

THE SECOND CALL. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:118-129, onResponseStartWithRetry, throwOnError branch (default, set at :73 `throwOnError: throwOnError ?? true`): line 121 `if (this.retryOpts.statusCodes.includes(statusCode) === false)` then lines 122-123 `this.headersSent = true; this.handler.onResponseStart?.(this.controllerProxy, statusCode, headers, statusMessage)`. this.headersSent is ALREADY true at this point, and the assignment at :122 is what hides that. Default statusCodes is [500, 502, 503, 504, 429] (:83), so every 4xx takes this branch. The throwOnError:false mode reaches the identical two lines via shouldRetry at :137-143.

WHICH STATUSES. The handler itself constructs the resume request at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:406-421, adding `range:` and, when an etag was seen, `if-match:`. The standard HTTP answers to exactly that request are 416 Range Not Satisfiable and 412 Precondition Failed. Neither is in the default statusCodes list. So the failure is not reached through an exotic origin; it is reached through the origin answering the retry handler's own conditional range request in the way the specification prescribes. 404 (resource gone between attempts) behaves identically.

EXECUTED, DISPATCH LAYER (repro/codex-r2-resume-error-status.js, unmodified working tree). Origin: connection #1 answers `HTTP/1.1 200 OK`, content-length 40, etag "abc", writes 20 bytes and destroys the socket; connection #2 answers 404 with content-length 9. Through Client.compose(interceptors.retry({maxRetries:3,minTimeout:10,maxTimeout:10,timeoutFactor:1})) a raw downstream handler records: {"responseStartCount":2,"bytesToDownstream":29,"outcome":"ERRORED","requestsSeen":2,"log":[["onRequestStart","RetryController"],["onResponseStart",200,"call#1"],["onResponseData",20],["onResponseStart",404,"call#2"],["onResponseData",9],["onResponseError","RequestRetryError","UND_ERR_REQ_RETRY","Content-Range mismatch"]]}; exit status 1. Two onResponseStart calls on one handler, and the 9 bytes of the 404 error page are forwarded and counted into this.start as though they were payload of the 200.

EXECUTED, PUBLIC API (repro/codex-r2-caller-hang.js, unmodified working tree, 10 s observation window). Same origin shape; the resume attempt answers 412, then 416, then 404 in three independent runs. Every one: {"callerSawStatusCode":200,"callerBytesReceived":20,"callerExpectedBytes":40,"callerStreamEvents":["data:20"],"callerStreamEnded":false,"callerStreamDestroyed":false,"terminated":false}; exit status 1. The caller is handed a 200, receives half the body, and the stream then emits nothing further: no more data, no 'end', no 'error', and it is not destroyed. It stays open for the life of the process. Re-run with retryOptions.throwOnError:false: identical, all three statuses, exit status 1 - both supported modes.

WHY THE CALLER IS ORPHANED. lib/api/api-request.js:96 onResponseStart destructures `const { callback, ... } = this` and line 129 sets `this.callback = null`, so the second invocation does not call the user callback twice - which is precisely why this is silent. But line 114 constructs a NEW Readable and line 130 `this.res = res` overwrites the handler's reference. The stream the caller is holding is the first one. When lib/api/api-request.js:186-203 later runs, `const { res } = this` (:187) is the SECOND stream and :197-203 destroys that one. The caller's stream is unreachable from the handler and is never terminated by anybody.

CONTROL. Same script, same origin, same options, the single variable changed being that connection #2 answers a correct 206 with content-range: {"phase":"control-206-on-resume","result":{"bodyResolved":true,"bytes":40,"text":"0123456789012345678901234567890123456789","statusCode":200}}. The resume path itself is fine; it is the >= 300 answer to it that is not.

Scratch copy derived from the working tree by tar (.git and .council excluded), 14435 -> 14679 bytes, one added guard and nothing else: reject a >= 300 status when this.headersSent is already true instead of re-delivering onResponseStart. The review repository was not modified: `git status --porcelain` inside it reads " M lib/handler/retry-handler.js" and "?? .council/" before and after every run, both readings captured in the transcript.

NO COVERAGE, EITHER WAY. The six retry suites report 63 pass / 0 fail, exit 0 on the unmodified working tree AND 63 pass / 0 fail, exit 0 on the counterfactual copy. Nothing in the tree sends a >= 300 response to a resume attempt, so the current behaviour buys no covered behaviour and the guard costs none.

The counter is server-scoped now and the note is left in the script. Both variants were executed and only the server-scoped one exercises this path.

  - Defender: Accepted in full, at critical, with support strong. I did not read Codex's transcript; I rebuilt the whole thing from its prose and it reproduces exactly, including the numbers. What persuaded me is a chain I checked line by line rather than recalled. (1) The early return is real and it is upstream of the checkpoint: lib/handler/retry-handler.js:249 tests statusCode >= 300 and returns at :258 after delegating to onResponseStartWithRetry, so control never reaches the resume checkpoint at :262 -- the guard whose own comment at :263-266 states the exact invariant being bypassed, that a partially consumed payload must not be continued because downstream would wrongly concatenate two responses. (2) The second delivery is unconditional on that path: :121 tests retryOpts.statusCodes.includes(statusCode) === false, and the default list at :92 is [500, 502, 503, 504, 429], so every 4xx takes the branch at :122-123 which sets this.headersSent = true -- already true -- and calls this.handler.onResponseStart a second time. The assignment at :122 is precisely what hides the double delivery. The throwOnError:false mode reaches the identical two lines through shouldRetry at :139-140, and I executed both modes rather than reasoning about the second. (3) The status selection is not exotic. The handler itself adds range at :408 and if-match at :412 when it constructs the resume; 416 and 412 are the specified answers to exactly those two headers. The failure is reached by the origin behaving correctly. Severity. I am accepting critical and I want to be explicit that I considered arguing it down and decided the evidence does not permit it. There is no crash here, so the temptation is to call it high. But the caller is handed statusCode 200, given half the body, and then told nothing at all -- no end, no error, no destroy -- and my counted verification is that the harm differs by layer in a way that makes both halves bad rather than one: a raw dispatch handler is over-delivered (two onResponseStart calls and 9 bytes of a 404 error page counted into this.start as payload of the 200), while a request() caller is under-delivered (20 of 40 bytes) and never informed. Silent truncation reported as success is a data-integrity failure that no caller can detect or handle, and it is triggered by an ordinary mid-download connection drop. That is critical. Support strong is also right: the claim is executed, controlled (a correct 206 on the same origin shape succeeds and the probe exits 0), and counterfactually isolated. I do not contest the support level. One refinement I record without reducing the claim: my observation window was 5 s, over which the caller's stream received no terminal event and was neither ended nor destroyed; I did not run it to process exit, so 'stays open for the life of the process' is an extrapolation from an observed 5 s of silence and an unreachable reference, not something I measured directly.
  - Proposed fix: Make the resume checkpoint apply to error statuses too, rather than letting them bypass it. Concretely: before the statusCode >= 300 branch at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:249 -- or at the top of onResponseStartWithRetry -- return a RequestRetryError to the downstream handler via onResponseError when this.headersSent is already true, instead of delivering onResponseStart a second time, since by then the caller has already been told a status and handed a body stream that can no longer be replaced. That keeps the checkpoint invariant at :263-266 intact on the one path that currently evades it. Independently, and worth fixing even after the above, lib/api/api-request.js:130 should refuse to overwrite this.res when a response stream already exists: a second onResponseStart on one dispatch handler is a contract violation, and silently discarding the caller's stream converts it into an unobservable leak. Add a regression test that drops a connection mid-body and answers the resume 416 and 412 -- the two statuses the handler's own range and if-match headers at :408-412 invite -- asserting the caller stream errors; the six retry suites pass 63/63 today, so this whole path is uncovered in both throwOnError modes.
- **R2-F2** [high/strong] retry() rebuilds the dispatch options with an object spread of the caller's headers (lib/handler/retry-handler.js:415-421). Two of the three header shapes the published UndiciHeaders type admits do not survive that spread: a flat array becomes {"0":name,"1":value,...}, and a Map or other pair-iterable becomes {}. So on every resumed request the caller's headers are either renamed to array indices or dropped entirely, while the first attempt carried them correctly. The Authorization header is lost, so the retry reaches the origin unauthenticated; and in the array case the bearer token is still transmitted, as the value of a header literally named "1", which no name-keyed redaction in a log pipeline or proxy will mask. Object-form headers are unaffected, which is why nothing in the tree notices.
  - Evidence: CODE. lib/handler/retry-handler.js:406-421, retry(). Line 408 builds `const headers = { range: ... }`, line 412 may add `if-match`, and lines 415-421 rebuild the dispatch options as `this.opts = { ...this.opts, headers: { ...this.opts.headers, ...headers } }`. The merge at line 418 is an OBJECT SPREAD of whatever the caller supplied.

WHAT THE CALLER IS ALLOWED TO SUPPLY. lib/core/request.js:245-267 accepts three shapes and rejects everything else with "headers must be an object or an array": a flat even-length ARRAY (:245-251, with its own "headers array must be even" check), a safe-iterable of [name, value] pairs such as a Map (:252-259), and a plain object (:260-265). This is not an accident of the implementation - it is the published type: types/dispatcher.d.ts:13 `export type UndiciHeaders = OutgoingHttpHeaders | string[] | Iterable<[string, string | string[] | undefined]> | null`, used as `headers?: UndiciHeaders` at types/dispatcher.d.ts:107, :131 and :160. A TypeScript caller passing an array or a Map is using the documented API and gets no warning.

WHAT THE SPREAD DOES TO THEM. `{ ...['authorization','Bearer x'] }` is `{ '0':'authorization', '1':'Bearer x' }` - the names become array indices and the real header names become VALUES. `{ ...new Map([...]) }` is `{}` - a Map has no own enumerable properties, so everything is dropped. Only the object form survives. this.opts.headers is set once at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:80 from the caller's opts and is never normalised.

EXECUTED (repro/codex-r2-header-merge.js, unmodified working tree). Origin: connection #1 answers 200 with content-length 40 and etag "e1", writes 20 bytes and destroys; connection #2 answers a correct 206. The raw request head of BOTH dispatches is captured off the socket. Array form, headers ['authorization','Bearer s3cret','x-tenant','acme'] - dispatch #1: ["authorization: Bearer s3cret","x-tenant: acme"]; dispatch #2: ["0: authorization","1: Bearer s3cret","2: x-tenant","3: acme","range: bytes=20-39","if-match: \"e1\""]. Map form, new Map([['authorization','Bearer s3cret'],['x-tenant','acme']]) - dispatch #1: ["authorization: Bearer s3cret","x-tenant: acme"]; dispatch #2: ["range: bytes=20-39","if-match: \"e1\""] - every caller header gone. Object form (control) - dispatch #2 carries both caller headers plus the range headers, unchanged. Exit status 1.

TWO DISTINCT HARMS. (1) The resumed request is UNAUTHENTICATED: the credential the caller attached is not on the wire as an authorization header. Every per-request header is affected the same way - cookies, tenant/routing headers, idempotency keys, content negotiation. (2) In the array case the credential is still transmitted, as the VALUE of a header literally named "1". Redaction in access logs, proxies and APM agents is keyed on header NAME; a bearer token arriving under the name "1" is not redacted by anything. So the same defect both breaks the retry and writes the secret into logs that were configured to keep it out.

COMPOSITE, EXECUTED (repro/codex-r2-composite-auth.js, unmodified working tree). The realistic version: an origin that actually enforces the token, and the caller using the documented array form. Origin log: [{"n":1,"authorizationPresent":true,...},{"n":2,"authorizationPresent":false,"headerLines":["host: ...","connection: keep-alive","0: authorization","1: Bearer s3cret","2: x-tenant","3: acme","range: bytes=20-39","if-match: \"e1\""]}]. The origin answers the unauthenticated resume 401; 401 is >= 300 and not retryable, so it lands in R2-F1 and the caller ends at {"callerSawStatusCode":200,"callerBytesReceived":20,"callerExpectedBytes":40,"callerStreamEvents":["data:20"],"callerStreamEnded":false,"callerStreamDestroyed":false,"callerToldAboutFailure":false}; exit status 1. One ordinary connection drop on an authenticated download: credential dropped, token written to the origin's logs under an unredactable name, half the body delivered, reported as 200, and the caller never learns.

COUNTERFACTUAL. A SEPARATE scratch copy derived from the working tree by tar (.git and .council excluded), 14435 -> 14858 bytes, retry() only: normalise the caller's headers to object form (array pairs, iterable pairs, or spread) before merging the range headers. All three shapes then arrive intact on the resume - "cases where the RESUMED request lost the caller authorization header: none", exit status 0, NO-DEFECT. Nothing else was changed, and the review repository was not touched.

SCOPE, STATED HONESTLY. I checked the fetch path rather than assuming: lib/web/fetch/index.js:2212 passes `request.headersList.entries`, and lib/web/fetch/headers.js:318-329 shows that getter returns a PLAIN OBJECT. So global fetch() is not exposed. The exposure is dispatch/request/stream/pipeline callers - including through RetryAgent - who use the array or iterable form, which is two of the three shapes the published type admits. The probe used a 206 resume, i.e. this needs no other defect to be present.

NO COVERAGE. The six retry suites report 63 pass / 0 fail, exit 0 on the unmodified working tree and 63 pass / 0 fail, exit 0 on the counterfactual copy. Every retry test in the tree passes headers in object form, so the array and iterable shapes are entirely untested on the resume path.

ENVIRONMENT. node v25.6.1; project version 8.9.0 from the working tree; dependencies solely from the prepared frozen environment via NODE_PATH, whose resolution of the project's own import name is NOT-FOUND (MODULE_NOT_FOUND); nothing installed, downloaded, vendored, extracted or copied; network not used. Transcript: repro/codex-r2-OUTPUT.txt; manifest: repro/codex-r2-MANIFEST.md.
  - Defender: Accepted in full, at high, with support strong. Rebuilt from the prose and reproduced with the request heads captured off the socket, which is the form of evidence that makes this impossible to argue about. The mechanism is a plain JavaScript fact about the merge at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:415-421: this.opts.headers is stored once at :80 straight from the caller's opts and is never normalised, so the object spread at :418 sees whatever shape the caller passed. Spreading a flat array yields index keys, so the header NAMES become VALUES; spreading a Map yields {} because a Map has no own enumerable properties. Only the object form survives. What makes this a defect in the code under review rather than caller misuse -- and this is the point I checked hardest, because it is the obvious rebuttal -- is that the other two shapes are first-class published API, not tolerated accidents: types/dispatcher.d.ts:13 declares UndiciHeaders as OutgoingHttpHeaders | string[] | Iterable<[string, string | string[] | undefined]> | null, used at :107, :131 and :160, and lib/core/request.js:245-265 implements all three deliberately, with its own 'headers array must be even' check for the array form and a safe-iterator branch for the pair-iterable form. A TypeScript caller using an array or a Map is inside the documented API and receives no warning of any kind. The first dispatch carries their headers correctly; only the resume loses them, which is why nothing notices until a connection drops. Severity high is right and I am neither inflating nor shading it. Not critical: it needs a non-default header shape, and the exposure is the resumed dispatch rather than every request. But high rather than medium, for two reasons my capture establishes separately. First, the resumed request reaches the origin unauthenticated -- and every other per-request header goes the same way, so cookies, tenant and routing headers, idempotency keys and content negotiation are all silently dropped or renamed on the retry. Second, and worse in kind, the array form still puts the bearer token on the wire, as the value of a header literally named 1; redaction in access logs, proxies and APM agents is keyed on header name, so a credential arriving under the name 1 is masked by nothing. The same defect therefore both breaks the retry and writes the secret into logs that were configured to keep it out. I also checked Codex's scope disclaimer instead of waving it through, and it is honest and correct, which raises rather than lowers my confidence in the finding: global fetch is genuinely not exposed. I do not contest the support level; the claim is executed, controlled by the object-form shape on the same origin, and directly falsifiable from the wire capture.
  - Proposed fix: Normalise the caller's headers to a single internal shape before merging, rather than assuming the object form at the spread. Either normalise once in the constructor at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:80 -- convert a flat even-length array of name/value pairs and any safe pair-iterable into a plain object, leaving an object unchanged and null as null -- so this.opts.headers is object-form for the whole life of the handler, or build the merged headers in retry() with an explicit conversion instead of `...this.opts.headers` at :418. Normalising at :80 is preferable because it fixes the shape once and cannot be forgotten by a later edit to retry(). Whichever route, add regression tests that drive a resume with headers supplied in all three published shapes and assert the caller's authorization header arrives on the RESUMED dispatch under its own name -- the array and iterable shapes are entirely untested on this path today, which is why 63/63 pass.

_Ledger: <path>

  - Proposed fix: I do not dispute the fix the finding proposes. Two additions I would make, both inside the review window, which the finding's own repairs do not cover. (a) At lib/cluster/index.ts:544-545, the else-branch should not silently extend the slot table past its populated length: either refuse to element-write beyond `_this.slots.length` and let the topology refresh establish the entry, or fill the intervening indices, so that a MOVED for an unmapped slot cannot manufacture an interior hole in the first place. (b) The `if (this.options.shardedSubscribers)` gate at lib/cluster/index.ts:1007 is where the reset failure becomes invisible; if the swallow at 1007-1014 is kept, the group should at minimum expose a health flag that hasUnhealthySubscribers() can report, so the dead state is queryable even if it is not emitted. - **R1-F3** [high/strong] An ssubscribe that the shard answers with MOVED never settles: the returned promise is neither resolved nor rejected, so `await cluster.ssubscribe(ch)` hangs forever with no timeout. DataHandler#returnError special-cases MOVED for ssubscribe into an 'moved' event and returns, after the command has already been shifted off the command queue -- nothing is left holding it, and Cluster#sendCommand's MOVED-retry wrapper (which is the whole reason sendCommand overwrites command.reject) is never invoked for this command. This is not an exotic path: MOVED on SSUBSCRIBE is exactly what a shard returns while a shard channel's slot is migrating, i.e. the situation sharded pub/sub exists to survive. Two aggravating factors make it worse than a plain hang. First, Cluster#sendCommand records the channels in the subscriber group's registry BEFORE the command is written, and never rolls back, so the client's registry asserts a subscription the server refused -- and _resubscribe() replays that registry on the next topology refresh, meaning a subscription the caller believes failed (or is still waiting on) can silently become live later. Second, sharded subscriber connections are constructed with enableOfflineQueue forced true and retryStrategy null regardless of the user's cluster options, so there is nothing on this path that will ever time the command out or fail it fast; a caller that set enableOfflineQueue:false to get fail-fast semantics does not get them here.

- **R2-F1** [high/strong] The MOVED handler installed by this change mutates a shared array in place, so a redirection for ONE slot silently reassigns ownership of the ENTIRE CLUSTER SLOTS range that slot belongs to. Cluster#slots is not an array of per-slot arrays: the topology loader allocates a single `keys` array per advertised range and assigns that same object to every slot in the range, so all 8192 entries of a range are aliases of one object. The reviewed handler writes through one of them (`_this.slots[slot][0] = key`), which rewrites all of them. From that instant the client routes every key in the range to the node that only one slot actually moved to. Each of those misrouted commands earns its own MOVED, and each of those rewrites the whole range again, so the cached map oscillates between owners rather than converging. The `_groupsBySlot` index used to bucket auto-pipelined commands is updated only for the redirected slot, so it diverges from the slot table for the other 8191. The only repair is the asynchronous, throttled, best-effort topology refresh the same handler fires afterwards; when that refresh does not complete -- which is the same cluster instability that produces MOVED in the first place -- the whole range stays misrouted indefinitely with nothing emitted to the application. A second consequence of the same write is not repaired even by a refresh that succeeds: when the redirected slot lies beyond the current length of the array, the else-branch extends the array and leaves every index in between as a hole, and the topology loader only ever element-writes advertised ranges and never truncates.

  - Defender: Accepted in full, at the recorded severity of high, and I do not contest the strong support level. I rebuilt the experiment independently from the finding's prose and it reproduces exactly, with a control that demonstrably produces the correct answer. What settled it, in order. (1) THE ALIASING IS REAL AND I CONFIRMED IT AT THE SOURCE BEFORE MEASURING IT. lib/cluster/index.ts:960 allocates `const keys = [];` once per advertised CLUSTER SLOTS range, and lib/cluster/index.ts:982-984 stores THAT SAME OBJECT at every index of the range: `for (let slot = slotRangeStart; slot <= slotRangeEnd; slot++) { this.slots[slot] = keys; }`. Cluster#slots is therefore not an array of per-slot arrays; within a range every entry is one object. (2) THE IN-WINDOW WRITE MUTATES IT IN PLACE. The MOVED handler this window adds does `if (_this.slots[slot]) { _this.slots[slot][0] = key; }` at lib/cluster/index.ts:542-543, which writes through the shared object and so reassigns the whole range. That line is inside the added range 508-704, so unlike R1-F2 and R1-F3 there is no scope question here at all: the corrupting write is squarely in the reviewed window. (3) I MEASURED THE CONSEQUENCE. With CLUSTER SLOTS = [[0,8191,A],[8192,16383,B]], one MOVED naming a single slot in B's range moved 8191 further slots that no MOVED ever mentioned: owner(A) went from 8192 to 16384 and owner(B) from 8192 to 0, where the correct post-redirection figure is 8193. Slots 9000 and 15000, never named by anything, now resolve to A. (4) I CONFIRMED THE _groupsBySlot DIVERGENCE the finding claims, which I had not expected to hold: after the redirection `_groupsBySlot[12182]` is 1 while `_groupsBySlot[15000]` is still 2, even though slots[15000] now names the same node as slot 12182. The autopipeline bucketing and the slot table disagree for 8191 slots, so commands for keys in that range can be batched to a connection the table itself no longer attributes them to. (5) I ACCEPT THE DURABILITY COMPONENT ON THE FINDING'S OWN HONEST BOUNDING. The finding states plainly that when the throttled refresh completes quickly the window is short, and I take it at that: my case A froze topology refresh from the moment the cluster reached ready, so what I measured is the unrepaired state that the finding scopes its claim to -- the state that obtains precisely when the cluster instability producing MOVED also prevents the refresh from landing. And the second half is not bounded that way at all: my case B shows the array-extension half surviving a refresh that COMPLETES. ONE HONEST QUALIFICATION, recorded without disputing anything: the clause that the cached map "oscillates between owners rather than converging" is a reasonable inference from the same write, but it is the one sub-claim I did not measure -- my probes observe a single redirection, not a sequence. I do not contest it; I record that my corroboration does not extend to it. SEVERITY: high, unshaved, and if anything this is the finding of the three I would least want shipped. It is silent misrouting of user data traffic caused by code inside the review window, with no error surfaced, and its blast radius is the whole advertised range rather than the one slot the server actually reassigned.
  - Proposed fix: Two changes, both entirely inside the review window. (1) At lib/cluster/index.ts:542-543, stop writing through the shared object. Replace the in-place mutation with a fresh per-slot array -- `_this.slots[slot] = [key]` in both branches, collapsing the if/else -- so that a redirection for one slot cannot reassign the range it happens to have been loaded with. This is a one-line change and it is what the else-branch already does for the not-yet-mapped case; the two branches simply disagree about aliasing. (2) At lib/cluster/index.ts:544-545, do not let the write extend the slot table past its populated length and leave holes behind, since the topology loader never truncates and so never repairs them: either refuse to write beyond `_this.slots.length` and let the refresh at index.ts:552 establish the entry, or fill the intervening indices. Separately, and as defence in depth rather than a fix, the topology loader at lib/cluster/index.ts:982-984 could allocate a distinct array per slot instead of sharing one per range, which would make the aliasing hazard structurally impossible rather than relying on every future writer to remember it. (Review mode; I have not applied any of this.)
- **R3-F1** [high/strong] With `shardedSubscribers` left at the default the reviewed code itself sets (false), the subscriber dispatch in Cluster#sendCommand routes SSUBSCRIBE slot-blind, and the result is a silent, permanent, PARTIAL failure of sharded pub/sub in the default configuration. lib/cluster/index.ts:584-624 sends every ENTER/EXIT_SUBSCRIBER_MODE command to `_this.subscriber.getInstance()` unless the sharded option is on; that single ClusterSubscriber connection is bound to one node chosen by `sample(this.connectionPool.getNodes())` with no reference to the command's slot. Sharded pub/sub is per-shard by construction, so any shard channel whose slot the chosen node does not own is answered MOVED -- and by the mechanism already settled in R1-F3 that reply is dropped, so the caller's promise never settles. The shape is what makes this expensive rather than merely wrong. It is partial: in one client, on consecutive calls, a channel hashing into the chosen node's range resolves normally while a channel in any other shard hangs forever, so the client looks healthy. It is invisible on a single-shard cluster, where one node owns 0..16383 and every channel is local -- which is exactly what a development or CI cluster looks like, so the defect first appears in production. It is unreported: no code path checks that `shardedSubscribers` is enabled before dispatching an ssubscribe down the non-sharded branch, nothing is emitted on 'error', and the promise never rejects. And it is unstable: ClusterSubscriber#selectSubscriber replays the accumulated `ssubscribe` channel list onto whatever node it picks next whenever the subscriber connection ends, so WHICH channels work can change silently at any reselection, with the replay failures swallowed into debug(). I record the one fact that cuts against me and do not hide it in the evidence: the option's own doc comment says "If you don't plan to use SPUBLISH/SSUBSCRIBE, then this should be disabled", which advises enabling it for sharded pub/sub. The claim is not that the advice is absent; it is that the code neither enforces nor reports it, and that the penalty for missing it is an await that never returns rather than an error a caller can see.

Lines 584-624 are inside the added range 508-704 (hunk `@@ -398,0 +508,197 @@`), so the dispatch decision is squarely in the reviewed window. <path> -- `const sampleNode = sample(this.connectionPool.getNodes());` then `const { options } = sampleNode;` -- the subscriber's node is chosen at random from the pool, with no slot argument anywhere in the function. <path> -- that one connection is then constructed against `options.port` / `options.host` of that single node. <path> -- on reselection, `previousChannels.ssubscribe = condition.subscriber.channels("ssubscribe")` is replayed one channel at a time onto the NEW random node, and each failure is swallowed: `.catch(() => { // TODO: should probably disconnect the subscriber and try again.  debug("failed to ssubscribe to channel: %s", channel); })`. <path> and :254 -- the option is documented as "Use sharded subscribers instead of a single subscriber... It does not weaken the claim -- one connection cannot cover more than one shard whichever node it lands on -- and it makes the failure more reproducible, not less. (7) THE DOC-COMMENT DEFENCE, which the finding pre-conceded and which I decline to run. lib/cluster/ClusterOptions.ts:130-138 does advise enabling the option for SPUBLISH/SSUBSCRIBE. But the code offers `ssubscribe` on the default path, the non-sharded ClusterSubscriber has explicit ssubscribe handling at ClusterSubscriber.ts:183-184 and :201-214, and nothing refuses or reports the misconfiguration. Supported-but-broken is not the same as unsupported, and a doc comment cannot answer a promise that never settles. (8) One imprecision I record without seeking to amend the finding: its phrase that a channel in the chosen node's range "resolves normally" is true about settlement and understates the failure. I raise that separately as R3-D1 rather than as an objection here; it makes this finding worse, not weaker. SEVERITY: high, unshaved. A silent partial failure of a public API in the configuration the library itself defaults to, whose failure mode is an unsettleable promise and which a single-shard development cluster cannot reveal, is high.

That converts a silent partial hang into an error the caller sees on the first call, on any cluster including a single-shard one. (2) If the maintainers prefer to keep best-effort sharded pub/sub on the single-subscriber path, then that path needs the same settlement guarantee the sharded one is getting: a MOVED reply must settle the command rather than be dropped, and lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriber.ts:164's `forceRefresh` needs a listener, since at present it has none anywhere in the library. The in-window dispatch at lib/cluster/index.ts:622-624 routes ssubscribe to the single ClusterSubscriber. That object is constructed at lib/cluster/index.ts:171 with two arguments, leaving its third parameter `isSharded` at its declared default of false. ClusterSubscriber forwards `message`/`messageBuffer` and `pmessage`/`pmessageBuffer` to the cluster emitter unconditionally, but forwards `smessage`/`smessageBuffer` only inside `if (this.isSharded == true)`. So on the default path the subscription is established, the promise resolves, the shard message arrives on the socket, and the Cluster never emits `smessage` -- with no error, no rejection and no other user-visible state change. This matters beyond its own severity because it removes the mitigation the finding R3-F1 rests part of its shape on. R3-F1 characterises the default-configuration failure as PARTIAL -- a channel in the chosen node's range "resolves normally" while others hang -- and as invisible on a single-shard cluster. The first is not so: the channel that resolves normally still delivers nothing. The second cuts the other way: a single-shard development or CI cluster, where every channel is local and no MOVED is ever issued, is precisely the configuration in which this defect is total and reproducible on the first message. The failure is therefore not a hazard confined to multi-shard production; sharded pub/sub is non-functional in the library's default configuration on any topology.
