### T24 — Verdict 2

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 2/3 · Findings: 3

**NO-SHIP: 2 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] With sharded subscribers enabled, the supported zero-argument `SUNSUBSCRIBE` form throws synchronously instead of unsubscribing all shard channels. `Command.getSlot()` returns null when there is no key, but the sharded path passes that value to `getResponsibleSubscriber`, which dereferences a nonexistent slot entry.
  - Evidence: `lib/Command.ts:275-280` sets the slot to null when `getKeys()[0]` is absent; `lib/cluster/index.ts:587-592` routes every `sunsubscribe` through `getResponsibleSubscriber(targetSlot)`; `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:50-52` unconditionally evaluates `this.clusterSlots[slot][0]`; `test/functional/spub_ssub.ts:42-53` demonstrates that zero-argument `sunsubscribe()` is a supported command form.
  - Defender: I accept this in full, at the claimed severity, because I reproduced it rather than reasoned about it. What persuaded me, in order: (1) `lib/Command.ts:275-280` really does set `this.slot = key == null ? null : calculateSlot(key)`, and the command's own metadata makes the no-key case legal — its spec is `{"arity":-1,"keyStart":1,"keyStop":-1,"step":1}`, so zero arguments is a valid arity and yields zero keys and therefore a null slot; (2) the zero-argument form is not incidental, it is a generated public overload of the cluster commander at `lib/utils/RedisCommander.ts:8782` (`sunsubscribe(callback?: Callback<unknown>)`) and the standalone client's own test exercises it; (3) the review window at `lib/cluster/index.ts:591-592` passes that null straight into `getResponsibleSubscriber`, which dereferences `this.clusterSlots[slot][0]` unconditionally at `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:51`; and (4) the failure is worse than a rejected promise — it is a synchronous TypeError thrown out of `sendCommand`, so it bypasses both the promise and the callback contract and lands in whatever frame invoked the command. Severity high is right on the evidence and I am not arguing it down: a documented, typed API form makes a caller's process throw, and the guard immediately below it (`if (!sub) command.reject(...)`) shows the code intended to reject rather than throw here. I do not contest the strong support level; my own independent reproduction, built from the finding's prose rather than from any of its author's artifacts, corroborates it.
  - Proposed fix: In the review window, before consulting the group, treat an absent slot as the documented "unsubscribe from everything on this connection" case rather than as a slot lookup. Concretely, in `lib/cluster/index.ts` inside the sharded branch: if `command.name == "sunsubscribe" && command.getKeys().length === 0`, fan the command out to every started sharded subscriber (or, minimally, reject with the same `AbortError` the `!sub` guard already uses) instead of calling `getResponsibleSubscriber(targetSlot)`. Independently, harden `ClusterSubscriberGroup.getResponsibleSubscriber` at `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:50-51` to `const entry = this.clusterSlots[slot]; if (!entry) return undefined;` so a non-numeric or out-of-range slot returns undefined and takes the existing rejection path rather than throwing. The `!sub` guard already downstream shows that returning undefined is the shape the caller expects.
- **R1-F2** [high/strong] Sharded subscription bookkeeping is committed before Redis confirms the command. A terminally failed `SSUBSCRIBE` remains recorded and can later be subscribed during recovery despite the caller receiving rejection; conversely, a failed `SUNSUBSCRIBE` is removed from the recovery set, so reconnect can silently lose a subscription that remained active before disconnection.
  - Evidence: `lib/cluster/index.ts:603-614` calls `addChannels`/`removeChannels` before selecting the Redis instance, while the actual command is not sent until `lib/cluster/index.ts:701`. `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:83-89` immediately persists additions and `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:111-116` immediately persists removals. Recovery later treats that mutable map as authoritative and issues subscriptions from it at `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:336-367`. No success callback or rejection rollback connects command outcome to these mutations.
  - Defender: I accept this in full, at the claimed severity and support level, for both arms. I went looking for the rollback the finding says is missing, and for a second layer that might mask it, and neither exists. Specifically: (1) the ordering is exactly as cited — `lib/cluster/index.ts:603-611` mutates the group's channel map, and the command is not handed to a connection until `lib/cluster/index.ts:701`; (2) a repository-wide search for the two mutators finds only those two call sites (`addChannels` at `lib/cluster/index.ts:604`, `removeChannels` at `lib/cluster/index.ts:608`) plus their definitions, so there is no compensating call on rejection anywhere; (3) the map is the only source for recovery, read at `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:346` inside `_resubscribe()`, which `reset()` invokes at `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:281` and which the cluster drives from `lib/cluster/index.ts:1007-1014` on every slots-cache refresh; and (4) the obvious mitigation does not apply — the connection-level auto-resubscribe in `lib/redis/event_handler.ts:345-351`, which replays only server-confirmed subscriptions, is unreachable for these connections because `ShardedSubscriber` constructs its Redis instance with `retryStrategy: null` (`lib/cluster/ShardedSubscriber.ts:66`, commented "Disable auto reconnection for subscribers. The ClusterSubscriberGroup will handle the reconnection"). That last point cuts against me, not for me: it removes the only place server-confirmed state could have corrected the group's optimistic record. I then demonstrated both arms rather than resting on the reading. Severity high is correct and I am not arguing it down: arm one silently re-establishes a subscription the caller was told had failed, and arm two silently drops one the caller was told it still held — both are wrong in the direction the application cannot detect. I do not contest the strong support level. One scoping remark that does not reduce the finding: the CROSSSLOT case is not affected, because `addChannels`/`removeChannels` return -1 before mutating (`lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:76-79` and `105-109`), so the only unsound commits are the ones the finding names.
  - Proposed fix: Tie the bookkeeping to the command outcome instead of to command dispatch. In `lib/cluster/index.ts`, keep only the CROSSSLOT validation before dispatch (call a pure `validateSameSlot(command.getKeys())` that returns -1 without mutating), then attach the mutation to the command's settlement: on `ssubscribe`, `command.promise.then(() => this.shardedSubscribers.addChannels(keys), noop)`; on `sunsubscribe`, `command.promise.then(() => this.shardedSubscribers.removeChannels(keys), noop)`. Because the cluster's reject override can retry the same command (MOVED/ASK/TRYAGAIN/CLUSTERDOWN/failover all re-enter `tryConnection`), binding to the settled promise rather than to each dispatch also removes the duplicate `addChannels` call that a redirected command currently performs. If a pre-dispatch record is wanted so that a subscribe issued during a reset is not lost, it must be a separate pending set that is promoted to the recovery set on success and discarded on rejection, never the set `_resubscribe()` reads.
- **R1-D1** [medium/strong] Distinct from R1-F2 (which is about bookkeeping running ahead of the command outcome), the sharded-subscription bookkeeping is also wrong when BOTH commands succeed: a shard channel named with a Buffer can never be removed from the subscriber group's recovery set. The review window hands `command.getKeys()` — typed `Array<string | Buffer>` — to `ClusterSubscriberGroup.removeChannels`, whose removal predicate is `!channels.includes(c)`. `Array.prototype.includes` uses SameValueZero, so two distinct Buffer objects holding the same bytes never compare equal and the entry survives every removal. The caller is told the unsubscribe succeeded, the server does unsubscribe, but the client-side recovery set keeps the channel forever, so every subsequent slots refresh silently re-subscribes to a channel the application has explicitly left and resumes delivering `smessage` events for it. The same mismatch also defeats mixed-form usage (subscribe with a Buffer, unsubscribe with the equivalent string, or vice versa). Buffer channel names are a supported, generated-typings-blessed form of the API, not an abuse of it.
  - Evidence: Call site inside the review window: `lib/cluster/index.ts:607-611` passes `command.getKeys()` to `removeChannels`; the symmetric addition is `lib/cluster/index.ts:603-605`. `lib/Command.ts:283-285` types `getKeys(): Array<string | Buffer>`. The removal predicate is `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:114`: `const updatedChannels = slotChannels.filter((c) => !channels.includes(c));`. The recovery set thus retained is the sole source for `_resubscribe()` at `lib/cluster/ClusterSubscriberGroup.ts:336-376`, which is reached from `lib/cluster/index.ts:1007-1014` on every slots-cache refresh. The public form is typed at `lib/utils/RedisCommander.ts:8783-8791`: `sunsubscribe(...args: [...shardchannels: (string | Buffer)[]])`. Demonstrated end to end against two mock cluster nodes, with a string control arm and a non-emptiness assertion on the captured command log; archive `_rerun2/T24-armX/repro/`, script `scripts/d1_buffer_channel_resurrection.ts`, capture `out/d1_buffer_channel_resurrection.txt`, clean rerun from a scrubbed copy of the working tree via `run.sh` (node v25.6.1, ts-node 10.9.2, typescript 4.7.4, every declared runtime pin matched exactly: @ioredis/commands 1.10.0, cluster-key-slot 1.1.1, debug 4.4.3, denque 2.1.0, redis-errors 1.2.0, redis-parser 3.0.0, standard-as-callback 2.1.0). Captured output, exit status 0: `ssubscribe(Buffer 'foo') err = null` / `ssubscribe('bar') err = null` / `sunsubscribe errs = null / null` / `recovery set after BOTH unsubscribes: {"5061":[],"12182":["Buffer<foo>"]}` / `commands re-issued after refresh: ["node2: ssubscribe foo"]` / `RESULT: Buffer channel 'foo' re-subscribed after unsubscribe = true` / `RESULT: string channel 'bar' re-subscribed after unsubscribe = false` / `ASSERT non-empty capture: commands observed after refresh = 5`. The string control proves the harness can register a successful removal, so the Buffer result is not a false positive of the probe.
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: Canonicalize channel identities or compare by bytes. For example, remove an entry when both values are Buffers and `Buffer.equals` succeeds, or when their normalized byte representations match. Add same-form Buffer and mixed string/Buffer unsubscribe-and-refresh regression tests.

_Ledger: <path>
```

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 8

**NO-SHIP: 4 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] RetryHandler silently concatenates an unrelated error response onto a partially delivered body and reports it downstream as a successful, complete response. In lib/handler/retry-handler.js, onResponseStart (183-197) returns early for every statusCode >= 300 and hands off to onResponseStartWithRetry BEFORE the this.headersSent resume checkpoint at 199-237. That checkpoint is the only code that enforces the invariant stated in its own comment at 201-204 ('should not be retried because it would result in downstream wrongly concatenate multiple responses'). With the default throwOnError:true, onResponseStartWithRetry takes the 'not eligible for retry' branch at 76-78 whenever the status is not in retryOpts.statusCodes ([500,502,503,504,429]); that branch sets this.headersSent = true and calls this.handler.onResponseStart a SECOND time on a downstream handler that has already received onResponseStart and body bytes for a different response. Because that branch never sets this.error, onResponseData (307-315) then forwards the error response's body to the same downstream sink, and onResponseEnd (317-328) takes the !this.error path, resets retryCount and delivers a normal end. The caller therefore receives the first response's status and headers, a truncated prefix of the real resource, the error page's bytes appended to it, and no error at all. This is reachable on any mid-body disconnect (the retry path this handler exists for): 412 is the canonical case because retry() at 335-337 attaches an If-Match validator itself, so an origin whose ETag rotated between attempts answers the resume with exactly the status that triggers the bug. Through the shipped public surface (Client.compose(interceptors.retry()) + .request()) the same sequence instead produces a permanent hang: lib/api/api-request.js:90-130 builds a NEW Readable on the second onResponseStart and assigns this.res, while this.callback is already null, so the stream already handed to the caller is orphaned and never receives push(null) or an error.
  - Evidence: Executed repro archive at <path> (repro-1-resume-4xx-concat.js, repro-2-negative-control.js, repro-3-public-api.js, repro-6-status-matrix.js, run-all.sh, TRANSCRIPT.txt). Environment recorded in TRANSCRIPT.txt lines 1-16: node v25.6.1 at /opt/homebrew/bin/node, Darwin arm64 25.5.0, undici@8.2.0 working tree at <path> with no node_modules of its own; deps only from the prepared audited env via NODE_PATH; bare 'undici' resolves NOT-FOUND (MODULE_NOT_FOUND) from that env, so the module under review can only be the working tree (require.resolve prints <path>). No network, no installs. || repro-1 (exit 0): server answers attempt 1 with 200, content-length: 20, ETag "v1", writes 10 bytes, then destroys the socket; attempt 2 arrives with Range: bytes=10-19 and If-Match: "v1" (both added by retry() at 330-346) and answers 412 with a 10-byte body. Downstream event sequence captured: ["onResponseStart:200","onResponseData:10","onResponseStart:412","onResponseData:10","onResponseEnd"]; body "AAAAAAAAAAXXXXXXXXXX"; byteLength 20 (non-empty, so the comparison is not vacuous); terminal error NONE. The delivered length equals the advertised content-length of 20, so a downstream length check also passes on corrupt bytes. || repro-2 negative control (exit 0), byte-identical except attempt 2 answers correctly with 206 + Content-Range bytes 10-19/20 + matching ETag: onResponseStart count = 1, body "AAAAAAAAAABBBBBBBBBB", assertions report BUG REPRODUCED: false. The probe can fail. || repro-6 status matrix (exit 0), maxRetries 1, one process per row: statuses 301, 400, 404, 412, 416 and 451 each produce onResponseStart count=2, terminal=success, body "AAAAAAAAAAXXXXXXXXXX", CONTAMINATED=true; the in-script control row 503 (the one status in the default retryOpts.statusCodes) produces onResponseStart count=1, terminal=UND_ERR_REQ_RETRY, body "AAAAAAAAAA", CONTAMINATED=false. That contrast isolates the 76-78 branch, not the scenario, as the cause. || repro-3 public API (exit 2 = watchdog): Client.compose(interceptors.retry({maxRetries:3,minTimeout:10,maxTimeout:20,timeoutFactor:1})) + .request(). Staged logging shows 'request() resolved with statusCode 200', the server then serves the 412 resume, and res.body.text() never settles within the 20s watchdog - no value, no rejection. Caller-visible outcome: told 200 OK, then hung on a leaked Readable. || Every existing retry suite passes on this tree, so nothing in the project's own tests covers this path: test/retry-handler.js 20/20, test/retry-handler2.js 22/22, test/retry-agent.js 1/1, test/interceptors/retry.js 11/11 (TRANSCRIPT.txt lines 115-164). || Review repo unmodified before and after: git status --porcelain reports only ' M lib/handler/retry-handler.js' and '?? .council/' (TRANSCRIPT.txt lines 10 and 166-168).
  - Defender: I rebuilt this from the prose alone and it reproduces. What decided it is the control contrast, not the assertion: on this tree the only difference between contamination and correctness is which branch of onResponseStartWithRetry the resume takes. The code path is exactly as stated — onResponseStart's statusCode >= 300 early return at 187-197 hands off before the this.headersSent checkpoint at 199-237, so the invariant the comment at 201-204 states ('would result in downstream wrongly concatenate multiple responses') is never evaluated for a non-2xx resume; the 76-78 branch then sets this.headersSent = true and calls this.handler.onResponseStart a second time without setting this.error, so onResponseData at 307-315 forwards the error page's bytes to the same sink and onResponseEnd at 317-328 takes the !this.error path and reports a clean end. I also confirmed the public-surface consequence in lib/api/api-request.js:90-130 by reading it: this.callback is nulled on the first onResponseStart, so a second one builds a fresh Readable, assigns this.res and delivers it to nobody, orphaning the stream already handed to the caller. Two corrections that make the finding BROADER, not narrower, and that any fix must cover: (1) the same missing headersSent guard exists on the throwOnError:false path at 87-88 and at 94-95, so with throwOnError:false a RETRYABLE status contaminates too — my 503 row, which is the clean control under the default, produces two onResponseStart calls and 'AAAAAAAAAAXXXXXXXXXX' with terminal success once throwOnError is false and retries are exhausted; (2) the trigger is therefore not limited to statuses outside retryOpts.statusCodes. I accept the severity at high: the caller is told 200 with a body of the advertised length that is partly someone else's response, which is silent corruption of authenticated data, and on the shipped request() surface it is an unbounded hang instead.
  - Proposed fix: Make the headersSent invariant unconditional rather than a property of the < 300 path. In onResponseStartWithRetry, every site that is about to call this.handler.onResponseStart (76-78, 87-88, 94-95) must first check this.headersSent: if headers have already gone downstream, the response cannot be forwarded — set this.error (or throw RequestRetryError 'server does not support the range header and the payload was partially consumed', matching 206-210) so that onResponseData suppresses the foreign bytes and the caller receives a terminal error instead of a silently concatenated body. Equivalently, hoist the this.headersSent checkpoint at 199-237 above the statusCode >= 300 branch at 187 so every response, not just 2xx, is validated against the resume invariant. Independently, lib/api/api-request.js:90-130 should treat a second onResponseStart after this.callback has been nulled as a protocol error on the existing this.res rather than silently replacing it, so a handler bug can never present as a hang.
- **R1-F2** [high/strong] The resume path silently strips the caller's request headers, including Authorization, whenever headers were supplied in either of the two non-plain-object forms the public type explicitly allows. retry() at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:330-346 rebuilds the request as { ...this.opts, headers: { ...this.opts.headers, ...headers } }. types/dispatcher.d.ts:13 declares UndiciHeaders = OutgoingHttpHeaders | string[] | Iterable<[string, string|string[]|undefined]> | null and DispatchOptions.headers is that type (types/dispatcher.d.ts:107); lib/core/request.js:246, 342 and 380 all branch on Array.isArray(headers), so the array form is a first-class supported input, not a misuse. Object-spreading a string[] produces numeric keys ('0','1','2','3'), and object-spreading an Iterable such as a Map produces {}. In both cases the retried request goes out WITHOUT the caller's real headers - no Authorization, no tenant or trace headers - while still carrying the Range and If-Match this code added. The bytes returned by that unauthenticated, unscoped request are then appended to the authenticated bytes already delivered downstream, and (per R1-F1's machinery) delivered as success. This is a trust-boundary defect, not a cosmetic one: the retried request is a different request than the one the caller authorized, and in the string[] case the credential is still transmitted on the wire under the meaningless header name '3'. The corruption is confined to the this.start !== 0 branch, i.e. it only fires after a mid-body disconnect, which is why no existing test catches it.
  - Evidence: Executed repro: <path> exit status 0, transcript at TRANSCRIPT.txt lines 54-72. Same environment as R1-F1 (node v25.6.1, undici@8.2.0 working tree, deps only from the prepared audited env via NODE_PATH, bare 'undici' NOT-FOUND from that env, no network, no installs). Server answers attempt 1 with 200 + content-length 20 + ETag "v1", writes 10 bytes, destroys the socket; attempt 2 answers 206 with Content-Range bytes 10-19/20. Headers are read off the wire by the server, so this is observed transmission, not inference. || CASE A, headers passed as ['x-tenant-id','tenant-42','authorization','Bearer SECRET']: attempt 1 on the wire = {"host":...,"connection":"keep-alive","x-tenant-id":"tenant-42","authorization":"Bearer SECRET"}; attempt 2 (the resume) on the wire = {"0":"x-tenant-id","1":"tenant-42","2":"authorization","3":"Bearer SECRET","host":...,"connection":"keep-alive","range":"bytes=10-19","if-match":"\"v1\""}. authorization and x-tenant-id are present on attempt 1 and absent on attempt 2; the credential survives only as the value of a bogus header named '3'. || CASE B, headers passed as new Map([['x-tenant-id','tenant-42'],['authorization','Bearer SECRET']]): attempt 1 carries both headers; attempt 2 carries only host, connection, range and if-match - every caller header is gone. || Both cases assert A1 'attempt 2 actually happened' = true, so neither comparison is vacuous on an absent second request, and A4 confirms the resume did carry a Range header (i.e. it really is the this.start !== 0 branch at 331). BUG REPRODUCED: true. || The four retry suites still pass 20/20, 22/22, 1/1 and 11/11 on this tree (TRANSCRIPT.txt lines 115-164), so the supported non-object header forms are untested across a retry. || Review repo unmodified: git status --porcelain unchanged before and after (TRANSCRIPT.txt lines 10 and 166-168).
  - Defender: Reproduced independently, and the object-headers control is what settled it: the same scenario with the same server, differing only in the form of the caller's headers, preserves authorization on the resume when headers are a plain object and drops it when they are the array or Iterable form. The mechanism is exactly as described — retry() at 339-345 rebuilds opts as { ...this.opts, headers: { ...this.opts.headers, ...headers } }, and object-spreading a string[] yields numeric keys while object-spreading a Map yields {}. That both forms are first-class input is not arguable on this tree: types/dispatcher.d.ts:13 declares UndiciHeaders = OutgoingHttpHeaders | string[] | Iterable<...> | null, DispatchOptions.headers is that type, and lib/core/request.js branches on Array.isArray(headers) at 246, 342 and 380. I accept high severity. My one framing note, which does not reduce it: the credential is not disclosed to a new party — it goes to the same origin under the meaningless name '3' — so the weight is not exfiltration but that the resumed request is a DIFFERENT request from the one the caller authorized (no Authorization, no tenant or trace scoping), and its bytes are then appended to authenticated bytes and reported as success by the R1-F1 machinery. On this tree that is high on its own: silent loss of tenant scoping across a retry is a correctness and access-control fault, not a cosmetic one.
  - Proposed fix: Normalize the caller's headers to a single representation once, in the constructor, before storing this.opts — the codebase already has the machinery (lib/core/request.js uses Array.isArray(headers) ? parseHeaders(headers) : headers at 342 and 380) — and have retry() merge the Range/If-Match into that normalized object. Alternatively, in retry() at 339-345, branch on the shape: for Array.isArray(this.opts.headers) append the two new header name/value pairs to a copy of the array instead of spreading it, and for an Iterable materialize it via parseHeaders or Object.fromEntries before merging. A regression test should assert, off the wire, that a caller-supplied Authorization survives a resumed request in all three declared header forms.
- **R1-F3** [high/strong] A resumed download that ends short of the length the handler itself computed is delivered downstream as a complete, successful response. onResponseStart records the expected final offset in this.end (from Content-Range at 255-263, otherwise from content-length at 267-270) and onResponseData tracks bytes delivered in this.start at 312, but onResponseEnd (317-328) never compares them: on the !this.error path it simply resets retryCount and forwards onResponseEnd. Consequently, when a resumed response completes its own framing correctly but carries fewer bytes than the range that was requested - a chunked 206 that terminates early, which is exactly what a truncating cache, a proxy, or a byte-range-lying origin produces - the handler neither retries nor errors. Downstream is left holding the ORIGINAL response's headers (content-length: 20) alongside a 13-byte body and a clean end, so even a caller who validates Content-Length against what it received has no signal, because the handler suppressed the resumed response's own headers on the this.headersSent path at 200-236. This is silent data loss on the very code path whose purpose is to guarantee the caller gets the whole body.

  - Defender: ACCEPT in full, at high severity, on the reopened scope. I separate what I merely re-verified from what actually moved me. Re-verified, and unchanged since round 2: the completion path never consults the target offset the handler computed for itself. grep -n of this.end over the reviewed file returns 69, 234, 239, 263, 267, 269, 274 and 332 - not one of them inside onResponseEnd at 317-328, which on the success path only resets retryCount at 323 and forwards at 324. What is NEW in your round-3 escalation, and what I tested rather than took on your word, is the assertion that nothing in the SHIPPED stack acts on the mismatch either. Driven only through Client.compose(interceptors.retry(...)).request(), my cl-truncate row RESOLVES: public_phase resolved, public_statusCode 200, public_errorCode null, body AAAAAAAAAABBB, 13 bytes, against a caller-visible content-length of 20; res.body.text() does not reject. My chunk-truncate row reproduces identically with no length signal at all (caller_visible_content_length null, caller_visible_content_range null). My cl-full control is byte-identical except the resume delivers all ten remaining bytes and yields 20 bytes with SHORT_BODY_DELIVERED_AS_SUCCESS false, so the probe can fail. I then went and answered the deciding-evidence question you posed against yourself - a citation of any code path in lib/ that compares bytes delivered against content-length on this response. There is exactly one such check in the whole stack, lib/dispatcher/client-h1.js:719 (request.method !== HEAD && contentLength !== -1 && bytesRead !== contentLength, destroying the socket with ResponseContentLengthMismatchError), and it is scoped to one wire response measured against that response own framing, so it cannot see a body assembled across two attempts. My cl-truncate-cl control makes that concrete and gives the probe a SECOND, independent way to fail: when the resumed response advertises its own content-length 10 and then delivers only 3 bytes before EOF, that check does fire and the caller is rejected with UND_ERR_RES_CONTENT_LENGTH_MISMATCH. That boundary is exactly the one your claim already draws - a resumed response that completes its OWN framing correctly while carrying fewer bytes than the range it was asked for - so the existence of that check corroborates the finding instead of narrowing it, and it also tells a fixer that the wire layer will never do this job for them. Severity: I do not argue it down. A short body handed to the caller as a resolved success is silent data loss on the one code path whose entire purpose is to guarantee the whole body. One point I re-affirm because it is load-bearing for the fix and it originated as a correction from this seat in round 2, which your recorded proposed_fix now carries: on the chunked variant this.end is null at completion (the destructure at 231 binds start, size and end only to feed the asserts at 233-234 before returning at 236, so the resume own size is discarded), and therefore a completeness check written as this.end != null && this.start !== this.end + 1 would not fire there at all. Adopting end, or size - 1, from the resume own Content-Range at 231 has to come first.
  - Proposed fix: Two parts, because the recorded fix covers only one of the two variants. (1) Adopt the resume’s own framing at the checkpoint: at 231, after the asserts at 233-234 pass, set this.end = end ?? (size != null ? size - 1 : this.end) so that a resume which states its content-range gives the handler a target even when the first response advertised no length. Without this, this.end is null on the chunked path and no completeness check can fire. (2) Then, on the success path in onResponseEnd at 322-325, before resetting retryCount and forwarding, compare delivered against target: if this.end != null && this.start !== this.end + 1, the body is incomplete — re-enter retry(controller) (this.start is already the correct resume offset and the range/If-Match machinery at 330-346 is in place), or, once retries are exhausted, deliver a RequestRetryError through onResponseError. The terminal signal must not be success. (3) Regression tests for BOTH variants: one where attempt 1 advertised a content-length and one where it did not, since only the second is invisible to a length-checking caller.
- **R1-F4** [medium/strong] retryOptions.retryAfter is a documented, typed, defaulted option that is never read - setting it to false does not disable Retry-After handling. The constructor destructures retryAfter at line 30 and stores retryAfter: retryAfter ?? true at line 42, but nothing in the file ever reads this.retryOpts.retryAfter; the default retry implementation reads headers['retry-after'] unconditionally at 167-180 and lets it dominate the backoff via Math.min(retryAfterHeader, maxTimeout) at 176-177. docs/docs/api/RetryHandler.md:28 and docs/docs/api/RetryAgent.md:25 both document it as 'It enables automatic retry after the Retry-After header is received. Default: true', and types/retry-handler.d.ts:95 declares retryAfter?: boolean, so callers are told an opt-out exists. The consequence is that an origin controls the client's retry pacing with no way to refuse: each attempt can be stalled up to maxTimeout (default 30000 ms) by an attacker-chosen or misconfigured Retry-After, for up to maxRetries attempts, and the operator's explicit retryAfter:false has no effect. This is a degraded-dependency and availability exposure hiding behind an option that reads as a safety control.
  - Evidence: Executed repro: <path> exit status 0, transcript at TRANSCRIPT.txt lines 74-91. Same environment as R1-F1 (node v25.6.1, undici@8.2.0 working tree, deps only from the prepared audited env via NODE_PATH, bare 'undici' NOT-FOUND from that env, no network, no installs). Two cases run in the same process against the same server, differing ONLY in the retryAfter flag; base options {maxRetries:3, minTimeout:10, maxTimeout:30000, timeoutFactor:1}, so with Retry-After ignored the backoff would be about 10 ms. Server answers attempt 1 with 503 + 'Retry-After: 3' and attempt 2 with 200. Measured elapsed time between serving the 503 and the request completing: retryAfter:true -> 3010 ms; retryAfter:false -> 3002 ms. Both cases served 2 attempts, so the A1 'a retry actually happened' assertion rules out a vacuous timing comparison. BUG REPRODUCED (opt-out has no effect): true. Roughly 300x the configured backoff in both cases, with no measurable difference between the documented default and the documented opt-out. || Static confirmation that the option is dead: grep -rn 'retryAfter' over lib, types, test and docs in the review repo returns only lib/handler/retry-handler.js:12, 13 (the calculateRetryAfterHeader parameter, a different binding), :30 and :42 (destructure and store), :167-177 (the header, not the option), types/retry-handler.d.ts:95, test/issue-5137.js:41, docs/docs/api/RetryHandler.md:28, docs/docs/api/RetryAgent.md:25 and docs/docs/api/Dispatcher.md:1024. There is no read of retryOpts.retryAfter or retryOptions.retryAfter anywhere in lib. || The four retry suites pass 20/20, 22/22, 1/1 and 11/11 on this tree (TRANSCRIPT.txt lines 115-164); none asserts that retryAfter:false changes anything. || Review repo unmodified: git status --porcelain unchanged before and after (TRANSCRIPT.txt lines 10 and 166-168).
  - Defender: Confirmed both statically and behaviourally. The constructor destructures retryAfter at 30 and stores retryAfter: retryAfter ?? true at 42, and my own grep over lib/, types/, test/ and docs/ on this tree returns no read of retryOpts.retryAfter or retryOptions.retryAfter anywhere — the only lib/ hits are the unrelated calculateRetryAfterHeader parameter at 12-13, the destructure and store at 30 and 42, and the header handling at 167-177, which reads headers['retry-after'] unconditionally and lets it dominate via Math.min(retryAfterHeader, maxTimeout) at 176-177. The option is declared at types/retry-handler.d.ts:95 and documented as an enable/disable switch defaulting to true at docs/docs/api/RetryHandler.md:28 and docs/docs/api/RetryAgent.md:25, so callers are told an opt-out exists that does not exist. What decided it for me was the paired timing run: with everything else held constant, retryAfter:true and retryAfter:false are indistinguishable, both pacing at the origin's number rather than the operator's. I accept medium severity as recorded: an origin — or anything able to inject a response header — sets the client's retry pacing up to maxTimeout (default 30000 ms) per attempt with no supported way to refuse, which is an availability and degraded-dependency exposure rather than a correctness or data-integrity one.

- **R2-F1** [high/strong] A resumed download silently splices bytes from a DIFFERENT representation of the resource onto the caller's partial body and reports the hybrid as a complete, successful response, whenever the first response carried no ETag or a weak one. retry() at lib/handler/retry-handler.js:330-346 attaches a precondition only when this.etag != null (335-336), and this.etag is null in exactly two ordinary cases: the origin sent no ETag at all (279 leaves it null), or it sent a weak one, which 284-290 deliberately discards. The handler's only defence against version skew is a strong ETag the origin volunteered; without one it degrades silently instead of refusing, and the two configurations are exhaustive - with a strong ETag the If-Match it sends is what makes a rotated origin answer 412, which is R1-F1's trigger, so on this tree there is no state in which a rotated resource is handled correctly. ### T10 — Verdict 2
```

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 1/3 · Findings: 1

**SHIP WITH FIXES: 1 accepted finding(s) below high severity.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [medium/strong] The `retryAfter` option is dead configuration: setting `retryAfter: false` does not disable server-directed delays, so a response with `Retry-After` can still postpone a retry by as much as `maxTimeout`. This is user-visible and can violate latency/deadline expectations.
  - Evidence: lib/handler/retry-handler.js:23-55 destructures and stores `retryAfter: retryAfter ?? true`, but lib/handler/retry-handler.js:121-175 does not destructure or test `retryOptions.retryAfter`; it unconditionally reads `headers?.['retry-after']` and uses a positive value to calculate `retryTimeout`.
  - Defender: I went looking for a reason to reject this and could not find one. What settled it was building exactly the experiment the finding named as deciding evidence and watching it fail. Three things convinced me, in order. (1) The code path is literally as described: lib/handler/retry-handler.js:20-33 destructures `retryAfter` from `retryOptions` and :42 stores `retryAfter: retryAfter ?? true` on `this.retryOpts`, but the destructuring list in the default retry callback at :128-136 (`maxRetries, minTimeout, maxTimeout, timeoutFactor, statusCodes, errorCodes, methods`) omits it, and `retryAfter` appears nowhere else in the file — `grep -n retryAfter lib/handler/retry-handler.js` returns only :12, :13, :30, :42 plus the unrelated local `retryAfterHeader`. The stored value is therefore write-only for the shipped default callback. (2) The header is read unconditionally at :167 and, when positive, wins the timeout selection at :175-178, so the stored option cannot influence the delay. (3) The intended semantics are not my inference — they are stated in the repository's own declarations: types/retry-handler.d.ts:88-95 documents `retryAfter` as 'It enables to automatically infer timeout between retries based on the `Retry-After` header. Default: true', and docs/docs/api/RetryHandler.md:28 and docs/docs/api/RetryAgent.md:25 say the same. So this is a documented option that is inert, not an undocumented internal. I also confirmed there is no test protecting the false case: the only occurrence of `retryAfter` anywhere in test/ is test/issue-5137.js:41, which sets it to `true` (the default), so the whole project suite passes with the option dead — and it does: 44/44 across test/retry-handler.js, test/retry-handler2.js, test/retry-agent.js and test/issue-5137.js. I accept at the recorded severity, medium, and I think medium is the right calibration rather than a courtesy: it is a latency/deadline defect with no correctness or data consequence, the delay is bounded by `maxTimeout` (default 30s), and a caller who needs the behaviour today can supply their own `retry` function. I do not contest `strong` support — the cited lines carry the claim on their own, before my repro. Note for scope, offered as agreement rather than qualification: the gap is confined to the shipped default callback, since a custom `retry` function still receives `retryAfter` inside `opts.retryOptions` at :109 and :386 and may honour it; that narrows who is affected but does not weaken the claim, because the default callback is what `RetryAgent` and every caller who does not pass `retry` get.
  - Proposed fix: In the default retry callback, add `retryAfter` to the `retryOptions` destructuring list (lib/handler/retry-handler.js:128-136) and gate the header read at :167 on it, e.g. `let retryAfterHeader = retryAfter === false ? undefined : headers?.['retry-after']` — or equivalently wrap the :167-173 block and fall through to the exponential branch. Falsy-but-not-false values should be treated the same way the constructor's `retryAfter ?? true` treats them, so an explicit `false` disables and anything else keeps today's behaviour. Add a test alongside test/retry-handler.js that mirrors 'Should use retry-after header for retries' but passes `retryAfter: false` with a small `minTimeout` and asserts the elapsed time is near `minTimeout`; the current suite passes with the option dead, which is why this shipped unnoticed.

_Ledger: <path>
```

### T16 — Verdict 1

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 1/3 · Findings: 3

**NO-SHIP: 1 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] RESP3 push notifications are skipped using unbounded recursion in both synchronous and asynchronous parsers. A sufficiently long sequence of pushes before an ordinary reply will exhaust Python's recursion limit, raise RecursionError, and leave the connection with unread replies, so a noisy or adversarial server can break client operations.
  - Evidence: redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:165-177 recursively calls self.read_response() after every non-requested push; redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:294-307 does the same with await self.read_response(). Neither path bounds or unwinds the recursion before reading the next push.
  - Defender: I reproduced this independently rather than reasoning from the diff. Both parsers recurse once per non-requested RESP3 push and Python does not eliminate the tail call, so the frame count grows linearly with the number of consecutive pushes preceding an ordinary reply. What changed my mind is the measured threshold: with the interpreter default recursion limit of 1000, 900 consecutive pushes followed by '+OK' still returns b'OK', and 1200 raises RecursionError, on the sync path and on the async path alike - i.e. roughly one stack frame per push, exactly as claimed. The line citations in the finding are off (the recursive calls are at redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:199-203 for the sync parser and 319-321 for the async one; the cited 165-177 / 294-307 ranges are the signature, the reader binding and the NOT_ENOUGH_DATA loop), but the substance is correct and the corrected lines make the same point. I also confirmed the escape hatch does not save the caller: push_request=True returns early, but a normal command reply is read with push_request=False, so any command issued on a connection receiving a long invalidation/pubsub burst is on the recursive path. RecursionError is neither ConnectionError nor TimeoutError, so redis/connection.py:1385-1391 catches it only via the bare `except BaseException` re-raise; the caller sees a RecursionError with unread bytes still in the parser. I do not dispute the 'high' severity: it is remotely triggerable by the peer and needs no client misuse.
  - Proposed fix: Turn the tail call into iteration. Wrap the whole gets()/NOT_ENOUGH_DATA/push-dispatch body of read_response in a `while True:` loop; where the code currently returns self.read_response(...) for a non-requested push, `continue` instead so the next reply is parsed in the same frame. That is a behaviour-preserving rewrite for every other branch and removes the stack growth entirely on both the sync and async parsers.
- **R1-F2** [medium/moderate] The synchronous can_read() disconnect check is racy: on_disconnect() can clear _sock after can_read verifies _reader, causing None to be passed to the readiness implementation and leaking TypeError or another platform-specific exception instead of the documented ConnectionError.
  - Evidence: redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:92-94 sets self._sock and self._reader to None; redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:96-103 checks only self._reader and later reads self._sock separately. redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:30-52 passes that value to sock.pending(), poller.register(), or selector.register(), none of which handles None as a closed Redis connection.
  - Defender: Verified, and on both branches the finding names. can_read() at redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:114-122 tests self._reader, then reads self._sock in a separate statement and hands it to _socket_can_read(); on_disconnect() at redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:110-112 clears both. I built the interleaving deterministically instead of racing threads - a proxy whose has_data() performs the disconnect and returns False, which is precisely the window described - and can_read() then raised builtins.TypeError ('argument must be an int, or have a fileno() method') on the poll branch and builtins.ValueError ('Invalid file object: None') on the selectors fallback, where the documented contract (and the behaviour when on_disconnect() lands before the call) is redis.exceptions.ConnectionError. Two things pushed me from 'plausible' to 'accept'. First, the same file already treats this exact hazard as real and handles it correctly two functions later: read_from_socket() binds sock and reader locally and raises ConnectionError if either is None (redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:125-132), and read_response() binds the reader for the same stated reason (170-175) - can_read() is the one entry point left with the unhardened two-step pattern. Second, the repository already carries regression tests for the hardened pair (tests/test_connection.py:296-310 parametrised over _reader and _sock, and :312-325) and none for can_read(), so the gap is an omission rather than a deliberate exclusion. Severity medium is right: it needs a concurrent disconnect to land in a narrow window, but the consequence is a non-retryable TypeError/ValueError surfacing where callers are written to expect ConnectionError.
  - Proposed fix: Mirror read_from_socket(): at the top of can_read(), bind `reader = self._reader` and `sock = self._sock` once, raise ConnectionError(SERVER_CLOSED_CONNECTION_ERROR) if either is None, and pass the local sock to _socket_can_read(). Optionally also make _socket_can_read() treat a None sock as a closed connection, so no future caller can reintroduce the leak. Extend the existing parametrised regression test in tests/test_connection.py to cover can_read() with _sock cleared, on both the poll and selectors branches.
- **R1-F3** [medium/moderate] A concurrent socket close can cause read_from_socket()'s timeout-restoration cleanup to mask the intended retryable ConnectionError or TimeoutError with a raw OSError. The code explicitly anticipates concurrent disconnect, but unconditionally calls settimeout() on the locally retained, potentially already-closed socket in finally.
  - Evidence: redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:108-114 binds the socket locally and documents concurrent disconnect handling; redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:119-141 converts EOF, socket.timeout, and nonblocking failures into Redis exceptions; redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:142-144 unconditionally executes sock.settimeout(self._socket_timeout) for custom timeouts, with no handling for a socket closed concurrently.
  - Defender: The mechanism is real and I reproduced all three of the cases it can eat. First the primitive: settimeout() on a socket whose fd has been closed raises OSError [Errno 9] Bad file descriptor (a merely shutdown() socket does not - only close()/detach() does, which is what Connection.disconnect() performs at redis/connection.py:1221-1226). Then end to end: driving read_from_socket(timeout=1.0) with the socket closed during recv_into - the concurrent disconnect landing in the window the finding names - the intended exception is destroyed in every case. EOF (bufflen == 0, which should raise ConnectionError), socket.timeout (which should raise redis TimeoutError) and BlockingIOError EAGAIN (which should raise redis ConnectionError) all emerge instead as OSError [Errno 9] from the `finally: sock.settimeout(self._socket_timeout)` at redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:160-162. The inconsistency the finding leans on is genuine: this is the one function in the file that explicitly documents concurrent disconnect and guards its entry (redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:125-132) while leaving its own cleanup unguarded. One thing I checked that cuts partly against the finding and that I record rather than trade on: the raw OSError does not reach application code unchanged, because Connection.read_response() catches OSError at redis/connection.py:1382-1384 and re-raises it as a redis ConnectionError. The practical damage is therefore narrower than 'a raw OSError escapes' - it is the substitution of ConnectionError for TimeoutError plus the loss of the original diagnostic - and it is felt most in the custom-timeout caller, PubSub.parse_response at redis/client.py:1262-1264, which is the only production path that passes an explicit timeout and so the only one where the finally block runs at all. I am not using that to argue the severity down: medium is defensible for a cleanup path that can convert a timeout into a connection error under concurrent close, and the claim as written - that the cleanup masks the intended exception - is exactly what I measured.
  - Proposed fix: Make the restore best-effort so it can never displace the in-flight exception: `finally: if custom_timeout: try: sock.settimeout(self._socket_timeout) except OSError: pass`. Restoring the timeout on a socket that is already closed has no value, so suppressing the failure loses nothing. The identical `finally: sock.settimeout(...)` in SocketBuffer._read_from_socket at redis/_parsers/socket.py:90-93 has the same exposure and should get the same guard, since a fix applied only to the hiredis parser leaves the pure-Python parser masking the same exceptions.

_Ledger: <path>
```

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 5

**NO-SHIP: 3 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/strong] Both read_response() implementations drain RESP3 push notifications by tail-recursing into themselves instead of looping, so stack depth grows one frame per buffered push. Fewer than 1000 pushes queued ahead of a command reply on a RESP3 connection (client-side-caching invalidations, pubsub traffic, or a maintenance-notification burst) turns an ordinary command into an uncaught RecursionError. RecursionError is not a redis ConnectionError/TimeoutError, so redis.retry.Retry will not retry it, and Connection.read_response's `except BaseException` handler disconnects the connection on the way out - the caller loses both the command and the connection, and the failure mode is load-dependent so it will not show up in any unit test. The fix is mechanical: reuse the existing `while response is NOT_ENOUGH_DATA` cycle so push draining costs O(1) stack.
  - Evidence: redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:196-203 (sync): after `response = self.handle_push_response(response)`, the non-push_request path executes `return self.read_response(disable_decoding=disable_decoding, push_request=push_request, timeout=timeout)`.
redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:317-321 (async): identical shape, `return await self.read_response(...)`.
Repro driving the REAL hiredis 3.4.1 Reader with REAL RESP3 frames (N `>2 invalidate` push frames followed by `+OK`): <path>
Captured log: <path>
  parser module under test: <seat>/repo/redis/_parsers/hiredis.py   hiredis 3.4.1, CPython 3.11.15, sys.getrecursionlimit() = 1000
  === SYNC _HiredisParser.read_response() ===
       900 buffered pushes -> ok -> b'OK'
      1000 buffered pushes -> RecursionError: maximum recursion depth exceeded in comparison
     10000 buffered pushes -> RecursionError: maximum recursion depth exceeded in comparison
  === ASYNC _AsyncHiredisParser.read_response() ===  (same thresholds: 900 ok, 1000+ RecursionError)
  === control ===  iterative consumption of 10000 pushes: OK after 10000 pushes
The control run proves the payload itself is well-formed and that only the recursion imposes the limit.
Blast radius: redis/connection.py:1386-1392 - `except BaseException: if disconnect_on_error: self.disconnect(); raise`. Normal commands reach the recursive branch because Connection.read_response passes push_request=False by default (redis/connection.py:1368-1373).
  - Defender: Verified by reading and by an independently written experiment, not by taking the critic's word. redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:193-203 (sync) and :314-323 (async) both tail-call read_response() after handle_push_response() on the non-push_request path, so each buffered push costs one Python frame while the NOT_ENOUGH_DATA loop directly above it (lines 182-187 / 302-307) already provides an O(1)-stack cycle that could have absorbed it. What changed nothing in my mind but confirmed the claim is my own run: with a REAL hiredis.Reader fed real RESP3 `>2 invalidate` frames, the sync and async parsers both return normally at 900 buffered pushes and both die with RecursionError at 999 and above, while a purely iterative drain of the identical byte payload consumes 10000 pushes and returns b'OK'. So the payload is well-formed and only the recursion imposes the ceiling. I also confirmed the blast-radius half of the claim by type check rather than by assertion: RecursionError is not a subclass of redis.ConnectionError, redis.TimeoutError, or OSError, so redis.retry.Retry will not retry it and Connection.read_response falls into `except BaseException: if disconnect_on_error: self.disconnect(); raise` (redis/connection.py:1385-1391), losing both the command and the connection. Reachable on ordinary commands because Connection.read_response passes push_request=False by default. I accept at severity high as claimed and do not contest the support level. One honest narrowing that does not change the verdict: the threshold is per read_response() call, so it needs ~1000 pushes queued ahead of a single reply, not ~1000 over the connection's lifetime; the critic's own framing already says this.
  - Proposed fix: Drain pushes in the existing cycle instead of recursing. In both read_response() implementations, wrap the gets()/read_from_socket() sequence in an outer `while True:` and, on a non-push_request push, call handle_push_response() and `continue` rather than re-entering read_response(). Stack cost then becomes O(1) in the number of buffered pushes.
- **R1-F2** [high/strong] _socket_can_read() converts the caller's timeout to poll(2) milliseconds with `timeout * 1000` and passes it straight to select.poll().poll(). poll(2) treats ANY negative millisecond value as 'wait forever', so a negative timeout blocks the calling thread indefinitely with no error, no timeout and no log entry - on every POSIX platform, since _HAS_POLL is the production branch. Every sibling path in the same library rejects the identical input immediately: the selectors fallback returns False in a millisecond, and the non-hiredis parser raises ValueError from sock.settimeout(). The value arrives unfiltered from a public API (PubSub.get_message(timeout=T) -> parse_response(block=False, timeout=T) -> conn.can_read(timeout=T)); get_message clamps with max(0.0, ...) only on the not-yet-subscribed branch, so once subscribed the caller's value passes through untouched. An unbounded silent hang is strictly worse than the loud ValueError the other backends produce. Fix: clamp with `timeout = max(0.0, timeout)` (or raise) inside _socket_can_read before the millisecond conversion.
  - Evidence: redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:28  `_HAS_POLL = hasattr(select, "poll")`  (True on Linux and macOS).
redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:43-49  `poller = select.poll(); poller.register(sock, select.POLLIN); poll_timeout = None if timeout is None else timeout * 1000; return bool(poller.poll(poll_timeout))` - negative is never screened; only `None` is special-cased.
Measured end-to-end through a REAL redis.connection.Connection wired to a socketpair (no server required): <path>
Log <path>
  hiredis parser (_HiredisParser)    timeout=0      -> returned False
  python parser  (_RESP3Parser)      timeout=0      -> returned False
  hiredis parser (_HiredisParser)    timeout=-0.1   -> STILL BLOCKED after 3.0s
  python parser  (_RESP3Parser)      timeout=-0.1   -> ValueError: Timeout value out of range
Unit-level confirmation of the branch asymmetry, <path>
  poll branch      -> STILL BLOCKED after 2.0s (no data will ever come)
  selectors branch -> returned False in 0.001s
  sock.settimeout(-0.5) -> raised ValueError: Timeout value out of range
Reachability: redis/client.py:1253 `if not conn.can_read(timeout=timeout):` inside PubSub.parse_response(block=False); fed by redis/client.py:1477 `self.parse_response(block=(timeout is None), timeout=timeout)` in get_message(); the only clamp, redis/client.py:1471 `timeout = max(0.0, timeout - time_spent)`, sits on the `if not self.subscribed` branch and is skipped once subscribed. redis/connection.py:1348-1353 forwards the value to the parser unmodified.
  - Defender: Confirmed by reading and by my own end-to-end measurement. redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:48 computes `poll_timeout = None if timeout is None else timeout * 1000` and line 49 hands it to select.poll().poll(); only None is special-cased, and poll(2) treats any negative millisecond value as 'block forever'. _HAS_POLL is True on this platform (line 28), so this is the production branch, not a fallback. I did not rely on the critic's numbers: I wired a real redis.Connection to a socketpair that never receives data and drove Connection.can_read() through a real _HiredisParser and a real _RESP3Parser, each call on a watchdog thread joined with a wall-clock budget. timeout=0 and timeout=-0.0 return False immediately on both; timeout=-0.1 and timeout=-1 are still blocked after 3s on the hiredis path, while the _RESP3Parser path raises ValueError('Timeout value out of range') in under a millisecond and the selectors branch (with _HAS_POLL patched False) returns False in 0.000s for the same input. That is the asymmetry the finding describes, measured three ways. I also re-walked the reachability chain in the source rather than trusting it: PubSub.get_message (redis/client.py:1477) calls parse_response(block=(timeout is None), timeout=timeout); the only clamp, `timeout = max(0.0, timeout - time_spent)` at redis/client.py:1471, is inside the `if not self.subscribed:` branch and is skipped once subscribed; parse_response then reaches `if not conn.can_read(timeout=timeout)` at redis/client.py:1253, and Connection.can_read (redis/connection.py:1337-1348) forwards the value to the parser unmodified. So no guard exists on that path, which is exactly the deciding evidence the critic asked for and it comes out in the critic's favour. Accepted at severity high: an unbounded, silent, unloggable block of the calling thread is worse than the loud ValueError every sibling path produces, and a negative value is what ordinary deadline arithmetic (deadline - now) yields. I do not contest the support level. Narrowing worth recording: it requires the caller to supply a negative timeout, so this is not a spontaneous failure - but the library elsewhere treats that input as an error rather than as permission to hang.
  - Proposed fix: In _socket_can_read (redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:31-52), before the branch: `if timeout is not None and timeout < 0: timeout = 0.0`, so both backends agree that an elapsed deadline is a non-blocking check. If a loud failure is preferred instead, raise ValueError there so the hiredis path matches what sock.settimeout() already does on the pure-Python path - but do not leave 'block forever' as the meaning of a negative timeout.
- **R1-F3** [high/moderate] read_response() binds `reader = self._reader` once before its read loop, but read_from_socket() re-binds `self._reader` and `self._sock` from the instance on EVERY call. If the connection is disconnected and then reconnected between two iterations of the loop - the exact scenario the module's own comments name as the reason for the local binding - on_connect() installs a brand-new hiredis.Reader and a new socket, and from then on read_from_socket() feeds the NEW reader while the loop keeps polling the STALE one. The stale reader can never complete, so the loop cannot terminate: with redis-py's default socket_timeout=None it blocks forever, and while blocked it drains every byte off the reconnected socket into a reader nobody is reading, leaving the live connection's parser desynchronised in a protocol-error state. The defensive local binding therefore converts a loud, immediately diagnosable AttributeError into a silent hang plus stream corruption on a connection that is otherwise healthy. Fix: after read_from_socket() returns, verify `self._reader is reader` and raise ConnectionError(SERVER_CLOSED_CONNECTION_ERROR) otherwise - a reconnect underneath an in-flight read is not resumable and must not be papered over.
  - Evidence: redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:173  `reader = self._reader` - bound once, before the loop.
redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:182-187  `while response is NOT_ENOUGH_DATA: self.read_from_socket(timeout=timeout); response = reader.gets(...)` - the loop condition always consults the ONCE-bound reader.
redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:125-126, :140  read_from_socket re-reads `sock = self._sock; reader = self._reader` on every call and feeds THAT reader.
redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:110-112 (on_disconnect) and :91, :102 (on_connect installs a new socket and `self._reader = hiredis.Reader(**kwargs)`) - the two bindings provably diverge across a reconnect.
The code asserts this threat model itself: redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:127-130 "Another thread may disconnect this connection while we are here (e.g. a shared client closed via `with redis:`)" and :170-172 "Bind the reader locally so a concurrent disconnect ... can't turn a later .gets() into an AttributeError".

Note the three `+PONG` replies that arrived on the reconnected socket were consumed and thrown away - they were never returned to any caller.

What changed my mind is a distinction I re-derived myself rather than taking on trust: WHEN the disconnect lands relative to read_from_socket's entry. Round 1 measured only the case where on_disconnect() completes BEFORE read_from_socket is entered, where the guard at redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:131-132 fires and both arms give ConnectionError - and then generalised from it. The core defect (read_from_socket feeding a NEW reader across a reconnect while the loop polls the STALE one, so the loop cannot terminate) was already accepted in round 1 on my own measurement and I accept it again at severity high. On reachability I also checked the escalation at source level rather than accepting the assertion: `grep -n "_lock" redis/client.py` shows PubSub._lock created at :1004 and taken at exactly one site, :1099 (execute_command), which is off the read path (:1251-1263 try_read -> conn.can_read / conn.read_response under self._execute at :1267), while `grep -n "connection.disconnect()\|conn.disconnect()" redis/client.py` shows disconnect called with no lock at :1033 (reset), :1145 (the last statement of _reconnect, reached unconditionally from _execute at :1157 when conn.should_reconnect() is set) and :1735. The minimal form the critic proposes - after `self.read_from_socket(timeout=timeout)` at redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:183 add `if self._reader is not reader: raise ConnectionError(SERVER_CLOSED_CONNECTION_ERROR)` - stops the non-terminating loop, and I support it. It does not stop read_from_socket from having already consumed a chunk off the RECONNECTED socket and fed it to the new reader (my round-1 predecessor measured whole replies being drained and discarded that way). The stronger form is to make read_from_socket operate on the sock/reader the caller bound - pass them in, or have it re-check `self._sock is sock and self._reader is reader` before recv_into at :137 - so a reconnect underneath an in-flight read fails before it can touch the new connection's stream. The async parser at :302-307 reads self._reader every iteration and read_from_socket feeds self._reader, so the two never diverge and no AttributeError arises there; a reconnect mid-loop would instead silently splice the new connection's bytes into the in-flight reply, so the same identity check belongs there for that reason rather than the one stated.

The raw TypeError/ValueError escapes ConnectionPool.get_connection() to application code and no reconnect is attempted, so a transient teardown race surfaces as a non-retryable programming-error exception instead of the intended reconnect. Fix: bind sock and reader locally in can_read and raise ConnectionError(SERVER_CLOSED_CONNECTION_ERROR) when either is None, exactly as read_from_socket already does.

- **R2-F1** [medium/moderate] read_response()'s `timeout` parameter is not a deadline for the call, only a per-recv socket timeout that is restarted on every loop iteration, so a caller that asks for a bounded read can be blocked without limit and without any error. read_from_socket() applies sock.settimeout(timeout) afresh on each invocation and the NOT_ENOUGH_DATA loop invokes it repeatedly; nothing tracks elapsed time. Any peer that delivers at least one byte more often than once per `timeout` therefore never trips it. That breaks the documented contract of the only public API that supplies this parameter: PubSub.get_message(timeout=T) promises "the system will wait for `timeout` seconds before returning" and reaches the parser through parse_response -> Connection.read_response(timeout=T). A reply spanning several recv calls is enough - a pubsub payload larger than socket_read_size, or a congested or hostile link - and the stall is silent: no exception, no log, no metric. Because get_message is also what PubSubWorkerThread.run() polls in order to notice its stop flag, a stalled read defers PubSubWorkerThread.stop() by the same unbounded amount. I am claiming the call-level semantics are wrong, not that any single recv misbehaves - the per-recv timeout works, as my control shows. Fix: take one monotonic deadline on entry to read_response() and pass the remaining budget to each read_from_socket() call, raising TimeoutError when it is exhausted.
  - Evidence: redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:182-187 - `while response is NOT_ENOUGH_DATA: self.read_from_socket(timeout=timeout); response = reader.gets()` - the same `timeout` value is handed to every iteration.
redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:133-136 - `custom_timeout = timeout is not SENTINEL` / `if custom_timeout: sock.settimeout(timeout)` - a fresh full budget per recv; :160-162 restores self._socket_timeout in the finally. No elapsed-time bookkeeping anywhere in the class.
redis/client.py:1453-1462 get_message docstring: "If timeout is specified, the system will wait for `timeout` seconds before returning."; :1477 `self.parse_response(block=(timeout is None), timeout=timeout)`; :1251-1263 try_read sets read_timeout = timeout and calls `conn.read_response(disconnect_on_error=False, push_request=True, timeout=read_timeout)`; redis/connection.py:1355-1372 forwards it to the parser unchanged.

WHAT WAS ALREADY AGREED AND STILL HOLDS (my X1). read_from_socket applies sock.settimeout(timeout) afresh on every call (redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:150-151) and the NOT_ENOUGH_DATA loop at :197-202 hands the same value to every iteration with no elapsed-time bookkeeping anywhere in the class. Against a peer delivering one byte every 0.040s with a caller-supplied timeout of 0.050s, Connection.read_response was still blocked when my wall-clock watchdog expired at 4.0s - 80x the requested timeout, 85 recv calls, with the socket timeout in force at each recv measured as 0.05. My positive control (one recv slower than the timeout) raised redis.TimeoutError in 0.058s, so the per-recv path is intact and only the accumulation is unbounded.

WHAT CHANGED MY MIND. My predecessor's rejected component (a) was that the call-level semantics are not this parser's, they are the library's existing cross-parser contract, so the finding is misattributed. The critic's round-3 evidence is that half of the same library means the other thing, and I rebuilt it rather than trusting it. ARM C, reproduced independently: async redis.asyncio.connection.Connection.read_response(timeout=0.050) with protocol 3, a real _AsyncHiredisParser and a real asyncio.StreamReader fed by the identical dribble, returned None in 0.052s (1.0x) with disconnect_on_error False AND True; conn._reader stayed bound and parser._connected stayed True; and a resume on the SAME connection then returned the full 1000-byte body - the partial reply survived the deadline. That is the critic's round-3 result, obtained from scratch on my side. I then ran the end-to-end comparison neither seat had run, which is what actually settles it for me: with one identical 1-byte-per-0.050s peer and one identical caller timeout of 0.100s, sync PubSub.get_message over a REAL socketpair was still blocked after 5.0s (50x, peer had delivered 87 of 2034 bytes, no exception and no log), while async PubSub.get_message returned None in 0.102s (1.0x) and recovered the complete payload on the next call. Both APIs carry the SAME docstring, which I read verbatim in the checkout rather than taking from the ledger: redis/client.py:1219-1222 and redis/asyncio/client.py:1246-1248 both say 'When block=False: timeout is USED, method returns after timeout expires', both parse_response docstrings promise 'None if no message is available within the timeout period', and both get_message docstrings say 'the system will wait for `timeout` seconds before returning'. So 'this is simply what timeout has always meant here' is not sustainable: it is what it means on the sync side only, and the documented meaning is the one the async side implements.

WHAT SURVIVES OF THE ROUND-2 COUNTER-EVIDENCE, AND WHAT IT ACTUALLY PROVES. I reproduced it too, and it cuts my own way only in a narrower sense than it was used for. Driving the identical dribble through the pure-python parsers with hiredis removed from the picture: _RESP2Parser blocked 80x with 86 recv calls, and _RESP3Parser blocked 80x with 86 recv calls; the structure is the same by construction (redis/_parsers/socket.py:45-92 re-applies settimeout per call and :110-117 loops over it). That proves the remedy cannot land in the file under review alone without making the two sync parsers disagree about a documented public parameter - it does not prove there is no defect. The critic's round-3 remedy now says exactly that ('make it a library-wide change ... not a deadline bolted onto this parser'), so the scope disagreement is over rather than unresolved.

I ALSO RETIRED MY OWN REJECTED COMPONENT (b) WITH AN EXPERIMENT THAT CUT AGAINST ME. Round 2 rejected the remedy because a deadline would convert a legitimately slow but successful large pubsub read into a redis.TimeoutError and, under retry_on_timeout, into a dropped connection and a lost message. I tested whether the non-destructive form is actually available on the sync side: after a sync read_response aborted mid-reply with redis.TimeoutError having fed only 64 of 1009 bytes, a second read_response on the SAME parser and the SAME hiredis Reader returned the full 1000-byte body. The partial reply is retained on the sync side exactly as it is on the async side, so 'return no-complete-message-yet and resume next call' is implementable, and PubSub.parse_response already passes disconnect_on_error=False (redis/client.py:1262-1264). The concrete harm I had objected to is avoidable, so the objection falls.

SUB-CLAIMS I CHECKED RATHER THAN ASSUMED. PubSubWorkerThread.stop() (redis/client.py:1656-1659) only clears the running flag - its own comment says 'the run loop will close the pubsub connection' - so the loop must return from get_message to notice it; the deferred-stop consequence is real. The narrowing both seats have already recorded also stands and I do not want it lost: with a silent peer, get_message(timeout=0.1) returned None in 0.101s, because conn.can_read(timeout) does bound the wait for data to ARRIVE; the unbounded region is only a reply already in flight, which needs a reply spanning several recv calls and a peer slower than the reply but faster than the timeout.

SEVERITY AND SUPPORT. I accept at the claimed severity (medium) and I do not argue it down. I do not contest the claimed support level of moderate, though I record that the evidence is deterministic, needs no race, and now reproduces end-to-end through the public API in two independently written harnesses.
  - Proposed fix: Adopt the critic's narrowed remedy, and pick ONE of its two branches deliberately rather than drifting between them.

Branch 1 (behaviour). Introduce a single monotonic deadline on entry to the read and pass the REMAINING budget to each socket read, in BOTH sync read paths so the two parsers keep agreeing about a documented public parameter: redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:197-202 feeding redis/_parsers/hiredis.py:139-177, and redis/_parsers/socket.py:45-92 / :110-117. Surface exhaustion the way the async arm already does rather than as an error: my x2 Q2 run shows the sync hiredis Reader retains the partial reply after an aborted mid-reply read and a later call completes it, and PubSub.parse_response already passes disconnect_on_error=False (redis/client.py:1262-1264), so get_message can return None on exhaustion and resume on the next call. Do NOT ship the form in the claim's own 'Fix:' sentence - raising TimeoutError on exhaustion - because with retry_on_timeout=True that reaches PubSub._execute's failure_callback (redis/client.py:1170-1172) whose _reconnect ends in conn.disconnect(); conn.connect() (:1145-1146), dropping the connection and the partially-read message. The inner recv loop in redis/_parsers/socket.py:63-72 must also be made deadline-aware, since it can spin without returning to the caller.

Branch 2 (documentation, if branch 1 is too invasive for this change). Correct the promise instead of the code, in all four places that make it: redis/client.py:1219-1222 and :1456-1462, and redis/asyncio/client.py:1246-1248 and :1459-1463, to say that on the sync path the value bounds the wait for data to arrive and each individual socket read, not the whole call - and say explicitly that the async path does bound the whole call and returns None. Leaving the two arms with identical docstrings and 1.0x versus 50x behaviour is the one outcome that should not survive review.

Either way this is not a change to the file under review alone, and the reviewer's action item should be recorded that way.
```
