```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 4

**NO-SHIP: 4 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F2** [high/strong] `Reply.prototype.hijack` permanently disables client-disconnect detection for the request. Beyond clearing the handler-timeout timer, it also removes the `close` listener that aborts the request's AbortSignal and nulls the stored reference (lib/reply.js:129-136), so after `reply.hijack()` the signal never aborts when the peer goes away. This contradicts the repository's own documented contract for `request.signal` - docs/Reference/Request.md:45-46, 'An `AbortSignal` that aborts when the handler timeout fires or the client disconnects' - and the hijack documentation only claims that the *timer* is cleared (docs/Reference/Server.md:259-261, docs/Reference/Lifecycle.md:47-48); nothing documents the loss of disconnect abort. Hijack is precisely the mechanism used for long-lived upgraded connections (WebSocket, SSE, raw streaming), which is the one case where disconnect-driven cancellation is load-bearing: a handler that awaits `request.signal` to release a database cursor, upstream fetch, or subscription now waits forever, so every dropped hijacked connection leaks the handler's pending work and whatever it holds. The removal is unconditional whenever `kRequestSignal` is set, which with `handlerTimeout` configured is every request on the route (lib/route.js:534-536), and without it is every request whose handler read `request.signal` before hijacking (lib/request.js:215-227).
  - Evidence: Code: lib/reply.js:126-138, specifically `if (this.request[kRequestSignal]) { clearTimeout(...); this.request[kTimeoutTimer] = null; if (this.request[kOnAbort]) { this.request.raw.removeListener('close', this.request[kOnAbort]); this.request[kOnAbort] = null } }`. The listener being removed is the abort trigger: lib/request.js:215-227 registers `this.raw.on('close', onAbort)` where `onAbort` calls `ac.abort()`, and lib/route.js:547-554 registers the eager equivalent. Documented contract: docs/Reference/Request.md:45-46 ('aborts when the handler timeout fires or the client disconnects'); docs/Reference/Server.md:259-261 and docs/Reference/Lifecycle.md:47-48 mention only the timer being cleared on hijack.

EXECUTED REPRO (scratch env, checkout unmodified, loaded by absolute path with NODE_PATH at the scratch modules; source archived at /Users/michaeltraw/Dev/council-bench/_scratch/<seat>/out/e4-hijack-signal.js). The handler reads `request.signal`, attaches an 'abort' listener, optionally calls `reply.hijack()`, and never responds; a raw socket client sends a request, waits 250 ms, then destroys the socket; 400 ms later the abort state is printed.

Output (node v25.6.1):
  RESOLVED reply: <path>
  RESULT no handlerTimeout, NO hijack : request.signal -> ABORTED
  RESULT no handlerTimeout, hijack()  : request.signal -> no-abort
  RESULT handlerTimeout,    NO hijack : request.signal -> ABORTED
  RESULT handlerTimeout,    hijack()  : request.signal -> no-abort

The only variable between the ABORTED and no-abort rows is the `reply.hijack()` call, i.e. the block at lib/reply.js:129-136. Not covered by the project suite: the full upstream run from a scratch copy is green ('tests 2241 / pass 2237 / fail 0 / skipped 4'), and test/handler-timeout.test.js asserts hijack clears `kTimeoutTimer`/`kOnAbort` bookkeeping without ever asserting that disconnect still aborts the signal afterwards.
  - Defender: I rebuilt the experiment from the description and it reproduces exactly, and reading the code shows there is no recovery path, which is what settled it for me. `Reply.prototype.hijack` (lib/reply.js:126-138) removes the `close` listener and nulls `kOnAbort`, but leaves `kRequestSignal` set. The `signal` getter (lib/request.js:215-227) short-circuits on a cached controller - `let ac = this[kRequestSignal]; if (ac) return ac.signal` - so a handler that reads `request.signal` again after hijacking gets back the same controller with no listener attached and no way to re-register. `setupResponseListeners`'s cleanup (lib/reply.js:954-962) only runs on raw 'finish'/'error', which a hijacked long-lived connection never emits. So after hijack the disconnect abort is gone for good. My four-cell matrix reproduces the filed result verbatim, and an additional ordering probe shows the critique's scoping is accurate rather than overstated: the only surviving combination is 'no handlerTimeout AND `request.signal` never read before hijack', which is precisely the case where `kRequestSignal` is unset when `hijack()` runs. With `handlerTimeout` configured (lib/route.js:534-536 sets `kRequestSignal` on every request) disconnect abort is lost regardless of ordering. The documented contract is unambiguous and carves out no exception - docs/Reference/Request.md:45-53 states the signal 'aborts when the handler timeout fires or the client disconnects' and goes on to say 'on client disconnect it is a generic AbortError', while docs/Reference/Server.md:258-259 and docs/Reference/Lifecycle.md:46-48 mention only the timer being cleared on hijack. I accept at the filed severity of `high`: it is silent, it violates a written contract, and it removes cancellation in exactly the long-lived-connection scenario the API exists to serve. I do not contest the `strong` support level.
  - Proposed fix: In lib/reply.js:128-137, clear only the timeout timer on hijack and leave the disconnect abort wired: keep `clearTimeout(this.request[kTimeoutTimer]); this.request[kTimeoutTimer] = null`, and drop the `removeListener('close', kOnAbort)` / `kOnAbort = null` block. The route-level `onAbort` (lib/route.js:547-554) also calls `clearTimeout` on an already-cleared timer, which is harmless. If the maintainers decide the current behaviour is intentional, the alternative fix is documentation: state the hijack carve-out in docs/Reference/Request.md alongside the `signal` contract and in the `.hijack()` section of docs/Reference/Reply.md, since today they promise the opposite. Proposal only - Review mode applies nothing.
- **R1-F3** [critical/strong] The handler-timeout crash is not confined to the HTTP/2 chunked write path: the same unhandled-exception process kill is remotely triggerable on plain HTTP/1 by any route that replies with a stream. The defect is the pairing of two things. First, the timer at lib/route.js:538-545 guards on `if (!reply.sent)`, and `reply.sent` (lib/reply.js:99-105) reports response COMPLETION (`raw.writableEnded`), not response COMMITMENT (`raw.headersSent`). Second, the fallback error path at lib/error-handler.js:38-46 wraps its first `writeHead` in try/catch but its retry `reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode)` inside the catch block is unguarded, so when headers are already committed that retry throws synchronously out of the timer callback and nothing catches it. Every stream reply opens the window, because `sendStream` sets headers and calls `payload.pipe(res)` (lib/reply.js:812-865) with `res.end()` deferred until the source ends - `writePayload`/`writeHttp2Payload` (lib/reply.js:684-727) is never entered for a stream, so the HTTP/2 chunk-size logic is not involved in any way. Two ordinary triggers both work with `handlerTimeout` configured: a source slower than the timeout, and a client that simply stops reading (backpressure holds the pipe open). This is filed separately from R1-F1 because a fix scoped to R1-F1's stated mechanism - the 64 KiB threshold, the HTTP/2 branch, the drain deferral - does not touch the HTTP/1 stream case, and would leave a remote whole-process crash live for exactly the workloads (file serving, proxying, long responses) most likely to configure a handler timeout in the first place.
  - Evidence: Code: lib/reply.js:99-105 (`sent` getter returns `(this[kReplyHijacked] || this.raw.writableEnded) === true`); lib/reply.js:653-655 (`if (typeof payload.pipe === 'function') { sendStream(payload, res, reply); return }` - dispatched BEFORE the `writePayload` call at lib/reply.js:681, so the chunking path is unreachable for streams); lib/reply.js:812-865 (`sendStream` sets headers via `res.setHeader` then `payload.pipe(res)` at line 864; `res.end` arrives only via pipe completion or `sendStreamTrailer` at lib/reply.js:924-926); lib/route.js:538-545 (timer body `if (!reply.sent) { ... reply.send(err) }`); lib/error-handler.js:36-46 (`try { reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode, reply[kReplyHeaders]) } catch (error) { ...log...; reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode) }` - the retry is not guarded by `reply.raw.headersSent`).

EXECUTED REPRO. Scratch environment at /Users/michaeltraw/Dev/council-bench/_scratch/<seat>; deps installed from a copy of the checkout's own package.json (no lockfile in the checkout, so resolution is by range - resolved versions in repro/logs/npm-ls-depth0.txt); the module under review is loaded from the checkout by absolute path with NODE_PATH pointed at the scratch modules, and each script prints `require.resolve` at runtime. The checkout was never modified and never installed into. Scripts, commands, versions and captured output archived at <path> (README.md, scripts/d-stream-scope.js, scripts/g-stream-stalled-client.js, scripts/rerun.sh, logs/clean-rerun.log).

scripts/d-stream-scope.js: Fastify({ http2: <mode>, handlerTimeout: 300 }); GET /slowstream replies with a Readable that pushes 1 KiB once (committing headers) and then never pushes or ends; the client reads promptly; state is printed at t+200 ms; `process.on('uncaughtException')` prints and exits 7, otherwise it prints SURVIVED and exits 0.

Command: NODE_PATH=$SCRATCH/env/node_modules node scripts/d-stream-scope.js http1
Output:
  RESOLVED reply: <path>
  node v25.6.1
  client status = 200
  [t+200ms] raw.headersSent=true raw.writableEnded=false reply.sent=false
  !!! UNCAUGHT: ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT Cannot write headers after they are sent to the client
  exit=7

Sampled across every Node major the project's CI declares (.github/workflows/ci.yml `node-version: [20, 22, 24, 26]`), binaries installed into the scratch directory; all four reproduce, plus the host runtime:
  v20.20.2 -> !!! UNCAUGHT: ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT, exit=7
  v22.23.2 -> !!! UNCAUGHT: ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT, exit=7
  v24.19.0 -> !!! UNCAUGHT: ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT, exit=7
  v26.7.0  -> !!! UNCAUGHT: ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT, exit=7
  v25.6.1  -> !!! UNCAUGHT: ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT, exit=7

Second trigger, no slow source needed - fast producer, stalled reader (scripts/g-stream-stalled-client.js, 32 MiB stream in 64 KiB pushes, client pauses on response):
  node g-stream-stalled-client.js http1 -> !!! UNCAUGHT: ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT, exit=7
  node g-stream-stalled-client.js http2 -> !!! UNCAUGHT: ERR_HTTP2_HEADERS_SENT, exit=7

CONTROL showing the chunking path is not implicated: the same buffered-payload script on HTTP/1 at 32 MiB survives (`node scripts/a-timeout.js http1 33554432 300` -> SURVIVED 3s, no crash, exit=0), because for a buffered payload `res.write` + `res.end` both run synchronously and `writableEnded` is true before the timer fires. The difference between that control and the stream case is solely whether `res.end()` is deferred - not whether the HTTP/2 64 KiB chunking code ran.

Not covered by the project suite: I ran the full upstream suite myself from a scratch rsync copy of the checkout with the project's configured runner (`node node_modules/.bin/borp`) and it is green - `tests 2241 / suites 20 / pass 2237 / fail 0 / cancelled 0 / skipped 4 / todo 0`, exit 0 (repro/logs/upstream-suite.log). No test combines `handlerTimeout` with a stream reply that has not finished flushing.
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: Agreed on where the fix belongs, with one addition my fixC runs showed is necessary. (1) Stop the timer from initiating a second response on a committed reply: at lib/route.js:539 test commitment, not completion - `if (!reply.sent && !reply.raw.headersSent)`, or set a kReplyCommitted flag in `safeWriteHead` and before `payload.pipe(res)` in `sendStream` / the header loop in `sendWebStream`, and test that. (2) Guard lib/error-handler.js:36-47 as a whole, not just the retry at line 45: when `reply.raw.headersSent` is true, do not writeHead, do not `reply.raw.end(payload)`, and abort the transfer in a peer-detectable way - HTTP/1 `reply.raw.destroy()` (ECONNRESET), HTTP/2 `reply.request.raw.stream.close(NGHTTP2_INTERNAL_ERROR)` rather than `res.destroy()`, which my runs show closes the HTTP/2 stream cleanly and makes the truncation invisible. (3) Decide the documented meaning of `reply.sent` (lib/reply.js:99-105 vs docs/Reference/Reply.md:614-619) - as a guard against sending twice it should be true once the response is committed. (1)+(2) together are the minimum; (2) alone is not, per R2-F1. Proposal only - Review mode applies nothing.
- **R1-F1** [critical/strong] The HTTP/2 chunked write path makes `reply.sent` report false while the response is already committed on the wire, which turns any configured `handlerTimeout` into a remotely triggerable, unhandled process crash. `onSendEnd` commits the response with `safeWriteHead` (lib/reply.js:679) and then calls `writePayload` (lib/reply.js:681). For an HTTP/2 reply whose serialized payload exceeds 64 KiB, `writePayload` hands off to `writeHttp2Payload` (lib/reply.js:688-727), which writes in 64 KiB chunks and, on backpressure, defers the rest to `res.once('drain', writeChunk)`; `res.end()` is only reached later, via `done()` -> `sendTrailer` (lib/reply.js:867-872). Until then `Reply.prototype.sent` (lib/reply.js:99-105) is `this[kReplyHijacked] || this.raw.writableEnded`, i.e. false. The handler-timeout timer (lib/route.js:538-545) tests exactly that flag: `if (!reply.sent) { ... reply.send(err) }`. So a client that simply reads slowly keeps the flush open past the timeout, the timer fires on a response whose headers and body bytes have already gone out, and the second send walks `reply.send` -> `onErrorHook` (lib/reply.js:940) -> `handleError` -> `fallbackErrorHandler`, whose retry `reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode)` in the catch block (lib/error-handler.js:45) is not itself guarded and throws `ERR_HTTP2_HEADERS_SENT` out of a timer callback -> uncaught exception -> whole-process exit. Any unauthenticated HTTP/2 client that requests a >64 KiB response and stops reading kills the server for every other in-flight connection. The threshold is purely the payload size: at or below `HTTP2_WRITE_CHUNK_SIZE` (lib/reply.js:63, 689) `res.write` + `res.end` still run synchronously and the bug is absent, and it is absent on HTTP/1 at any size, so the exposure is created by this path and nothing else. A second consequence of the same defect, independent of `handlerTimeout`: the documented 'already sent' guard is silently wrong for >64 KiB HTTP/2 replies, so any user or plugin code that consults `reply.sent` before sending can emit a second response onto a committed one.
  - Evidence: Code: lib/reply.js:63 (`const HTTP2_WRITE_CHUNK_SIZE = 64 * 1024`); lib/reply.js:99-105 (`sent` getter returns `(this[kReplyHijacked] || this.raw.writableEnded) === true`); lib/reply.js:679-681 (`safeWriteHead(reply, statusCode)` then `writePayload(payload, res, reply)`); lib/reply.js:684-727 (`isHttp2Reply`, `writePayload`, `writeHttp2Payload` with `res.once('drain', writeChunk)` and `done()` only after the loop drains); lib/reply.js:867-872 (`sendTrailer` is the only caller of `res.end` on this path); lib/route.js:538-545 (timer body `if (!reply.sent) { const err = new FST_ERR_HANDLER_TIMEOUT(...); ac.abort(err); reply[kReplyIsError] = true; reply.send(err) }`); lib/error-handler.js:35-47 (fallback `try { reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode, reply[kReplyHeaders]) } catch (error) { ...log...; reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode) }` - the retry inside the catch is unguarded).

Ten crashing runs in which the client never calls pause, all reporting ERR_HTTP2_HEADERS_SENT out of the timer callback.

The `relay` shape is a plain TCP forwarder in p-boundary.js: it accepts the client connection, dials the server, pipes client->server directly, and forwards server->client at 64 KiB per 10 ms tick. The client attaches a data listener at setup and never pauses; the only difference from the surviving `data` rows is link speed.

CAUSAL CONNECTION, unchanged and not re-litigated: headers are committed by safeWriteHead (lib/reply.js:679) before writePayload (lib/reply.js:681); above HTTP2_WRITE_CHUNK_SIZE (lib/reply.js:63) the remainder is deferred to res.once('drain', writeChunk) and res.end() arrives only via done() -> sendTrailer (lib/reply.js:688-727, 867-872); Reply.prototype.sent (lib/reply.js:99-105) reports writableEnded, so it is false for the whole of that window; the timer at lib/route.js:538-545 tests exactly that flag; the second send reaches the unguarded retry at lib/error-handler.js:45 and throws out of a timer callback. The three variables I measured — payload size, peer read rate, timeout budget — all act on this single predicate: is the flush still open when the timer fires.
  - Defender: I am a fresh reviewer and inherited nothing but the ledger. I rebuilt every probe this round from prose, re-ran the round-1 and round-2 archives cold, and treated the critic's round-3 escalation as a claim to be tested rather than a report to be relayed.

ACCEPTED, and independently re-verified rather than carried over.

(1) The mechanism and the severity. `Reply.prototype.sent` is `(this[kReplyHijacked] || this.raw.writableEnded) === true` (lib/reply.js:99-105); `onSendEnd` commits headers via `safeWriteHead` (lib/reply.js:679) before `writePayload` (lib/reply.js:681); above `HTTP2_WRITE_CHUNK_SIZE` (lib/reply.js:63) `writeHttp2Payload` defers the remainder to `res.once('drain', writeChunk)` and `res.end()` is reached only via `done()` -> `sendTrailer` (lib/reply.js:688-727, 867-872); the handler-timeout timer tests exactly that flag (lib/route.js:538-545); the second send reaches the unguarded `reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode)` in the catch at lib/error-handler.js:45 and throws out of a timer callback. Every one of those citations resolves in the checkout today, and the crash reproduces from the round-1 archive on the host runtime and on all four Node majors the project's CI declares.

(2) THE REPLACEMENT CHARACTERISATION IS CORRECT, and this is the component the critic asked to be tested. The deciding variable is neither payload size nor the peer's read behaviour but whether the flush is still open when the timer fires. I held the payload at 33554432 bytes and the client shape at a promptly draining `data` listener, and varied only the budget: the flush completes at t+36..41 ms, and the process dies at 10 ms and at 20 ms while surviving at 40 ms, 80 ms and 300 ms. Two controls that the predicate also explains: at the chunk size the payload survives even a 10 ms budget, because that path writes and ends synchronously and never opens a window; at 131072 bytes with a prompt reader the flush closes at t+3 ms and a 10 ms budget survives.

(3) NO ADVERSARIAL PEER IS REQUIRED, and this materially widens the reach of the finding beyond anything else on the ledger. I built a plain TCP forwarder that moves 65536 bytes per 10 ms tick (6.4 MiB/s) with real upstream backpressure, and a client that attaches a data listener at setup and never pauses. At a 300 ms budget that ordinary slow link kills the process, on the host runtime and on v20.20.2, v22.23.2, v24.19.0 and v26.7.0 - ten crashing runs in which no peer ever stalls. The HTTP/1 control over the identical link survives, because there write and end are synchronous. So the precondition is not 'an attacker who stops reading'; it is 'a response whose flush outlives the configured budget', which a congested mobile link or a large export over a slow WAN produces by itself.

(4) I ALSO RAN THE FALSIFIER THE CRITIC NOMINATED, and it goes the critic's way. Same 32 MiB payload, same never-pausing client, same 6.4 MiB/s link, budget raised to 8000 ms while the flush measured 7443 ms: RESULT SURVIVED, full transfer, clean end. Same size, same peer, same link, opposite verdict, and the only thing that changed is whether the timer fires inside the window. That is the strongest single cell in the finding's favour and I did not find a counterexample to it in any cell I ran.

I do not contest `critical` or `strong`.

REJECTED, one clause, and it is NOT in dispute - the critic conceded it in round 3 and my runs agree with the concession. The claim as filed says 'The threshold is purely the payload size'. It is not, and the round-2 replacement ('131072 and above crash unconditionally') is also wrong. On the review checkout, at a 300 ms budget, a promptly draining client survives at 65537, 65540, 66560, 131072, 1048576 and 33554432 bytes while a stalling client dies at every one of them, identically on all four declared majors - 24 of 24 cells in my cold re-run of the round-2 archive. The surviving one-directional statement is that at or below the chunk size the defect is absent. I record this because the claim text is what the ledger closes on, and because a regression test written to the as-filed threshold - a large payload and a well-behaved client - passes against an unfixed tree.

I record a measurement hazard for any re-runner: this shell is zsh, which does not word-split unquoted parameter expansions, so routing a probe's argument list through one variable collapses it into a single argv entry and makes every probe report survival. All my invocations pass arguments literally.
  - Proposed fix: Guard the timer AND give the sink an explicit answer, and do not redefine the existing `sent` getter. I built this myself as a scratch copy (d3R) rather than copying the critic's tree, and measured it: (1) lib/route.js:538-545 keeps `ac.abort(err)` and wraps `reply[kReplyIsError] = true; reply.send(err)` in `if (reply.raw.headersSent === false)`; (2) lib/error-handler.js:35-48 hoists `if (reply.raw.headersSent === true) { abortCommittedResponse(reply, error); return }` ABOVE the try/writeHead, where the helper logs and then closes an HTTP/2 stream with NGHTTP2_INTERNAL_ERROR or destroys the HTTP/1 response; (3) lib/reply.js adds a NEW non-enumerable `committed` accessor returning `this.raw.headersSent === true`, leaving `sent` untouched, plus a correction to docs/Reference/Reply.md, which says today that `.sent` indicates 'if a response has been sent via reply.send()' - a description that is false during exactly the window this finding is about. Measured: every crash cell cleared, the full 33554432-byte transfer delivered with a clean end on both transports with and without a declared content-length, the project's suite exit 0 at the baseline's exact counts (tests 2241 / suites 20 / pass 2237 / fail 0 / cancelled 0 / skipped 4), and both of the project's linters exit 0 with no output. Redefining `sent` instead (fixS) still turns the suite red - exit 1, pass 2234 / fail 1, three already-sent-headers tests failing, two of them at the 30 s test timeout - which I re-measured in the cold re-run of the round-2 archive. Proposal only; Review mode applies nothing.
- **R2-F1** [high/strong] Guarding the crash at the sink is not a fix: the fallback error path decides to emit an error body without ever asking whether a response body is already in flight, so removing only the throw converts the remote process kill into a client-undetectable corrupted or truncated 200. lib/error-handler.js:35-48 performs three unconditional steps - `writeHead(statusCode, kReplyHeaders)` (line 37), a retry `writeHead(statusCode)` in the catch (line 45), and `reply.raw.end(payload)` (line 47). None of them is conditioned on `reply.raw.headersSent`, and line 47 in particular is reached with no test at all of whether bytes are already on the wire. Today line 45 throws first, which is why the observed symptom is the uncaught exception in R1-F1/R1-F3; the write-a-body-anyway decision at line 47 is simply masked by it. The minimum remedy named in this debate - guard the retry at line 45 - unmasks it. Executed on scratch copies: with the retry skipped, an HTTP/1 route streaming a 32 MiB body to a stalled reader delivers HTTP 200, `transfer-encoding: chunked`, 852114 body bytes instead of 33554432, with a `{"statusCode":503,"code":"ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT",...}` JSON blob concatenated onto the binary payload, and the response is ENDED CLEANLY - the client reports no error and no truncation. A client streaming that response to disk writes a corrupt short file and believes it complete. This is worse than the crash it replaces, because the crash is loud and this is silent. It is not an artefact of one guard shape: the two alternatives the defender names in the same breath, `reply.raw.destroy()` and `res.end()`, both leave HTTP/2 delivering a cleanly-ended 200 carrying 131070 / 131072 bytes of a 33554432-byte transfer with no client-visible error, because destroying an `Http2ServerResponse` closes the stream without an RST the peer can distinguish from success. The defect is therefore the remediation DECISION, not its execution site: once `raw.headersSent` is true the timer at lib/route.js:538-545 has no valid action available that involves sending a response, and any fix that leaves `reply.send(err)` reachable in that state trades a crash for silent data corruption. A correct fix must both stop the timer from initiating a second response on a committed reply AND terminate the transfer in a way the peer can detect (HTTP/1 socket destroy producing ECONNRESET; HTTP/2 `stream.close(NGHTTP2_INTERNAL_ERROR)` rather than `res.destroy()`).
  - Evidence: Code, review checkout, unmodified: lib/error-handler.js:35-48 - `fallbackErrorHandler(error, reply, function (reply, payload) { try { reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode, reply[kReplyHeaders]) } catch (error) { ...log...; reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode) } reply.raw.end(payload) })`. Line 47 `reply.raw.end(payload)` has no `headersSent` test on any path that reaches it. lib/route.js:538-545 is the caller that reaches it on a committed reply (`if (!reply.sent) { ...; reply.send(err) }`), and lib/reply.js:99-105 is why `reply.sent` is false there.

WHICH TREE PER EXPERIMENT. The candidate patches below exist ONLY in scratch rsync copies of the checkout (../fixC, ../fixD, ../fixE; `.git`, the harness-owned untracked directory and node_modules excluded, node_modules symlinked to ../env/node_modules). Section G of clean-rerun-r2.log diffs each copy against the checkout so the exact patch is visible. Patch under test in ../fixC - the minimum remedy named in this debate, guarding lib/error-handler.js:45 only:
  -        reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode)
  +        if (!reply.raw.headersSent) {
  +          reply.raw.writeHead(reply.raw.statusCode)
  +        }
Crash is gone (r1-stream-scope.js against ../fixC: `SURVIVED 3s, no crash`, exit 0, on both http1 and http2 - clean-rerun-r2.log section E). What the client then receives (r4-corruption.js, 8-byte stream that commits headers and stalls, handlerTimeout 300 ms):
  http1: [t+2000ms] ended=true bodyBytes=154 body="AAAAAAAA{\"statusCode\":503,\"code\":\"ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT\",\"error\":\"Service Unavailable\",\"message\":\"Cannot write headers after they are sent to the client\"}"
  http2: [t+2000ms] ended=true bodyBytes=137 body="AAAAAAAA{\"statusCode\":503,\"code\":\"ERR_HTTP2_HEADERS_SENT\",...}"
Identical on every declared CI major: v20.20.2, v22.23.2, v24.19.0, v26.7.0 all print the same 154-byte http1 body with `ended=true` (clean-rerun-r2.log section F).

File-transfer shape (r5-truncate.js, 32 MiB stream in 64 KiB pushes, reader stalled 1.2 s, handlerTimeout 300 ms), against ../fixC:
  http1 no content-length: client status = 200, transfer-encoding = chunked -> RESULT expectedBytes=33554432 receivedBytes=852114 cleanEnd=true clientError=null; tail = "aaaaaaaaaaaaaa{\"statusCode\":503,\"code\":\"ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT\",...}"
  http1 with content-length: RESULT ... cleanEnd=false clientError=null (detectable only because content-length was declared)
  http2 no content-length: RESULT expectedBytes=33554432 receivedBytes=131201 cleanEnd=true clientError=null

The other two guard shapes the defender names, tested the same way:
  ../fixD (`if (reply.raw.headersSent) reply.raw.destroy() else writeHead(...)`): http1 r5 -> receivedBytes=826682 cleanEnd=false clientError=ECONNRESET (loud, acceptable) BUT http2 r5 -> receivedBytes=131070 cleanEnd=true clientError=null (silent).
  ../fixE (`if (reply.raw.headersSent) reply.raw.end() else writeHead(...)`): http1 r4 -> ended=true bodyBytes=8 (silently truncated clean 200) on v20.20.2, v25.6.1 and v26.7.0; http2 r5 -> receivedBytes=131072 cleanEnd=true clientError=null.
So all three named shapes leave at least one transport delivering a cleanly-ended 200 whose body is short and whose shortness the peer cannot detect.

Causal connection: the timer at lib/route.js:538-545 initiates a second response on a reply whose headers are already committed; the fallback callback at lib/error-handler.js:35-48 executes that decision unconditionally at line 47. Guarding line 45 removes the exception without removing the decision, so the error payload is appended to the committed body and the response is ended. Nothing between the timer and line 47 consults `raw.headersSent`.

Not covered by the project suite: the full upstream run from a scratch copy of this tree is green (tests 2241 / suites 20 / pass 2237 / fail 0 / skipped 4), archived at /Users/michaeltraw/Dev/council-bench/_scratch/<seat>/out/full-suite.log. No test asserts what a client receives when a handler timeout fires on a committed response.
[R3 escalation] Same archive as my R1-F1 response: /Users/michaeltraw/Dev/council-bench/_scratch/<seat>/r3/ (README.md, make-trees.py, p-boundary.js, p-doublesend.js, p-retention.js, rerun-r3.sh, clean-rerun-r3.log, logs/suite-*.log), reusing ../r2/r5-truncate.js and ../r2/r1-stream-scope.js unchanged. Candidate remedies exist ONLY as scratch copies (../r3T, ../r3S, ../r3R, ../r3base and the round-2 ../fixC); section H of the log diffs each against the checkout, and ../r3base diffs empty. The checkout was never modified and never installed into (section I: git status --porcelain = ' M lib/reply.js' plus the harness-owned untracked directory; git diff --stat = 1 file changed, 458 insertions). Every probe prints require.resolve for fastify.js, lib/reply.js, lib/route.js and lib/error-handler.js.

PATCHES (make-trees.py; anchors matched verbatim against the checkout, one occurrence each, or the build aborts):
  r3T = lib/route.js:538-545, keep ac.abort(err), wrap `reply[kReplyIsError] = true; reply.send(err)` in `if (reply.raw.headersSent === false)`
  r3S = the `sent` getter also returns true on this.raw.headersSent (round-2 item (c) as written)
  r3R = r3T, plus lib/error-handler.js:35-48 with `if (reply.raw.headersSent === true) { abortCommittedResponse(reply, error); return }` hoisted ABOVE the try/writeHead, where abortCommittedResponse logs and then calls raw.stream.close(NGHTTP2_INTERNAL_ERROR) when a raw HTTP/2 stream is present and raw.destroy(error) otherwise, plus a new non-enumerable `committed` accessor on Reply

THE CONCEDED MEASUREMENT, rebuilt by me (../r2/r5-truncate.js: 32 MiB stream in 64 KiB pushes, reader stalled 1.2 s then resumes, handlerTimeout 300 ms):
  r3T http1 nocl -> RESULT expectedBytes=33554432 receivedBytes=33554432 cleanEnd=true clientError=null
  r3T http1 cl   -> receivedBytes=33554432 cleanEnd=true clientError=null
  r3T http2 nocl -> receivedBytes=33554432 cleanEnd=true clientError=null
  r3T http2 cl   -> receivedBytes=33554432 cleanEnd=true clientError=null
  and on the declared majors, http1/http2 nocl: v20.20.2 33554432/33554432, v22.23.2 33554432/33554432, v24.19.0 33554432/33554432, v26.7.0 33554432/33554432 — 8 of 8, cleanEnd=true, clientError=null.
  r3R gives the same four full-transfer rows on the host, so the corrected remedy does not reintroduce truncation on the timeout path.

THE CALLER-INITIATED ROUTE, no handler timeout configured (p-doublesend.js: 8-byte stream that commits headers and stalls; at t+400 ms the handler prints the guard state and sends again):
  CHECKOUT http1 -> GUARD raw.headersSent=true raw.writableEnded=false reply.sent=false -> RESULT UNCAUGHT ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT
  CHECKOUT http2 -> same guard line -> RESULT UNCAUGHT ERR_HTTP2_HEADERS_SENT
  r3T      http1 -> same guard line -> RESULT UNCAUGHT ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT
  r3T      http2 -> same guard line -> RESULT UNCAUGHT ERR_HTTP2_HEADERS_SENT
  fixC     http1 -> RESULT status=200 bodyBytes=156 cleanEnd=true clientError=null body="AAAAAAAA{\"statusCode\":500,\"code\":\"ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT\",...}"
  fixC     http2 -> RESULT status=200 bodyBytes=139 cleanEnd=true clientError=null body="AAAAAAAA{\"statusCode\":500,\"code\":\"ERR_HTTP2_HEADERS_SENT\",...}"
  r3R      http1 -> GUARD ... reply.committed=true -> RESULT status=200 bodyBytes=8 cleanEnd=false clientError=ABORTED body="AAAAAAAA"
  r3R      http2 -> GUARD ... reply.committed=true -> RESULT status=200 bodyBytes=8 cleanEnd=false clientError=ERR_HTTP2_STREAM_ERROR body="AAAAAAAA"
  r3R on the declared majors, http1/http2: v20.20.2, v22.23.2, v24.19.0, v26.7.0 all ABORTED / ERR_HTTP2_STREAM_ERROR with cleanEnd=false and bodyBytes=8 — 8 of 8 loud, none silent.

RETENTION, the cost of the timer guard (p-retention.js: 32 MiB stream, peer pauses on response and never resumes, handlerTimeout 300 ms, observed at t+8000 ms):
  CHECKOUT http1 -> RESULT UNCAUGHT ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT ; http2 -> RESULT UNCAUGHT ERR_HTTP2_HEADERS_SENT
  r3T http1 -> RESULT held=true signalAborted=true rawClosed=false rawHeadersSent=true rawWritableEnded=false clientBytes=0 clientEnded=false clientError=null
  r3T http2 -> RESULT held=true signalAborted=true rawClosed=false rawHeadersSent=true rawWritableEnded=false clientBytes=0 clientEnded=false clientError=null
  r3R http1 / http2 -> identical held=true rows, because the sink is not reached from the timer either.
The abort signal fires, so handler work is cancelled; the response and its transport are not reclaimed by anything in this code within the observation window, which is more than twenty-six times the configured budget.

SUITE, the project's own runner from scratch copies: r3base exit 0, tests 2241 / suites 20 / pass 2237 / fail 0 / skipped 4; r3T exit 0 identical; r3R exit 0 identical; r3S exit 1, pass 2234 / fail 1 / cancelled 2, three failing already-sent-headers tests, two of them reaching the 30 s test timeout. The project's eslint on the three files r3R touches exits 0. Logs at r3/logs/suite-r3base.log, suite-r3T.log, suite-r3R.log, suite-r3S.log.
  - Defender: ACCEPTED, and the acceptance is now broader than the round-2 defender's, because I built the corrected remedy myself and it vindicates the finding's central point.

(1) The sink defect is plainly in the code. The fallback callback at lib/error-handler.js:35-48 takes three unconditional steps - `writeHead(statusCode, kReplyHeaders)` at line 37, the retry `writeHead(statusCode)` in the catch at line 45, and `reply.raw.end(payload)` at line 47 - and none of them is conditioned on `reply.raw.headersSent`. Line 47 in particular is reached with no test of whether bytes are already on the wire.

(2) Guarding only the throw does unmask the write-a-body-anyway decision. Reproduced cold from the round-2 archive: with the retry guarded, an 8-byte stalled stream at a 300 ms budget yields `status=200 bodyBytes=154 cleanEnd=true clientError=null` on HTTP/1 with a 503 ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT document concatenated onto the payload (137 bytes on HTTP/2), and the 32 MiB file-transfer shape yields 852114 of 33554432 bytes on HTTP/1 and 131201 of 33554432 on HTTP/2, both cleanly ended and both client-undetectable. The two alternative guard shapes each leave at least one transport silent.

(3) The sink is independently reachable, with no handler timeout configured at all, and this is what makes R2-F1 more than a corollary of R1-F1. My probe: a handler commits headers with a stalled stream, then consults the documented guard and is told `raw.headersSent=true raw.writableEnded=false reply.sent=false`, and sends again. On the review checkout that is an uncaught ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT / ERR_HTTP2_HEADERS_SENT; on a tree where only the timer is guarded it is still an uncaught throw, because the timer guard does not touch that route.

(4) PEER-DETECTABLE TERMINATION IS THE RIGHT ANSWER FOR THAT ROUTE, and I verified the critic's prescription rather than assuming it. On the same probe, guarding only the retry gives a cleanly-ended 200 carrying the original 8 bytes plus an error document, `clientError=null` - silent. The corrected sink, which closes an HTTP/2 stream with an internal-error code and destroys an HTTP/1 response, gives `cleanEnd=false clientError=ABORTED` on HTTP/1 and `cleanEnd=false clientError=ERR_HTTP2_STREAM_ERROR` on HTTP/2, with the peer receiving only the 8 bytes that were genuinely sent - loud on both transports, on the host runtime and on all four declared CI majors, 10 of 10 rows.

(5) THE CORRECTED THREE-PART REMEDY IS THE ARTEFACT A MAINTAINER WOULD APPLY, and it measures clean. I rebuilt it from the critic's prose on a scratch copy - timer guard; committed-response test hoisted above the write in the sink with peer-detectable termination; a new non-enumerable `committed` accessor plus a documentation correction instead of redefining `sent` - and measured it end to end. It clears every crash cell including the buffered HTTP/2 case, the rate-limited-link case and the short-budget case; it delivers the whole 33554432-byte transfer with a clean end on both transports with and without a declared content-length, so it does not reintroduce truncation on the timeout path; the project's own runner exits 0 at the baseline's exact counts (2241 / 20 / 2237 / 0 / 4, byte-for-byte the counts my unmodified control tree produces in the same run); and both of the project's linters exit 0, eslint with no output at all. The critic's report of exit 0 at those counts plus a clean linter run is confirmed on an independently built tree.

(6) THE DISCLOSED RESIDUAL IS CONFIRMED, and it is larger than disclosed. Under a guarded timer, a peer that stops reading holds a committed response with the abort signal fired, the response not ended and nothing reclaiming it, and the peer having received zero bytes - at 8000 ms against a 300 ms budget, and still at 20000 ms, which is 66 times the configured budget. It is identical on both transports and on all five runtimes, and identical with and without the sink change, because the sink is not reached from the timer at all. So the configured timeout silently stops bounding anything for a committed response: the crash was the only thing that used to end that request, and removing it converts a remotely triggerable crash into a remotely triggerable resource hold. I did not find any second mechanism that reclaims it inside the observation window. One honest measurement caveat: on the HTTP/2 compatibility response object the `closed` and `destroyed` properties read `undefined` in these runtimes, so my HTTP/2 rows rest on `writableEnded === false` plus the peer having received nothing, not on a `closed` reading; the HTTP/1 rows do report `closed=false destroyed=false` directly.

I do not contest `high` or `strong`. The finding is about a remediation decision, and the round-3 evidence shows that decision has to be made deliberately: the naive guard corrupts silently, the timer guard alone leaves the caller-initiated route killing the process, and even the corrected remedy leaves a bound that no longer binds.

REJECTED, one conjunct, and it is NOT in dispute - the critic conceded it in round 3 and my runs agree. The claim as filed asserts universally that 'a correct fix must both stop the timer from initiating a second response on a committed reply AND terminate the transfer in a way the peer can detect'. On the handler-timeout path the second conjunct is not merely unnecessary, it is harmful: with the timer guarded, the transfer the crash used to destroy completes correctly - 33554432 of 33554432 bytes, cleanly ended, on both transports with and without a declared content-length, on the host runtime, and terminating it would throw away a transfer that is going to succeed. The conjunct survives only for the caller-initiated route, which is where I accept it in full at (4) above.
  - Proposed fix: Ship the three-part remedy as corrected, and then close the residual, which the three parts do not touch. Parts one to three are exactly the d3R tree described above and measured clean on suite and both linters. The residual needs a fourth decision that is out of this finding's filed scope but should not be lost at the close: once the timer stops initiating a second response, nothing bounds a committed response whose peer has stopped reading, and I measured that hold persisting at 66 times the configured budget with the peer receiving nothing. The two shapes worth considering are (a) on timeout of a committed reply, arm X short grace timer and then terminate the transfer through the same peer-detectable path the corrected sink already provides, so the budget still bounds something, or (b) document explicitly that the handler timeout does not bound a response that has begun, and require a connection-level or server-level timeout to be configured alongside it. Either is a decision a maintainer should make consciously; today it is made by accident, and on the unpatched tree it is made by crashing. Proposal only; Review mode applies nothing.

_Ledger: <path>
```

### T14 — Verdict 2

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 4

**NO-SHIP: 3 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F2** [high/moderate] HTTP/2 replies using reply.trailer() are likely broken because onSendEnd unconditionally installs the HTTP/1.1-only `Transfer-Encoding: chunked` connection header before sending every trailer-bearing response. HTTP/2 rejects connection-specific transfer-encoding headers, so this path can throw or reset the response rather than deliver its body and trailers.
  - Evidence: lib/reply.js:580-592 unconditionally calls `reply.header('Transfer-Encoding', 'chunked')` whenever trailers exist, with no protocol check; lib/reply.js:679 then applies those headers through safeWriteHead. The same module explicitly detects HTTP/2 at lib/reply.js:684-699, demonstrating that HTTP/2 responses traverse this implementation but the trailer-header path does not branch for them.
  - Defender: Accepted in full, and the hedging in the claim ('likely broken', 'can throw or reset') understates what actually happens. I reproduced it end to end rather than relying on the code reading. repro/f2-http2-trailers.js starts a real HTTP/2 Fastify server with a route that registers reply.trailer('ETag', ...) and sends a JSON body, loads the module under review from the checkout by absolute path (require.resolve printed in every captured run), and drives it with a real node:http2 client. On every Node major the project's CI declares — v20.20.2, v22.23.2, v24.19.0, v26.7.0 — the process dies with an uncaught TypeError [ERR_HTTP2_INVALID_CONNECTION_HEADERS]: 'HTTP/1 Connection specific headers are forbidden: "transfer-encoding"', EXIT_STATUS=1. The chain is exactly the one the finding names: onSendEnd at lib/reply.js:581-592 sets Transfer-Encoding: chunked whenever reply[kReplyTrailers] is non-null with no protocol branch; safeWriteHead (lib/reply.js:564-574, called at :679) hands reply[kReplyHeaders] to Http2ServerResponse.writeHead; Node's header mapper rejects the connection-specific header. What the finding did not anticipate is the second-order effect: the first throw unwinds into lib/error-handler.js, whose fallbackErrorHandler (error-handler.js:140 -> :45) calls writeHead again with the same header set and throws a second time outside any try/catch. The result is process termination, not a reset stream — an unauthenticated request to any trailer-bearing route on an HTTP/2 server kills the server. That is at least 'high'; I would not argue against 'critical'.

This is not an unsupported protocol combination: docs/Reference/Reply.md:260-267 documents .trailer() and states the Transfer-Encoding: chunked behaviour unconditionally, with no HTTP/2 caveat. Nor is it caught anywhere: I ran the project's own suite in a scratch copy verified byte-identical to the checkout (sha256 e2c528a7b8fc75bba5804fd13019eafc829910aad96693d5d8338937e6762668), and test/reply-trailers.test.js exercises trailers only through inject (HTTP/1) while test/http2/*.test.js never registers a trailer — 53 pass / 0 fail on Node 20 and 52 pass / 0 fail on Node 24, with nothing crossing the two.

One scope observation, stated because it is true rather than as a dodge: the Transfer-Encoding block at lib/reply.js:581-592 falls outside the added hunks of the review window (@@ -1,3 +1,67 @@ and @@ -617,3 +681,397 @@), and the crash occurs at safeWriteHead before writePayload runs, so it does not depend on the in-window HTTP/2 chunked-write helpers at lib/reply.js:684-727. I accept it regardless: the review target is the working tree as it stands, the module is HTTP/2-aware inside the window, and the framing forbids treating the synthetic pre-diff baseline as evidence about behaviour. Separately, I probed the in-window chunked-write path itself for defects and found none (repro/probe-http2-chunked-write.js: bodies of 0 / 1 KiB / 64 KiB / 64 KiB+1 / 300 KiB / 5 MiB as Buffer and as string, GET and HEAD, plus a slow reader at a pinned 2 ms cadence to force the res.once('drain') resumption branch — exact byte counts, no errors, no hangs, on all four Node majors).
  - Proposed fix: Branch on protocol in onSendEnd (lib/reply.js:589-591): keep setting the Trailer header for both protocols, but set Transfer-Encoding: chunked only when the reply is not HTTP/2 — the module already has the predicate at lib/reply.js:684-686, so `if (!isHttp2Reply(reply)) reply.header('Transfer-Encoding', 'chunked')`. sendTrailer's existing res.addTrailers path (lib/reply.js:883) works unchanged on the HTTP/2 compat layer. I applied exactly this to a scratch copy (never to the review repository) and verified it on Node 20.20.2 / 24.19.0 / 26.7.0: the small JSON response and a 300 KiB response — the latter also exercising the >64 KiB chunked write path — both complete with body and trailer intact, and the project's own trailer and HTTP/2 tests still pass with no HTTP/1 regression. A regression test belongs in test/http2/ registering reply.trailer() and asserting body plus trailer over a real HTTP/2 client, which is precisely the case the suite lacks today.
- **R2-C1** [high/strong] A trailer function that returns its value synchronously leaves the response permanently unfinished. In the trailer flush (lib/reply.js:867-922) the pending counter is decremented for each trailer function and only restored by the completion callback; a synchronous return value is neither passed to that callback nor recognised, because the promise test at lib/reply.js:914 requires typeof result === 'object'. The counter never returns to zero, send() never runs, and res.end() is never reached — while the skipped branch at lib/reply.js:921 is disabled because a function was registered. The response headers and body are already on the wire, so the client waits forever and the connection is held open. This is the FIRST form shown in the project's own reference documentation for the API (docs/Reference/Reply.md:273-276), it is not covered by any test, and the server logs nothing beyond 'incoming request'.
  - Evidence: Reproduced against the checkout under review (mode A, require.resolve printed at runtime) on every Node major the project's CI declares — v20.20.2, v22.23.2, v24.19.0, v26.7.0. repro/r2/n10-doc-example-hang.js uses the documentation's example verbatim on an otherwise ordinary JSON route and drives it with a real TCP client: repro/r2/out-n10-doc-example-hang.txt reports {"outcome":"NO-RESPONSE-AFTER-5s","socketStillOpen":true,"bytesRead":226} on all four majors — headers and body chunk delivered, terminating chunk never sent, connection never closed. The matrix in repro/r2/out-n9-trailer-matrix.txt isolates it: for the sync-return style, both the in-process injection transport and a real socket report completed:false / TIMEOUT for a JSON payload and for a buffer payload, on all four majors, while the callback, delayed-callback and async-function styles all complete and deliver. Observability: repro/r2/out-n13-hang-observability.txt — at trace level the only lines are the listening line and 'incoming request', and getConnections reports 1 open connection after 3 s. Code: lib/reply.js:867-922, in particular handled-- at :893, the cb that restores it at :899-914, and the promise test at :914; the API's own validator at lib/reply.js:302-309 accepts any function. Docs: docs/Reference/Reply.md:273-276. Coverage: every case in test/reply-trailers.test.js uses the done callback or an async function; no test registers a synchronously returning trailer function. Fix validated on a scratch copy only (repro/r2/candidate-fix.diff, repro/r2/out-n11-candidate-fix.txt): feeding a non-undefined synchronous return value to the same completion callback makes the documentation's example return 200 with its trailer on Node 20/24/26, and the project's own suite is unchanged at 2241 tests / 2237 pass / 0 fail / 4 skipped before and after (repro/r2/out-n12-suite-baseline-vs-patched.txt).
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: After invoking the trailer function, continue handling thenables as promises, but pass any non-undefined synchronous return value through `cb(null, result)`. Add a real-socket regression test using the exact documented synchronous-return example, and document that an undefined return requires callback completion.
- **R2-C2** [medium/strong] When the payload is a stream, registered trailers are announced but never delivered to a real client. sendStream (lib/reply.js:812-864) registers the trailer flush on the payload's 'end' event via sendStreamTrailer (lib/reply.js:924-926) and then pipes the payload into the raw response; pipe installs its own 'end' handler which ends the response. Because the flush completes its value on a later turn — process.nextTick(send) at lib/reply.js:910 for every style — the response is already finished by the time send() calls res.addTrailers() and res.end(), so the trailer section is empty. The response still advertises Trailer and Transfer-Encoding: chunked, so the client is told to expect a trailer that never arrives. The project's own test asserts the opposite (test/reply-trailers.test.js:117-143) and passes, because it verifies through the in-process injection helper rather than a real connection.
  - Evidence: repro/r2/n9-trailer-matrix.js against the checkout under review (mode A, require.resolve printed at runtime), 3 payload shapes x 4 trailer styles x 2 transports on v20.20.2, v22.23.2, v24.19.0, v26.7.0. In repro/r2/out-n9-trailer-matrix.txt every stream-payload cell reports the real socket as trailerDelivered:false on all four majors, for the synchronous-callback, delayed-callback, async-function and synchronous-return styles alike — while the same runs report the injection transport as trailerDelivered:true for the synchronous-callback and async-function styles, which is precisely the divergence that lets the project's stream-trailer test pass. repro/r2/out-n8-stream-trailers.txt shows the wire view: status 200, 65536 bytes, "trailerHeader":"etag", "transferEncoding":"chunked", "trailers":{} — announced and empty. Buffer and JSON payloads deliver correctly in the same runs, which localises the defect to the stream path. Code: lib/reply.js:864 (the pipe that ends the response), :923-926 (the flush registered on the same event, ahead of pipe's handler), :910 (the deferral). Fix validated on a scratch copy only (repro/r2/candidate-fix.diff, repro/r2/out-n11-candidate-fix.txt): when trailers are registered, letting the trailer flush rather than pipe end the response makes every stream cell deliver on Node 20/24/26, with the project's suite unchanged at 2241 / 2237 pass / 0 fail / 4 skipped (repro/r2/out-n12-suite-baseline-vs-patched.txt).
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: When trailers are registered, pipe with `{ end: false }` and let sendTrailer exclusively add the trailers and end the response. Add a real HTTP/1 integration test asserting both the streamed body and the client's received trailers; injection-only coverage is insufficient.
- **R1-F1** [high/strong] When the response emits an error and the route has any onResponse hook, the original transport error is discarded. The hook runner invokes onResponseCallback with only the hook's result, so a successful hook causes the failed response to be logged as "request completed" instead of "request errored". This creates a material observability blind spot for connection and response failures.
  - Evidence: lib/reply.js:947-973 captures `err` from the raw response but does not pass it into `onResponseHookRunner`; lib/hooks.js:230-264 shows the runner callback receives only hook errors; lib/reply.js:987-999 logs `request completed` whenever that callback error is null.
[R2 escalation] Defender evidence quoted in the ledger: `out-f1c-natural.txt`, `out-f1d-abort.txt`, and `out-f1e-http2-abort.txt` report `rawResponseErrorEvents:[]` across Node 20/22/24/26. The deterministic divergence remains established by `out-f1-onresponse.txt`: without an onResponse hook, level 50 `request errored`; with a no-op hook, level 30 `request completed`.
[R3 escalation] Defender's new evidence in `repro/r2/out-n1-node-emit-sites.txt` enumerates response error-emission sites across Node 20/22/24/26; `out-n4-h1-emit-conditions.txt` confines HTTP/1 emission to same-tick write-after-end; `out-n6-write-order.txt` reports `SHAPES_ENTERING_EMIT_WINDOW=0` across 640 framework shapes. The residual defect remains reproduced in `out-r1-archive-rerun.txt`.
  - Defender: ACCEPTED - the mechanism, and again not on inheritance. I re-ran round 1's mechanism script COLD out of its archive on all four CI-declared majors this round (round 2 re-ran two of them): without an onResponse hook the raw response's error yields {"level":50,"msg":"request errored","err":"boom-transport"}; with a single no-op hook it yields {"level":30,"msg":"request completed","err":null}, on v20.20.2, v22.23.2, v24.19.0 and v26.7.0. The code path is exactly as the finding describes and every cited line still resolves: lib/reply.js:944-978 captures err from the raw response's 'finish'/'error' listeners (:976-977) and forwards it to onResponseCallback only in the no-hook branch (:972), while the hook branch (:964-970) hands onResponseCallback straight to onResponseHookRunner, and hookRunnerGenerator (lib/hooks.js:230-264, bound at :272) calls cb(err, request, reply) with the hook chain's own error only, so lib/reply.js:987-999 takes the info branch. Not contested, and never was.

ACCEPTED - Codex's round-3 position: medium, not high. Both seats now hold the same severity for the same reasons on the same evidence.

NEW EVIDENCE THIS ROUND, on the one thing round 2 left described but not demonstrated. Round 2's honest residual was "application or plugin code that writes to the raw response in the same tick it was ended". That was an assertion about reachability; this round I entered the window deliberately rather than asserting it (n14, n15: mode A against the checkout, require.resolve printed at runtime, four majors, ordinary routes plus ONE line of application-level code, nothing synthesised - the 'error' event is emitted by Node itself as a consequence of that write). Two results, both uniform on 20/22/24/26:
 (a) n14, small body: the window does emit ERR_STREAM_WRITE_AFTER_END, but 'finish' has already fired, so the framework's listener has already run and removed itself, and the request is logged {"level":30,"msg":"request completed"} WITH AND WITHOUT the hook. In that regime the branch this finding names makes no difference at all. A write one tick later emits nothing, matching n4.
 (b) n15, 6 MiB body with a peer that stalls 700 ms before reading, so the response is still flushing: the listener order is ["error","finish"] and the divergence appears exactly as the finding claims - {"level":50,"msg":"request errored","err":"ERR_STREAM_WRITE_AFTER_END"} without the hook, {"level":30,"msg":"request completed","err":null} with it, 6291582 body bytes delivered in both.
(b) is the first demonstration in this debate of the divergence WITHOUT synthesising the event. I report (a) alongside it because (a) narrows the defect further and only (b) supports the severity I am agreeing to. Medium stands on (b); I am not trading it downward on the strength of (a).

REJECTED - the recorded claim's two remaining components: the severity "high", and the characterisation "a material observability blind spot for connection and response failures". Both seats have now withdrawn "high", but the ledger text still carries it, so I reject it explicitly rather than let it stand by silence. On the characterisation: the trigger demonstrated in n15 is application or plugin code writing after end - not a connection failure and not a transport failure. Re-run cold on four majors this round: Node's own implementations give the response object exactly one self-emit site on HTTP/1 (write-after-end / write-after-destroy) and none whatsoever on the HTTP/2 compat response; six real HTTP/1 and TLS peer failures, two HTTP/2 handler shapes under RST_STREAM(CANCEL), four timeout / pipelining / 100-continue regimes and 168 framework-shape measurements all report an empty response-error list, with the failures landing on the socket as ECONNRESET / EPIPE and on the server's clientError; and the 640-shape sweep of the module itself reports SHAPES_ENTERING_EMIT_WINDOW=0 on every major with an empty resErrors list in all 424 completed shapes.

PROTOCOL OBSERVATION - Codex's round-3 message carries no new checkable evidence of its own. Its evidence field cites four artefacts and all four are mine: three round-2 probe outputs and my re-run of round 1's archive. That is not an objection to its conclusion, which I agree with; it is a fact about the reopening, and it is the second consecutive round in which the reopening rests entirely on the defender's evidence. The runner's transition table expects a reopening to carry new checkable evidence; whether that expectation is mechanically enforced is a separate question I did not test, and I record the observation in notes rather than leaving it implicit.
  - Proposed fix: Coalesce the transport error into the hook callback at lib/reply.js:964-970: onResponseHookRunner(ctx.onResponse, reply.request, reply, (hookErr, req, rep) => onResponseCallback(hookErr != null ? hookErr : err, req, rep)). Codex's wording is the same change. Which error wins when both exist is a judgement call: as written the hook error wins, and if the intent is that a transport failure must always be reported, invert the coalesce.

On the regression test, I can now be more specific than round 2 was. Round 2 said no peer behaviour will emit the event for you, so a test that tries to provoke it naturally would pass vacuously - that remains true of peer and transport failures. But repro/r3/n15-residual-window-race.js is a natural-path test that does NOT synthesise the event: a route that sends a body large enough to still be flushing, a peer that stalls before reading, and one same-tick write to the raw response after send, asserted with and without an onResponse hook registered. On the working tree that test records level 50 without the hook and level 30 with it, on all four CI majors; with the coalesce applied both record level 50. A direct-emit test is still worth keeping alongside it for determinism, but it is no longer the only option, and the pair documents both regimes n14 and n15 distinguish.

_Ledger: <path>
```
