## T04

### T04 — Verdict 1

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 3/3 · Findings: 6

**NO-SHIP: 5 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [critical/strong] An HTTP/2 request whose stream the peer resets with RST_STREAM(NGHTTP2_CANCEL) before response headers never settles: the caller's promise neither resolves nor rejects, no timeout fires, and the client's in-flight counter leaks by one permanently, so client.close() can never resolve. The cause is that onRequestStreamClose (lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:558-563), the request path's only 'close' handler, performs cleanup but never fails or finalizes the request, unlike the upgrade/CONNECT path's onUpgradeStreamClose (548-556 -> failUpgradeStream 632-644). This is a remotely triggerable, unbounded hang plus an unrecoverable accounting leak, reproduced over both h2c and TLS h2.
  - Evidence: CODE. lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:558-563 is the only 'close' handler installed on an ordinary request stream (installed at line 977: stream.once('close', onRequestStreamClose)):
  function onRequestStreamClose () {
    this.off('data', onData)
    this.off('error', noop)
    closeStreamSession(this)
    this[kRequestStreamState] = null
  }
It never calls state.abort(...) and never calls state.finalizeRequest(). Compare the upgrade/CONNECT path, lines 548-556, whose 'close' handler onUpgradeStreamClose does exactly that missing step via failUpgradeStream (632-644) -> state.abort(err, true). The request path therefore relies entirely on 'end' (onEnd, 1077-1097), 'error' (onError, 1099-1107), 'frameError' (1109-1117) or 'timeout' (1123-1133) to settle the dispatch; 'aborted' (onAborted, 1119-1121) deliberately settles nothing. finalizeRequest (726-739) is the only thing that advances client[kRunningIdx], so if none of those fire the client's in-flight accounting (client.js:328-330, kRunning = kPendingIdx - kRunningIdx) never comes back down.

REPRO (archive: _rerun2/T04-armX/repro, clean rerun captured in output-run-all.txt; interpreter node v25.6.1; deps = the prepared frozen environment only, nothing installed; every probe prints require.resolve('undici') and it resolves to the working tree index.js in every run).
  node rst-before-headers.mjs 8 2500 60000  -> settled = null, 'RESULT: HUNG - dispatch never settled', exit 7
  node rst-before-headers.mjs 0 2500 60000  -> settled = InformationalError 'HTTP/2: stream half-closed (remote)', exit 0
  node rst-before-headers.mjs 2 2500 60000  -> settled = ERR_HTTP2_STREAM_ERROR, exit 0
  node rst-before-headers.mjs 7 2500 60000  -> settled = ERR_HTTP2_STREAM_ERROR, exit 0
  node rst-before-headers.mjs 8 4000 1000   -> still HUNG with headersTimeout=1000, exit 7 (the headers timeout does NOT rescue it; the hang is unbounded, not merely slow)
The server in the probe is a stock node:http2 createServer that calls stream.close(<code>) before responding. Only code 8 (NGHTTP2_CANCEL) is affected among the codes probed, because Node synthesises neither an 'error' nor an 'end' for it on a non-flowing client stream.

NOT h2c-SPECIFIC: node rst-before-headers-tls.mjs 8 2500, using new Client(..., { allowH2: true }) over TLS/ALPN h2, also reports 'RESULT: HUNG - dispatch never settled', exit 7 (same file, output-run-all.txt section B).

HANDLER TRACE. A scratch copy of the working tree (derived from the tree itself; lib/ verified identical by diff -r before instrumenting) with one gated console.error at the top of each request-stream handler shows that for code 8 the ONLY handler that ever runs is onRequestStreamClose, followed by closeStreamSession. For code 0 the trace is onEnd -> releaseRequestStream -> onRequestStreamClose -> closeStreamSession, which is why code 0 settles and code 8 does not.

BLAST RADIUS. node wedge-after-cancel.mjs 2 3000 (output-run-all.txt section C): after two cancelled requests, both dispatch promises are still unsettled, client[kRunning] = 2 permanently, client[kPending] = 0, and 'client.close() resolved = false' - graceful shutdown deadlocks because close() waits on a counter that can never drain. The leak is +1 per event and is never reclaimed, so it also degrades the concurrency gate in the h2 context's busy() (client[kRunning] >= client[kMaxConcurrentStreams], lines 274-276) and the client's reported stats.

COVERAGE ASYMMETRY. test/http2-dispatcher.js:365 'Dispatcher#Upgrade rejects if stream closes before response headers' and :398 'Dispatcher#Connect rejects if stream closes before response headers' both exist and both pass here; there is no equivalent test anywhere in test/ for the ordinary request path (grep for 'closes before response headers' returns only those two). The gap in the tests mirrors the gap in the code.
  - Defender: Accepted at critical, on my own reproduction rather than on Codex's say-so. What persuaded me was not the prose but three things I built independently from it and can point at. (1) The mechanism is exactly where the finding says it is. lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:977 installs stream.once('close', onRequestStreamClose) and that handler (558-563) does this.off('data', onData), this.off('error', noop), closeStreamSession(this), this[kRequestStreamState] = null - it never calls state.abort(...) and never calls state.finalizeRequest(). finalizeRequest (726-739) is the only thing that advances client[kRunningIdx], and lib/dispatcher/client.js:328-330 derives kRunning from kPendingIdx - kRunningIdx, so if close is the only handler that runs the counter never comes back down. The upgrade/CONNECT twin two functions up, onUpgradeStreamClose (548-556), captures the state into a local before nulling it and hands it to failUpgradeStream (632-644) -> state.abort(err, true); the request path simply omits that step. (2) I reproduced the hang from scratch and it is code-specific, not probe-specific: RST_STREAM code 8 hangs while codes 0, 1, 2 and 7 all settle in the same script, so the probe demonstrably can report the other answer. (3) I instrumented a scratch copy of the working tree (lib/ verified identical by diff -r before instrumenting) to log every event the request stream emits, and for code 8 the stream emits only 'finish' and then 'close' - so onRequestStreamClose really is the only handler that runs, which is the causal link between the code reading and the hang. I also checked the two things that might have made this survivable and neither does: a 1 s headersTimeout does not rescue it (still unsettled 4 s later), and the peer subsequently destroying the entire session does not rescue it either - so 'unbounded' is right, not 'merely slow'. On severity I agree with critical and would not accept it lower: three cancelled requests leave client[kRunning] = 3 permanently and client.close() unresolved, which is an unrecoverable accounting leak in a long-lived pooled client, it is driven entirely by the peer, and NGHTTP2_CANCEL is the ordinary code an intermediary or a cancelling proxy emits rather than an exotic one. I did not verify the finding's incidental note that 'aborted' deliberately settles nothing beyond reading onAborted (1119-1121), which only detaches onData; that is consistent but it is a code reading, not something my probes exercised.
  - Proposed fix: Give the request path the step its upgrade twin already has: in onRequestStreamClose, capture the state into a local before nulling the field and then fail the request through it if it has not already been settled - i.e. mirror failUpgradeStream's guard (if state == null or state.responseReceived or request.aborted or request.completed, return; otherwise releaseRequestStream(state.stream) and state.abort(new InformationalError('HTTP/2: stream closed before response headers'), true)). That keeps the normal onEnd/onTrailers completion paths untouched, because by the time close fires on those the request is already completed, and it settles exactly the case where nothing else will. The two existing tests at test/http2-dispatcher.js:365 and :398 are the template for the regression test the ordinary request path is missing.
- **R1-F2** [high/strong] A truncated HTTP/2 response - peer sends headers, part of the body, then resets the stream - is never reported as an error on the h2 path. The body stream neither ends nor errors, no content-length validation exists (unlike lib/dispatcher/client-h1.js:403/:759), and the only thing that eventually surfaces the truncation is the body timeout: 300 s by default (lib/dispatcher/client.js:275) and never at all when bodyTimeout is 0. Reproduced for reset codes 0, 2 and 8.
  - Evidence: CODE. Nothing on the h2 response path validates that the received body matches the advertised content-length, and no handler treats an abrupt stream termination after headers as an error. Once onResponse (lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1047-1075) has run, completion depends on onEnd (1077-1097) or onTrailers (1135-1149); if the peer resets the stream mid-body, the traced run below shows neither runs, nor onError/onFrameError/onRequestStreamClose. Compare the HTTP/1 path, which does have this check: lib/dispatcher/client-h1.js:403 and :759 raise ResponseContentLengthMismatchError (defined lib/core/errors.js:200-214). The h2 file never imports or references it (only RequestContentLengthMismatchError, line 7).

REPRO (archive: _rerun2/T04-armX/repro/rst-midbody.mjs, captured in output-run-all.txt section D; node v25.6.1; prepared environment only). Server sends 200 with content-length: 10, writes 3 bytes, then resets the stream 50 ms later:
  node rst-midbody.mjs 8 2500 60000 -> headers delivered after 8 ms, outcome = null, 'RESULT: HUNG', exit 7
  node rst-midbody.mjs 0 2500 60000 -> outcome = null, HUNG, exit 7
  node rst-midbody.mjs 2 2500 60000 -> outcome = null, HUNG, exit 7 (INTERNAL_ERROR is not surfaced either)
  node rst-midbody.mjs 2 4000 1000  -> outcome = BodyTimeoutError 'HTTP/2: "stream timeout after 1000"', exit 0
  node rst-midbody.mjs 2 4000 0     -> outcome = null, HUNG, exit 7
So the ONLY thing that ever surfaces a truncated h2 response is the body timeout. Its default is 300000 ms (lib/dispatcher/client.js:275, this[kBodyTimeout] = bodyTimeout != null ? bodyTimeout : 300e3), i.e. a five-minute stall per truncated response with stock options; and bodyTimeout is explicitly allowed to be zero (client.js:187-189 rejects only negatives/non-integers), in which case the read never completes at all - the last line above.

HANDLER TRACE. On the instrumented scratch copy (derived from the working tree, lib/ verified identical by diff -r before instrumenting), the mid-body reset with code 2 produces exactly two handler entries: onResponse and onData. onEnd, onError, onFrameError, onTrailers and onRequestStreamClose never run, so the response body stream is simply abandoned in a readable state.

CONSEQUENCE. A caller that awaits res.body.text()/json() on a truncated response gets neither an error nor an end-of-stream; with the default it blocks for five minutes and then gets BodyTimeoutError, which is indistinguishable from a genuinely slow peer, and with bodyTimeout: 0 it blocks forever. A caller that instead relies on content-length being honoured has no signal at all that the payload is short - the HTTP/1 path would have raised ResponseContentLengthMismatchError.
  - Defender: Accepted, and I think the recorded severity of high is if anything understated - I am not arguing it down, and I record the argument for it being worse below rather than asking to move it. What persuaded me: I rebuilt the probe from the finding's prose and got the same five outcomes, and then I built one control the finding does not have, which turns the claim from 'the error is late' into 'the error can be absent'. The code reading holds up: grep -rn ResponseContentLengthMismatchError lib/ returns only lib/core/errors.js (200-214, 468) and lib/dispatcher/client-h1.js (11, 403, 759); lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js imports RequestContentLengthMismatchError at line 7 and never mentions the response-side error at all, so there is genuinely no content-length validation on the h2 response path. lib/dispatcher/client.js:275 is 'this[kBodyTimeout] = bodyTimeout != null ? bodyTimeout : 300e3' and client.js:187-188 rejects only negatives and non-integers, so zero is accepted, both as claimed. The severity argument: with a mid-body RST the read stalls until the body timer fires (five minutes on stock options, forever at bodyTimeout: 0), which is what the finding says. But when the peer advertises content-length: 10, delivers 3 bytes and the connection then simply goes away without any RST_STREAM, the h2 caller is handed a successful 200 with a 3-byte body and no error at all - the body timer never even enters - while the identical scenario over HTTP/1.1 on the same client rejects with ResponseContentLengthMismatchError. That is silent truncation of response data, which is a different and worse failure mode than a slow error, and it is caused by the same missing check the finding identifies. I am recording it here as corroboration inside R1-F2 rather than filing it separately, because it is the same defect and a near-duplicate finding would be an abuse of the mechanism.
  - Proposed fix: Track bytesRead against the advertised content-length on the h2 response path and raise the error the HTTP/1 path already raises. Concretely: record the advertised content-length on the per-request state in onResponse, accumulate in onData, and in onEnd/onTrailers, when state.responseReceived is set and the accumulated length does not match a non-null advertised length (excluding HEAD, as client-h1.js:758 does), fail the response with ResponseContentLengthMismatchError instead of calling request.onResponseEnd. Separately, an abrupt stream termination after headers should fail the read rather than be left to the body timer - which is the same missing 'close means fail if not already settled' step as R1-F1, so one fix in onRequestStreamClose covers both the pre-headers and post-headers abandonment while the content-length check covers the silent-truncation case in section G.
- **R2-F1** [high/strong] A response body that the peer truncates with RST_STREAM after headers is handed to the caller as a normal, successful, COMPLETE response on the h2 path. onEnd (lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1077-1097) branches on one thing only - state.responseReceived - and calls request.onResponseEnd({}) followed by state.finalizeRequest(); it never consults stream.rstCode or stream.closed, although Node emits 'end' on a stream it has already destroyed with the peer's reset code recorded. Through the ordinary promise API this yields HTTP 200 with 3 of an advertised 10 bytes, in ~57 ms, with no error anywhere, for RST_STREAM codes 8 (CANCEL) and 0 (NO_ERROR), and identically when the response advertises no content-length at all. This is silent corruption of response data, not a late error, and it is remotely triggerable by any peer or intermediary that cancels a stream. It is NOT covered by the fixes proposed for the two accepted findings: the content-length comparison proposed for R1-F2 cannot fire when no content-length is advertised, and the 'fail on close if not already settled' step proposed for R1-F1 cannot fire because the request is already completed by the time 'close' is emitted. The ordinary request path is also the only path in the file that ignores the reset code - the upgrade/CONNECT path reads this.rstCode at lines 649-650 and fails the request on it.
  - Evidence: CODE. lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1077-1097, onEnd:
    stream.off('end', onEnd)
    releaseRequestStream(stream)
    // If we received a response, this is a normal completion
    if (state.responseReceived) {
      if (!request.aborted && !request.completed) { request.onResponseEnd({}) }
      state.finalizeRequest()
    } else { state.abort(new InformationalError('HTTP/2: stream half-closed (remote)'), true) }
There is no reference to stream.rstCode, stream.closed or stream.aborted anywhere in the branch. grep -n rstCode lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js returns exactly two hits, both inside onUpgradeStreamError (649-650: if (typeof this.rstCode === 'number' && this.rstCode !== 0) failUpgradeStream(...)). The upgrade path consults the reset code; the ordinary request path never does. The same asymmetry that produced R1-F1.

REPRO (archive: _rerun2/T04-armX/repro; clean rerun captured in codex-r2-output-run-all.txt, 74801 bytes, sections A and B; interpreter node v25.6.1; dependencies = the prepared frozen environment only, reached by NODE_PATH, nothing installed; ls -d $ENVDIR/node_modules/undici -> No such file or directory; every probe prints the absolute path it loads the library from and in every run that is the working tree under review). Server: stock node:http2 createServer, responds 200, writes 3 bytes, then calls stream.close(<code>) 50 ms later. Client: the project's H2CClient, ordinary promise API, await res.body.text().
  codex-r2-truncation-rst.mjs cl   8 2500 60000 -> {"kind":"SUCCESS","statusCode":200,"advertisedContentLength":"10","bytesDelivered":3,"ms":57}, exit 8
  codex-r2-truncation-rst.mjs nocl 8 2500 60000 -> SUCCESS, advertisedContentLength null, bytesDelivered 3, ms 57, exit 8
  codex-r2-truncation-rst.mjs cl   0 2500 60000 -> SUCCESS, 3 of 10 bytes, ms 57, exit 8
  codex-r2-truncation-rst.mjs nocl 0 2500 60000 -> SUCCESS, no advertised length, 3 bytes, ms 57, exit 8
  CONTROL codex-r2-truncation-rst.mjs cl 2 2500 60000 -> BODY_ERROR 'Stream closed with error code NGHTTP2_INTERNAL_ERROR', exit 0 - the probe demonstrably prints the other answer.
  CONTROL codex-r2-truncation-rst.mjs clean 0 2500 60000 -> SUCCESS with all 10 of 10 bytes, exit 0.

EMISSION TRACE (section D, codex-r2-emitorder.mjs; node:http2 is wrapped inside the probe process only, so the working tree is NOT instrumented). Mid-body reset, code 8: the stream emits finish, response, resume, data, then 'end' with destroyed=true closed=true rstCode=8 readableEnded=true, then 'close'. So undici's onEnd runs with the reset code sitting on the stream object and treats it as a clean end. The comparison run on a complete response (D11) emits 'end' with rstCode=0 destroyed=false closed=FALSE; the RST_STREAM(NO_ERROR) run emits 'end' with rstCode=0 destroyed=false closed=TRUE. The discriminating information is therefore present at onEnd time in both reset cases (rstCode!==0 for code 8, stream.closed===true for code 0) and is simply not read. Backpressure does not change the outcome: pause-rst 8 and pause-rst-nocl 8 also end successfully with 3 bytes.

COUNTERFACTUAL (section B). A scratch copy of the working tree, derived from the tree itself (the transcript records 'LIB IDENTICAL BEFORE PATCHING' from diff -r, the full patch as diff -u, and 'REST OF LIB IDENTICAL AFTER PATCHING'), with onEnd changed to fail the response when the stream was reset (rstTerminated = (typeof stream.rstCode === 'number' && stream.rstCode !== 0) || stream.closed === true):
  patched, cl 8   -> BODY_ERROR InformationalError 'HTTP/2: response truncated by RST_STREAM (code 8)'
  patched, nocl 8 -> same error
  patched, cl 0   -> BODY_ERROR ... (code 0)
  patched, nocl 0 -> same error
  patched, clean  -> still SUCCESS with all 10 bytes
That is the causal link: the single missing check in onEnd is what turns a truncated response into a reported success, and adding it does not disturb normal completion.

WHY THE ACCEPTED FIXES DO NOT COVER IT. The R1-F2 fix proposed by the defender compares accumulated bytes against an advertised content-length; runs A2 and A4 advertise none, and streaming h2 responses (SSE, chunked JSON, downloads without a length) routinely advertise none. The R1-F1 fix proposed by the defender fails the request from onRequestStreamClose only when it has not already been settled; the trace shows 'end' precedes 'close', so request.completed is already true and the guard returns. Neither fix reaches this case.
  - Defender: Accepted at the recorded severity, on my own reproduction - and I want to be plain that I accept it despite failing to reproduce it the way the finding's own repro describes, because the failure turned out to be a property of the probe rather than of the claim.

What I could not reproduce. Built from the finding's prose with a stock node:http2 peer that responds 200 with content-length: 10, writes 3 bytes and then calls stream.close(8) fifty milliseconds later, the caller does not receive a short-bodied success here; it receives nothing at all and hangs. The reason is in section B of my archive: node's stream.close() calls stream.end() on a writable side that is still open, and a node server that has advertised ten bytes and written three cannot legally end that stream, so the SERVER's own session collapses with 'ERR_HTTP2_SESSION_ERROR Session closed with error code 1' and no frame reaches the client. With no content-length advertised the same peer does deliver a short-bodied success in 57 ms, matching the finding's numbers - but section C shows that in that case node's server sent END_STREAM first and only then the RST_STREAM ('end' arrives with rstCode=0, closed=false, destroyed=false), i.e. the peer framed a complete three-byte response, and reporting success for that is defensible rather than a defect. So on the finding's own instrument, one variant is unreproducible here and the other is arguably correct behaviour.

What settles it in the finding's favour. I built a peer that emits the frames by hand over a plain TCP socket so that DATA carries no END_STREAM before the RST_STREAM - a genuine mid-body reset, which node:http2 cannot express. Against that peer the claim reproduces exactly, including the headline: content-length: 10 advertised, 3 bytes delivered, RST_STREAM(CANCEL) mid-body, and the ordinary promise API returns statusCode 200 with a 3-byte body and no error, in 56 ms. The same with no content-length, and the same with code 0. The emission trace explains why and confirms the finding's mechanism precisely: 'end' is emitted with destroyed=true, closed=true, rstCode=8 for code 8, and with closed=true, rstCode=0 for code 0, so the discriminating information really is sitting on the stream at onEnd time, and onEnd (lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1077-1097) branches on state.responseReceived alone. grep -n rstCode over the file returns exactly two hits, both at 649-650 in onUpgradeStreamError: the upgrade path consults the reset code, the ordinary request path never does.

I also checked the finding's two coverage arguments rather than taking them. R1-F2's content-length comparison cannot fire on the runs with no advertised length - confirmed, those runs advertise none and still succeed with three bytes; note however that it WOULD cover the advertised-10-of-3 variant, so the two fixes overlap there and only the no-content-length case is strictly uncovered. R1-F1's 'fail from close if not already settled' cannot fire - confirmed by the trace, 'end' precedes 'close' in every mid-body run, so the request is already completed when close arrives and the guard returns.

On severity I accept high and am not arguing it down. I record that I considered critical: this is silent corruption of response data handed to the caller as a success, it is driven entirely by the peer, and CANCEL and NO_ERROR are the ordinary codes an intermediary or a cancelling proxy emits. I leave it at the recorded high because the caller at least receives a truthful byte count and, where a content-length is advertised, the fix already accepted for R1-F2 would also catch it.
  - Proposed fix: In onEnd, before treating a received response as a normal completion, consult the stream's terminal state the way the upgrade path already does at 649-650: if the stream was reset (a numeric rstCode other than zero, or closed before the response was framed as complete), fail the response rather than calling request.onResponseEnd. Section E shows the information is present on the stream object at that moment in both the CANCEL and the NO_ERROR case. This is complementary to, not a substitute for, the content-length check accepted under R1-F2: the reset check covers responses that advertise no length, the content-length check covers a peer that frames a short body as complete.
- **R2-F2** [high/strong] The h2 client retires queue slots by INDEX rather than by identity - finalizeRequest (lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:726-739) does client[kQueue][client[kRunningIdx]++] = null - while the same file advertises defaultPipelining: Infinity (line 234), so several requests are in flight at once and completions are routinely out of order. A single out-of-order completion therefore permanently misaligns the queue: the slot that is nulled belongs to a DIFFERENT, still-in-flight request, and the completed request's own slot stays inside the running window with request[kRequestStreamId] already set to null by detachRequestFromStream (128-132), reached from onEnd -> releaseRequestStream (1011-1032). getGoAwayPendingIdx (110-126) then reads that stale slot, sees a non-number stream id, and reports it as the first request the peer did not accept; onHttp2SessionGoAway (409-457) closes and re-queues EVERYTHING from that index onward. Three distinct failures follow, all observed in one run: (a) a request the peer had already received and was still processing, on a stream at or below the GOAWAY's lastStreamID - explicitly declared processed by the peer - is RST_STREAMed and re-sent on a fresh connection, i.e. duplicate delivery, which is exactly what RFC 7540 section 6.8's lastStreamID exists to prevent; (b) the already-completed request is re-dispatched, trips assert(!this.completed) in lib/core/request.js:311 inside writeH2's try block (792-798) and is handed to util.errorRequest, so a caller that already received a successful response receives a SECOND terminal callback carrying an AssertionError; (c) the genuinely in-flight request whose slot was consumed vanishes from the queue entirely and is neither retried nor errored - its caller never settles at all. The retry gate canRetryRequestAfterGoAway (155-159) looks only at the body kind, never at the method or at request.idempotent, so nothing downstream prevents (a).
  - Evidence: CODE. finalizeRequest, lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:726-739, the whole retirement step is 'client[kQueue][client[kRunningIdx]++] = null' - the slot at the running index, not the slot of the request that finished. connectH2's returned context sets defaultPipelining: Infinity at line 234 and busy() (269-305) admits further idempotent requests while client[kRunning] > 0, so concurrent streams are the normal case. onEnd (1077-1097) calls releaseRequestStream (1011-1032), which calls detachRequestFromStream (128-132): request[kRequestStreamId] = null; request[kRequestStream] = null. getGoAwayPendingIdx (110-126) scans client[kQueue] from kRunningIdx to kPendingIdx and returns the first index where 'typeof request[kRequestStreamId] !== "number" || request[kRequestStreamId] > maxAcceptedStreamID'. onHttp2SessionGoAway (409-457) then loops i from that index to the previous kPendingIdx, calling closeRequestStream(request) (161-171, which RSTs the stream with NGHTTP2_REFUSED_STREAM) and pushing the request into retriableRequests when canRetryRequestAfterGoAway (155-159) is true - that predicate is 'body == null || util.isBuffer(body) || util.isBlobLike(body)', with no reference to the method or to request.idempotent - and finally splices the queue at 436-440 so those requests become pending again. The loop never re-checks each individual request's stream id against lastStreamID; it trusts that queue order and stream order agree, which the index-based retirement above has already broken.

REPRO (archive: _rerun2/T04-armX/repro/codex-r2-goaway-dup.mjs, clean rerun in codex-r2-output-run-all.txt section C; node v25.6.1; the prepared frozen environment only, nothing installed; the probe prints the absolute path it loads the library from and it is the working tree in every run). Three GET requests on one H2CClient over h2c: A=/slow-a (stream 1, never answered), B=/fast (stream 3, answered immediately), C=/slow-c (stream 5, received by the server, never answered). B completes out of order; then the server sends GOAWAY(NO_ERROR, lastStreamID=5), so every open stream is at or below the last stream the peer declares it processed.
  C1  'ooo 5 2500 dispatch' -> queue after B's out-of-order completion is [null, /fast, /slow-c] with runningIdx=1, pendingIdx=3: A's slot was nulled although A is still in flight, and completed B's slot is still there. After the GOAWAY the server log reads:
      GET /slow-a streamId=1 conn#1
      GET /fast   streamId=3 conn#1
      GET /slow-c streamId=5 conn#1
      GET /slow-c streamId=1 conn#2   <- re-sent on a new connection
    paths delivered more than once = ["/slow-c"]; 'RESULT: DUPLICATE WIRE DELIVERY', exit 9.
    extra terminal callbacks = [{"tag":"B","first":"end","second":{"kind":"error","err":"AssertionError: The expression evaluated to a falsy value:"}}] - B had already been handed a successful response and is then handed an AssertionError.
    unsettled tags = ["A","C"]; A is gone from the queue ([null, /slow-c] at the end) and never settles.
  C2  CONTROL 'ctrl 5 2500 dispatch' - identical timing, identical GOAWAY, but nothing completes out of order: queue stays [/slow-a, /slow-b, /slow-c], no request is re-queued, no duplicate, no extra callback, exit 0. The trigger is specifically the out-of-order completion, not the GOAWAY.
  C3  'ooo 5 2500 promise' - the same duplicate wire delivery through the ordinary promise API, exit 9.
  C4  'ooo auto 2500 dispatch' - same result when Node chooses lastStreamID itself, exit 9.

SECOND-CALLBACK MECHANISM. writeH2 792-798 wraps request.onRequestStart(abort, null) in try/catch; lib/core/request.js:309-311 begins onRequestStart with assert(!this.aborted); assert(!this.completed). The re-dispatched completed request throws there and the catch calls util.errorRequest (lib/core/util.js:841-848), which calls request.onResponseError(err). That is a terminal callback delivered after a successful terminal callback on the same request.

HONEST LIMITS. The duplicate I demonstrated is a GET; I did not demonstrate a duplicated POST, because busy() refuses to dispatch a non-idempotent request while others are in flight, so a POST cannot easily be one of the concurrent streams. That gate is a caller-controllable default: request.idempotent may be set explicitly, and canRetryRequestAfterGoAway does not consult it. The re-sent request in C1 is nonetheless one the server had already received in full and was processing, which is duplicate delivery irrespective of method. I did not establish how long failure (c) persists beyond the 2.5 s observation window; the queue at the end of the run no longer contains A at all, so nothing in the GOAWAY path can settle it later.
  - Defender: Accepted at the recorded severity, on a reproduction I built from the prose, and I was able to push it one step past the finding's own honest limit.

The mechanism is exactly as described and I confirmed each link. finalizeRequest retires 'client[kQueue][client[kRunningIdx]++] = null' - the slot at the running index, not the slot of the request that finished - while the h2 context advertises defaultPipelining: Infinity at line 234 and busy() admits further idempotent requests while requests are in flight, so out-of-order completion is the normal case rather than a corner. onEnd reaches releaseRequestStream, which calls detachRequestFromStream, so the completed request's own slot stays inside the running window with a null stream id. getGoAwayPendingIdx then reports that stale slot as the first request the peer did not accept, and onHttp2SessionGoAway closes and re-queues everything from there. canRetryRequestAfterGoAway is 'body == null || util.isBuffer(body) || util.isBlobLike(body)' with no reference to the method or to request.idempotent, exactly as claimed.

All three consequences reproduced in one run, against a control that shows the trigger is specifically the out-of-order completion and not the GOAWAY. (a) A request the peer had already received on a stream at or below the GOAWAY's lastStreamID - a stream the peer explicitly declares it processed - is re-sent on a fresh connection. (b) A request that had already been handed a successful onResponseEnd is re-dispatched, trips assert(!this.completed) in lib/core/request.js:311, and is handed a second, terminal onResponseError carrying an AssertionError. (c) The genuinely in-flight request whose slot was consumed disappears from the queue and is neither retried nor errored; it never settles.

Past the finding's honest limit. The finding says it could not demonstrate a duplicated non-idempotent request because busy() will not admit one while other streams are open. That gate is caller-controllable through the documented idempotent dispatch option, and with it set the same run duplicates a POST carrying a body: the peer receives 'POST /slow-c streamId=5 conn#1' and then 'POST /slow-c streamId=1 conn#2'. I state the caveat plainly - this required the caller to opt in, so it does not show a default POST being duplicated; what it does show is that the only thing standing between this bug and duplicate delivery of a request with side effects is a dispatch-time gate on the incoming request, not anything in the retry path itself, which never consults the method at all.

On severity I accept high and am not arguing it down. Duplicate delivery of a request the peer has declared processed is the precise failure RFC 7540's lastStreamID exists to prevent, and a second terminal callback after a successful one is a contract violation that no handler can be expected to tolerate; I would not object to critical. I record one scope limit of my own: like the claimant, I did not establish how long consequence (c) persists beyond the observation window, only that the request is absent from the queue at the end of the run, so nothing in the GOAWAY path can settle it later.
- **R1-F3** [medium/moderate] onRequestStreamClose nulls stream[kRequestStreamState] while leaving the 'end', 'error', 'frameError', 'aborted', 'timeout', 'trailers' and 'response' listeners attached (it never calls removeRequestStreamListeners), and all of those handlers dereference that field with no null guard - unlike releaseRequestStream and failUpgradeStream, which both guard it, and unlike onUpgradeStreamClose, which captures the state into a local before nulling. Any post-close event on a request stream is therefore an uncaught TypeError from inside an event handler, which crashes the process rather than failing one request. Inference: I could not construct the firing order in the probes I ran.
  - Evidence: CODE. onRequestStreamClose (lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:558-563) nulls this[kRequestStreamState] but detaches only two listeners ('data' and the noop 'error'). It does NOT call removeRequestStreamListeners (998-1009), which is the function that exists precisely to detach the rest. Left attached after the state has been nulled are the handlers registered at lines 983-989: 'response' (onResponse), 'end' (onEnd), 'error' (onError), 'frameError' (onFrameError), 'aborted' (onAborted), 'timeout' (onTimeout) and 'trailers' (onTrailers) - and 'timeout' and 'aborted' are registered with .on, not .once.
Every one of those handlers dereferences the nulled field without a guard:
  onData 1034-1036: const { request } = stream[kRequestStreamState]
  onResponse 1047-1050, onEnd 1077-1080, onTrailers 1135-1138: same destructuring of state and state.request
  onError 1099-1106 / onFrameError 1109-1116: state.abort(...)
  onTimeout 1123-1132: state.responseReceived, state.bodyTimeout, state.headersTimeout, state.abort
Any of these firing after 'close' throws TypeError: Cannot read properties of null, from inside a stream event handler, i.e. an uncaught exception that takes down the process rather than failing one request.
That the authors consider a null state reachable is on the record two functions away: releaseRequestStream (1011-1032) opens with 'const state = stream[kRequestStreamState]; if (state == null) { return }', and failUpgradeStream (632-644) has the same guard. The equivalent close handler on the upgrade path, onUpgradeStreamClose (548-556), reads the state into a local BEFORE nulling it and hands that local to failUpgradeStream - so the upgrade path is safe against exactly this and the request path is not.

HONEST LIMIT (this is why the support level is moderate, not strong): I did not manage to construct an ordering in which one of these events fires after 'close'. In the mid-body probe the 'timeout' event does fire on an already-reset stream and does reach onTimeout (rst-midbody.mjs 2 4000 1000 yields BodyTimeoutError), but in that run 'close' had not yet fired, so the state was still populated; in the pre-headers probe 'close' fires but the timer is cleared first. The defect is therefore an unguarded dereference reachable in principle from six live listeners, demonstrated to be inconsistent with the file's own defensive pattern, rather than an executed crash.
[R2 escalation] Archive _rerun2/T04-armX/repro, section D of the clean rerun in codex-r2-output-run-all.txt, produced by codex-r2-emitorder.mjs. The probe wraps http2.connect and session.request inside its own process; the working tree is not instrumented and not modified (the transcript prints git status --porcelain and git diff --stat for the review repository before and after the run, and both show only the frozen review window and the runner's .council/ directory). Each emit line records the event name, whether the state symbol on the stream is already null, and destroyed/closed/rstCode/aborted/readableEnded.
  D1 pre 0 -> finish, readable, end, close; settles InformationalError 'HTTP/2: stream half-closed (remote)'.
  D2 pre 1 -> finish, ERROR (destroyed=true closed=true rstCode=1), close; settles NGHTTP2_PROTOCOL_ERROR.
  D3 pre 2 -> finish, ERROR (rstCode=2), close; settles NGHTTP2_INTERNAL_ERROR.
  D4 pre 7 -> finish, ERROR (rstCode=7), close; settles NGHTTP2_REFUSED_STREAM.
  D5 pre 8 -> finish, close, and nothing else; outcome null (this independently reproduces R1-F1 from outside the library).
  D6 mid 2 -> finish, response, resume, data, error, close.
  D7 mid 8 -> finish, response, resume, data, end, close.
  D8 mid 8 with the consumer returning false from onResponseData so the readable side is paused with buffered data when the reset lands -> finish, response, resume, data, end, close.
  D9 same as D8 with no content-length advertised -> same sequence.
  D10 caller aborts via AbortController while the peer is silent -> finish only, within the window.
  D11 complete response -> finish, response, resume, data, end (rstCode=0 destroyed=false closed=false), close.
In all eleven runs: 'listed events emitted after close = []' and 'uncaught = none'. Codes 1, 2 and 7 emit 'error' BEFORE 'close', with the state still populated - which is the positive mechanism, not merely an absence of counter-examples. Code reading unchanged and undisputed: onRequestStreamClose 558-563 detaches only 'data' and the noop 'error' and nulls the state; the seven handlers registered at 983-989 stay attached; removeRequestStreamListeners 998-1009 is never called from it; detachRequestFromStream 128-132 is never called from it either, so request[kRequestStream] keeps pointing at the destroyed stream.

  B.detach    (capture the state into a local, then detachRequestFromStream when state.request[kRequestStream] === this) -> queue at t=150 ms is [{"path":"/x","streamId":null,"streamIdType":"object","holdsStream":false,"cleanupStillSet":false}]; the request becomes visible to getGoAwayPendingIdx and is re-queued and re-delivered. SECTION D - negative results, recorded because they bound the claim. D1: a covering GOAWAY followed by the peer finishing the stream is handled correctly - 200 "hello" at +315 ms, a follow-up request succeeds on a fresh connection at +810 ms, kRunning back to 0, client.close() resolves. D2: a covering GOAWAY with a peer that never finishes settles with UND_ERR_HEADERS_TIMEOUT at +807 ms under headersTimeout 800, i.e. the headers timeout does rescue that case, unlike the pre-headers-CANCEL case where the stream is already destroyed and the timer is gone. Neither of these is broken and I say so.

POST-CLOSE SEARCH, the conceded component. Across my round-2 emission probe (eleven orderings), the defender's round-1 and round-2 traces, and this round's five stock-peer orderings in section A, no run has emitted 'response', 'end', 'error', 'frameError', 'aborted', 'timeout' or 'trailers' after 'close', and no uncaughtException has been raised. I am no longer asserting the crash.

METHOD NOTE, recorded because it invalidated my first attempt and is exactly the hazard the brief warns about: node resolves the NEAREST node_modules before consulting NODE_PATH. My first driver put one node_modules beside the probes and switched NODE_PATH between the working tree and the patched scratch copy, so all three 'patched' runs silently loaded the UNPATCHED tree and printed the unpatched result. The require.resolve line each probe prints is what caught it. The archived driver gives each side its own run directory with its own nearest node_modules and prints both resolutions before any probe runs.
  - Defender: I split this the way codex's own round-3 message splits it. On my own measurements the split now falls the same way for both of us, so it should close as agreed-narrowed, not as a live dispute.

ACCEPTED COMPONENT - the cleanup omission and the consequence codex reopened on. I rebuilt the experiment from codex's PROSE, not from its archive, on the unmodified working tree, and it reproduces independently. Code reading, re-checked line by line: onRequestStreamClose (lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:558-564) detaches only 'data' and the noop 'error', nulls this[kRequestStreamState], and calls neither removeRequestStreamListeners (998-1009) nor detachRequestFromStream (128-133); its twin onUpgradeStreamClose (548-556) reads the state into a local before nulling it, and releaseRequestStream (1011-1032) and failUpgradeStream (632-644) both guard on a null state - so the file's own defensive pattern is the one this handler skips. Measurement (section A of my archive): with a pre-headers RST_STREAM(NGHTTP2_CANCEL), after 'close' has run the destroyed stream still carries six handlers - response 1, end 1, error 1, frameError 1, aborted 1, trailers 1 - with streamStateStillOnStream = false, i.e. the state really has been nulled underneath them. The queued request still carries streamId 1 as a NUMBER and still holds the destroyed stream, with cleanupStillSet true, unchanged at t=150ms and t=1150ms. That numeric id is exactly the condition getGoAwayPendingIdx (110-125) tests, so it walks past the request and the GOAWAY recovery path - the last mechanism that could have rescued it - cannot see it. Controls rst=0, rst=2 and rst=7 settle in the same probe, and rst=8 with no GOAWAY strands identically.

REJECTED COMPONENT - the consequence the recorded claim text actually asserts: 'Any post-close event on a request stream is therefore an uncaught TypeError from inside an event handler, which crashes the process rather than failing one request.' No uncaughtException was raised in any of my five runs this round, and no listed event was emitted after 'close' in any of them; that is on top of the five orderings this seat traced in round 1, the eleven in codex's round-2 emission probe, the five raw-frame sequences in round 2, and codex's own five stock-peer orderings in round 3. Codex has now withdrawn the assertion on evidence of its own. I still record it as rejected because the finding is adjudicated on its claim text and that text still carries the crash.

SUPPORT LEVEL - a correction against my own seat, not against codex. In rounds 1 and 2 this seat contested the level down to 'weak' and codex accepted that. My measurements this round defeat that position: the narrowed component is now directly measured on the unmodified tree by both sides independently, so codex's self-assigned 'moderate' is the defensible level and I withdraw the contest. I would rather correct the record than be consistent with my predecessor.

SEVERITY - medium is right and I argue it in neither direction. The omission's demonstrated consequence is to remove the last recovery route for a request that R1-F1 (accepted, critical) has already stranded; on its own, after R1-F1 is fixed, what is left is a destroyed stream retained by a request that is about to leave the queue plus six handlers that are never fired. That is a medium, not a high.

ONE CAUTION ON THE PROPOSED FIX, because it is the same caution I raise against R3-F1 and it cuts both ways. Adding the detach makes a CANCEL-stranded request visible to getGoAwayPendingIdx and therefore re-queueable - and section C of my archive shows, on the UNMODIFIED tree with no patch at all, that the re-queue path it becomes visible to has no ceiling. The detach is still right; it should land together with a settle-on-close (R1-F1) so that the request leaves the queue rather than re-entering it, and not on its own.
  - Proposed fix: Give onRequestStreamClose the two steps its upgrade twin already has - read the state into a local before nulling the field, then call removeRequestStreamListeners(this) and detach the request from the destroyed stream through the guarded path releaseRequestStream already uses - but land it together with the settle-on-close that R1-F1 requires, not on its own. Order matters here: the detach alone makes a CANCEL-stranded request visible to getGoAwayPendingIdx and therefore re-queueable, and section C of my archive shows the re-queue path has no ceiling even on the unmodified tree. With the settle-on-close in place the request leaves the queue instead of re-entering it, and the detach is then pure cleanup. Pin it with a request-path analogue of the two upgrade/CONNECT 'closes before response headers' tests at test/http2-dispatcher.js:365 and :398, asserting both that the caller settles and that the three symbol-keyed request fields are null afterwards - the same assertion the two passing tests at :614 and :676 already make on the other two paths.
- **R3-F1** [high/strong] The GOAWAY re-queue path has no attempt budget, no backoff and no de-duplication, so re-delivery of a single request is unbounded. onHttp2SessionGoAway (lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:409-457) closes each selected request's stream and pushes it back in front of the pending window via the splice at 436-440; the only gate is canRetryRequestAfterGoAway (155-159), which tests the body kind and nothing else. Nothing anywhere records that a request has already been re-delivered: grep -nE 'attempt|retryCount|retries|backoff' over lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js and lib/dispatcher/client.js returns no match at all. Today the pre-headers-CANCEL request of R1-F1/R1-F3 never enters that loop only because the same omission that strands it also hides it from getGoAwayPendingIdx (110-126). The moment the minimal fix both sides have converged on makes such a request re-queueable, the identical peer that produced the silent hang instead produces an unbounded connect-and-re-deliver storm: 1378 fresh TCP connections and 1558 wire deliveries of ONE GET in 2.5 s, 551 deliveries per second, with the caller still never settled and client.close() still unresolved. The remediation for the accepted findings therefore converts a silent hang into remote-triggered duplicate delivery at connection-storm rate unless a retry budget is added at the same time - and duplicate delivery is the precise failure R2-F2 was accepted for.
  - Evidence: CODE, verified against the working tree. onHttp2SessionGoAway, lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:409-457: it takes pendingIdx = getGoAwayPendingIdx(client, lastStreamID), loops from there to the previous kPendingIdx calling closeRequestStream(request) (161-171) and pushing into retriableRequests when canRetryRequestAfterGoAway(request) is true, then rebuilds the queue with 'client[kQueue].length = pendingIdx; client[kQueue].push(...retriableRequests, ...remainingPendingRequests)' and sets client[kPendingIdx] = pendingIdx before calling client[kResume](). canRetryRequestAfterGoAway, 155-159, is exactly 'const { body } = request; return body == null || util.isBuffer(body) || util.isBlobLike(body)' - no method, no idempotent flag, and no attempt count. Nothing in the loop, in requeueUnsentRequest (151-153) or in the request object records that this request has already been sent once. Section E of my transcript: 'grep -nE "attempt|retryCount|retries|backoff" lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js lib/dispatcher/client.js' prints nothing and the transcript records 'grep exit status = 1  (1 = no match anywhere)'.

MEASUREMENT (archive: _rerun2/T04-armX/repro, section C of the clean rerun in codex-r3-output-run-all.txt, probe codex-r3-cancel-retry.mjs). The peer is a stock node:http2 server whose cancelling behaviour is a parameter: 'first' cancels wire delivery #1 with RST_STREAM(NGHTTP2_CANCEL) before any response headers and answers every later delivery with 200; 'always' cancels every delivery. In both cases it then sends GOAWAY(NO_ERROR) covering the reset stream. The probe counts wire deliveries and TCP connections seen by the peer.
  C1  unpatched working tree, policy=first  -> deliveries 1, connections 1, settled = null, client.close() resolved = false, exit 7
  C2  scratch copy + detach, policy=first   -> deliveries 2, connections 2, settled = {"kind":"OK","status":200,"body":"hello","atMs":111}, exit 0   [CONTROL: the fix works against a peer that cancels once, and the probe demonstrably prints the other answer]
  C3  unpatched working tree, policy=always -> deliveries 1, connections 1, settled = null, exit 7                                                    [CONTROL: the storm is not a property of the peer alone]
  C4  scratch copy + detach, policy=always, GOAWAY 100 ms after the reset -> 28 wire deliveries, 25 TCP connections in 2.5 s, settled = null, client.close() resolved = false, exit 9
  C5  scratch copy + detach, policy=always, GOAWAY with no delay          -> 1558 wire deliveries, 1378 TCP connections in 2501 ms = 551 deliveries/sec, settled = null, client.close() resolved = false, exit 9
The only difference between C3 and C5 is the six-line detach in onRequestStreamClose; the peer, the probe and the timings are identical. That is the causal link: the absence of a retry budget is what turns the fix's re-queue into a storm.

THE PATCH IS EXHIBITED IN FULL. The scratch copy is derived from the working tree itself; the transcript prints 'LIB IDENTICAL BEFORE PATCHING: yes (diff -r clean)', then the entire patch as diff -u (capture the state into a local, and if state.request[kRequestStream] === this call detachRequestFromStream(state.request); nothing else), then 'REST OF LIB IDENTICAL AFTER PATCHING: yes' from diff -r -x client-h2.js. codex-r3-patch.mjs refuses to run unless it finds the target function verbatim.

REACHABLE WITHOUT ANY PATCH, on the record already. getGoAwayPendingIdx selects any queued request whose stream id is not a number. R2-F2, accepted, establishes that an out-of-order completion leaves a COMPLETED request inside the running window with a null stream id - which is precisely that condition - and that the GOAWAY handler then re-queues from there and re-delivers a request the peer had already received. So the shipped tree already has a demonstrated route into this re-queue path; what this finding adds is that the path has no ceiling once entered.

HONEST LIMIT, stated in the claim and not worked around: the 551/sec storm was measured on a scratch copy carrying the fix, because on the unmodified tree the cancelled request is stranded before it can be re-queued (C1, C3). The code reading - that no attempt budget, backoff or de-duplication exists anywhere in the re-queue path - is established against the unmodified working tree alone.

  - Defender: Two separable claims are welded together here, and my own measurement settles them in opposite directions.

ACCEPTED COMPONENT - the defect itself, at the severity claimed. The code reading is correct and I verified every line of it against the working tree rather than taking it from the transcript. onHttp2SessionGoAway (lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:409-457) takes pendingIdx from getGoAwayPendingIdx, closes each selected request's stream, pushes the retriable ones into retriableRequests, rebuilds the queue with 'client[kQueue].length = pendingIdx; client[kQueue].push(...retriableRequests, ...remainingPendingRequests)', sets client[kPendingIdx] = pendingIdx and calls client[kResume](). canRetryRequestAfterGoAway (155-158) is three lines that read request.body and nothing else - no method, no idempotency flag, no attempt count. 'grep -nE "attempt|retryCount|retries|backoff" lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js lib/dispatcher/client.js' matches nothing, exit status 1, printed in section 0 of my transcript. Nothing anywhere records that a request has already been delivered. I accept that there is no attempt budget, no backoff and no de-duplication, that re-delivery of one request is consequently unbounded, and I accept it at high. I do not contest the support level; my own evidence raises it rather than lowering it.

REJECTED COMPONENT - the causal attribution, which is what makes this a finding against the change under review rather than against the file it lives in. The claim states that 'today the pre-headers-CANCEL request of R1-F1/R1-F3 never enters that loop only because the same omission that strands it also hides it from getGoAwayPendingIdx', and that the storm appears 'the moment the minimal fix both sides have converged on makes such a request re-queueable', so that 'the remediation for the accepted findings therefore converts a silent hang into remote-triggered duplicate delivery at connection-storm rate'. That is not so, and the honest limit codex records - that the 551/sec measurement was taken on a patched copy - is the reason the missing control was not run. I ran it. Section C of my archive, against the UNMODIFIED working tree with no patch anywhere, no RST_STREAM at any point and no stranded request: a peer that answers the first HEADERS frame with GOAWAY(NO_ERROR, lastStreamID=0) and closes the connection produces 28511 wire deliveries of ONE GET over 28511 fresh TCP connections in 2500 ms - about 10054 deliveries per second, roughly eighteen times the rate reported for the patched copy - with the caller never settling and client.close() never resolving. The control C2, the same probe with lastStreamID=1 so the GOAWAY covers our stream, produces 1 delivery on 1 connection and settles UND_ERR_SOCKET at +4 ms, so the probe demonstrably prints the other answer.

Three things follow, and I want each of them on the record separately.

(1) The remediation does not convert a hang into a storm. The storm is already there, and is faster without the remediation than with it. A retry budget is worth adding, but 'unless a retry budget is added at the same time' misstates the dependency: the budget is owed to the shipped tree today, not to the fix.

(2) The mechanism is outside the frozen review window. Section 0 of my transcript prints the hunk headers of git diff -U0: the change under review is the added regions 1-79, 128-133, 536-547, 558-564 and 998-1149. onHttp2SessionGoAway (409-457), getGoAwayPendingIdx (110-125), canRetryRequestAfterGoAway (155-158), requeueUnsentRequest (151-153) and closeRequestStream (161-171) are every one of them outside it, textually unchanged. The only added line in the storm path is detachRequestFromStream (128-133), a three-line field-nulling helper that is not where a budget would live and whose presence does not alter the loop. As a gate on whether this change ships, a pre-existing property of unchanged code is not a reason to block it; as a defect report against the file, it stands, which is why I accept the component rather than rejecting the finding.

(3) The 'duplicate delivery' framing is half right and the half that is wrong matters. When the GOAWAY's lastStreamID is below the stream's id, the peer has stated that it did not process the request; retrying is the correct client action under RFC 9113 section 6.8, and my C1 is that case. What is defective there is the absence of a ceiling, not the re-delivery. Genuinely duplicate delivery - the peer received and processed it, and receives it again - is R2-F2, which is already accepted at high on its own evidence and does not need this finding to carry it.

A method point worth recording because it is probably why this control was missed: node's session.goaway(code, lastStreamID) clamps lastStreamID up to the last stream the peer processed. Section B of my archive asks a stock node:http2 peer for lastStreamID=0 and the client observes lastStreamID=1. A stock node peer therefore cannot express 'I processed nothing', and the case is only reachable with a raw-frame peer, which is what section C uses.
  - Proposed fix: Two changes, and they are independent of anything this review window touches. First, put a ceiling on re-delivery: carry a per-request re-delivery counter, increment it where onHttp2SessionGoAway (409-457) pushes into retriableRequests, and have canRetryRequestAfterGoAway (155-158) refuse once the counter passes a small bound, failing the request with the GOAWAY error instead of re-queueing it forever; add backoff between attempts so that even within the bound a draining peer cannot be answered at four-figure connections per second. Second, pin it with a regression test that uses a raw-frame peer rather than node:http2's server - section B of my archive shows session.goaway() clamps lastStreamID up to the last processed stream, so a stock peer cannot express the case the test needs - asserting that one GET against a peer that repeats GOAWAY(NO_ERROR, lastStreamID=0) produces a bounded number of wire deliveries and a settled caller. Both changes belong to the pre-existing GOAWAY handler and can land before, with, or after the change under review.

_Ledger: <path>
```

### T04 — Verdict 2

```markdown
# Council Review Verdict — dbt-REDACTED

Target: working tree diff · Rounds: 2/3 · Findings: 2

**NO-SHIP: 2 accepted high/critical finding(s) require fixes.**

## Consensus — accepted findings

- **R1-F1** [high/moderate] An HTTP/2 stream that emits `aborted` and then `close` without an intervening `error`, `end`, or `trailers` leaves the request permanently in the running queue. The `aborted` handler only removes the data listener, while the close handler only updates stream/session bookkeeping; neither reports an error nor calls `finalizeRequest()`. This can stall subsequent dispatches, especially with low concurrency/pipelining limits.
  - Evidence: lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:977-989 registers independent `close` and `aborted` handlers. lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:558-563 (`onRequestStreamClose`) only removes listeners, decrements the open-stream count, and clears stream state. lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1119-1121 (`onAborted`) only removes `onData`. By contrast, lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1077-1117 and 1123-1148 are the paths that call `state.abort()`, `state.finalizeRequest()`, or complete the response.
  - Defender: Verified and reproduced end to end; I could not defend this. What persuaded me, in order. (1) The sequence exists. Sweeping every RST_STREAM error code against a node v25.6.1 client stream whose writable side is still open, with the data listener attached only from inside the response handler (the dispatcher's own listener discipline), code 8 CANCEL received before headers produces exactly 'aborted -> close' with no 'end', no 'error' and no 'trailers' anywhere in the sequence. Every other code except 0 injects an 'error'; code 0 injects an 'end'. So the claim's precondition is not hypothetical, it is one specific and legal peer behaviour. (2) The code reading in the finding is accurate. lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1119-1121 (onAborted) removes only the data listener; lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:558-563 (onRequestStreamClose) removes listeners, decrements session[kOpenStreams] via closeStreamSession and nulls stream[kRequestStreamState]; neither touches state.abort or state.finalizeRequest, and finalizeRequest at lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:726-739 is the only thing that advances client[kRunningIdx] and calls client[kResume](). (3) The consequence is real, not theoretical. A dispatcher-level run against an h2c peer that resets with CANCEL before responding leaves a POST with a slow async-iterable body recording exactly 'aborted -> close' and never settling. (4) The dispatch stall the finding predicts is real, including the 'especially with low concurrency limits' qualifier: with the peer advertising SETTINGS_MAX_CONCURRENT_STREAMS=1, the queued follow-up is never dispatched at all -- the server's received-path list contains only the first path. (5) A one-handler counterfactual confirms causation rather than correlation. I accept at the recorded severity high and do not contest support_level moderate; my own runs put the evidence at strong, but a finding's recorded level is the claimant's to state and I am not arguing it downward. One qualification that does not reduce the finding: for the commonest body shape -- a Readable piped by writeStream at lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1252-1274 -- the pipeline callback injects ERR_STREAM_PREMATURE_CLOSE and calls abort(), so that shape self-rescues; the stranding shapes are async-iterable bodies (writeIterable, lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1309-1365, which is suspended awaiting the next chunk and whose only 'close' hook is onDrain). Separately, my own finding R1-D1 records that the same defect has a strictly larger, more reachable sibling that a fix scoped to the aborted handler would not close.
  - Proposed fix: Make the aborted handler terminal rather than a listener-removal: fetch state = stream[kRequestStreamState], return if it is null, call releaseRequestStream(stream), then state.abort(new InformationalError('HTTP/2: stream aborted')) -- that is exactly the counterfactual in repro/COUNTERFACTUAL-client-h2.js and it settles the case without disturbing the shapes that already settle, because state.abort() is guarded by 'if (request.aborted || request.completed) return' at lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:741-744. Note this is necessary but not sufficient: per my finding R1-D1 the same stall is reachable with no 'aborted' event at all, so onRequestStreamClose at lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:558-563 needs the same treatment -- settle any request still bound to the stream when close arrives without a terminal event -- for the class to be closed.
- **R1-D1** [high/strong] Distinct from R1-F1 and not fixed by fixing it: a request stream that emits ONLY 'close' -- no 'aborted', no 'error', no 'end', no 'trailers' -- is also stranded forever, and this shape is far more reachable than the 'aborted' one. 'aborted' is only emitted by node while the stream's writable side is still open, i.e. only while a request body is still being written; every bodyless request (GET/HEAD, and any request whose body has already been written and ended, e.g. a Buffer body) therefore takes the close-only path instead. lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:558-563 (onRequestStreamClose) is the only handler that runs: it removes the data listener and the noop error listener, decrements the open-stream count, and nulls stream[kRequestStreamState]. It never calls state.abort() or state.finalizeRequest(), and finalizeRequest at lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:726-739 is the only thing that clears client[kQueue][client[kRunningIdx]] and calls client[kResume](), so client[kRunning] never drops and the request promise never settles. Measured, nothing rescues it: with headersTimeout and bodyTimeout both set to 500ms the request was still unsettled after 4000ms, so the onTimeout handler at lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1123-1133 does not fire once the stream has closed. Secondary code-reading observation, NOT demonstrated by my repros and not part of what I am asserting as reproduced: onRequestStreamClose nulls kRequestStreamState while leaving onError attached (it removes only the 'data' listener and the noop 'error' listener; removeRequestStreamListeners is reached only through releaseRequestStream), so an 'error' arriving after 'close' would dereference a null state at lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js:1099-1107.
  - Evidence: Node event-sequence sweep (repro/probe-codes-endstream.js, output repro/probe-codes-endstream.out, node v25.6.1): for a request opened with endStream:true, RST_STREAM code 8 (CANCEL) received before headers produces the sequence 'close' and nothing else -- no 'aborted', no 'error', no 'end'; codes 1-7 and 9-13 produce 'error(ERR_HTTP2_STREAM_ERROR) -> close' and code 0 produces 'end -> close', so the hole is specific to CANCEL. Instrumented dispatcher-level run (repro/repro-instrumented.js, output repro/repro-instrumented.out) against an h2c peer that resets the stream with CANCEL before responding, with per-stream listeners prepended onto the stream the dispatcher itself created: 'GET-no-body' records events 'close' and never settles inside the 2.5s budget (the recorded UND_ERR_DESTROYED is the forced client.destroy() at the end of the budget, not a settle); 'POST-buffer' records events 'close' and likewise never settles. Queue effect and permanence (repro/repro-getvariant-and-timeout.js, output repro/repro-getvariant-and-timeout.out, exit=10): with headersTimeout=500 and bodyTimeout=500 and a 4000ms budget, a bodyless GET reset this way stays PENDING, the queued follow-up stays PENDING, and the server's received-path list is ["/first-get"] -- the follow-up was never put on the wire at all. Counterfactual isolating this from R1-F1 (repro/COUNTERFACTUAL-client-h2.js, repro/cf-repro-instrumented.js, output repro/cf-repro-instrumented.out): a scratch copy of the working tree whose ONLY change is making the aborted handler release the stream and call state.abort() flips 'POST-slow-asyncgen' from stranded to settled while 'GET-no-body' and 'POST-buffer' remain STRANDED with events 'close'. So a fix scoped to the aborted handler demonstrably does not close this path.
  - Defender: 
  - Proposed fix: Make `onRequestStreamClose` terminal when its state still represents an unfinished request: capture the state before cleanup, remove all request-owned listeners, close session bookkeeping, clear stream state, then abort the request with an HTTP/2 stream-closed error. Preserve the current bookkeeping-only behavior when the request was already completed, aborted, or finalized through `end`, `trailers`, `error`, or timeout, so close cannot double-settle it.

_Ledger: <path>
```
